Summary

A knight from a distant land where magic and spirits are honored, an unchained force woven into the fabric of life itself. An heir to the ruling House of the Empire, where magic is something to be mastered, controlled, and wielded as a weapon by the worthy few. When the Empire unexpectedly opens its borders for the first time in decades, the knight is sent to uncover the truth behind their sudden hospitality. But in the heart of the enemy’s stronghold, she finds more than secrets—she finds the heir, a woman whose power is undeniable.

✓♥︎

Amour Courtois

by visadero

    Chapter 1

    -

    OR Knight!Rio, Princess!Agatha, and perhaps dragons.

    Practiced Forms “Ser, just beyond that ridgeline, I believe,” the boy called over the whipping wind.

    She brought their steed to a halt in a wide grassy clearing, dismounted, and followed the line of the boy’s outstretched arm.

    The Empire’s border was still half a day's journey on foot. There were quicker ways, but she remembered her training.

    “You are reckless!”

    “I am trying!”

    “Stop. Listen. I need you to hear me and remember this. Do you want to go fast? Or do you want to win?”

    She was no longer reckless. Or at least, now she chose to be so rather than it being her state of being. Wandering was in her bones. It had brought her far, to this place.

    The Wilds was her home and not once had there been reason to leave it. There hadn’t been opportunity either. And while the elders, the ones who had lived in a time before borders, had spoken of the differences between the realms… Seeing the reality was different than the visions her imagination could draw up.

    She had expected tales of the boundaries between kingdoms to be exaggerations. Bards spoke of how the trees twisted like skeletons, and nothing bloomed beyond the divide between The Wilds and The Empire.

    Between them lay a ravine, carved and scorched and damaged. The source was still debated. What her people knew was a truth the Empire had spent centuries denying.

    They claimed that dragons were a myth. A tall tale made up by the unruly and unlearned.

    As if the land didn’t still bear the scars of those battles. As if some of their own people were not born bearing that spirit mark. Of course, those people were rumored to be swiftly executed.

    The Empire saw these gifts as neither blessing nor birthright. That was the greatest difference between the two territories.

    The Empire denied that magic was a gift from the old spirits of the land. They claimed that anyone worthy could be taught spell craft.

    They, of course, had a vested interest in being very discerning about controlling the criteria of who they deemed worthy. And those select few were not spell crafters. They did not conjure or practice transmutation. They did not brew potions to heal or muddle soothing salves.

    Divination had long been outlawed there.

    They learned spells. Approved magic. Magic as a tool of utility. As yet another weapon in their ever-accumulating arsenal.

    But magic wasn’t like that. It wasn’t something to harness and control and keep away. It was in each spring song, each leaf and blade. All life carried that spark. It was everywhere. Perhaps that is what scared them most.

    It was a wild thing, an honored friend to the people and spirits of the Wilds. The Empire thought they could tame it.

    The land itself bore the mark of that divide.

    The Wilds thrived in unrestrained harmony, forests thick with ancient trees, rivers flowing with untouched waters, the wind carrying whispers of spirits and old gods. Even here, on the edge of the ravine, Rio could feel it humming beneath her boots, pulsing like a heartbeat in the earth. The air smelled of damp moss and cedar, alive with magic.

    Here, shrines were built to great spirits of the lands and seas. Children who possessed the gift were celebrated rather than shunted. Bakers delivered honeyed breads that didn’t go stale and smiths imbued wedding bands with minor protection spells. Her family had been farmers, singing to their crops and encouraging their growth. The land gave them plenty.

    Across the ravine, the Empire's lands told a different story. The trees stood in rigid lines, uniform and pruned as if the land itself had been broken and reshaped to fit their design. The soil bore scars of past battles, and even from this distance, Rio could see the flickering glow of the lantern towers marking the roads - magic contained, controlled, bent to the will of those who wielded it.

    The Empire did not believe in wild things. They did not believe in dragons, in spirits, in magic as something freely given. They took, refined, and decided who was worthy.

    And now, after twenty years of keeping their gates sealed, they were inviting the free world in.

    It was unsettling.

    Rio tightened her jaw, her gloved fingers curling around the hilt of her sword. The timing was too perfect. This festival of theirs, this sudden display of generosity, just as the treaty's anniversary approached. Her people remembered what the Empire had done, the blood spilled to forge that fragile peace. They did not forget as easily as those behind those pristine walls.

    "Half a day’s journey ahead,” Billy repeated, his voice breaking through her thoughts.

    Rio exhaled and turned toward him. He was young yet, still looking at the world with eyes that had not seen the worst of it. She had tried to keep him from that knowledge for as long as she could.

    She would not fail him now.

    "We make for the ravine," she said, pulling the reins of their horse. "We’ll find a way across. If the Empire is opening its doors, then it's not just their secrets we need to be wary of. We will camp tonight. Then tomorrow, we will enter by the main road."

    If the House of Harkness was revealing itself at last, then it was her duty to find a way in. She was a knight, not an oath breaker.

    When the Harkness clan finally fell, she prayed that it would be upon her sword.

    That night, the fire crackled low between them, its embers casting flickering shadows against the thick trunks of the trees. The Wilds stretched in all directions, a living thing that breathed and whispered with the night.

    Rio sat with her back to a fallen log, sharpening the edge of her blade with slow, deliberate strokes. Billy lay sprawled on his bedroll, hands tucked behind his head, gazing up at the stars peeking through the canopy.

    "So," he said, voice light but edged with curiosity, "now that I'm officially your squire, does that mean you'll finally tell me stories about the war?"

    Rio didn't look up from her sword. "I was but a child then," she said, keeping her tone even. She liked the boy, but she thought after years of her influence, he would have become a little less chatty. But no such luck.

    Billy scoffed. "Ser, you were my age, weren't you? And I know for a fact that everyone was involved, one way or another."

    She set the whetstone down with a sigh. "That doesn't mean I have any stories worth telling." "That’s not what I heard," Billy pressed, shifting onto his side to look at her more directly. "You were there. You must have seen things, done things."

    She had.

    Rio exhaled slowly and leaned back against the log, looking into the fire as if a suitable answer lay somewhere in the embers. "I was a messenger," she said at last. "A page. I took messages between our troops along the battle lines."

    It wasn’t untrue. She had carried messages. Slipped between the trees, small and swift enough to avoid notice, weaving between soldiers locked in a deadly struggle. She had run through the mud, through the blood, through the aftermath of battles fought and lost.

    Billy let out a low whistle. "That must've been terrifying."

    She gave him a small nod. "It was. But necessary. It was an honor to protect our home."

    It was a good story. A clean one. She hoped he believed it.

    Billy lay back down, mulling over her words. "You must've seen a lot, though. More than most, maybe."

    More than most children should.

    Rio let the silence stretch between them, letting the fire pop and hiss to fill the space. She wouldn’t tell him the real story. Not tonight. Not ever, if she could help it.

    She had not just been a messenger. She had been a retriever.

    Smallest and quickest, chosen for the terrible task of slipping past the lines, past the bodies left behind in the wake of battle. The Empire had resources, fine steel, enchanted weapons, maps… All things too valuable to leave to rot with the dead. And so the children were sent to collect them.

    She had knelt in the mud beside the fallen, hands shaking as she pried rings from stiff fingers, tore sigils from tattered cloaks, rummaged through pockets for letters that might contain intelligence. The dead had names, faces, eyes that sometimes remained open, staring accusingly into the sky. But she had never been told why they were enemies. Just that they were.

    Perhaps that was why she had trained as a knight.

    There was honor in a sword, in an oath, in facing an enemy as an equal rather than scavenging from the lifeless.

    She’d rather be a raven than a vulture.

    Rio sheathed her blade and glanced at Billy, his breathing already slowing as sleep crept in. He was still so young. Too young to know what war turned children into.

    The night was thick with the hum of insects and the whisper of leaves shifting in the wind. Billy slept soundly by the fire, curled up beneath a worn woolen cloak. Rio watched him for a moment before slipping away, moving through the clearing with practiced silence.

    A couple hundred paces away, she stopped, rolling her shoulders before bending to unlace her boots. It made for a better practice when she could feel the earth. Only when she was barefoot did she draw her sword.

    She began her forms as she had been taught. Each motion slow, precise, deliberate. The rhythm steadied her, as it always did. Her body moved through the familiar patterns, cutting through the air with purpose.

    It was supposed to be meditative. Her only focus on her blade as an extension of her arm. That had been the lesson it had taken her the longest to learn. To not see her sword as something that she carried but something that was part of her.

    “One movement. One being. If you think of it as separate from you, it will be separated from you.”

    But tonight there was too much to consider to linger on meditations.

    She thought of Billy, of how he had come into her care.

    It had been seven winters past. His parents, accused Empire spies, were gone before he even knew what was happening. Executed quickly, quietly. A warning to others. Rio had been present when the ruling came down, when the boy was left standing in the cold, orphaned in an instant.

    He hadn't cried. Not then, not when she knelt before him, offering a gloved hand and an uncertain promise of something beyond that moment.

    She hadn’t known if they were guilty. She still didn’t.

    It didn’t really matter… what they were was dead.

    A boy had no parents. And though she had no particular want nor need for a child, she had room in her home and food to spare.

    It became quickly apparent that the boy carried magic in his blood. A strange, gentle magic, wild and untamed like the wind sent howling through the trees. Small creatures seemed drawn to him. Birds settled easily on his shoulders, mice scurried up his sleeves without fear. It had been that way since he was young.

    When he first told her about the secrets the creatures shared with him, she’d dismissed it as a fit of childish fantasy. But he continued to tell her things that he couldn’t possibly have known himself.

    A cat that had seen his owner bringing a woman who was not his wife to bed. A snake who whispered about a child stealing shiny things and delivering them to a shrine.

    Once, they’d been walking together in the woods when a lumbering gray wolf showed its teeth. Rio had drawn her sword and ordered him behind her.

    But there was no need. He’d raised a hand and calmly said to the wolf that they would not make a fine meal for him that day, instead they’d be willing to share some jerky from their packs.

    And the beast had curled up and allowed the boy to rest his head against its thick fur.

    Her ward, even among the prevalence of magic users, was special.

    She had once been tested for magic, too.

    Rio moved through another form, sweeping her blade through the air in a tight arc. The memory came unbidden, her small hands outstretched, a rabbit quivering before her in the tall grass. She had tried to call to it, to reach for it with something beyond words. And it had simply… stopped.

    She had never tried again.

    Her parents had tried to comfort her, to tell her that life and death were part of the same cycle, that even endings had their place in the grand order of things. But before they could fully convince her of that, they were gone too.

    She pushed the thought aside, swinging her sword in a final sharp motion, exhaling through her nose as she steadied her stance.

    And then she saw it reflected on the edge of her blade.

    A glimmer of violet light.

    She glanced up, looking for the source. Far beyond their camp, rising from the tallest spire of a distant tower, it pulsed like a star against the darkness.

    Rio narrowed her eyes.

    The House of Harkness had always been shrouded in secrecy, its power a mystery even among the Empire’s ranks. And yet, here it was. A kind of magic, raw and unmistakable, casting its glow against the night.

    Witchcraft.

    Witchcraft within the walls of the Empire.

    “Do it again!”

    The little boy in her lap squealed and clapped his hands in delight.

    Agatha smiled, and even though the motion still felt foreign after years of lessons on how to be presentable, which boiled down to drawing as little attention as possible, it came easier at this moment.

    She closed her eyes and focused on the pressure she felt just behind her ribs, imagining it as a slowly growing orb as she inhaled. She opened her eyes and curled the first two fingers of her left hand, slowly exhaling as she did.

    A shower of purple sparks rained over the pair as the boy nestled in her lap giggled.

    She buried her smile against the top of his head, breathing him in. It had been a month since she’d seen him and would be at least another still once their visit was over. He was growing so quickly these days.

    “To the wardrobe, little sprout. Let’s see how much you have grown.”

    Agatha opened the doors to the ostentatious piece and shoved the hideous gowns inside to the side.

    “Do you need a boost?”

    He shook his head, “I can do it!” and pulled out the bottom drawer to stand in it before climbing up into the wardrobe.

    From a different drawer, she pulled a small file and scratched a mark just above his head. Their secret ritual. A measure of time.

    Tonight she’d scratch in his age and the date as close as she could remember it. She’d run her names over the other markings too. Each time they met, she recorded his height.

    She was fortunate that the visits were more frequent now than they used to be, but he was also growing more quickly too.

    “Did I grow? Can I see?”

    He turned around and traced a small hand, still chubby with baby fat, over the latest line. “You did grow; you’re getting very tall! I bet you’re going to be taller than me soon,” she teased, scooping him out of the wardrobe and spinning him before putting him on the floor. Sparing a glance at the carvings in the wood, her makeshift calendar, she was reminded of the month.

    “Nicky, did you know, we get to practice a new number the next time you visit here?”

    He nodded seriously and held up four fingers on his hand.

    “Four! My birthday!”

    Agatha nodded. “That’s right. And I promise we’ll do something very fun together.”

    She hadn’t figured out quite what that would be yet. If four would be different than three and he’d be less easily amused by magic lights and stories. She dreaded the day he’d ask her something more real.

    One day he would know when she was lying.

    “Will you do the lights one more time? The purple?”

    There were no time pieces in this room, so she glanced out the window. The moon was beginning to wink into sight. There wasn’t much more time.

    But who was she to deny a little prince?

    “If you can tell me the rule,” she whispered, kneeling down next to him.

    He cupped a hand against her cheek, still sticky from some earlier treat. “It’s our secret light.” She nodded. “Good boy. You are my light. And this is yours.”

    Agatha conjured one more shower of sparks and his eyes widened with such wonder she could easily watch them fall in the reflection of his irises.

    Both of them startled at the sound of a rap on the door.

    She didn’t mean to glare at him, but he put a finger to his lips and nodded carefully before standing and pressing a kiss to her cheek.

    “I’m here to fetch Sir Nicholas. His mother awaits,” an attendant called.

    They’d once at least had the courtesy to come through the door. She’d hated their pitying faces, but she hated cowards more.

    Agatha flung the door open, sneering at the woman outside. “Well, don’t keep Evanora waiting then.”

    She didn’t trail a hand through his hair as he left. She didn’t linger to watch him wave goodbye.

    Agatha closed the door. She counted until they had walked at least fifty paces away.

    Then she screamed.

    She wrenched her hands through the air and sent a beam of violet light towards the window. It shattered, raining glass down into the courtyard below.

    Evanora would be extremely displeased. And this pleased Agatha greatly. It was a small comfort.

    Agatha stood in the wreckage, her breath forming clouds in the cold air instead of fogging against the glass as usual. Below, the shards of glass winked up at her like falling stars, scattered across the pristine stone of the courtyard. Someone would be coming soon to reprimand her. They always did.

    She turned back to the room, heavier now. One less occupant made the airy space twist into something oppressive. She leaned against the fractured frame, gazing once more toward the distant Wilds.

    The trees stood dark and endless beyond the city walls, a world untouched by her mother’s careful curation. No courtyards adorned with statues of great rulers. No patrols or curfews to keep the people inside. In line. She thought again of the stories - the ones drilled into her mind since childhood, warning of the chaos and savagery beyond the Empire’s reach.

    On her thirteenth name day she'd been gifted a greenfinch in a beautiful cage. It was a little too on the nose for her liking. She'd thrown the entire cage through the window. That bird had wings, it could figure it out.

    A door slammed somewhere below, voices rising in alarm.

    Agatha smirked.

    Tomorrow was a celebration. A dreadful day. She at least deserved to have some fun with it. Preparations and Performance Chapter Summary The city prepares for the festival. Conversations and chance meetings take place. A tournament begins!

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes The Queen sat at the head of a long banquet table, untouched food spread before her, polished silver perfectly reflecting the contempt drawn on her face. She made no secret of her displeasure. Neither did her daughter.

    Agatha stood at the far end, arms crossed tight over her chest. “It’s a little late for dining, isn’t it Mother?”

    “I presumed that you would be hungry after your outburst. You should thank me for caring for you. Sit.”

    She perched instead, half sitting on the arm of an oak dining chair, the hem of her nightdress too high to be appropriate for public wear and she snuck a surreptitious glance at their small audience.

    A handful of servants lingered in the periphery, silent and still as statues. Agatha knew better. Even the most well trained had a breaking point. She caught the nervous flicker of glances, the way a foot shifted or a throat swallowed. They were waiting. Always in waiting.

    And so was she. Which is perhaps why they had such an understanding.

    The same hand held all the dogs’ leashes. At least they were unclipped and allowed to roam on occasion.

    She let the fabric of her nightdress slip just a little higher over her thigh and watched one of the footmen nearly choke on his own tongue before quickly averting his eyes. A slow, knowing smile curled on her lips.

    “My, my,” she murmured, tilting her head toward him. The Garners had served for a long time in the palace. But their eldest son was barely beyond boyhood. He didn’t yet understand that sometimes you kept your eyes downcast not out of reverence but rather self-preservation. “You look a bit flushed, Callum. Are you feeling unwell? I hope my mother isn’t keeping you too late.”

    “N-no, Your Highness,” the young man stammered, his hands clasped rigidly behind his back.

    “Strange,” Agatha mused. She swung one leg lazily, curling her foot in small circles.

    “Because you’re sweating.”

    Another footman, standing beside him, barely suppressed a cough. Agatha arched a brow at him next. “Oh? Something funny?”

    “No, your Highness.”

    She sighed, all mock disappointment. “Pity. I do enjoy having a laugh.”

    Evanora’s voice cut through the moment like a knife. “Enough.”

    Agatha turned, feigning innocence. “Whatever do you mean, Mother?”

    Evanora didn’t look at her, instead delicately placing her goblet back on the table. Someone moved to refill it but she shooed them away.

    “You forget yourself,” she said coolly. “Or perhaps you simply enjoy behaving like a tavern girl in front of the staff.”

    Agatha leaned forward, her elbow resting on the chair’s arm. “Oh, but I am helping them. It would be terribly unkind not to give them a little thrill now and then, don’t you think? It’s terribly boring being locked inside here day and night. There’s no telling what that would do to a person.”

    Evanora’s gaze finally snapped to her; there was no blade in the kingdom sharper than the steel in her eyes. “You mistake their discomfort for amusement.”

    Agatha held her mother’s stare and smiled slowly. “Do I?”

    “You mustn’t act this way Agatha. Not when this kingdom, our subjects, and every person here are toiling over your celebrations. It seems ungrateful.”

    “Spare them the trouble then,” Agatha challenged, chin tilted up in defiance. “Call it all off. No one asked for this.”

    Evanora hummed, tapping a single finger against the table’s polished surface. “Oh, but they did,” she countered. “The people adore you, my dear. Or at least, they adore the idea of you. Would you deny them such a simple pleasure?”

    Agatha scoffed. “They don’t even know me.”

    “They don’t need to,” Evanora said. “They need a princess. A symbol. Someone who is graceful, poised, and above all else, silent.”

    Agatha clenched her jaw.

    “Tomorrow for the opening ceremonies you will dress accordingly,” Evanora continued, eyes cool and assessing. “Your hair will be combed. You will be polite. Gracious. And you will not speak unless spoken to. The people want to see their princess. They do not care to hear from her.”

    Her nails dug into her palms so deeply that she knew she’d be forced to wear gloves tomorrow to cover the marks. “And what if I refuse?”

    Evanora only smiled. “You won’t.”

    Then, after a pause, she sighed, as if suddenly remembering something. “It’s such a shame, really. I had been considering allowing Nicholas to visit again off schedule. I thought he might stay with you tonight. As a nice treat for your Nameday. But now…” She trailed off, tilting her head, eyes sweeping over Agatha.

    “If you can’t even be trusted to attend a simple party in your honor,” she mused, “how could you possibly trouble yourself to care for a child properly? I wouldn’t want to burden your fragile constitution.”

    Agatha’s stomach twisted. There it was. The precise pressure point her mother had been waiting to dig her claws into.

    For a moment, Agatha imagined taking a torch to the whole damn festival, setting fire to the banners, the gilded chairs, the carefully curated illusion of peace. But even if she did, Evanora would still be there, untouched by the flames.

    Agatha exhaled sharply through her nose, rolling her shoulders back. “Fine,” she muttered. “I’ll do it.”

    “Good girl,” Evanora murmured, already turning away. “Do try to control yourself tomorrow. It’s unbecoming. Especially for a woman of your station. Do not forget your place.”

    She bit the inside of her cheek to keep from snapping something that would make her regret this whole exchange. She had won nothing here. Only conceded, only swallowed her pride in the name of something more important. But she would find her victories elsewhere.

    Evanora moved as if the conversation had already slipped from her mind, but Agatha knew better. Every word had been measured, every barb precisely placed for maximum effect. Her mother never wasted effort on a single strike when a thousand tiny cuts would do.

    “Pardon the interruption Your Majesty, Your Highness, Prince Nicholas is asking for his mother.”

    Both women turned towards the wet nurse.

    Evanora barely acknowledged the woman at first, merely tilting her head as if weighing the statement for its worth. Then, with a slow, deliberate motion, she turned fully to face her, fingers still idly trailing the rim of her goblet.

    “How sweet,” Evanora murmured. “The boy does love his mother. Thank you Sarah, I’ll be there shortly.”

    Agatha’s breath caught, but she willed her face into stillness. This one was a delicate game, and she could not afford to play it poorly.

    The woman hesitated a moment longer, eyes flickering toward Evanora before she gave a quick curtsy and disappeared down the hall.

    Evanora watched Agatha in the silence that followed, her face unreadable. Until it wasn’t. Her lips curled just slightly, the edges of a smirk forming, slow and cruel.

    “You should be grateful,” she said lightly, as though offering a kind word instead of a dagger to the ribs. “You’re freed from such tedious obligations. That poor girl spends all hours tending to a fussy child while you-” She gestured vaguely with her goblet. “You get to live. As you should.”

    Agatha turned toward the door, eager to be gone from this wretched game of submission, but her mother’s voice coiled around her as she turned to exit.

    “One more moment please, I heard about the incident with your window.”

    Agatha hesitated, though she did not turn. “Did you, Mother?”

    Evanora hummed in the affirmative. “It concerns me deeply, all these… disturbances.” She took a slow sip from her goblet. “It would be irresponsible of me not to ensure your safety.” Agatha’s jaw tightened. “I don’t need-”

    “You will be assigned a knight of the guard,” Evanora interrupted smoothly. “A permanent watch, effective as soon as a suitable candidate is found.”

    Agatha clenched her fists; her palms were bleeding now. “Ah. You wouldn’t want anything to happen to the dear princess,” she said dryly.

    Evanora smiled over the rim of her cup. “Exactly.”

    “I do hope this one lasts longer than the last. Poor man. Last I heard, he’d taken vows at a monastery. Sworn himself to a life of silence, even. Imagine that.”

    Evanora’s smirk sharpened. “Yes. Imagine that.”

    A sliver of unease crept up her spine before settling in Agatha’s chest.

    Her mother only smiled like that when she knew something Agatha didn’t.

    “Do not trouble your pretty head, darling,” Evanora crooned, setting down her goblet with a delicate clink. “Your next protector will be the most skilled combatant in all the land.” Agatha finally turned back, studying her mother with careful suspicion. “Is that so?”

    Evanora met her gaze, amusement dancing just beneath her composed exterior. For such a sour woman, she looked practically giddy. “Oh, yes.”

    “Well,” she said, dipping low into an overblown curtsy, “let’s see how long it takes me to break him.”

    Evanora only laughed softly. “We shall see.”

    Agatha returned to her chambers.

    But she didn’t stay put long.

    The guards obeyed her mother. But Agatha was the one they feared.

    It was easy enough to slip past them in the early hours. They either were half asleep or purposely looking the other way. She passed down the servants’ stairs and through the kitchen where the morning meal was being prepared.

    She cornered the girl who usually held the unfortunate task of delivering her tray and having to wake her each morning.

    “I do think something from breakfast didn’t agree with me. Please let my mother know that I am feeling poorly and will be resting in my chambers until noon.”

    The girl nodded quickly and Agatha gave her a genuine smile. She was meek and mild, which would either lead to a life of blissful ignorance or one spent as a doormat.

    For the girl’s benefit, Agatha hoped she’d one day reach around to her back and locate her spine. But her complete lack of any backbone served Agatha well for now.

    “As you wish, Your Highness. Would you perhaps like to take your meal here? Or take something with you?”

    “No. If you want, you can have it when you take it up to my room. It looks more realistic that way, don’t you think? You could use a good meal, and today is a day of celebration, is it not?”

    And before the girl could thank her or ask other questions, Agatha pulled a cloak hanging from behind the door and broke out into the dawn.

    The walled capital city of Neverfell, Jewel of The Empire, loomed ahead, its gates flung open in a rare gesture of welcome. It would be more inviting without the legion of pikemen stationed out front. But it was a point of entry. And it was certainly easier to walk in the front gate than to try to smuggle themselves in.

    Bright pennants snapped in the wind, their gold and crimson hues bleeding across the narrow streets. Paper lanterns in rich violets and deep blues dangled from the eaves, swaying gently as the pair passed beneath them. The scent of roasted nuts and spiced wine rose thick in the air.

    It was a heady scent, especially mingled with the musty staleness of too many bodies crammed into too little space.

    At a glance, it was a city in full celebration. Vendors had set up their stalls along the winding avenues, their voices raised in song-like cadence as they called out to each passersby. A troupe of musicians played at a street corner, their lilting tune momentarily lifting the spirits of those nearby.

    But Rio saw through it.

    Beneath the garlands and silk banners, the rot here was evident.

    She saw the gaunt faces tucked in the shadows of alleyways, the hollow-eyed children darting through the crowd with hands too quick for their own good. Only a few feet from the vendors selling sizzling meats, other merchants sold half-rotten fruit for full price.

    There was no mistaking it. The celebration was for the privileged; the rest were merely set dressing. This city had its chosen, and these people were not among them.

    Billy, of course, was blind to it all.

    “Look!” he exclaimed, tugging at her tunic sleeve. “They’re holding a minstrel contest!”

    Rio followed his gesture toward a town crier perched atop a wooden crate. The man’s voice boomed over the square.

    “Come one, come all! Test your wit, your charm, and your voice against the greatest minds of tThe Empire! Impress the judges and win a purse of silver! And later…”

    He spread his arms wide for effect. “The grand spectacle! The forty-man skirmish! Warriors from across these blessed lands shall fight for honor, for glory, and for an audience with Her Majesty herself!”

    There it was.

    Rio’s in.

    She barely heard Billy beside her as he practically bounced on his heels. “A performance contest! I could enter, don’t you think? I know plenty of songs!”

    He was all bright eyes and boyish excitement, his grin stretched wide.

    Rio, still watching the town crier, exhaled slowly. “You want to stand in front of a crowd and sing for a handful of coins? Why?”

    He didn’t catch her disdain and nodded eagerly. “Why not? I bet I’d do well!”

    She dragged a hand through her hair, considering him.

    Rio didn’t doubt he could win. He had a natural way with words, an earnestness people liked. But standing out, even for something as harmless as a song, could be dangerous. They were strangers here. Eyes would linger, and questions might be asked.

    She let the thought settle before answering.

    “We’ll see.”

    The boy seemed to take that as permission, and she caught him beginning to hum under his breath.

    He noticed her stare and at least had the decency to look a little sheepish. “Sorry, just warming up. Just in case.”

    “It’s fine. Stay around here. I’m going to sign up for the fight.”

    Billy nodded, still humming as he rocked back on his heels. “I’ll scope out the competition. Just in case!”

    Rio pushed through the throngs of festival-goers, her name freshly scrawled on the list for the skirmish. The sign-up had been laughably easy, a half-bored scribe barely looking up as she jotted down her alias. Now, she just had to find Billy.

    The sound of raucous laughter and cheers drew her toward a raised platform in the square. The contest. She frowned. Hadn’t it just started? But the crowd was already riled up, voices rising in hoots and hollers as someone held them utterly enchanted.

    Rio stood on her toes, craning her neck to see over the shoulders of the gathered spectators. The speaker’s voice rang clear and confident, thick with mischief. And then, through the shifting figures, Rio caught a glimpse of her.

    The woman stood tall despite her short frame. She stood with the poise of someone used to having an audience. A wild mess of dark brown hair framed her like a lion’s mane, making her silhouette twice as wide. It ballooned her presence even larger. She grinned as the crowd clamored, calling for one more, demanding it like greedy children begging for sweets.

    She obliged, her voice lilting into verse: "The old king lay with a scullery maid They howled and gave the queen a fright”

    Her hands twirled in the air as if she meant to cast a net to draw them all in closer.

    “Faced with the dagger, He lost all swagger.”

    Her impish gaze slid through the crowd - and landed on Rio. A beat of silence stretched between them, and the entire crowd seemed to lean forward. She winked, and Rio felt something twist low in her gut.

    “And the cook served sausage that night!"

    The final line hit the air like a firecracker, and the crowd erupted. Laughter boomed from every corner, tankards clashed together in toasts, and coins clattered to the stage in drunken tribute.

    It was infectious, and Rio caught herself nearly laughing even as the crowd jostled her and Billy expressed his contempt.

    “Bit crude, don’t you think? I don’t know that this crowd is going to enjoy my Morning Finch Song .”

    It took only a moment for her to turn to the squire and nudge him in the ribs. But by the time she’d done so and turned her attention back to the stage, the woman was gone.

    “Good day, dear Agnes!”

    The chime of the bell over the door was as jolly as the baker within, and Agatha’s good mood was further lifted by the sweet smell of cinnamon.

    “Good day. Tell me, Arthur, if you had recently come into… let’s say twenty silver pieces, what fine treat would you purchase for today’s celebration?”

    He laughed and dusted his hands on his apron. “If you told me you came into twenty silver pieces, I think I’d ask when the guard’ll be coming through my door behind you.”

    Agatha smiled and pulled her winnings from the contest, placing the pouch on the counter. “Honestly earned, I promise you.”

    “Well, twenty silver would buy me out. I reckon anything you’d like is yours.”

    She nodded and looked over the available pastries. “A sticky bun, then. And a cinnamon swirl.”

    “Miss Agnes, both of those are still under a silver piece. Anything else you’d like? Maybe something-”

    “No, Arthur,” she said, firm but not unkind. “Use the rest on whoever comes through the door after me. Everyone deserves a treat on a day like today.”

    “That’s quite generous. You sure you won’t be missing it?”

    She smiled and shook her head. “No, all is well…” she scrunched her nose as she recalled the last visit she’d made to town. “Anyone except that tailor’s girl. Didn’t catch her name. But she is quite rude.”

    They’d quarreled. The girl was a talker. Agatha had remarked that idle prattle was what husbands were for. She hadn’t taken kindly to that. And her husband hadn’t taken well to catching a glimpse of someone slipping out of his bedroom window as he returned home from the quarry.

    So, no treats for them.

    She tucked the bun into the pocket sewn into her skirt. The cinnamon swirl was all hers, a luxury made sweeter when eaten outside the palace walls.

    But Agatha didn’t have time to take a bite before a hand clasped around her wrist and pulled her into the side alley.

    “Jennifer! Such a pleasure to see you on this fine morning! I hear they’re giving away buns from the bakery.”

    She grinned and took a large bite of her prize. Even the interruption couldn’t spoil her good mood. Neither could the piercing look currently pointed at her.

    “Agatha.” No honorifics between the two of them. “It’s funny to find you here. I heard you were unwell.”

    “Cured! By the finest pastries, I’m sure.” She wasn’t done chewing the first bite, but moved to take another,. iIf only to annoy the other woman.

    “You’re supposed to be getting ready for the tournament.”

    “Come now Jen, surely my mother’s spymaster has better things to do than stop a princess from enjoying something sweet?” Her batted eyelashes never seemed to work on Jen, but it didn’t stop her from trying.

    Jen was unimpressed though, pulling her cloak a little tighter and standing a little taller. “You are right, your Highness. I have much better things to do. So if you would be so kind as to return with me… I can return to my work, and you can return to…”

    Jen gave her a thoughtful look as she tapped her chin. “Your very taxing day of being bathed, dressed, celebrated, and revered.”

    Agatha grinned and leaned in close. “Would you care to revere me?”

    “That would be as unwise as it is unlikely, Your Highness.”

    It was no wonder Jennifer Kale held the station she did. She was not only well connected and perceptive, but also shrewd. She also didn’t mind playing with teeth.

    And Agatha respected that.

    So she bowed in a sweeping gesture of “after you” and followed back to the palace, licking cinnamon sugar off her fingertips the entire way.

    By the time she returned to her quarters, the bath that had been drawn for her had run cold. She could, of course, throw a fit and ask for fresh water to be brought up.

    But she wasn’t ungrateful.

    She pressed into the basin and sank beneath the surface. Cool water felt better on her back anyway.

    She let herself linger beneath the water for a moment, eyes closed, the muted sounds of the palace filtering through the surface. For a moment, she was weightless. Unburdened. Alone. Then the door swung open, and a flurry of movement shattered her brief moment of peace.

    Ladies’ maids descended upon her in a coordinated attack, lifting her from the tub and wrapping her in warmed linens before she could so much as protest. They moved in practiced synchrony, toweling her dry, working fragrant oils into her skin, and dragging toothy combs through her hair.

    Agatha winced as a particularly aggressive braid was woven too tight at her scalp. “Must you be so vicious?” she muttered.

    “Apologies, your Highness,” the maid replied, not sounding particularly apologetic.

    More hands pulled at her, guiding her into layers of fabric that threatened to swallow her whole. The deep purple gown was one of the few she didn’t despise, though that was hardly a compliment. Silver fastenings gleamed at her throat, the high collar brushing against her jaw in a way that felt far too restrictive.

    She despised how stiff it was. How stifling.

    How perfectly it made her look the part.

    One of the maids stood back and murmured, “You look exquisite, Princess.”

    Agatha turned her head just enough to catch her own reflection in the gilded mirror. She looked… beautifully arranged. More doll than woman, but she supposed that was the point.

    They ushered her out before she had time to dwell, leading her through the winding halls toward the dais, where her mother would already be waiting.

    She was almost surprised to find herself looking forward to the skirmish. She enjoyed these events. Not for the spectacle, nor for any real interest in who emerged victorious, but because she so enjoyed watching men bleed.

    The dais loomed above the arena, affording a perfect vantage point. Agatha slid into the seat beside her mother, who did not spare her a glance. Not a word. It was for the best.

    Below, the combatants assembled in a loose throng of armor and steel. Agatha let her gaze sweep over them, assessing.

    Then, a figure caught her eye.

    Smaller than the others. Lither. An unusual build for a knight, and yet, they carried themselves proudly. She turned to Jen, her lips curving up into a cheeky grin.

    “That one,” she said, pointing with a gloved finger. “How long do you wager before he’s felled? Will he even have a chance to draw his sword?”

    Jen followed her gaze, humming in consideration. “Five minutes, perhaps?”

    Agatha smirked. “Generous.”

    She turned her attention back to the arena, watching as the signal was given and the skirmish began.

    Chapter End Notes If only you saw the bawdy poems that didn't make the cut...

    Blades and Barbs Chapter Summary The tournament takes place. An audience is held with the queen. Agatha meets the most irritating woman, but does not catch her name.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes “Rio, look close. Can you tell me what this is?”

    She knew the answer. A piece of yew that had until recently had been occupied with growing into a tree.

    “It’s a stick.”

    Wrong. She ducked just in time to feel the wood ghost just over her head.

    “It’s a staff,” she tried again.

    This time, she hopped half a step back to avoid a quick jab.

    “Try again. Answer the question. Can you tell me what this is?”

    She struck first this time, catching it in her hand, not allowing her trainer to complete the movement.

    “Yes,” she said more firmly now.

    Wrong again. A mighty shove sent her stumbling back a few paces. Her heel caught on a tangled root and she tumbled to the ground.

    “No,” she was corrected again. “You cannot tell me what this is. Because it isn’t something anymore. It was a sapling.”

    “Until you made me cut it down.”

    “And now,” her instructor was used to interruptions and unfazed by her snark, “it could be many things. It could be a walking stick. It could be a weapon. It could be kindling.”

    Rio pushed herself into a sitting position, dusting dirt from her palms. “So what? Everything is just… potential?”

    They crouched beside her, turning the piece over in their hands.

    “There is no magic in what is ,” they said, tracing their fingers along the grain of the wood. As they did, the bark peeled away, revealing the green wood beneath. Cut down too soon, while it was still growing.

    The exposed green pith curled into the pattern of three runes. As they pulled their hand away, each glowed faintly for a moment before fading.

    “Magic is in the could be. The unshaped. The undefined. There is only magic where there is possibility. ”

    Rio frowned, eyes flicking from the wood to her trainer’s steady expression. “Why define something and make it yours? I thought it was unnatural to try and tame nature.”

    “ Taming is forcing something into a shape it was never meant to take. Magic is not about force, it is about recognizing the shape something could take and guiding it.”

    They tapped the runes with a fingertip, and it hummed in return. “Possibility is a road, not a cage. The moment you choose a path, you close off others. But in that moment, you claim it.” Rio studied the grooves where the runes had etched into the surface. “So if I called this a torch…”

    “It would burn,” her trainer confirmed.

    “And if I called it a spear…”

    “It would sharpen.”

    Rio’s frown deepened. “And if I wanted it to be nothing ?”

    At that, their smile widened, and they nodded. “Yes, you’re understanding now. If you wanted it to be nothing, then you would have to unmake it.”

    Rio’s fingers flexed around the wood. “How?”

    Their hand covered hers, pressing firmly. “That, buinneán, is the lesson that comes last.” It was a staff for a long time. For so long, that the grain smoothed in the spots where it was held.

    Held by an orphan turned shadow. Held as that shadow took shape and pinned herself to the ground to stand up on her own. She carried it proudly on the day of her anointing.

    Rio held it for the last time before finally laying it to rest as a grave marker.

    She would carry a different weapon next.

    A falchion. A single-edged blade with a curved tip. A guard that curved like a stream with one end pointing back at her and the other toward whoever stood closest to the point. She wrapped the grip with leather. Dark green from a dye made of birch oil, marigolds, and muddled vines.

    Blessed by runes etched around the pommel, a promise was pulled from the forge.

    “All weapons are to be sheathed or worn along the back until the horn is sounded. The bout will continue until all but one have surrendered or succumbed. After the blow of the victory horn, healers will mend injuries in order of prognosis, not severity.”

    She barely heard the words as she found her breath and steadied it.

    Rio scanned the ring slowly, eyes narrowed, taking stock of the opponents clustered around her. Most were brawny and armored, puffed with bravado. Rio dismissed them quickly; they would tire easily, reckless with confidence.

    She lingered on the quieter figures, those who stood apart and watched like she did. They would wait and strike deliberately. They were dangerous.

    Her gaze caught on a towering warrior in battered plate armor, broad-shouldered and already gripping his hilt impatiently. He was powerful but unbalanced, muscles twitching with barely restrained eagerness. Rio marked him: strength but little discipline, easy enough to trip up. As she took one more sweep of her competitors, her eyes landed on a figure watching her openly. Broad-shouldered, tall, arms crossed with casual ease, a double ended flail hanging from his back.

    Rio adjusted the sword in its scabbard. She bounced on the balls of her feet, eager to be in motion. Then the horn sounded, sharp and piercing, scattering her thoughts. The crowd roared with excitement.

    She sprang forward as the battle erupted into chaos around her.

    Her first opponent charged recklessly, all heavy feet and no patience. He swung wildly, his blade carving clumsy arcs through the air. Rio sidestepped with practiced ease, ducking low as his momentum carried him past her, sending him stumbling into the dirt. She didn’t pause to watch him fall.

    The crack behind her told her that someone else had seized the opportunity she’d created. She need not look behind to know he was bleeding.

    Another attacker came swiftly from the right, blades slicing toward her throat. Rio twisted sharply, her sword drawn in a smooth arc, steel meeting steel with a ringing clash. She drove forward, muscles taut, shoving her opponent off-balance with her shoulder. He retreated hastily, surprised by her strength. But she offered him no time to recover, delivering a decisive strike to his wrist that sent his blade spinning from his grip.

    Around her, the battle raged in a blur of color and noise, shouts mingling with the clang of weapons and the growing roar of the crowd. They were not shy about their bloodlust.

    The air thickened as the clamoring forces kicked up dust and debris. Sweat and blood rained from all directions. Rio twisted sharply to avoid a spear thrust from behind, feeling it graze the leather at her waist. She spun swiftly, knocking the spear aside and slamming her elbow into the attacker’s jaw, sending him staggering backward into the waiting arms of two others eager to finish him off.

    Everywhere around her was the same state of chaos. Warriors grappled in the dirt, armor dented, blades ringing on contact like cruel bells through the arena. She ducked another swing aimed for her head, parrying two strikes at once, feeling the jolt of impact all the way through her arms and shoulders.

    There were no alliances here, no fairness, just violence and ambition. A man to her left took a blow that sent blood spraying across Rio’s visor. Another crumpled soundlessly to the ground, clutching a leg twisted unnaturally beneath him. She sidestepped quickly, narrowly avoiding a fallen body as someone tackled their opponent directly into her path.

    Her heart beat faster, adrenaline sharpening her vision. She scanned quickly, knowing hesitation could mean death. She lunged forward, sliding beneath a mace swung recklessly wide, using its wielder’s momentum to send him sprawling face-first into the sand.

    Through it all, Rio saw clearly. She tracked the tall warrior from earlier, watching him swing his double-ended flail with ruthless precision. Each strike was perfectly timed, efficient, unhurried. His opponents fell swiftly, dropping to the dust, one by one.

    As if sensing her stare, he paused, gaze locking briefly with hers. A flicker of recognition passed between them: respect, curiosity, a quiet challenge. Then he turned away to dispatch another combatant.

    As she finished off her own opponent, the penultimate fighter, Rio’s blood surged. She tightened her grip on her sword and advanced, ready to meet him.

    She stepped into his path, blade raised in silent challenge. Carnage and chaos and cheers swirled around them in discordant song.

    He met her eyes, expressionless beneath his helm, but he inclined his head slightly.

    Recognition of a respectable challenger, if not an equal.

    Rio exhaled and smiled tightly. They both raised their weapons.

    Agatha shifted forward slightly, chin propped delicately against her knuckles, attention sharpened now despite her earlier feigned boredom. At first, the skirmish had been predictable. But now the field had narrowed to something worth watching.

    The small knight she'd pointed out earlier had far lasted longer than expected. Now, only they and a brute remained in the center of the ring. She watched with interest as the hulking warrior swung a double-ended flail, twisted metal whirling dangerously through the air.

    Then it struck. The massive fighter’s blow connected hard, and the smaller knight went sprawling into the dirt. Agatha winced, leaning forward in her seat despite herself. Another swing tore the visor from his helm, scattering broken metal into the dirt.

    Agatha’s breath caught sharply.

    Beneath the helmet was a fairer face than she'd anticipated. A woman stared dazedly skyward, blood streaking from her temple, chest heaving as she struggled to breathe.

    The beast of a man stalked closer, planting a foot beside her shoulder in arrogant victory. The crowd rose around them, roaring their approval. Agatha could feel Jen tense beside her, her hand gripping Agatha's arm in excitement, but she barely noticed. She leaned forward further, her fingers gripping the railing tightly, eyes fixed on the fallen woman.

    The woman blinked slowly, then turned her head to the side. Even from this distance, she could see the red of blood spat into the dirt.

    He raised his flail, spinning it lazily as he turned to the crowd, soaking in their cheers. But before he could fully relish the moment, the woman rolled sharply out of reach. She was quicker than she had any right to be given her injuries and sprung back up to her feet, sword drawn.

    Agatha found herself rising from her seat, breath held. Her mother hissed something at her, but she’d already leaned toward Jen’s ear.

    "No wonder this one wasn’t felled so easily," Agatha murmured, lips quirking in amusement. “Men know no fiercer foe than a woman who has known blood.”

    Jen only blinked, clearly stunned into silence.

    Below, the woman faced her opponent once more, stance unwavering even as blood dripped down her cheek. She wiped it away impatiently, eyes locked on her attacker.

    He huffed and roared in frustration, his swings turning wild. One connected with enough force to dent the woman’s pauldron, but the rest went wide, failing to pose a real threat. It became a contest of stamina.

    A wily stoat cornered by a lumbering bear.

    The knight moved fluidly, effortlessly despite the weight of her armor. She dodged a relentless flurry of strikes, never wasting an ounce of energy, always waiting until the brute overextended himself. Agatha found herself gripping the polished wood of the balustrade as she watched.

    It was a spectacle, watching someone so clearly outmatched refuse to yield. A horror turned into humor. She had underestimated this knight. Not just her skill but her determination. The woman's patience was extraordinary. As if every action had been rehearsed.

    Finally, the brute stumbled forward, breathing heavily, exhaustion leaving him open. The smaller knight seized her chance, stepping neatly inside his guard and slamming the pommel of her sword into the underside of his jaw. A sharp crack echoed clearly above the gasps of the crowd. He staggered backward, dazed. Blood streamed down from his broken nose.

    She did not relent. Swift and precise, she brought her sword to bear, driving it against the man's throat until he dropped to his knees, palms out in surrender.

    The arena erupted.

    Agatha let out a breath, slowly easing herself back into her seat. Jen applauded enthusiastically beside her, eyes wide with shock. Agatha herself was smiling broadly, openly delighted.

    "I suppose I owe you a few coins," Jen murmured.

    Agatha laughed softly, waving off the wager, eyes still fixed on the victorious knight. "Keep your coins."

    She tilted her head, suddenly aware of the sting that announced her mother's gaze. Evanora was strangely silent. Agatha turned, ready for cold disapproval, but instead found her mother leaning forward with intent, calculating smile curling her face.

    The sight unsettled Agatha deeply. Evanora was rarely pleased, and never without cause.

    "What is it?" Agatha asked carefully, suspicion prickling up her spine.

    Evanora did not look away from the victorious knight below. A slow, dangerous smile spread across her lips. "Nothing, my dear. Just admiring our new champion."

    Agatha’s stomach twisted sharply. Her mother never admired; she calculated.

    And that was far worse.

    Doffing armor was a two person job. Removing the mangled and bent metal that surrounded Rio required at least three.

    She took stock of her body as Billy and two others worked to remove her gauntlets and cuirass. Her head swam too much for her to make out the details of their faces.

    She sat stiffly on the edge of the cot in the healer’s house, eyes narrowed against the sharp, persistent pain at her temple. Blood had crusted there, dark and stubborn. She winced as gentle fingers touched it.

    "Sorry, Ser," Billy whispered sheepishly, pulling back his hand. "I'll be careful."

    "You’re fine," Rio muttered, softer than she'd intended. Her body was still tight from the skirmish, her muscles tense with adrenaline, her heartbeat drumming in her ears. She kept replaying those final moments. She had been underestimated. She would never tire of proving people wrong.

    Billy dipped a cloth into a basin, carefully dabbing at her forehead again. The coolness was a relief, and Rio let her shoulders ease a fraction. Behind him, an older healer watched quietly, grinding herbs and occasionally murmuring instructions. The air was heavy with the scent of rosemary, willow bark, and copper.

    "There was a freakishly tall gentleman standing in front of it, but fear not as I saw most of it," Billy said, excitement bubbling into his voice. "You were amazing! They didn’t know what hit them."

    Rio managed a weak smirk. "Clearly, someone knew exactly what they were doing. I have the bruises to prove it."

    "Yes, but you won." Billy grinned, eyes bright with boyish admiration.

    She glanced away, mouth twitching faintly. "So I did."

    Billy wrung out the cloth and wet it again, his movements gentle and precise as he worked more of the dried blood from her hair. "Did you see the princess watching you? I think she noticed how good you were."

    She did not snap at him that she was too busy watching her back to spare any attention toward leering princesses. Instead, she shook her head. “I did not. But I would be surprised if there was anyone who did not take notice. It was quite a spectacle.”

    He hesitated, biting his lip before speaking again. "What will you say when you meet with the queen? Did our order give you a specific message to deliver to her?"

    This time Rio glanced sharply at him, her voice firm. "Your task is to watch and listen, Billy. Nothing more."

    Billy flushed, eyes dropping quickly. "Yes, Ser."

    Rio softened slightly, reaching out to gently squeeze his arm. "Pay close attention. I’m not upset with you. I just want you to be… You have more to learn here than swordplay. And I need you to be protected."

    He nodded seriously, returning to his work with quiet diligence. Rio watched silently as he wrapped clean cloth around her arm, covering abrasions she’d won today and scars she’d earned long ago.

    She allowed herself a slow, steadying breath. Her ribs hurt, but she didn’t suspect any of them were broken.

    That was something.

    Tomorrow held potential. Tonight, she would rest.

    The grand hall was cool despite the sunlight pouring in through the high windows. The marble floors were so polished that they reflected fragments of the queen’s seated form back at Rio, as if her very presence was so commanding that it extended outward into every surface around her.

    Evanora lounged on an elegant throne, fingers resting lightly upon the armrests as Rio knelt respectfully before her.

    “I see,” Evanora said, her voice cool and melodic, “So this is the champion who caused such a stir yesterday.”

    Rio inclined her head slightly, eyes lowered in practiced humility. “Your Majesty flatters me. I am but a humble knight. Fortunate in battle and nothing more.”

    “Come now, we both know luck has very little to do with victory,” Evanora murmured, leaning forward slightly. “Where do you hail from, fair knight?”

    Rio drew a careful breath, feeling the weight of the prodding gaze upon her. “I come from a modest holding at the edges of the northern Wilds, Your Majesty. A humble place, near the old forests. A last bastion at the Empire’s borders.”

    “Hmm,” Evanora hummed thoughtfully, her sharp gaze lingering on Rio for a beat too long.

    “An interesting place for someone of your talents. What has brought you all this way?”

    Rio had prepared this carefully, the words spilling smoothly and without hesitation. “My ward has outgrown the limited lessons I can provide, your Majesty. He deserves a chance to serve in a proper court. A place of prestige, where he might learn from minds far greater than my own. I have taken him as far as my training allows me to.”

    “How generous of you,” Evanora said softly. “And you thought my court might suit him?”

    “Yes, your Majesty.” Rio kept her gaze fixed respectfully toward the floor, her tone carefully deferential. “Your court is renowned across all lands. Where better for him to learn what I cannot teach?”

    Evanora rose slowly from her throne, gown whispering as she descended the dais. She stopped directly in front of Rio, lifting her chin gently with one elegant finger. Rio fought the urge to shirk under that cold stare. She battled with every instinct that begged her to flinch or snarl.

    “And what of you?” Evanora asked gently. “If you’ve reached the limits of your training for this boy, surely you seek something else yourself?”

    Rio hesitated for only a breath before meeting the queen’s gaze directly. “I seek only to fulfill my duty, your Majesty.”

    Evanora’s lips curled—something that was not quite a smile, but more the slow baring of teeth. But her eyes remained just as hard and cold.

    “I do believe that there is a place in my court for both your ward and yourself. I have a proposition for you.”

    She pressed her thumb into Rio’s chin, the edge of her nail just barely denting the skin. Rio held fast. She did not breathe at that moment. She did not speak. She held the queen’s gaze.

    Evanora’s thumb lingered for a long moment before sliding away, trailing down Rio’s jaw with almost mocking gentleness.

    “You see, the princess… my daughter can be a bit unpredictable. She is still growing into the ruler she is expected to be. She has not been worthy of her birthright." Evanora murmured, stepping back slowly as her hand fell gracefully to her side. "You have shown that you're uniquely suited to handle difficult circumstances. I believe you'll find guarding the princess both rewarding and challenging. She requires careful attention and a firm hand.”

    Rio’s curiosity sharpened, though she kept her expression carefully neutral. The queen’s words made clear what she truly meant. Rio would be more warden than guardian.

    Evanora’s lips curled again, slow and calculating. She passed her tongue over her teeth like a viper.

    “She tends to be impulsive. You seem disciplined enough to balance her... excesses.” She paused, eyes glinting. “My daughter needs protection, both from outside threats and from her own impulses. I’m certain you understand.”

    The queen finally withdrew, stepping back a half step and leaving the ghost of a chill behind. Rio straightened, shoulders back, gaze steady despite the cold trickle down her spine.

    “Yes, your Majesty,” she replied evenly. “I understand.”

    “Good.” Evanora turned slowly, moving toward her throne with calculated ease. “What say you, knight?”

    Rio hesitated only a breath, lowering herself into a respectful bow once more. “It is an honor to serve the crown. If your Majesty deems me worthy, then I shall obey.”

    “Excellent,” Evanora purred, settling herself back onto her throne. “I knew you would see reason. Welcome to my court. You and your ward will be shown to quarters… befitting your station.”

    Rio bowed deeply, eyes cast downward, though she felt the queen's penetrating stare lingering on her for several moments longer than necessary.

    She kept her head down as she backed back out of the hall. Only once the tall doors closed with a resounding thud did she inhale again.

    Agatha leaned casually against the frame of her door, eyes narrowed and tongue twirling against her cheek as she entertained herself with her latest prey. He was young, fresh-faced, and clearly already terrified; she almost felt guilty.

    No. She didn’t feel guilty. Contempt. Perhaps pity.

    “Tell me,” she began sweetly, tapping one finger against her chin, “did your captain neglect to mention that your job involves more than merely standing around looking terrified? I know looking intimidating is difficult with that baby face, but surely you can muster something resembling competence. You wouldn’t frighten a doormouse.”

    The knight stammered, pale face turning an alarming shade of red. “Y-yes, your Highness. Of course, your Highness.”

    “Good heavens, man,” she sighed dramatically, rolling her eyes skyward. “Has your sword arm gone as limp as your tongue?”

    He opened his mouth again to apologize (or plead, perhaps) when footsteps echoed down the corridor.

    Agatha glanced up sharply at the intrusion. Approaching was the knight from the arena. The champion who’d caused such a delightful stir. She wore polished armor, and a dark emerald tabard, her helm fixed over her head. Through the visor she met steady dark eyes and a gaze both unreadable and unflinching.

    “Oh, finally,” she murmured, clapping her hands together with mock enthusiasm. “At last someone sent a knight who actually knows how to handle a blade.”

    The woman halted in front of her, dipping her head in respect. “Your Highness.”

    Agatha’s eyes slid over her slowly, openly assessing. “I was expecting someone taller,” she said, voice edged with amusement. “But I suppose you'll do.”

    She pushed away from the doorframe, waving dismissively at the hapless knight beside her. “You’re relieved. Try not to be too relieved. And kindly attempt not to trip over yourself on the way out.”

    He scampered gratefully away, leaving Agatha alone with her new guardian. Agatha held the silence for a moment, scanning her eyes over the knight. Then, with deliberate mischief, she stepped closer, folding her arms loosely beneath her chest.

    “You must feel terribly proud, good Ser,” she teased. “Champion of the arena, best of forty men, and now assigned to play nanny to a princess.”

    The knight did not flinch. “I'm honored to be of service, your Highness.”

    Agatha narrowed her eyes. “Are you?”

    “Of course,” the knight replied easily, gaze calm and unshaken.

    Agatha hummed thoughtfully, circling the woman, noting the rigid stance and careful measure in every breath. Her hand brushed casually over the knight's pauldron, fingers dancing lightly over polished steel, testing for some kind of reaction that never came.

    She leaned close, her voice dropping to a whisper. Her breath was warm and close enough to fog the side of the knight’s helm.

    “Most knights assigned to me flee in terror within the week,” she murmured softly. “But something tells me you’re made of sterner stuff.”

    “I should hope so, your Highness,” the knight replied, voice soft but steadfast.

    “Well, seeing as you too are a lady, you needn’t stand outside. Join me in my chambers. I ought to speak to you of my… inclinations. We should have an understanding if we are to spend so much time in each other’s company.”

    “As you wish, your Highness.”

    Agatha studied those sharp eyes for a moment longer. “You call me your Highness, and not my lady?”

    “It is the honorific of your station. Would you prefer my lady instead?”

    “I’d prefer neither, but if we’re to be so familiar,” Agatha purred, stepping backward toward her chamber, “you may as well use my name.”

    The knight hesitated only a breath, following cautiously as Agatha opened the heavy door. “That would hardly be appropriate.”

    “Then you misunderstand my intentions entirely. I’m hardly ever appropriate.” Agatha teased, casting a smirk over her shoulder. “And what am I to call you, dear Ser?”

    The knight closed the door but came no further into the room.

    “Whatever pleases you, your Highness.”

    “Agatha,” she insisted.

    “Of course, your Highness.”

    Agatha paused, pivoting to face her fully, eyes narrowing with playful irritation. She moved deliberately closer, watching as the knight’s gaze remained fixed stubbornly forward.

    “If you insist on being tedious,” Agatha murmured, fingers tapping lightly on the knight’s breastplate, “I suppose I’ll have to guess.” She tilted her head thoughtfully. “You look like a Margaret. Perhaps a Beatrice? Or a Catherine. A little dull, but reliable. Good girls, Catherines are.”

    The knight didn’t react. Not even a twitch.

    “No?” Agatha sighed dramatically, circling slowly, as if inspecting a sculpture. “Perhaps you’re more exotic. Ophelia? Or maybe you’ve got a quaint little village name. Something like… Joan?”

    Still nothing. Agatha’s lips curled in annoyance, her voice sharpening slightly. “Well, if you refuse to give me a name, I suppose ‘coward’ will have to do.”

    At that, the knight finally turned. “I am no coward, your Highness.”

    “Oh?” Agatha challenged, stepping even closer. “Then prove it.”

    The knight’s gaze snapped up, fierce and unyielding. Her expression hardened, her voice dropping to a dangerous calm. “You mistake discipline for fear, your Highness. My name is irrelevant to my duty. I will never stray so far from you that you have need to use it.”

    Her amusement faltered briefly. Before she could retort, the woman gave a brisk bow, pivoted sharply, and stepped out of the chamber without another glance.

    Agatha stood frozen, mouth parted in genuine shock as the heavy door clicked shut behind her.

    She had been teased, mocked, challenged… but never dismissed.

    She knew how to fight. But this knight had not tangled with her snares and barbs. She had simply disengaged. In doing so, she had succeeded where all her predecessors had failed.

    Agatha was disarmed.

    Her shock quickly faded into irritation. Agatha clenched her fists tightly at her sides, lips pressing into a thin line. She wasn’t accustomed to losing, especially not skirmishes of wit. But even more irritating than defeat was the knowledge that she'd allowed herself to be thrown off balance so easily.

    With a sharp exhale, she turned back toward the ornate dressing screen. She stripped away the heavy, suffocating gown, letting it fall carelessly to the floor. Her fingers shook slightly as she tugged on a simple silk shift instead. The soft fabric felt lighter and less constricting, but it did nothing to soothe the sting in her pride.

    Every other knight had yielded easily to her teasing, succumbing to her provocations or fleeing outright. Each had been predictable. All except this woman, who stood unmoved as stone while Agatha danced around her, prodding for weakness.

    And then she’d simply walked away.

    Beneath her irritation, something stirred. Intrigue? Curiosity? It certainly wasn't respect; Agatha would never respect those sent by her mother. But still, she felt a strange sort of admiration. The knight had shown courage, even gall, in simply refusing to engage.

    Agatha smiled bitterly at herself in the mirror. She hated that she found it so appealing. She rose abruptly, padding barefoot toward the vast bed. Pulling back the curtains, she slipped under the sheets and stared upwards into the darkened canopy. Her thoughts swirled restlessly, already scheming, planning how best to turn this setback into her advantage.

    Her mother thought this knight would control her. Agatha would prove her wrong. Every knight had weaknesses, something hidden, something tender beneath all that rigid discipline. Tomorrow, she'd find the knight's weak spot, slip beneath her guard, and pry it open. No one was truly impenetrable, no armor without its gaps.

    Even knights had weak points; she only had to discover where this woman's was hidden.

    And she would.

    Chapter End Notes At last, we meet again... Up next our princess places the fair knight through a series of trials (and troubles).

    Rose by Name Chapter Summary A test of patience, information obscured, a name is given.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes Agatha leaned back against the plush cushions of her chaise, eyes fixed lazily on her new guard, who stood stiffly near the door.

    Same as she had the previous day. And the one prior.

    She stood with her eyes staring straight ahead, unmoving. It was maddening how easily this knight had slipped into the routine of dutiful silence. She’d barely spoken a handful of words in days, and each carefully polite deflection only heightened Agatha’s need to pry beneath that stubborn composure.

    The knight had endured nearly two full days without giving her name, but Agatha was nothing if not persistent. She leaned back further, stretching leisurely, allowing the silk of her gown to slip just enough to be provocative yet plausibly accidental. She was rewarded with a subtle tightening of the woman’s jaw, but nothing more.

    “Good knight,” she said sweetly, drawing the word out like honey, “fetch me a glass of wine, won’t you?”

    The woman’s gaze shifted slightly toward her, steady and unamused. “I am sure that you have many servants who would happily perform that task, your Highness.”

    “Oh, I’m aware,” Agatha murmured, tapping her fingers idly against the armrest. “But I’m asking you. You aren’t so different. Are you? You’re here to serve, are you not?”

    The knight didn’t move immediately, watching Agatha with cautious appraisal before finally walking silently toward the table and filling a goblet. She crossed the room gracefully, offering it with a small bow.

    Agatha accepted the goblet, deliberately brushing her fingers against her gauntlet, watching carefully for any sign of discomfort or irritation. Only the same steady gaze. She liked to imagine she’d at least garnered a quirked eyebrow. But with that helm on there was no way to be sure.

    “How tiresome it must be,” Agatha murmured, taking a slow sip. “Aren’t you warm beneath all that armor? Surely you must want to remove some of it.”

    “I am comfortable, your Highness.”

    “Oh, don’t be ridiculous. You look positively sweltering,” she insisted, her eyes sparkling mischievously. “Perhaps I should help you remove it myself?”

    “That won’t be necessary,” she replied, her voice still calm and neutral. But her eyes flickered briefly between the rim of the cup and Agatha’s mouth.

    “You know,” Agatha mused aloud, lazily turning a page in the book spread across her lap, “this strong, silent routine was intriguing at first, but now I’m afraid it’s become dreadfully dull. I don’t care for being bored.”

    Her knight said nothing as she moved back to her post. She returned to her still form, eyes fixed firmly on a distant spot above Agatha’s head.

    Agatha sighed dramatically. “What good is a companion who provides no company?”

    “By my understanding, you do not lack companions, your Highness.”

    Agatha snorted, and allowed herself to imagine the knight smirking too.

    “Oh?” Agatha purred. “Do I detect a hint of jealousy, knight?”

    “Merely an observation,” she replied evenly, gaze carefully trained toward the far wall, face again unreadable.

    Agatha tilted her head, eyes glinting playfully. She set the book aside, rising gracefully from her seat. “I did notice you disappear from your post outside my door last night. But I did not hear the clumsy stumbling of your replacement lumbering down the hall until hours later.

    Could it be that my choice of companion upset you?”

    “I assure you, your… activities are none of my concern.”

    “And yet,” Agatha pressed, stepping closer still, “you moved from your post, didn’t you?

    Afraid you might hear something scandalous? Or perhaps afraid you’d find yourself enjoying it?”

    Agatha noted with satisfaction the faint flush rising at the knight’s collar, barely visible in the gap between helm and breastplate.

    “I merely thought it respectful to grant you your privacy, your Highness.”

    “Respectful, discreet, and disciplined. You really are the perfect guardian, aren’t you?” No response. Annoying. But Agatha could fill silence well enough for the both of them.

    She allowed herself a smug smile and closed the distance between them, her voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper.

    “Careful, fair knight,” she murmured, lingering so close her breath threatened to fog the metal plate, “If you have no objection, I’ll give you plenty more opportunities to practice your discretion.”

    She lay her hand against the crest on the knight’s chest, drumming her fingers over the raised lines.

    “Is Raven your name?”

    “It is not.”

    “So you admit you do have one then?” She walked her fingers up toward the curve of her pauldron.

    “Everyone has a name. But I am surprised you seem so interested in mine. You did not have the inclination to call your lover’s last night.”

    Agatha paused the trailing of her fingers and flicked her tongue over her teeth. A slow, wicked smile curled at the corners of her mouth.

    “Why, good Ser, if I didn’t know better, I might think you’re turning green with envy. That appears to be your chosen color for your clothing but I don’t know that it suits you,” she said, tilting her head in exaggerated curiosity. Her fingertips tapped lightly against the polished steel of the pauldron, each movement deliberate. “Were you listening at my door, then?

    Counting how many times I did- or didn’t -call a name?”

    “‘Twas only an observation, your Highness.”

    “Oh, another observation,” Agatha echoed with mock seriousness. “I wonder what else you’ve noticed. Perhaps you’d like to share some of your thoughts with me?”

    “I’m sure that wouldn’t be appropriate, it is not my place,” she replied stiffly.

    “Well, that’s a relief,” Agatha breathed, “I prefer it when things aren’t appropriate.”

    There was a barely audible swallow, a slight bob of the knight's head. As if she were tilting her head towards the heavens in search of answers or reprieve.

    “You have my full attention,” Agatha whispered, stepping back slightly, though her fingers remained resting lightly against her armor. “As you seem so fixated on last night… perhaps next time you might simply join us? You’d get a much better view.”

    The woman held perfectly still, eyes sharp and alert, her breathing slightly uneven. “Your Highness…”

    Agatha pulled back with a soft laugh, interrupting her protest. “Relax, dear knight. Your virtue is safe from me. You are too boring for my taste, I think. Too bound by duty to find herself… unwound.”

    She turned elegantly, sauntering back toward her chaise with deliberate grace. She felt the knight’s eyes follow her every movement and smiled knowingly to herself.

    “If you change your mind,” she tossed over her shoulder, “you know precisely where to find me. And I suppose I know where to find you, too… if I called, would you come running?”

    There was a heavy sigh.

    “That would depend on the circumstance, your Highness.”

    “I am not used to being denied.” Agatha pouted, crossing her leg and leaning back so that she rested her elbows against the windowsill. “I thought you were oathbound to my protection.” “I am.”

    “So what if I screamed? Would you not come through my door then? No matter the circumstance?”

    There was a pause that stretched for ages. Agatha let it, if only to prove that she could be patient too.

    Finally, the knight spoke, voice low and measured. “If you were in true distress, nothing would stop me from reaching you.”
