    Agatha smiled slowly, her fingertips brushing lightly against her own lips. “True distress? And who decides what counts as true, knight? You?”

    The knight hesitated for only a moment. “You do, your Highness.”

    Agatha tilted her head thoughtfully, watching the woman carefully. Her stoic expression gave nothing away, but Agatha knew the strength of her own wiles well enough. Why would this woman be different from the others? Yes, she could sense the faintest crack in her resolve. She felt herself drawn to it, compelled to widen that tiny fissure.

    “Ah,” she mused softly, “then perhaps I ought to scream simply for fun, and see how quickly you come running.”

    “If you screamed now,” the knight answered stiffly, eyes fixed straight ahead, “it would hardly be convincing.”

    Agatha laughed sharply, genuinely delighted by the woman’s boldness. “Well, fair knight, it seems you have me figured out.”

    She traced the edge of the chaise with her fingertips, considering carefully before glancing up again. “If I promise to be good, will you finally trust me with your name?”

    “Goodness is a matter of perspective, your Highness,” the knight murmured dryly.

    Agatha’s laughter softened into something more genuine. “Very well,” she sighed, eyes sparking with mischief , “I suppose we shall continue playing this game. And it will be such fun, won’t it?”

    “As you wish, your Highness.”

    Agatha smiled knowingly, sinking back into the cushions and returning to her book. She would find this knight’s limits soon enough. Until then, she would savor the slow unraveling of each carefully bound thread of composure.

    Even steel had the capacity to bend under enough duress.

    Rio tore a piece from the loaf, passing the remainder to Billy. The bread was coarse and dense, but fresh enough. She chewed slowly, making the morsel last as Billy eagerly leaned forward.

    "So, is it true, then?" Billy asked, eyes wide with conspiratorial excitement. "They say she summons lovers to her chambers after midnight, masked so they won’t know her face… but no one ever sees them leave. And even though magic is forbidden here, they say she once hexed a knight who offended her, turned his armor red-hot until it blistered his skin."

    Rio swallowed her bread, narrowing her eyes slightly. "You shouldn't believe every idle whisper you hear from stableboys."

    Billy rolled his eyes, unimpressed. "I'm just trying to piece together what she's actually like. We came here to learn about the royal family, didn't we?"

    Rio hesitated, breaking off another small bite. "We did. But there's something… unseemly about repeating every bit of gossip like it's truth. She deserves some dignity."

    Billy raised his eyebrows, a smirk tugging at the corner of his mouth. "You're not getting soft, are you?"

    "Careful," Rio warned gently, leveling him with a stern look.

    He held up his hands, bread still clenched in one fist. "All I'm saying is, it won't hurt us to swap notes. You spend all your time standing silent watch at her door, and I’m stuck shoveling stalls. We've both got ears, maybe we should use them."

    Rio exhaled slowly, leaning back against the worn wooden chair. "And what exactly have your careful observations uncovered, then? Besides the fact that horses shit the same everywhere."

    Billy shot her a withering glance. "Very funny. I've learned plenty." He paused, a wicked smile twitching at his lips. "For instance, Her Highness's horse bites."

    Rio let out a soft snort of amusement. "Valuable intel."

    "More valuable than the complete silence you're offering," Billy retorted, though his voice was playful now rather than annoyed. "She must’ve given you something worth reporting?"

    Rio hesitated again, frowning slightly as she stared down at her bread. She thought of Agatha’s sharp gaze, her teasing smile, her carefully crafted provocations. Each moment they shared felt oddly private, like glimpses behind a curtain she had no right to pull back for others.

    Finally, she shook her head. "Nothing yet. She’s… difficult to read."

    Billy sighed dramatically, popping another chunk of bread into his mouth. "Fine, keep your secrets, Ser."

    Rio smiled despite herself, grateful he’d let it drop.

    “Have you met anyone of note? You have more opportunity than I do.”

    “Oh yes. Dawn, Thistle, and Alister are very-”

    She swatted the back of his head. “Not the horses, boy!”

    Billy rolled his eyes and offered the last piece of bread to her. Rio waved him away. He was still too scrawny, and mucking stalls took more energy than ignoring incorrigible ladies. “I have met no one of note but there is a woman I heard of. She runs the smithy, The Three of Swords.”

    Rio scoffed at the ridiculous name. Even so, a blacksmith was certainly a person worth knowing.

    “Go on.”

    “This place… seems to have stricter ideas of a woman’s place. The way the others spoke of her… It seems like she’s respected for her craft, but they are also carrying a grudge? There may be something there. She may be worth a visit.”

    Rio nodded and tallied the days in her head. “Very well. That’s good to know. Perhaps on my first day of leave, we can pay her a visit. I’d like to see about having my visor repaired. This helm I’ve been loaned reeks.”

    Three raps sounded at the door.

    The pair glanced between each other for a moment before Rio rose to answer it.

    An emerald cloaked woman with keen eyes stood in the entry. She scanned over Rio with a discerning look. Rio was used to this type of scrutiny, the feeling of being sized up, but she couldn’t remember a time she’d received it from someone who wasn’t a fellow martialist.

    “Good day,” the woman had a beautiful, almost hypnotizing voice, “I am Jennifer, serving this house as spymaster. No need for formalities among the oathbound though, you may call me Jen.”

    Rio nodded and extended her hand. “Well met. Would you care to sit?” She glanced over her shoulder at Billy and indicated with a jerk of her head that he should vacate the only other available chair.

    “Right Ser! I should be returning to my duties.” He scrambled to collect his pouch and flask, darting through the door with a quick bow in the direction of the spymaster.

    Jen watched Billy retreat with faint amusement before turning her sharp, calculating gaze back to Rio. She gracefully took the vacant seat, her emerald cloak pooling artfully around her.

    “You’ve lasted longer than expected,” she remarked dryly, leaning back with a confident ease. “Agat- The princess typically sends her guards running much sooner.”

    Rio matched the woman’s steady stare, wary but refusing to show discomfort. She recognized another predator when she saw one. “I’ve faced worse.”

    “I don’t doubt it.” Jennifer’s lips twitched, eyes gleaming with quiet interest. “Still, I must ask. Do you have any questions about our princess? Anything relevant to your duties?”

    Rio hesitated. The spymaster’s presence was clearly no mere courtesy. She was here to gauge her, to determine her intentions or perhaps probe for weakness. Revealing too much was dangerous, yet refusing to engage would only arouse suspicion. Carefully, Rio chose her words.

    “The princess seems… confined to her chambers. Is there a particular reason?”

    Jennifer’s eyebrow arched delicately. “Confined?” she echoed. “No, not exactly. She’s merely not permitted to wander the grounds unaccompanied, due to certain... issues with decorum.” Rio let out a soft chuckle. “Issues with decorum? Is there a story behind that?”

    Jenn regarded her silently for a moment, expression unreadable. After a careful pause, the spymaster sighed and leaned forward.

    “A story?” Jen smiled faintly. “There are plenty, though here’s a recent favorite: during a crucial diplomatic dinner meant to secure trade agreements with the southern provinces, Agatha took it upon herself to critique the visiting ambassador’s choice of bride publicly and at length. She was… brutally honest, shall we say? By the meal’s end, the ambassador had stormed from the hall in tears, and it took nearly a month and several costly gifts to mend the relationship.”

    Rio’s eyebrows lifted in amused surprise. “I see.”

    Jennifer’s expression turned slightly fond, if still guarded. “The princess has a remarkable talent for provocation. I would be careful not to allow her to get her claws in you. I do hope your mind is as well-tempered as the rest of you.”

    Rio inclined her head thoughtfully. “I’ll keep that in mind.”

    The spymaster studied her carefully once more, clearly weighing Rio’s response. Then, apparently satisfied, she stood with an elegant motion. “I trust you’ll continue to handle her well. I’ve heard no reports otherwise, and surprisingly no complaints from her Highness. But if you require assistance, or insight, don’t hesitate to seek me out.”

    Rio rose and offered a respectful nod. “Thank you. I’ll remember that.”

    Jen lingered briefly at the doorway, eyes sharp as knives. “Do be careful. You seem capable enough, but I imagine you’ve yet to see our princess at her full capacity. It seems you’re both on guard around each other.” She shrugged as she turned, her voice trailing behind her “I don’t know how worried by that you should be.”

    Rio watched Jennifer vanish into the dim corridor, the spymaster’s final words lingering like a quiet warning. She exhaled slowly, flexing her fingers absently, lost in thought.

    There was truth in what the woman said: she had not yet seen Agatha at full strength. The princess’s sharp tongue and sharper gaze hinted at a keenness Rio had no intention of underestimating.

    But perhaps, Rio mused as she reached for her gauntlets, she could redirect that formidable energy rather than resist it. If she could win even a small measure of Agatha’s favor, perhaps she might gain more freedom to operate within these guarded walls. She needed to be trusted here, not just tolerated. Perhaps a rapport with the difficult princess could open doors that force alone could not.

She was sharp- minded, fiery, capable, and yet kept like a stabled horse. It was no wonder she lashed out; anyone with such a restless spirit would.

    Agatha may have claws, but Rio was no stranger to sharp edges. If she could forge even a small bond with the princess, perhaps she could turn her confinement into opportunity.

    As Rio neared Agatha’s chambers, the princess’s voice drifted down the corridor, sweet and dangerous in equal measure.

    Agatha tilted her head, batting her eyelashes in exaggerated innocence. “Ah, my hero.

    Perhaps you’ll assist me with my troublesome wardrobe? I find myself in need of a change.” Rio stepped calmly inside, inclining her head politely. “Of course, your Highness,” she replied dryly, holding Agatha’s gaze. “If you’d kindly hand over the key.”

    Agatha paused, caught off guard, lips parting slightly in annoyance before curving into an amused smirk. “Clever.”

    With deliberate slowness, she reached beneath the neckline of her gown and withdrew a small brass key, dangling it enticingly between her fingertips.

    Rio approached, expression neutral despite her heart beating slightly faster as she extended her palm. Agatha dropped the key lightly into Rio’s gloved hand, eyes glittering with something between irritation and admiration.

    “Your move, Ser Knight,” Agatha murmured softly.

    Agatha slipped behind the dressing screen and changed into a simple navy frock. She had no appearances or duties to attend to and required comfort over formal presentation.

    The knight stood stiffly beside the wardrobe, seemingly unaffected by the earlier exchange. “Perhaps,” the knight began carefully, “some fresh air would suit you, Your Highness. If you’ll indulge me, a walk through the gardens might help us better understand one another.” Agatha arched an eyebrow. A favor from the knight disguised as consideration. Interesting. She made a show of considering it. “I suppose,” she sighed dramatically. “But remember, knight, if you bore me, I’ll have to act out. And you do not know all the passages of the grounds as well as I do. It would be a shame for anyone to see you searching frantically for me like a bird hound instead of trailing dutifully behind. They would question your reputation.”

    “I’ll endeavor not to disappoint,” the knight replied evenly, stepping aside to let her pass. The air was pleasantly cool as they stepped out into the palace gardens, and Agatha inhaled deeply, grateful to escape the confines of her chambers. She glanced sidelong at the knight, who kept a respectful distance back and to the left of her.

    “Come knight,” Agatha called, gesturing toward a delicate fountain topped with marble nymphs. “I suppose you’d like an official tour. Here is the spot where I kissed Lady Marcela. Quite scandalous, really, considering she was betrothed at the time.” She watched the knight closely for a reaction.

    The knight merely tilted her helm slightly, remaining impassive. “I see.”

    Irritating. But no matter, she had more stories.

    She gestured toward a secluded bench beneath a canopy of roses, tangled and overgrown.

    “And there,” she said conspiratorially, leaning slightly closer, “is the very spot where the ambassador’s daughter taught me exactly how diplomatic relations should be conducted. She was impressively thorough. A gifted orator.”

    The knight cleared her throat softly, though still appeared unbothered. Agatha frowned again. Perhaps this one would have been better suited for the clergy. She seemed annoyingly impassive.

    As they moved deeper into the gardens, the knight paused, looking thoughtfully at a cluster of wildflowers spilling onto the path. “If you’ll allow a question… Is the garden meant to have such a wild feel, or does it lack a caretaker?”

    Agatha stilled, momentarily thrown off balance. Her gaze lingered on the tangled blooms, genuinely unsure how to answer.

    Recovering herself, she turned slowly toward the knight, regaining her playful smirk. “Oh, it’s intentionally wild. You see, I prefer things untamed and unruly. Cultivation tends to spoil the fun.”

    The knight regarded her silently. The quiet scrutiny made Agatha’s skin prickle again, and she couldn’t claim it was fully from annoyance. She turned swiftly, continuing their stroll. “Now come along, knight. I have far more scandalous stories to share. Assuming, of course, your stalwart heart can handle them?”

    There was no response and she turned on her heel with a huff. She did not care for being bored, but being ignored was unacceptable.

    The knight was crouched, a gentle hand cupping one of the blooms that had sprung up from the pavement.

    Agatha’s annoyance tempered slightly at the unexpected delicacy of the knight’s touch. The little pink bloom seemed to curl closer to her hand. Still, she stepped closer, folding her arms as she watched the knight crouched before the flowers.

    “You know, if you lifted your visor you’d have a better view,” she said, voice dipping into irritation. “And then you wouldn’t need to kneel quite so low. Not that I object to you kneeling, of course. But there are far more suitable places for it than the middle of the garden.”

    The knight remained still, hand frozen mid-touch.

    Agatha narrowed her eyes. Without thinking it through, she moved forward, closing the distance between them. Her fingers settled on either side of the knight’s visor. She felt the woman tense beneath her touch, yet she made no move to stop her.

    Agatha paused, feeling her pulse quicken at the intimacy of the gesture. This was certainly not allowed. It made her want to do it even more. With slow deliberateness, she lifted the visor.

    Her breath caught sharply in her throat.

    She’d become accustomed to the knight’s dark, watchful eyes. There were deeper than heartwood, unflinching and unreadable. But nothing had prepared her for the soft, dark waves framing her face, or the delicate smattering of freckles across her skin. Her gaze caught on a faint scar tracing one cheekbone, and she fought the sudden urge to reach out and trace its path with her thumb.

    The knight slowly stood, never once breaking eye contact. Though she stood only an inch taller, in that moment it felt as if she were towering above her. Agatha tilted her head upward, heart fluttering in her chest. She felt oddly vulnerable, as though she were the one who’d been unmasked.

    “There,” Agatha finally murmured, barely keeping her voice steady, “that wasn’t so difficult, was it?”

    A smile. One that would be an undoing.

    “If you say so, your Highness.”

    She caught herself leaning forward but before she could fully forget herself she wrinkled her nose.

    “Your helm is sour with sweat. I’d request in the future you do not wear it around me.”

    The knight wet her lips as if she were about to speak. But before she could, Agatha pushed the visor back down into place.

    “I have seen you, good Ser and you have seen me. I ask you now again… No, I command you as your charge, will you give me your name?”

    For a heartbeat, silence hung thick between them, broken only by the rustling of leaves of a willow stirred by the faint breeze. Then, slowly, the knight lifted a hand and pushed the visor fully open again, revealing those eyes once more.

    “Rio,” she said softly, her voice low yet clear. “My name is Rio.”

    Agatha’s breath stilled briefly in her chest, and something inside her twisted sharply, almost painfully sweet.

    “Rio,” Agatha repeated quietly, testing the sound of it. She tilted her chin upward, regaining her playful edge. “Was that truly so difficult?”

    Rio’s mouth quirked faintly. “Only because you made it so your Highness.”

    Agatha’s lips curved into a satisfied smile. “Well then, Rio, it seems we’ve finally found a suitable place to begin.” She turned away briskly, her heart hammering in her chest, hoping desperately the flush she felt on her skin didn’t show. “Come along. We have plenty left to admire. And now with your name settled, I have many more questions for you.”

    “As you lead, your Highness.”

    And so they walked together in the garden, Agatha slowing her pace so that for a time they strolled side by side.

    Chapter End Notes This is a delight to write and share with you all, hope you’re enjoying the slowest burn. Favors Chapter Summary A day of rest interrupted by a princess in great distress.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes When Rio was a child, she’d learned how to whistle through the gap between her teeth. It made a light trilling sound, and she carried a soft tune with her so often that the birds along her usual trails learned it too. Not once had she felt self-conscious about it.

    Not until Agatha.

    Of course, she hadn’t said anything; she was a lady after all. And maybe it wasn’t about her teeth. Maybe it was the scar that curled behind her right ear, or the one across her temple. Or the complexion of her skin was too weathered from years spent in the sun instead of cloistered in the dark.

    If she’d touched Agatha, she would’ve suspected her disdain was due to the roughness of her hands. She didn’t need to feel Agatha’s touch to know that her fingers trailing anywhere would be softer than the finest silks.

    But they hadn’t touched. There would be no reason for it. If some threat came so close to Agatha that Rio needed to place her hands on her to remove her from harm's way, it would mean that Rio had already failed in her duty.

    And any other reason to touch was so improper that even to think of that warmed her ears. That wasn’t a matter worth lingering on. Not at all.

    Because, as Rio understood it, Agatha must find her a revolting creature indeed. There was no other explanation for the change in her attitude since Rio had revealed her face to her. Today, the princess had barely glanced at her, breezing past with an indifferent flick of her wrist. Clearly the princess found her just as beneath her station as all of the other workers of the house. Not worth any further thought.

    Rio told herself she didn’t mind - it was easier this way. Cleaner.

    But the truth gnawed quietly at the space between her ribs. And with no meat to satiate it, that beast of doubt grew into a desperate and feral thing.

    Beasts didn’t frighten her. If it could bleed, it could be felled. If it had a mind, it could be reasoned with. But this malaise of spirit, now occupied with hollowing her out, was not easily distracted or displaced.

    When Agatha did deign to look at her, it was through lowered lashes, expression tight as though irritated by the very sight. She’d tilt her chin just slightly, lips pursing in silent judgment as she brushed by.

    And sometimes, when Rio caught her staring, Agatha would sharply look away, frowning as if embarrassed she’d been caught lingering on something so distasteful.

    In fairness, though, if she were in Agatha’s position, she couldn’t imagine looking any differently at someone complicit in her confinement.

    Nevertheless, the constant scrutiny made her question if something foul had happened to her face without her noticing. She didn’t ask Billy about it, it wouldn’t be right to trouble her squire with such a foolish question.

    But when, after a fortnight of service, she was granted a day of rest, she resolved to find some basin in town to catch a glimpse of her reflection. Just in case.

    Billy was pleased to be allowed a day out of the stables. The princess hadn’t taken the news so well.

    “You mean to abandon me?” Agatha demanded dramatically, placing a delicate hand to her heart. “What if assassins creep in during your absence, Rio? Or bandits scale the walls, intent on whisking me away for ransom? Think of the scandal!”

    Rio suppressed a bemused smile, attempting to keep her expression neutral. “I’m confident the palace guards can handle any threat that arises, your Highness.”

    She wasn’t actually. But the princess wasn’t her sole priority here.

    Agatha pursed her lips, eyes narrowing in playful skepticism. “Please don’t make me laugh, the guards are hopeless. One of them tripped on his own scabbard this morning. If danger finds me, I’ll be utterly defenseless without you. What, then, good Ser?”

    “I doubt you’ve ever been truly defenseless in your life,” Rio replied gently.

    Billy had picked up more rumors. None of them were meant to be flattering, but Rio couldn’t help but be curious.

    She’d seen no indicators of magic potential. No rune markings, no accessories to spellcraft. But she’d also seen how clever and conniving Agatha could be. She pitied whoever was assigned to her temporary guard.

    “True,” Agatha conceded with a sigh, stepping closer and lowering her voice conspiratorially, “but who else will patiently endure my insults and provocations without fleeing in tears?” Rio felt warmth spread across her cheeks and lowered her gaze to hide it. Despite herself, a quiet pride stirred in her chest.

    “I promise not to stray long,” she said softly. “You have my word.”

    Agatha regarded her silently for a moment, something unreadable passing across her features. “See that you aren’t,” she finally said, turning away with practiced indifference.

    Rio dipped into a low bow before moving to the door.

    She made a mistake and paused in the entry for a moment.

    Next to the newly repaired window, Agatha stood in profile. The stars seemed to illuminate a crown over her head. Moonbeams cast her skin in an opalescent glow, and she felt the breath passing through her lungs stop.

    She forgot herself for a moment. And Agatha noticed.

    The princess whipped her head towards her, but her lips didn’t curl into something cruel and biting. For a moment, she looked nearly wistful.

    “It’s cruel how someone can look so much softer by moonlight,” Agatha murmured, before shaking her head and turning to move behind the dressing screen.

    Rio didn’t know how to respond to that. So she left, closing the door behind her as softly as she could manage.

    On the other side of the door, she allowed herself for a moment to wonder. To imagine some distant, elegant figure befitting a princess’s gaze.

    Certainly not a knight with battle scars and calloused hands.

    “Ser, I have our whole day planned! There’s a tailor that was recommended, and I definitely could use some new breeches that aren’t permanently tinged with-”

    “Shit? I’d appreciate that. Your odor seems to be permeating my clothes, too. And the princess has delicate sensibilities,” Rio scoffed.

    Billy grumbled, “I hear she’s not that delicate.”

    Rio chewed at the inside of her cheek as they made their way down the hill. Billy continued to prattle on, mentioning a bakery, a street corner with a “reliable” news crier, and a few other destinations.

    “Ser? Does that sound-”

    “Yes. Yes, of course,” she mumbled, scanning the intersection ahead of them. “That all sounds fine. I’m going to the smithy, I’ll find you after?”

    Billy seemed to pout for a moment. “Oh, yes, of course, I mean I thought we could use the time to catch up.”

    Rio smiled and lightly shoved his shoulder. “I’m afraid your fumes might catch with the first spark from the forge. Why don’t you first see if there’s a bath house and then see to the rest of your errands?”

    Billy rolled his eyes but smiled anyway, giving a sarcastic salute. “As you command, Ser. I’ll make myself presentable enough for royalty.”

    Rio chuckled softly, watching him weave through the crowded streets toward the tailor. The momentary lightness faded as she turned toward the smithy, a squat stone building tucked away at the end of a narrow alley.

    Above its entrance swung a battered wooden sign depicting three blades crossed in a delicate triangle: The Three of Swords .

    Inside, the space was smaller than Rio expected, but tidy. Tools were neatly arranged along walls blackened by soot. A fire crackled softly in the forge, casting flickering shadows across the neatly swept stone floor. She paused at the threshold, brow furrowing slightly when she noticed no burly smith hammering away—only an older woman humming softly as she adjusted tools on a table.

    Her grey hair, generously streaked with silver, was piled atop her head in a loose bun, held in place by a carved wooden comb. The woman wore a yellow apron, the bright fabric more suited to an herbalist who might dwell in the woods than a master of metal.

    Rio’s hesitation drew the woman’s attention, and her sharp eyes instantly found Rio’s. The older woman smiled knowingly.

    “Come in, Ser,” she said warmly, setting down a pair of tongs. “I’d expected you sooner.” Rio blinked in surprise. “You knew I was coming?”

    The woman’s smile broadened slightly, amusement dancing in her large eyes. “The victor of the festival skirmish, mysterious new guard of our infamous princess? You’re not exactly subtle.”

    Rio flushed slightly, stepping fully inside. “I suppose not.”

    The woman approached slowly, her eyes briefly flickering over her. Rio shifted uncomfortably beneath her scrutiny, but she didn’t pull away.

    “My name is Lilia,” the woman offered gently, wiping soot-covered hands against her apron. “And before we discuss business, you look as though you could use a cup of tea.”

    Rio hesitated only briefly before nodding gratefully. “Tea would be very welcome, thank you.”

    Lilia’s eyes softened as she turned to retrieve the kettle from a small stove in the corner. “Come sit,” she said, gesturing toward a simple table tucked away from the forge’s heat.

    “We’ll have much to discuss.”

    The way she prepared the tea was almost ritual. It was a light orange and cardamom blend, and the fragrance quickly filled the space.

    Rio took a sip and smiled gratefully. “This is very nice. Thank you. I was wondering-”

    Lilia tsked as she brought her own cup to her mouth. “Patience. It’s rude to talk business before the leaves have settled.”

    That must be a local custom. Rio nodded and made a mental note to ask Billy to add it to his log.

    They sipped their tea in silence, and she took the time to look around the shop. Her gaze followed the weapons on the walls up to the rafters, where she noticed bundled herbs hanging. They seemed intentionally placed, and she traced over the pattern with her eyes. “Sage and rosemary. A few sprigs of lavender. The beams are cedar.” Lilia provided, tilting her cup up.

    Rio met her eyes again and nodded. “That explains why this is the most pleasant-smelling forge I’ve stepped foot in,” she said kindly.

    She found herself continuing, providing more information than she meant to. Something about Lilia’s presence seemed to compel it. “Where I hail from, that combination is thought to be used for-”

    “Protection. Yes. It’s a cleansing ward,” Lilia cut in, watching her hawkishly now.

    Rio paused, the realization dawning sharply. She had not expected to find someone like this here. Witchcraft was forbidden in the Empire, and mentioning it directly was dangerous, yet Lilia had laid the truth bare between them.

    Cautiously, Rio met her gaze, holding it long enough to acknowledge their shared understanding without speaking it aloud.

    She took another sip of tea, measuring each of her words. “Has the cleansing ward served you well?”

    Lilia laughed, low and bitter. “Well enough, I suppose. I placed it when my husband fell ill, hoping to cleanse whatever darkness had taken hold of him.” She paused, her expression growing distant. “Instead, it seemed intent on ridding me of him entirely.”

    Rio inclined her head, easily filling the gaps. “I see.”

    Lilia’s fingers tapped lightly against her cup, a faraway look softening the sharp lines of her face.

    Rio cleared her throat gently. “You must have spent many good years with him to be able to take up his hammer and keep this forge running.”

    Lilia sighed quietly, gazing down into her tea. “Some were very good years, indeed.

    Others…” Her voice trailed off into quiet contemplation, “...were simply long years.”

    “My helm,” Rio prompted gently. “I was told it was sent here for repairs?”

    Lilia glanced up, her expression shifting back into a polite smile. “Yes. It arrived the day before yesterday, and I'm seeing to it personally. Though it may take some time.”

    Rio raised an eyebrow, puzzled. “I would’ve thought a smith of your skill could handle a few dents rather quickly.”

    The older woman’s smile grew knowing, her voice dropping into a lower register. “Perhaps I could… if it were merely dents. But it isn’t quite so simple to repair something rune-touched in broad daylight, now is it?”

    Rio stiffened slightly, though she quickly schooled her expression. There it was, confirmed openly. No ordinary smith would have recognized the subtle runes etched on the inside of the visor.

    Only someone attuned to magic would see them clearly.

    She exhaled slowly. “No,” she agreed quietly, choosing her words with great care. “I suppose it isn’t.”

    Lilia regarded her shrewdly for a long moment, eyes glittering thoughtfully. “Worry not,” she said gently, as if sensing Rio’s caution. “Your secret is safe here.”

    Rio nodded, feeling strangely reassured despite herself. “I appreciate your discretion.”

    The older woman smiled, warmth finally fully reaching her eyes. “Good. Now, tell me, knight—what exactly have you come seeking?”

    She wished she still had her tea to sip as she considered her answer.

    “Cleansing,” she finally said.

    Lilia tutted and placed Rio’s cup back on the table, turning it back to face her.

    The dregs of the tea leaves and petals formed a small crescent curve at the bottom of the cup. “A change in plans,” Lilia said softly.

    The hairs on the back of Rio’s neck stood stiff, and she quickly glanced behind her. But there was no looming threat to be seen.

    Rio forced herself to release a steadying breath, eyes narrowing slightly as she met Lilia’s knowing gaze again.

    “A change in plans?” she asked, trying to force the tension from her voice.

    Lilia gave a slight shrug, eyes drifting to the window, as if watching something far beyond. “Your princess is at the center of many things, good knight.”

    Rio hesitated, feeling herself drawn to the subject despite her caution. “Do you know the princess well?”

    “No one truly knows the princess well,” Lilia said with a quiet sigh. She gathered the cups gently, moving toward a small basin to rinse them. “She was born beneath a blood moon. An omen, some say. A promise of trouble and transformation.”

    Rio tilted her head thoughtfully, studying the woman’s back. “Do you share that belief?”

    Lilia paused, placing the cups gently on a drying cloth. “Belief is irrelevant. The truth remains that trouble plagues her, or perhaps she seeks it out.” The older woman shook her head slowly.

    “She found her way here once, years ago. She must’ve been about sixteen. It was late…

    much later than a young woman should have been out alone. At first, I thought she was simply a girl seeking refuge from whatever had chased her that night.”

    Rio leaned forward slightly. “What happened?”

    Lilia turned, resting her hip against the counter, fingers tangled gently in the fabric of her apron. “She was frightened but proud, wild but vulnerable. She bled. And I…” She hesitated, eyes meeting Rio’s, sharp and clear. “I did my best. She stayed only until dawn, and then left as suddenly as she arrived.”

    Rio felt an odd pang of something, but quickly dismissed it as misplaced. “Did you ever find out what she was running from?”

    “No,” Lilia replied quietly, shaking her head. “But I have my suspicions.” The older woman’s expression softened again, studying Rio closely.

    “Mind your step, knight. A princess born under a blood moon is bound to change everything in her path.”

    Rio straightened, allowing herself a faint smile. “I’ll keep that in mind.”

    “See that you do.” Lilia’s voice held a quiet urgency beneath the calm. “There are many blights in this kingdom that need a clever blade to prune them.”

    “Might I send my squire in a few days to retrieve my helm?”

    Lilia nodded, not meeting her eyes directly. “If he is to be trusted.”

    “Worry not, fair knight. I’ll see what can be done.”

    Rio bowed her head. “Is there anything I might do in return?”

    “Tread carefully. Stay surefooted. Mind your oath. When there is more, I will tell you.”

    She nodded.

    “And perhaps on your next day of rest, make your way back here. I have not met someone from… outside these walls in quite some time. You have a refreshing spirit.”

    Rio thanked her for the tea and conversation before leaving the smithy and seeking out her squire.

    She found Billy exactly where she'd expected; in the tailor’s shop, arms laden with neatly wrapped parcels. The boy lit up when he spotted her.

    “Ser! Did you know they have hats with feathers here?” he exclaimed, clearly delighted.

    “Feathers?” Rio shook her head, chuckling. “How fancy. Come along. Let’s put all that away; we’ve still got daylight left.”

    “I suppose you’re ready to test your sword arm, squire?”

    Billy practically bounced with excitement. “Absolutely, Ser!”

    Rio led him to the gardens Agatha had shown her. The memory brought a quiet smile to her lips, though she quickly banished it. She needed focus, not distraction. They found an open patch of grass among the tangled blooms, sunlight streaming down, warming the earth beneath their boots.

    “Feet apart, knees slightly bent,” Rio instructed calmly, echoing the gentle patience of her own mentor. She felt a small pang of nostalgia as she adjusted Billy’s grip on the sword. “Keep your elbows loose. Trust your body. This is more of a dance than a march.”

    Billy nodded solemnly, eyes narrowing with determination. Then, almost immediately, he stumbled over his own feet, sprawling onto the ground. He groaned before looking up at her sheepishly as if expecting a scolding.

    Rio bit back a smile. “Up again. Perhaps those boots are getting in the way.”

    Billy scrambled upright, eager despite his embarrassment. Rio knelt, unlacing her own boots and tossing them aside. Billy quickly followed suit, wiggling his toes in the soft grass. “Better,” Rio encouraged, flexing her own feet against the cool ground. “Feel your footing. Listen to your body - don’t think so much.”

    They resumed training, wooden blades clacking rhythmically. Billy still fumbled, but improved steadily as Rio gently corrected his stance and form. Sweat soaked through their simple tunics, the summer heat unrelenting, but Rio hardly noticed. It felt good to move, to feel the earth beneath her bare feet again.

    As she stepped back to reset their positions, Rio’s gaze flicked upward, drawn instinctively toward the princess’s window. For a brief instant, she was certain the curtain fluttered, revealing a glimpse of shadow and movement. Perhaps just a breeze through the open window.

    Before she could make sense of it, a sharp crack landed firmly against her ribs, jolting her back to the moment.

    Billy froze, eyes wide with shock - and then spun into delight.

    “I hit you!” he cried triumphantly. “I really hit you!”

    Rio laughed despite herself, pressing a hand to her ribs, more surprised than hurt. “So you did. Well done.”

    Billy beamed, straightening proudly.

    Rio glanced once more toward the window, finding it now perfectly still.

    But the moment of quiet was broken by a shout, a cry of her name.

    “Rio!”

    Rio stiffened, blood turning to ice at the desperate edge in that voice - Agatha’s voice. She broke into a sprint without another thought, leaving her boots behind as her bare feet pounded against stone and marble, flying through the garden and into the cool dark of the palace halls.

    Her heart thundered against her ribs, every muscle coiled in readiness. She shoved a startled handmaiden roughly aside, sending the tray she carried clattering down the spiral staircase. Rio didn’t pause, didn’t apologize, barely heard the shattering porcelain echoing behind her. As she sprinted, she mentally ran through the scenarios in her mind. Subdue the attacker. There was a letter opener that Agatha usually kept on the desk. The poker next to the fireplace. The window was always an option.

    Then, tend to whatever wounds the princess had endured. There was more than enough fabric about to tear into a tourniquet…

    She vaulted the last few steps, lungs burning, heart hammering wildly in her chest as she reached the princess’s corridor. It was empty. No guards in sight. She swore loudly at their negligence, rage mingling with her mounting dread.

    “Princess!” she shouted, racing to the princess’s door. It was locked. Another cry echoed from within, gasping and urgent.

    “Agatha!” Rio slammed her shoulder into the door. It creaked but held firm. She stepped back and threw herself against it again, desperation lending her strength. Wood splintered and hinges groaned.

    On the third blow, the door burst inward.

    She skidded to a stop, breath catching sharply in her throat.

    Agatha stared back at her, wide-eyed and flushed, mouth parted. Her hair was a tangled, wild cascade down bare shoulders. The sheets were twisted beneath her, leaving no doubt as to the source of her cries.

    The princess quickly pulled a thin sheet over herself, though the gesture did little to preserve her modesty.

    Rio froze, face instantly hot, humiliation flooding through her. She swallowed hard, heart pounding even louder than before. Quickly, she averted her eyes, though out of habit she scanned the corners of the room.

    Just to be sure that Agatha was truly alone.

    “I-I heard…” she stammered, voice tight with embarrassment. “I thought you were in danger, your Highness.”

    Agatha took a deep, steadying breath, recovering her composure far quicker than Rio ever could. A slow, wicked smirk curled her lips.

    “Oh, dear,” Agatha purred, voice thick and amused, eyes glittering wickedly.

    “You burst through my door without armor, without boots, armed only with—” She tilted her head to examine Rio’s wooden sword, barely stifling her laughter. “—what appears to be a toy. Tell me, Rio, exactly what did you imagine you'd do against an attacker? Give them splinters?”

    Rio’s mouth opened, but nothing came out. Her mind was still racing, heart pounding from adrenaline that now had nowhere to go. “I—I didn’t think. I just heard…”

    “Oh, I’m certain you heard plenty,” Agatha teased, drawing the thin sheet tighter around her body, though it only served to highlight her body rather than conceal it. She arched an eyebrow, gaze sliding down Rio’s sweat-soaked form.

    “But tell me, since you’re here and clearly prepared for battle, do you intend to stay and... be of service?”

    Rio’s jaw tightened, humiliation blooming hot and sharp in her chest. Her breath came quick and ragged, frustration overtaking her embarrassment.

    “Enough!” she snapped, harsher than she intended. “I know I’m hardly appealing, especially right now, sweat-stained and clearly making a fool of myself… but you don’t have to be so cruel about it.”

    Her voice trembled slightly with the force of her words, and she turned sharply away, struggling to steady her breath. The wooden sword hung uselessly at her side, her knuckles white around its hilt.

    Behind her, the room fell silent.

    “Rio,” Agatha said quietly, all trace of teasing abruptly gone. “Cruelty was not my intention.” She glanced back, startled by the sincerity in the princess’s voice. Agatha’s expression had softened. A rare vulnerability flickered across her features before she could mask it again. “I didn’t…” Agatha began, then paused, eyes briefly dropping to her lap. “I wasn’t mocking you. Not truly.”

    The words settled awkwardly between them. Rio exhaled slow and deep, the anger draining away to leave her feeling raw and exposed.

    “Forgive me, your Highness,” she murmured, gaze lowered. “I overstepped.”

    “No,” Agatha’s eyes were still fixed intently on her. “You misunderstand entirely.”

    Agatha hesitated, clearly weighing her next words. She tilted her head, the corners of her lips pulling up in a faint, uncertain smile. “Though, if you wish to linger, I suppose I could find... use for your bravery and agility.”

    The flirtation was half-hearted, almost shy, and Rio saw the instant regret flash across Agatha’s face. The princess shook her head, quickly retreating from the attempt. “Never mind. I spoke in haste.”

    Rio’s shoulders tightened again, guilt surging forward. “I—my apologies once more, Princess. Truly. I’ll take my leave. I didn’t intend for you to ever see me so... improperly.” She turned sharply on her heel to go, but froze as Agatha rose smoothly from the bed. Her bare feet made almost no sound on the polished floor as she crossed to her dresser, silk sheet slipping dangerously as she pulled a robe around herself. Rio quickly averted her eyes, focusing instead on a distant corner of the ceiling.

    “I’m hardly scandalized,” Agatha said quietly, her voice softer now, almost kind. She opened a small drawer, pulling something out.

    Rio remained rigidly in place, eyes locked stubbornly away from the princess’s figure as Agatha approached her again.

    “Here,” Agatha murmured gently. “For your brow.” Rio risked a glance down, heart fluttering unsteadily. Agatha’s hand was extended, holding a delicate embroidered handkerchief. The cloth was pristine, its careful stitching delicate enough to make Rio hesitate.

    “I…” Rio cleared her throat, shaking her head. “I shouldn’t. My sweat would sully something so fine.”

    Agatha’s smile was tender, genuine, as she reached forward and neatly tucked the small cloth into Rio’s breast pocket. “Consider it a favor, then. A small token of my gratitude. For your discretion, and your apology.”

    Rio swallowed hard, heart hammering fiercely beneath the embroidered favor. “Of course,”

    she managed quietly. “Thank you, your Highness.”

    Agatha stepped back, a playful smirk returning briefly. “And Rio?”

    “Yes?”

    “You’re dismissed.”

    Rio nodded hastily, grateful for the chance to escape, even as something warm and unfamiliar took root in her chest.

    As she closed the door and leaned her head back against it, the handkerchief felt heavier than it possibly could be.

    She resisted the temptation to examine it further until she’d made her way back to the garden, assuaged Billy’s worries, and collected her boots.

    That night, she finally pulled out the fabric and turned it in her hand.

    A simple white linen square. Delicate lavender blooms embroidered around the edges. In the center, two hares caught in a circular chase of one another.

    Without thinking, she pressed it to her face and inhaled.

    Her eyes closed involuntarily, savoring the brief, stolen intimacy of the moment. Her heart thudded steadily in her chest, quickening as she imagined Agatha's slender fingers working the delicate needle through fabric.

    She exhaled shakily, lowering the cloth, and traced the pattern with her thumb. Two hares forever chasing one another in their playful, endless dance. It was innocent, whimsical even… completely at odds with everything she'd heard whispered about the princess.

    Yet perhaps that was the point. Perhaps this was Agatha, as few saw her.

    Or perhaps Rio simply hoped it was.

    She carefully folded the handkerchief, tucking it gently beneath her pillow before lying down. Sleep eluded her, mind restless with thoughts of deep blue eyes, sharp smiles, and whispered apologies.

    When dreams finally claimed her, they were tangled, lavender-scented, and full of chasing shadows.

    And an inescable echo of her name.

    Cleansing Oath Chapter Summary Agatha performs a foul ritual. Rio is caught in a crisis of order and oath.

    Chapter Notes TW: Parental abuse and physical harm.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes The first time Rio’s name left Agatha’s lips had been an accident. Truly.

    Agatha pressed her face against the cool side of her pillow, groaning softly. Even now, she could still feel the adrenaline pulsing through her veins. Every part of her stirred by the raw force with which Rio had burst through her door.

    Though the room was painfully hot, she shivered. There had been a wild intensity in the knight’s eyes: dilated, fierce, utterly primal. She had been breathtakingly undone, barefoot and sweat-soaked, wielding nothing but that absurd wooden sword.

    Agatha had never been more enthralled.

    She knew she should feel guilty for causing Rio such humiliation; the knight’s wounded expression had bothered her.

    But not enough to stop her.

The sheen of sweat on skin, muscles shifting fluidly beneath a soaked tunic, bare feet steady on the soft earth. She’d watched shamelessly, imagining the texture of calloused palms sliding along her skin, tracing patterns on her hips, her thighs…

    Agatha’s breath caught again, heat flaring in her chest and lower still.

    She rolled onto her back, staring at the ceiling as she replayed the thrilling moment of Rio breaking through her door.

    It wasn’t about the strength or the shock, but the sheer desperation in the act: born from a fierce need to protect.

    How exhilarating to be the object of such devotion.

    To feel wanted, pursued, and desired enough to tear down barriers. To have a woman breaking down a door to preserve her honor.

    She sighed softly and ran her tongue across her lower lip. She’d bit it in surprise when Rio came through the door, and it tasted like blood. How fitting that Rio’s first act of passion had been destruction. Breaking rules, defying decorum, and claiming entrance without permission.

    Disaster. Distraction. Devastation. Decadence.

    Her thoughts tangled deliciously, conjuring images of Rio’s powerful hands gripping her waist, lips pressed fervently against her neck, pinning her against the very door she'd shattered.

    She couldn’t deny how exhilarating it had felt to see Rio finally lose her composure. How captivating the knight had looked. How breathless she had seemed.

    There were more compromising positions she could have been caught in.

    And perhaps it had been those positions she’d been imagining when she raised her voice the second time. When she’d made sure her voice would carry out the window and into the garden below.

    It was three hundred steps from the courtyard to the top of the tower she resided in. As she worked her hand against herself again, she wondered how many of those Rio had taken two at a time.

    She knew Rio was likely still just outside, perhaps leaning against the very door she'd splintered in her haste. Agatha allowed herself to imagine the knight lingering there. Her breath still ragged. The knot in her brow as she fought some internal war between duty and desire.

    She imagined the handle turning slowly, quietly, the knight slipping back inside to kneel gracefully at her bedside. To offer her fealty in breathless whispers against her thighs. Her eyes fluttered closed as she lost herself fully in the fantasy. Imagining how strong Rio’s hands would feel against her skin. They would be steady and sure, yet tender. Calloused fingertips would trail with gentle reverence down her spine, tracing the curves of her hips and thighs.

    She imagined Rio capturing her wrists in a careful, commanding grip, drawing them gently behind her back. She could almost feel the warmth of Rio's breath, the soft murmur of her voice saying her name again and again and again.

    A sharp knock at the door shattered the spell, jolting Agatha abruptly back into reality. She groaned irritably, glaring at the ceiling as she hastily wiped her hand against the bedsheets. "One moment," she snapped impatiently, pulling her robe tightly around herself. She took a deep breath to calm her racing heart, quickly smoothing her tangled hair before crossing the room to open the door.

    In the hall, her desired was nowhere to be seen. Instead stood the most sour and unpleasant of her mother’s courtiers.

    “Princess.” It was truly amazing how the dour woman could elongate the word long enough to wrap her lips into a sneer. “Her Majesty is requesting you join her for the cleansing.” Every part of of her blood which only moments before had been boiling grew thick and cold. She glanced out the window but the moon hadn’t risen enough yet to tell the phase.

    “So soon Beatrice?” She sighed, twirling the tie of her robe in one hand.

    “I only deliver messages as my lady commands. Shall I inform her that you are on your way? Though… I do not see your steward in these halls. She has left her post unattended. Perhaps you need an escort.”

    Agatha’s spine stiffened, a surge of protective anger slicing through the cold ice of dread. She forced her expression into practiced boredom, though her fingers tightened so fiercely on her robe’s tie that her knuckles paled.

    “My knight was here only moments ago,” Agatha drawled coolly, arching a delicate brow at Beatrice. “I sent her away on an errand myself. Surely my mother doesn’t object to her orders being followed?”

    The woman's lips pinched tight before she corrected her expression into a grim smile. “Of course not, Princess,” she replied stiffly. “But still-”

    “Still, I am quite capable of making myself decent and finding my own way. Tell my mother I’ll join her shortly, unless she’d prefer I walk the halls half-dressed. Either way, I'm sure you'd enjoy gossiping about it.”

    Beatrice’s cheeks flushed an angry shade of red. “Of course, your Highness.”

    “Run along then. Don’t keep mother waiting.”

    Agatha shut the door firmly, leaning her forehead against the cool wood.

    Her footsteps echoed softly against the marble stairs as she descended into the belly of the palace.

    The first time she’d come here as a child, she had walked hand in hand with her nursemaid. She had been excited. It wasn’t often she saw her mother during those days, and Adaline had promised her that her mother had insisted on having some time just between them.

    She’d been told of great treats that she would be allowed to try. Honeycomb from the bees that she watched flit in the garden. A bathtub large enough that she could sink into it entirely. Her mother had even brought pretty silk ribbons from a distant trader to decorate her hair with.

    They were purple. They were Agatha’s favorite color.

    The second time she’d been carried to her cleansing.

    Hauled over a guard’s shoulder, she cried and flailed. She beat her tiny fists against his back until they bled. Her nursemaid carried the scar on her arm where she had bitten her for the rest of her days.

    And now she walked of her own accord.

    At the corridor’s end stood an ornate oak door. Leering gargoyles, wretched things, stood watch on either side. She took a shaky breath and pushed it open, stepping into the lavishly appointed chamber.

    The air in the stone chamber was thick and warm, scented with rich sweetness. A silver tray sat to one side, offering a selection of honey-drenched cheeses, delicate fruits, and a crystal decanter. Everything arranged just so.

    Dominating the center of the room stood an immense copper bath, steam rising gently from the milky liquid within. Petals drifted lazily across its surface, the pale liquid shimmering faintly under candlelight.

    With slow, resigned movements, she slipped off her clothes, letting the linen pool silently at her feet. The humid air prickled against her bare skin as she stepped forward, climbing carefully into the bath. She sank down slowly, the warm liquid enveloping her, a gentle embrace that felt nauseatingly sweet.

    It was too hot.

    Her eyes closed briefly, teeth clenched as she waited in silence.

    Behind her came the quiet click of the latch, then the gentle whisper of silk across stone. Agatha’s heart skipped, and she forced her eyes open and turned her head.

    She could make out her mother’s silhouette through the dressing screen beside the bath.

    “Agatha, my dear,” Evanora murmured, voice soft and cloying. She carefully locked the door behind her, turning again with that measured, chilling grace. “How wonderful of you to arrive on time. Punctuality is a good virtue.”

    Agatha’s jaw tightened, her nails digging sharply into her palms beneath the milky surface. She forced herself to breathe evenly.

    “Of course, Mother,” she replied evenly, eyes locked carefully ahead.

    Evanora approached slowly, circling the bath with a gentle glide, her fingertips skimming lightly over the polished copper edge.

    “I heard that there was a disturbance today.”

    Agatha sank so that she was submerged up to her chin.

    “A misunderstanding, I’m sure.”

    Evanora’s smile sharpened, though it never reached her eyes. She crouched gracefully beside the tub, the candlelight catching on the jeweled rings that adorned her fingers.

    “Of course,” she echoed softly, brushing a stray petal from the edge of the tub back into the water. “But misunderstandings can be so... disruptive. Especially when little ears are nearby.”

    She tilted her head, tone still gentle. “We wouldn’t want to frighten the prince.”

    Agatha’s jaw clenched tighter. She forced herself not to rise, not to react. The water lapped softly against the sides of the tub.

    “No,” she said after a beat, voice low and tight. “I wouldn’t want him frightened either.” Evanora hummed in approval, rising to her full height once more and gliding toward the wine tray.

    “He is such a very sweet boy,” she said, lifting a crystal goblet and turning it slowly in her hand. “Very lucky to be so adored. His tutors tell me he’s quite gifted with language. Already grasping the Old Tongue. Can you imagine?”

    She smiled as she poured herself a measure of red wine, the scent of copper and spice thick in the air. “He reminds me so much of you at that age. When he laughs, it’s almost uncanny. But then, he is much gentler than you ever were.”

    Agatha’s breath hitched again, this time with something more than dread. “How... is he?” she asked, carefully keeping her voice flat, neutral, as though the answer didn’t threaten to unravel her.

    Evanora sipped delicately from the goblet, eyes never leaving her daughter’s. “Thriving,” she said simply. “Happy. Well cared for.”

    Agatha turned her face away slightly, as if studying the shimmer of steam rising from the bath.

    “And what of your new guard?” Evanora asked suddenly, voice light, casual, as though discussing the weather. “She seems to be well-liked among the staff. But I notice you’ve been rather quiet about her. That’s a change for you.”

    Agatha stilled completely.

    This wasn’t a real question. It was a lure. Evanora was fishing, though for what, Agatha wasn’t certain.

    She treaded carefully. “She’s... disciplined. Competent.”

    Evanora’s smile deepened as she turned back toward her, glass in hand. “Is she? That must be a refreshing change for you.”

    Agatha met her gaze, unblinking. “I find she takes her duties seriously. That’s all I require.” “And nothing more?” Evanora asked, deceptively light, though her gaze never wavered.

    Agatha smiled faintly, though her stomach twisted. “I would never entertain familiarity with someone in my service, mother. You raised me better than that.”

    “So I did,” she hummed, pushing her long sleeves to the elbows. “I think you’ve soaked long enough. Lean forward.”

    Agatha did as she was told, curling her knees up against herself and wrapping her arms around them.

    On the surface of the bath, her mother’s reflection danced, and she watched as she drew a jeweled dagger from her belt.

    Slowly, she inhaled and then held her breath as long as she could.

    The edge of the blade kissed the back of her neck.

    It traveled down her left shoulder, cleanly removing the downy hairs from her neck and back. She did not shiver, she did not react. She did not exhale.

    Agatha closed her eyes and waited for the heavy sound of the knife being set aside.

    “It doesn’t seem to have spread. You have been behaving,” Evanora murmured clinically, finally setting the blade down against the rim of the tub.

    Evanora rose and crossed back to the wine tray without another word, giving Agatha the illusion of privacy. Agatha remained hunched forward for a moment longer, ribs tight from the breath she’d held, chest aching with restraint.

    She slowly straightened, the warmth of the bath replaced by a sudden draft that prickled along her skin as steam gave way to chill.

    She stepped from the water, drying only as much as she needed to before pulling on the linen trousers and breastband laid out for her. No tunic. No robe. Her shoulders were to be left bare.

    Slowly, Agatha walked to the hearth. The flames there were low but constant, fed by a wood that never quite burned down. She reached out to retrieve the basin of oil beside the fire, one of many laid out for this purpose.

    But before her fingers could close around it, a cold hand settled at the back of her neck. Agatha froze.

    Evanora’s voice was soft and close now, breath against the shell of her ear. “You’ll need to keep your hair out of the way, dear. You always forget.”

    Agatha didn’t speak. She didn’t move. She merely lowered herself to the furred rug in front of the fire, her body going still like an obedient doll as Evanora sat in the chair behind her. The queen’s fingers worked with practiced ease, collecting Agatha’s damp curls and brushing through them with long, precise strokes. The silence stretched. Only the crackle of the fire filled the room.

    “I do wonder,” Evanora mused lightly as she began to braid, “if your knight has seen it yet.” Agatha's mouth drew into a tight line. She didn’t ask what . They both knew what it referred to.

    “No,” she replied flatly.

    “Mmm. That’s good.” Evanora’s fingers tightened briefly, then loosened again, weaving Agatha’s hair into a long braid down her spine. “No one else must.”

    Agatha nodded once. She could feel the way the braid lay against her back, settling like the excess cord of a noose. Her mother had always been meticulous. Always careful.

    “I’ve never even seen it myself,” she said quietly.

    Evanora’s hands paused for a moment before continuing.

    “No,” she agreed. “And you shouldn’t. You’re better protected this way.”

    Agatha looked toward the fire, letting the warmth creep across her face, trying to anchor herself in it. The truth was: she didn’t know if it was a mark or a curse or something far worse. She only knew it had first appeared the year she started seeing things she shouldn’t. The year her voice began to crackle with something she couldn’t name.

    The year Evanora began this monthly cleansing.

    As her mother tied off the braid with a thin ribbon, Agatha bit the inside of her cheek hard enough to draw blood.

    Soon, the cleansing would begin. And then, like always, her mother would make sure she remembered exactly who she was.

    And what she was not allowed to become.

    “Shall I help you with this?” Her mother asked.

    Mothers were meant to help. To love, to cradle, to keep safe.

    Or so she had read.

    Agatha shook her head and reached for the heavy glove and the basin of oil “No. I can manage on my own.”

    “See to it then.”

    Evanora’s chair creaked softly as she reclined, the firelight painting shadows across her high cheekbones.

    Agatha turned away from her and knelt beside the hearth, reaching for the basin of oil. The scent hit her first: sharp cloves and bitter yarrow, cut through with the copper tang of ashroot. It made her stomach turn, even as her hands moved with steady familiarity.

    She slid on the glove-thick, elbow-length, lined in iron threading- and dipped it into the warm oil. It coated the leather with a slick sheen, the scent intensifying as the glove absorbed it. Her arm began to tingle almost instantly. She exhaled through her nose.

    Early on, she had learned that any hesitation only made it worse.

    Agatha raised her gloved hand behind her, hovering just above the space between her shoulder blades. She couldn’t see the mark, but she could always feel it.

    A second heartbeat just beneath the skin. It pulsed faintly now, responding to the oil. She pressed the heel of her palm into it.

    The pain was immediate.

    Her breath caught in her throat, but she didn’t cry out. She never did. Not anymore. She dragged the glove in a slow, practiced circle, oil smearing over skin, seeping in. Her muscles seized, and her vision tunneled at the edges, but she didn’t stop.

    There was no spell spoken aloud. The ritual didn’t require words. Only obedience.

    And silence.

    Agatha pressed harder.

    The mark always burned at first, hot and sharp, like being branded from the inside. But it was the second stage that undid her. The cold. That sudden, bone-deep chill that slithered through her limbs and into her teeth. The part where she stopped feeling anything at all.

    She bit harder into the inside of her cheek until she tasted iron. Her hand slowed, then finally dropped away, the glove limp and reeking with the weight of what it had absorbed.

    Behind her, her mother gave a slow, approving nod. “Good girl.”

    Agatha didn’t answer. She reached for the cloth to wipe the glove clean, trying to ignore the tremble in her arms.

    “You’ll sleep soundly tonight,” Evanora murmured, rising from the chair. “The burden is lessened, for now.”

    Agatha looked into the flames. She did not feel lighter. She felt hollowed out.

    “Dress,” her mother said over her shoulder. “And take your rest. I know how you need it.” Agatha remained kneeling for one long moment, the mark on her back now cool and inert.

    Dormant. She could almost pretend it wasn’t there at all.

    But she knew better.

    Because it always woke again. And someday, she feared, it would wake hungry.

    She slept on her stomach that night.

    Not because it was more comfortable, but because she could not bear the feel of linen against her back. Even the softest fabric seemed to scrape against the skin there, raw and bruised in ways no eye could see.

    She lay atop the blankets instead, a thin shawl draped over her legs and feet to fend off the draft, the long braid down her spine like a tether grounding her to the bed.

    The moonlight filtered in faintly through her high windows, silver and distant. She did not draw the curtains.

    For a long time, she simply stared out.

    She had long stopped cursing herself for her compliance. It was a tired game. She’d spent years railing internally, hating the choice of her own submission. But hate didn’t change the reality. Obedience. Measured, visible, complete obedience was the only thing that allowed her to see Nicky. It was the only thing that kept him safe.

    And if she had to crawl through flame to ensure his future, she would.

    Without hesitation.

    She did not pray. Except on these nights.

    The cleansing began in her fifth year. During her sixteenth, she started a ritual of her own. Not aloud. Never aloud. Just a whisper in her mind, barely formed thought shaped like a plea. Let him remain untouched. Let him grow, and laugh, and love, and never know the fire I carry.

    That was all she ever asked.

    And though she said no more, she spared a thought, just a flicker of warmth in the hollowed cavern of her chest, for the knight who had earlier dashed so valiantly to her defense.

    She pictured her again, flushed with sweat, blade in hand, eyes wild with urgency. The memory alone sent something warm and treacherous curling in Agatha’s belly. She would not name it. Not tonight.

    Still, she wished that the knight were here now. That someone would press a cool cloth to her temple and run steady fingers through her hair. That someone would see her like this and not flinch.

    But the room was silent, and she was alone.

    As always.

    Agatha rolled her head to one side, eyes fluttering closed.

    Tomorrow, she would rise. She would dress, and smile, and be the obedient daughter the court expected.

    But tonight, just for a little while, she let herself rest as something small and quiet and human.

    In another part of the palace, Rio woke to the sound of dry heaving.

    It took her a moment to realize that she was the one making the awful wheezing noise.

    She swung her feet to the side of the bed and braced her hands on her knees as she coughed. Spit and bile splashed onto the floor. Her body shook, cold with an awful sweat.

    “Ser? Are you well?” Bill mumbled, rolling over in his bed to face her.

    “Fine. Need some air.” She choked out.

    Still coughing she pulled her tunic on as she stumbled out of their shared chamber. She staggered, using the wall to support herself as she stumbled out to the nearest courtyard door. The night air hit her like a firm hand to the side of her face. Sharp with the scent of soil and moss. Rio staggered a few more paces into the grass before collapsing to her knees, fingers digging into the earth as though it might anchor her to something solid.

    Her breath still came in ragged bursts, throat raw, mouth sour.

    She spat again.

    The stars above were pale and indifferent. The courtyard stretched around her, cloaked in that strange stillness that came just before dawn. Somewhere, a fountain trickled. Elsewhere, a cicada rasped too early in the season.

    She pressed her palms into the dirt, closed her eyes, and tried to ground herself in the feeling of it.

    Cool. Real. Here.

    The dream still clung to her like a second skin, wet and heavy. It had been with her for as long as she could remember. Always the same.

    She stood alone on a rocky lakeshore, the sky a deep, thunderous gray. The water stretched out before her, ink-dark and utterly still. And then the rumble began…

    A low vibration in her chest, in the soles of her feet. The rocks clattered, some skipping into the lake as if cast out of her hand. Then the surface shattered, and the wyrm rose.

    Great, endless coils. Scales shining and sharp like wet slate. Eyes like molten gold. It towered above her, terrible and magnificent.

    It opened its great maw not in fury, not to roar. Only opened it, the way a door might open into a temple. An invitation. A compulsion.

    And every time, just as she took a step forward, she woke.

    Was it calling to her? Warning her? About to consume her?

    She didn’t know. She’d never been brave enough to stay asleep long enough to find out.

    Rio groaned softly and let herself fall back into the grass, limbs trembling. She stared up at the sky until her breathing slowed.

    Her tongue felt thick, sour. She pushed herself upright and scanned the beds of the garden around her. By a cracked flagstone, she spied a cluster of mint. She crawled toward it, tore off a leaf, and tucked it past her lips.

    The fresh taste calmed her, sharp and alive against her tongue.

    She chewed slowly, the sting in her chest finally easing.

    Whatever that dream meant, whatever it was becoming… it was getting stronger. Closer. No longer confined to sleep.

    She sighed and pressed both hands to her face. The heels of her palms digging against her eyes until she saw spots.

    Rio pulled her hands away and opened her eyes. She waited for her vision to stop blurring. A tower. A window. A princess.

    An image settled over her, inescapable and far too vivid. Agatha, haloed by firelight and moonshine. Her lips parted. Her breath ragged. Her voice…

    Rio groaned and dropped her head back onto the grass with a soft thud , her hair splaying beneath her like a halo. The stars above remained steady. Indifferent.

    What in all the gods’ names was I thinking?

    The princess had been in her bed—half-dressed, flushed, vulnerable—and Rio had burst through the door like some damn storybook fool. No armor. No weapon but a training stick. Sweat-soaked and wild-eyed. The image made her cringe so deeply that she curled a fist into her stomach.

    She had overstepped. It was clear. She’d acted on instinct, on emotion, not on sense. Not like a knight.

    And yet… and yet, as she lay there in the grass with the scent of mint thick on her breath and her heart still pounding softly in her chest, she couldn’t deny how alive it had made her feel. How real . How utterly electric.

    Rio rolled onto her side and pulled her knees up, trying to make herself smaller. Her mind was still back in that chamber… the way Agatha had looked at her, had spoken to her with that mix of mirth and impetulance. That teasing edge. That lingering question.

    But what if the princess hadn’t seen it that way? What if the flush in her cheeks had been embarrassment, not thrill? What if she woke tomorrow filled with shame?

    Rio’s breath caught.

    What if she lashes out? she thought grimly. One complaint, one sideways glance from Agatha to the queen, and Rio would be dismissed. Reassigned. Worse.

    Being removed from Agatha’s guard would not only cut her off from everything she had started to gather. It would end the mission. It would end everything .

    Her quest.

    The word settled like ice at the base of her spine.

    She stared up at the sky, the stars fractured through the branches above, and clenched her jaw. She had made an oath. A binding, blood-sworn oath. And the longer she lingered in the princess’s orbit, the more impossible that oath felt.

    “I can’t…” she whispered, then stopped.

    Because can’t was a dangerous word for someone like her. Someone with orders. With a purpose. With a price. A duty and an oath and a vow and a responsibility.

    Rio closed her eyes and lifted one hand, pressing it over her brow.

    She didn’t know if she was cursing herself or praying.

    Either way, no one answered.

    She rolled onto her knees and bowed her head.

    She rolled onto her knees and bowed her head, fingers digging into the soft earth as if it might ground her to something true. Something real.

    The coolness of the grass kissed her skin, and still the weight of what she carried pressed down on her like armor she couldn’t take off.

    Her breath shuddered in and out.

    She could still hear her commander’s voice. Calm. Steady. Absolute.

    “Learn why the Empire has opened its borders.”

    “Eliminate the Harkness line of succession.”

    “Preserve the peace between the Wilds and the Empire—no matter the cost.”

    No matter the cost.

    Rio’s jaw clenched until her teeth ached.

    She had accepted the mission. She had said the words. But she had not expected Agatha.

    She had not expected the laugh behind those sharp eyes, or the way her smirk softened when she was caught off guard. She had not expected cleverness buried in all that venom, nor vulnerability stitched between her barbs.

    And she had not expected to want to know her. To want to protect her.

    She could not believe that the presumed heir was locked away simply because she was unruly.

    People tamed the animals that they feared. That could be used. But even a cat that lived its whole life on a rug in front of a hearth carried claws. If it slipped out the window while its owners slept, it could hunt.

    At twenty-some years of age, the Princess should be at the center of succession preparations. She should be under tutelage, making allies and contacts.

    But she was not.

    Why was she not?

    As a child, her mentor told her that, in The Empire, they did not produce their meat from the hunt.

    There was not the honor of tracking through the woods. There was not the risk of being bested by a beast. There was no ritual of honoring the body and thanking the creature by making full use of its sacrificed life.

    Her mentor had spoken of the difference as they helped her set her first snare.

    “Butchers buinneán. They raise their animals in pens. And when they are full and fat, they hang them and cut their necks. And do you know what is the worst of it? The animals go willingly.”

    “Because they are dumb?” She had asked, wrinkling her nose as the twig slipped from her fingers and snapped against her knee.

    “No. They know no difference. They know no other way to be.”

    She swallowed the bile rising in her throat and pressed her forehead to the dirt.

    Not the orders, then. Not the commander’s voice, but her own. The words that mattered more.

    Her oath.

    She whispered it low, like a child repeating a prayer she barely understood: “I vow to kindle the light in the dark places.

    To shelter those who need refuge, to stand against the tide that would drown them.

    Where hope fades, I will carry it. Where wonder falters, I will keep it.

    Where beauty is trampled, I will protect it.

    With heart unshaken, with blade unsheathed—

    I will not falter while my breath remains.”

    The wind stirred the grass around her, and still no divine answer came. But the words… her words settled in her chest like an echo of her heartbeat.

    And therein was the fracture.

    Her orders were clear. Her oath was clearer.

    And she? She was caught in the rift between the two.

    Agatha was more than a name in a command. She was a person. A flame. A truth. A complication.

    And Rio had no idea how to save her and obey her orders at the same time.

    “I can’t,” she whispered again, not in defeat this time, but in defiance. “I can’t do both.” She lay there until the sky turned pink. She took one last breath and stood. Where her head had rested, she noticed a small dandelion had sprung up, somehow uncrushed under her weight.

    She picked it, careful not to let too many seeds fall.

    Rio wished. And then carried on to start her day.

    Chapter End Notes The hurt before the comfort. I swear on my sword that Evanora will meet a fitting end (eventually)

    Curious Summons Chapter Summary Three trials with only spirit as her guide. A summoning and a conversation.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes At eight years old, her mentor told her she was still too young to be taught to wield a weapon.

    “But you let Ellin learn last spring,” she’d protested, arms crossed tight and bottom lip pushed forward. She was a child anxious to grow up, but not too old to pout.

    “Ellin was ready,” her mentor replied without looking up from the herbs they were grinding. “Are you?”

    Her mentor did not make a habit of asking questions they did not already have an answer for. Rio knew this, but her protest still tumbled out of her.

    “I’m small but I am strong! Like an ant is. I’m fast - faster than the other children.”

    Much faster than Ellin. She’d raced her the other day through briar and bramble. Ellin had kept to the path. Rio had not.

    “That’s not what I asked. Patience, little one. Your time will come. Be a child while you can.” The words frustrated her, though they were unsurprising. Nothing came freely in their household, not even answers.

    Life in the cottage was shaped by rhythm. Mornings were filled with various tasks: gathering water from the well, sorting roots and stems, feeding the hens with handfuls of cracked grain. Her mentor taught through stories, silences, and often gentle correction.

    Rio learned to read the weather by the smell of the wind and tell time by the moss on the stones. She learned that it was best to share fresh berries with the neighbors, even if they hadn’t gone to the trouble of picking them for themselves. Even when they had nothing to offer in return.

    Evenings were for tending the fire, for tea and quiet questions, for watching the shadows dance on the beams and hearing the stories the house liked to whisper around them.

    She lived a lucky life, and she was happy to live here—even when it confused her and left her wanting more, especially then.

    Still, she bristled at the waiting. She burned to do, not just be.

    When the wind rattled the rafters that evening and the stew began to simmer, her mentor humming an old song in the kitchen, Rio noticed something.

    The attic hatch was open.

    It had always been shut before. Not locked. Just closed. And in a home like theirs, doors that stayed closed meant something .

    But tonight it was ajar. Rio, barefoot in her nightshirt, stared up at it like it might snap shut if she waited too long.

    She climbed the ladder, small hands unable to fully wrap around each rung. The air up there was thick with dust and old musty smells.

    The attic wasn’t large, but adventuring there as a trespasser made everything seem bigger. She could make out the outlines of old furniture beneath moth-eaten sheets, bundles of forgotten herbs hanging from beams, scattered jars catching the moonlight and fracturing it like crystal.
