    And in the corner, half-covered in a scrap of wool and faintly glowing, was a wooden box. It had a spiral carved into the lid, the kind that meant something if one was clever enough to know how to look. She didn’t know what it meant, as she hadn’t learned the old runes yet. But there was no harm in looking.

    Rio crept toward it, heart loud in her chest.

    She reached out. The lid clicked open the moment her fingers brushed it.

    Inside was a stone. Pale and iridescent, warm with a light that cast no shadows on the walls around her. When she brushed her fingertips against it, the stone let out a hollow, muted sound like a bell being struck underwater.

    She didn't flinch.

    "Curiosity will show you the door."

    Rio whirled. Her mentor stood in the stairwell, arms folded, the edge of a smile on their lips. "But cunning will tell you when not to open it."

    She hunched a little, cupping the box to her chest. "I didn’t break anything."

    Her mentor stepped into the attic fully now, her bare feet making no sound against the wooden planks.

    "No," she said. "But you've begun."

    Rio looked down at the stone again, then back up. "What does it do?"

    Her mentor crouched beside her, resting their elbows on their knees.

    "We shouldn’t look at things just to use them, mo chroí. That’s Empire thinking. It's not a question of what itdoes. Ask instead: what does it mean?"

    Rio blinked. “Okay. How do I know what it means?”

    Her mentor smiled and nodded.

    "That's a better question."

    The stone pulsed faintly in her hands.

    A kettle began to whistle downstairs, the only answer to be given that night.

    The following day, her mentor placed a thick oatcake in her hand and slung a satchel over her shoulder. No explanation. No instructions.

    Rio followed.

    They walked through the mossy glen in silence, apart from the birdsong that flitted between the trees, nature’s gossips announcing their arrival. Dew clung to her bare ankles, and Rio tried not to shiver as she skipped to keep up with her mentor's longer stride.

    They stopped at a bend in the stream just before the old willow tree where the water slowed. Her mentor crouched and gestured to the opposite bank.

    “Watch.”

    At first, Rio saw nothing. Then, as her eyes adjusted to the mottled light, a creature emerged —small and mossy, like a stone given legs. It had no real face, but two voids sparkled the hint of something metallic beneath its shaggy fern-like covering. It made a sound like sniffing the air, then took a careful step toward the water.

    Rio reached for the net she kept at her side.

    Her mentor did not look at her. “Think. If you trap a thing just to name it, you’ll never know what it truly is.”

    Rio froze, fingers hovering. “But if I catch it… I could ask it something.”

    “If it lets you.”

    The net forgotten, Rio stepped forward, small and barefoot and open. The creature turned, not afraid, but cautious. Curious. Like her.

    Crouching, she whispered, “Can you talk?”

    The creature tilted its lichen-covered head.

    “If you can… what do you sound like?” she asked, almost hopeful. “Can you hear me? What do I sound like?”

    The creature blinked—if such a thing could be called a blink. A shimmer moved over its mossy body like wind over grass. The metals hidden below where eyes should be sparkled.

    Rio leaned forward. “Have you always lived here? Do you have a name?”

    No answer, but the shimmer pulsed again.

    “Do you… do you like being alone?”

    Still no answer. But this time, the creature stepped closer.

    “Do you have a family? Would you like a friend?”

    She stayed still, watching the creature dip its toes into the water. The surface rippled, but betrayed no reflections except her own. Minutes passed. A breeze lifted with the scent of clover.

    Beings found in the woods without a name were usually honored spirits. Some were great and powerful, others small and worthy of equal wonder. It was said to be lucky to meet one. Auspicious was the word that the grown-ups used, but she always got tongue-tied when she tried to repeat it.

    It padded over to her and placed a pebble in her open palm. Then the spirit vanished into the brush, leaving no more trace than the sigh that followed a good story.

    Her mentor turned to her and smiled. “That was the first.”

    Rio looked down at the pebble. It shimmered faintly, warm in her hand.

    She curled her fingers around it and whispered a small blessing.

    By late afternoon, the sun had warmed the hills into a golden lull. The pair came to a rise blanketed with moonflowers, their silver-veined leaves curled tight beneath the sun.

    Stopping at the very edge, Rio’s guide appraised her.

    Rio squinted at the flowers, then back at her mentor. "Do I pick them?"

    A shake of the head. Not the correct question. "Only if you wish to ruin them."

    Rio frowned. "So I wait?"

    "You listen."

    The breeze had died. Even the insects had quieted. There was nothing to be heard, nothing to mark the passage of time. So she waited.

    Rio sat cross-legged in the grass, gaze locked on the blooms. They looked like they were holding their breath, also caught in that liminal space between wanting and knowing. She folded her arms and stared harder, thinking that maybe if she willed them open, they would respond.

    They did not bend to her will. They didn’t seem to care at all.

    Time dragged. She itched at the back of her neck. She shifted. Her mentor said nothing. Only watched. So Rio lay back and watched the sky instead.

    She’d done this before with someone else. She had lain against a warm full chest and called out the creatures she had seen in the shapes parting above her.

    But Rio needed to be serious now. She didn’t comment that one looked like a wyrm nipping at its own tail.

    When the shadows finally stretched long and soft across the meadow, Rio sat up again.

    One of the moonflowers had opened.

    It unfurled slowly, petal by petal, a secret told one syllable at a time. Pale blue at first, then a faint cream glow. Another followed. Then another. All at once, the field began to bloom in chorus.

    Rio stared, wide-eyed.

    "What did I do?" she whispered.

    "You waited. You listened. You let the world come to you."

    Rio reached out, not to pluck, but to touch a petal. It was cool, trembling slightly with the breeze.

    "That," said her mentor gently, "was the second."

    Rio smiled, the moonflowers reflecting against her eyes.

    The final test came when the wind changed direction and the air smelled of loam and firewood. They handed her a staff—old, polished smooth with use, and wrapped in green-

    dyed leather. It felt warm in her hands, like something that still remembered being alive. Like something that still was.

    "We protect what we understand," her mentor said quietly. "And we do not strike until we understand what must be protected."

    They turned toward the forest. The light was slipping from the world, and the first shy stars were beginning to blink into existence. Ahead, a winding game trail, a path for those with eyes to recognize it.

    “Walk until the second bell. Not to test your fear, but to greet it. Return before the third." Rio hesitated, tiny fingers tightening around the staff twice as tall as she was.

    "Will you wait here?" she asked.

    Even warriors of the Wilds carried gentle smiles for children. "I always do."

    So Rio walked.

    The forest welcomed her, dark but not frightening. Crickets sang. Something larger rustled in the underbrush, but she didn’t run. She followed the stream, her steps steady. With the staff she tapped a steady rhythm against the ground.

    She passed under branches heavy with moss, through clearings lit with bioluminescent foxfire. Once, she thought she saw a pale spirit watching her from between the trees—not threatening, just present. Witnessing. She bowed her head in quiet greeting.

    When she raised it, she was alone once more.

    The bells rang in the distance. The second had come.

    But she did not turn back.

    She followed the sharp scent of mint.

    And there, tucked between the roots of a great willow, she found a patch growing wild. Rio knelt and brushed her fingers over the leaves, careful not to pluck too many. She whispered thanks, as she'd been taught.

    She returned in silence, arriving well after the third bell. Her cheeks were flushed, her braid half-loosed by the wind.

    As promised, her guardian stood waiting, silhouetted by the fire.

    "You’re late, réaltín."

    Rio held up the sprig of mint as an answer.

    "There were voices that wanted me to follow," she said quietly. "But I didn't. I followed the green. I followed the light."

    Her mentor took the mint from her, and she inhaled deeply.

    "You found your own path."

    Rio nodded.

    "Then," her mentor said, stepping back to make room by the fire, "you are ready. Tomorrow the sun rises. And we will start again."

    Lying in the grass of the courtyard garden, Rio pressed her fingers to her temple.

    The sprig in her mouth now was bitter and sharp—but it brought her back.

    Curiosity, cunning, compassion.

    And now? Now she walked in a forest full of shadows again. Answering a call to protect something she was forged to destroy.

    Agatha.

    The moonflowers of the Empire, blooming on their own terms.

    She did not know if her mentor would be proud of her now. And she chose not to dwell on that. But she remembered the words they whispered when she returned from the woods late, heart pounding, fists full of trembling greens.

    “Being late is not failure. Not if you return with your truth.”

    The sky above her was a pale hush of lavender, rose, the faintest edge of gold. There was no judgment in the light. Only time passing. Only the world waiting to be woken again.

    She let herself breathe the morning in. Even here, the grip of Empire had neither authority nor ability stifle the scent of damp earth. It could not stop her from feeling the way the grass prickled against her skin, or from recognizing the way the earth hummed under her bones.

    She had been taught to listen. To wait. To let things speak in their own time.

    But how was she meant to do so here?

    In a palace where nothing breathed freely. Where each plant was either trimmed or deemed unruly and left to rot and every hall echoed. The Empire was not a forest—it was a wall.

    Agatha was not a flower tucked within it. She was growing through it. Bent toward light that did not exist. That shouldn’t exist.

    How could she come to understand Agatha and survive this place?

    Rio clenched her jaw.

    Maybe she couldn’t. Maybe no one could.

    Her fingers curled in the grass.

    Was it madness to wonder what Agatha might be like somewhere else? Somewhere wild and wide and full of strange, soft things.

    A place where the wind could tangle her hair and the moonflowers would bloom for her too, without having been asked. Would her sharpness dull in the open air? Or would it sharpen further, into something ungovernable? Something wild.

    Would she laugh? Would she be frightened?

    Would she follow, if asked?

    No. That was foolish.

    Even if such a place still existed in this fractured empire, it was not hers to offer.

    But still, the thought nestled in her chest like an ember.

    A selfish wish. And yet… Maybe not so selfish.

    Rio exhaled slowly, mint and morning fog on her breath.

    Protect what you understand.

    She closed her eyes.

    Do not strike until you understand what must be protected.

    Perhaps that meant staying still a little longer. And asking better questions.

    The sun had just breached the eastern ridge when Rio rose from the garden grass, brushing dew from her palms and shoulders. She walked slowly, her path winding back toward the servants' entrance.

    She had barely stepped into the stone hallway when a voice called, crisp and far too awake for the hour.

    "Ser Rio."

    She turned to find the most unpleasant of the staff she’d met, Beatrice, standing with her hands folded and usual sour face.

    "The princess has requested you. Immediately."

    Rio blinked. "Requested?"

    "Indeed. She is waiting for you in the eastern conservatory."

    Agatha didn’t often go anywhere outside her rooms. Rio had only seen the conservatory once — it was a high-vaulted gallery of glass and iron and overpruned vines. The gilded hall was too sharp with sunlight to feel natural, too stifling for any of the plants within to thrive. Beatrice lingered long enough to make it clear she noted the state of Rio’s hair, the faint dirt on her breeches, the absence of her proper tabard.

    "Shall I inform the princess you'll arrive at your leisure?"

    "No. I'll go now."

    She walked the rest of the way with her heart pounding. Not because she feared reprimand, though that was likely. But because the weight of last night still pressed hot and heavy over her shoulders. The image of Agatha wrapped in silk and shadow was branded behind her eyes.

    Fool, she reminded herself. You let yourself forget your place.

    Agatha stood at the far end of the room, haloed by the morning light spilling through the glass. Her robe was loose but regal, deep plum stitched with silver thorns. One hand rested on the back of a carved bench, the other holding a small plate with slices of pear and a curl of honeycomb.

    She looked like a portrait.

    Not the kind painted and displayed in ancestral halls, but the sort found half-forgotten in the corner of a ruined chapel. A divinity that couldn’t be fully captured by mortal eyes and hands. She looked like she belonged among moss and sunlight, not encased in marble and mirrors. Her expression was unchanging, a study in stillness, and yet—

    She breathed.

    And in that single, simple sound, Rio's stomach twisted. Because she wasn’t a painting. She wasn’t a statue or an illusion conjured by morning haze.

    She was the pull of tide and root, of wild things that couldn’t be named. She was a footprint etched in soil, only until washed away by the gathering rain. The kind of magic Rio had once knelt before in the woods, not knowing if it would reach to touch her or swallow her whole. She was a spirit, not of fire or fury, but of bloom and blood. And Rio…

    Rio didn’t know if she could speak her tongue. But gods above and below, she wanted to try. And then Agatha shifted, her head tilting, and just like that, the spell cracked.

    “Ah,” Agatha said lightly, without looking up. “The fair knight does answer summons. I was beginning to think I’d been mistaken.”

    Rio bowed. "Your Highness."

    "So formal this morning," Agatha said, finally meeting her eyes. "Perhaps you thought I was still in bed."

    Rio flushed but did not speak to confirm her misstep.

    "I assume you received my note. I’d like to understand why it was ignored."

    Rio's stomach dropped. "Of course not, your Highness. I—"

    Agatha held up a hand. "Don’t lie to me, Ser. It’s unbecoming."

    "I wouldn’t lie," Rio said quietly. "I was in the gardens. I didn’t know a message had been sent."

    Agatha studied her face. Whatever she saw there caused her lips to twitch.

    "Then you're merely unreliable, not willfully cruel. That's... slightly more tolerable."

    "I never meant—"

    "No," Agatha cut in, setting the plate down on the bench. "You didn’t mean to. No one ever means to. Yet here I am, making excuses for you to the court while they whisper about why a trained knight would break a door to reach me in the dead of night."

    Rio swallowed. "I thought you were in distress."

    "And how chivalrous of you to assume I couldn’t handle it."

    Agatha exhaled and turned back toward the glass. Her voice, when she spoke next, was quieter.

    "You were meant to be different. Not like the others."

    Gently now, she stepped forward. "Then if it pleases you, allow me to be."

    Agatha glanced at her sidelong. "You missed your chance."

    They stood like that for a moment, both too proud to bend, too uncertain to break.

    "You could have come back. I said you didn’t have to go."

    Rio wished, not for the first time, that she understood the rules of this game. That the forest had taught her what to say when someone was not speaking plainly.

    "I will always come when you call."

    Agatha turned fully to face her then. Her eyes were unreadable.

    "Good," she said after a beat. "Then let this be your call. I expect you at my side tonight. There is to be a gathering. I would rather not face it alone."

    Rio nodded. "As you wish."

    Agatha stepped past her toward the door, pausing just close enough for her sleeve to brush Rio’s arm.

    "And Ser?" she said without turning back.

    "Yes, your Highness?"

    "Next time you come crashing into my chambers, try knocking first. Unless, of course, you like putting on a show for the castle."

    Rio felt the heat crawl up her neck, but didn’t look away.

    Agatha turned her head just to make sure Rio caught the last glimmer of her smirk, her tongue darting between her teeth.

    "It’s terribly disruptive," she added, "unless I ask for it."

    Rio found Billy in the training yard just as he finished sparring with one of the junior stablehands. His tunic clung to him with sweat, his cheeks flushed from effort and sun.

    "Ser!" he grinned when he saw her, then immediately looked sheepish. He fumbled in his pocket. "I meant to give this to you earlier. Really. I did. But you weren’t in your bunk, and then I got... distracted."

    He handed over a folded piece of parchment, the purple wax seal bearing an image of a crescent moon and three pinpricks resembling stars.

    Rio took the missive silently. Her lips pressed into a thin line as she opened it and read. The script was sharp, looping.

    If you meant to scandalize the entire upper court by charging through my door like a reckless, foolhardy knight from some half-remembered ballad—congratulations. You've succeeded.

    You owe me an apology. You owe me an explanation. And you owe me your company.

    I’ll wait for you in the conservatory to discuss the details. Mind you, I am not known for patience.

    Billy winced. "You’re upset. I can tell. You do that thing with your jaw when you’re upset." She didn’t answer, carefully refolding the note.

    "I can make it up to you!" Billy said quickly. "I’ve been gathering intel. Useful stuff. About some gathering that’s going tonight. It’s going to be a big one. Fancy, with important people. Court officials, out-of-town lords. And there’s talk of a formal toast from the Queen herself. Might be political. Might be personal. You know how these people are."

    He waved his arms a bit too eagerly. "There’s even going to be dancing. Maybe dueling, depending on how much wine gets poured. The Princess always makes a statement at these things."

    "I suppose I’ll be part of that statement."

    Billy blinked. "Wait… you’re going ?"

    She nodded, tucking the note away. "Apparently, I’ve been summoned."

    Billy whistled low. "Ser, if I didn’t know any better, I’d say you were honored. "

    "I’m cautious," Rio corrected. But her heart, traitorous thing, fluttered all the same.

    Once Billy had scampered off toward the kitchens, Rio reached again for the note. She ran her thumb along the fold, opened it one more time, and reread the last lines.

    Tonight. Wear something sharp—I do like to be stared at. If you show up, I may even forgive you for leaving.

    She allowed herself the smallest of smiles.

    And then a shadow passed over her shoulder.

    "Cryptic letters from royalty?" came a dry, familiar voice. "You’ll want to be careful with those. They have a tendency to explode."

    Rio turned. Lady Jen stood behind her, hands tucked neatly behind her back, gaze sharp as ever.

    "The Queen has eyes everywhere, Ser. I’d take care not to lose your footing."

    Rio nodded slowly, schooling her face into neutrality. "I know where I stand."

    Jen’s smile was all sharp teeth. "Do you?"

    She stepped closer, voice lowering to a murmur. "I haven't yet decided if that makes you a liability... or something far more interesting."

    Rio said nothing, but her hand folded the note a little tighter.

    "Enjoy the gathering," Jen said lightly, already turning away. "The court is always more fun when it’s off-balance."

    She vanished down the corridor, leaving Rio to watch the empty space she’d left behind.

    Rio stood before the mirror, fastening the last strap of her bracer. It wasn’t armor, not truly, just formal wear stitched in a military style. Polished leather, decorative silver buckles, and deep green fabric were pressed smoothly. No sword at her side tonight, just a ceremonial dagger. It was blunt and useless in any real fight.

    But it felt good to have something at her side, even if it wasn’t useful. Some things were carried simply because of tradition.

    In The Wilds, it was said that the body remembers long before the mind does. So before festivals and funerals, a warrior always wore a blade. Not for violence, but for grounding. For reminding the spirit who it belonged to.

    Her mentor had once called it carrying your spine in your hand.

    So Rio did. Out of respect. Out of superstition. Out of habit.

    She adjusted the clasp again. The dagger glinted faintly in the lamplight. It looked harmless. It wasn’t.

    Neither was she.

    Still, it felt like preparing for battle.

    The motions were too familiar. Buckle. Breathe. Adjust. Breathe. Square the shoulders.

    Breathe.

    But this wasn’t the field. There would be no blood in the ballroom, just sharp smiles, false praise, and invisible blades embedded in every kind word.

    She’d been younger than Billy the first time she’d seen real blood spill. Her first kill had been fast—a man three times her size, still laughing when she ran him through. She’d felt nothing at the time except the strange lightness of surviving.

    It was the second one she returned to most.

    Not because it had been messy, though it had been. Not because she’d hesitated, though she had. But because the man, the boy, had looked at her like he recognized her. Like he knew something about her that she didn’t.

    She didn’t even know his name.

    Some nights, when the wind was too still or her bed too soft, she thought of him. Not with guilt exactly. But with… weight.

    She’d killed more since. Keeping count in a place no one could see. She’d learned where to strike for silence. Where to strike for speed. Where to strike to make it clean.

    Rio always tried to keep it clean.

    But not all things in this world could be made so. Living was a messy, ugly thing. Death sometimes moreso. Sometimes less.

    She looked at herself in the mirror now and wondered if this mask of civility, this high court theater, was just another kind of battlefield. A war fought with fabric and posture, with words in place of steel.

    She wasn’t sure which was more dangerous.

    Chapter End Notes Giving little Rio a lisp because I can. Next, a ball and a bomb.

    Almost Chapter Summary A dance, a spectacle, a spirit in chains. Agatha reaches. Rio does not retreat. In the Empire’s gilded mouth, they taste the first hint of blood.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes The first time Agatha attended a formal gathering, she was thirteen.

    The sleeves of her gown itched. Her corset was too tight, caging her torso. The powdered perfume that had been dusted along her collarbones made her sneeze every few minutes, earning her a pinched look from her mother.

    “One must learn to endure many small discomforts,” Evanora murmured, straightening Agatha’s bodice with intentionally too-tight fingers. “Endurance separates queens from girls who disappear quietly from history.”

    Together they walked from the shadowed corridors into the light-drenched maw of the grand chamber.

    Evanora’s hand rested lightly at the nape of Agatha’s neck, not as a gesture of affection, but rather as a quiet grip of ownership. It wasn’t overly tight, but unyielding all the same. The way one might guide a hound into a collar or a fragile stem into just the right position before cutting it and adding it to a vase.

    Each step felt like it echoed too loudly. Her slippers were velvet. Even still, the stone beneath seemed to amplify her presence - too big, too clumsy, too loud.

    The hall ahead, the altar, waited, full of jeweled eyes and vicious tongues.

    And she was the offering.

    “Shoulders back. Chin high. Do not blink too much. It makes you look soft.”

    Agatha didn’t respond. She only walked, as instructed, into the dazzling hush that preceded the announcement of her name.

    One would never have guessed it was night outside due to the way the hall filled with light. Torchlight bounced from mirrored panels and every surface was polished to reflect back each expectant smile and calculating glance.

    Agatha hated it.

    She’d been warned that the event was a diplomatic endeavor—an introduction to the heirs and daughters of noble houses. Her mother preached the importance of learning to move among them. How to cultivate allegiances. How to draw people in, accumulating power like a spider plucks thread. How to plant and cull and reap.

    Evanora let her eyes linger on Agatha’s too-thin shoulders, her still flat chest, and smiled. “You’ll meet someone here,” she said, as if it were inevitable. “Someone you’ll marry one day when you are of age. These things must be arranged early.”

    At thirteen, Agatha hadn’t known how to name the fury that bloomed behind her ribs. But she’d known enough to weaponize it.

    She watched the youngest son of House Dorel fumble his court bow. He’d been a head shorter and still afraid of both her shadow and his own. So she’d taken his hand during a quiet dance, bent low, and whispered in his ear that she much preferred his sister's company. He’d stammered, blushed scarlet, and tripped over his own foot into a punch bowl.

    She’d been escorted out before dessert.

    Evanora didn’t speak to her for three days. The punishment came on the fourth.

    No public rebuke. No lecture. Just silence.

    Agatha’s personal maids were dismissed. Her meals were brought late and cold, if at all. No visitors. No books.

    On the fifth day, a carpenter arrived with specific instructions to board her windows.

    When she finally asked why, her mother’s steward delivered the message in a single sentence: “Her Majesty feels that isolation may improve your sense of discipline.”

    But the worst of it hadn’t come from her mother’s mouth.

    It arrived days later, as she sat alone with her ear pressed to the door, desperate to hear a voice that was not her own.

    It was a lucky night. Two court advisors had passed nearby, close enough for her to catch their murmured exchange.

    “Tragic, what happened to House Dorel isn’t it? Whole line—gone. Slaughtered by mercenaries. Bandits, they say, but…”

    “Too precise to be common raiders. Too clean.”

    “A shame. The boy was sweet, if dim.”

    “Aye, and their daughter. Such a lovely face to be wasted.”

    Agatha did not cry. She pressed her back to the cold stone and curled her fingers into her skirts until her nails bit skin. She did not speak of it. She never dared to ask.

    But she knew.

    Her ladies' maids finished fastening the last hooks of her bodice and brushed powdered shimmer over her collarbones. Agatha looked at her reflection and allowed herself a smile. Power had a price. She’d underestimated the cost, too young to know better, and others had died to settle her debt.

    Tonight, her gown was made of indigo silk, cut high at the collar but split daringly at the calf. It was impressive what the seamstress would allow in exchange for a gaudy bracelet. Agatha had no use for trinkets. Her hair had been drawn half up, the rest guided into waves over her shoulders.

    The jewels at her ears were small but sharp-edged. Tonight she wore sapphires, not diamonds.

    “Do you think I look dangerous?” she asked the girl lacing her shoes.

    The girl flushed. “Yes, Your Highness.”

    “Good.”

    She rose from the vanity and turned toward the door.

    Let the nobles of the Empire gather and whisper. Let them weigh her smile and study her silence.

    Someday, they would recognize that not all glittering things are harmless.

    Agatha waited behind the tall, gold-inlaid doors, the sound of strings swelling on the other side.

    The herald's voice rang clear above the din, “Her Royal Highness, Princess Agatha of House Harkness.”

    Ahead, a sea of candlelight and opulence. Silk banners and crystal chandeliers danced in the air like frost caught mid fall. Heads turned. Fans fluttered. A chorus of bows and curtsies followed her first step.

    She took them all in without blinking. Let them look.

    With each step, she surveyed the throng. Her eyes cast over the nobles she’d grown up with, the ones who had smiled at her and prayed for her ruin in the same breath. Alliances, betrayals, quiet resentments. Nothing she hadn’t survived before.

    But then, she saw her.

    Across the ballroom, beside the towering columns wrapped in trailing ivy and silver ribbon, stood Rio.

    And for a moment, everything else blurred over into a haze.

    It was like seeing her for the first time. A reintroduction. Not cloaked in grit and sweat, not bearing armor or anything else that would obscure her. She was… composed.

    Cleaned up, made presentable, but still rugged in a way that stood stark among the polished lacquer of the Empire.

    She wore a deep green tunic of fine weave, cut trim to her waist with rich silver embroidery curling vine-like along the hem. A wide belt sat low at her hips, her trousers tucked into dark leather boots clearly designed for movement, not aesthetic.

    Her outfit bore no sigils, no house colors She carried herself with the strength in her posture and the clarity in her gaze - earth and stone, standing proud among marble and gold.

    And when their eyes met, Agatha swore that everything else went still, as if the universe itself deemed this moment of seeing worth settling into. As her breath caught, the world seemed to draw a final gasp alongside her.

    But Rio, seemingly immune, did not falter.

    From where she stood, she dipped into a low bow, not exaggerated, not forced. Her eyes never left Agatha’s.

    And Agatha smiled.

    It was not the kind she wore for the court, but something keener.

    Together, as if pulled by the same current, they began to move—Agatha down the shallow steps of the dais, Rio cutting across the edge of the dance floor.

    Their steps matched effortlessly, meeting near the base of the grand staircase, just out of earshot of the murmuring courtiers.

    “You clean up dangerously well, Ser,” Agatha drawled. “Any better and I might feel compelled to make a scandal of it.”

    Rio's eyes flicked briefly to the nobles clustered nearby and Agatha wondered how quickly she’d sized up each threat in the room. “I doubt that would go unnoticed.”

    Agatha's smile deepened. “Let them notice. No one bothers me when I’m plotting someone’s ruin.”

    She wasn’t entirely sure whose ruin she meant.

    “You look radiant tonight, Your Highness.”

    It wasn’t spoken softly. Anyone could hear and surely there were many ears turned in their direction. A perfunctory compliment, it was not the kind that carried any weight.

    Arching a brow, she cast out a line, reaching -always reaching- for more. “Is that all?”

    “I thought it was the proper thing to say.”

    Agatha stepped a fraction closer, enough so that their shoulders might brush if either of them dared to shift. It was close enough to still be heard as she dropped her voice low. “Try once more. Haven’t you heard propriety bores me? Don’t tell me that you’re boring just as I’ve made up my mind that you aren’t.”

    Rio hesitated for a beat, and Agatha counted herself lucky to be close enough to watch her lips twitch up just before she laughed softly. She had heard that lilt before and immediately longed to hear it again - to be the cause of it.

    Meeting her eyes fully now, Rio spoke barely loud enough for Agatha to catch the words as she leaned in closer. “Princess, you wear a gown made of the night before stars were flung into it. I’m sure there are many gathered here who would cast their wishes upon you.”

    Agatha froze, finding herself speechless once more.

    Just for a moment — barely long enough for her to hate herself for allowing any modicum of surprise to show. The words settled over her skin like the kiss of something dangerous: not full roaring flame, but a smolder.

    “My knight,” she said, voice smooth but stretched thin, “you should leave such grand declarations to the bards. Or the drunk. They, at least, don’t know any better.”

    “You requested impropriety. Among this company, I imagine that the most scandalous tale is the truth,” Rio said softly.

    “Be careful with honesty,” Agatha whispered. “You might find it used against you.”

    Rio tilted her head, watching her with that same practiced calm. “I would not mind, if it were you who used it. I have no reason to be dishonest with you. If you will allow it.”

    Like a bird startled mid flight, wings flaring in self-defense, Agatha’s laugh ripped from her chest.

    “Come then,” she said, voice regaining its practiced glide. “You’ve already caused a stir. We must not allow it to turn into a riot.”

    She turned without waiting for Rio to follow. She could feel the knight just behind her, as close as her own shadow.

    If she let herself, she might’ve called it protection. But she knew better.

    It was temptation.

    For a time, they wove among the crowd. Pleasantries were exchanged. Noses and chins were turned. This continued until Rio lingered by a display case set into the wall.

    When Agatha turned to pull her forward, she noticed the shift in her guard’s expression.

    Not alarm. Not admiration. Something stern.

    She stepped to her side, arms folded. “If you tell me that’s a long-lost family heirloom, I may be forced to have the glass shattered and have it returned to you.”

    Rio’s eyes did not waver from the case. “No. Nothing so direct.”

    Agatha followed her gaze.

    Inside sat a twisted branch sealed under glass. Inlaid with dull gemstones, the whole thing was bound in golden thread, arranged carefully on a velvet cushion. A plaque beneath read: “Relic of the Wild War – Seized from enemy forces.”

    She almost rolled her eyes at the display. Of course a knight would find offense at such a thing. It was their blood that bought what was pulled from the battlefield.

    “How very distasteful, to part a warrior from their weapon.”

    Rio’s voice was quiet, the kind of quiet that came from memory. “That is no weapon.”

    Agatha tilted her head. “Isn’t it?”

    “It’s a cradle charm,” Rio murmured. “For protection. The runes—they’re wrong for a blade. They’re a simple charm meant to keep nightmares away.”

    Agatha was quiet for a long moment. “You recognize the markings?” she asked, softer now.

    Rio nodded once. “The language is old. But the meaning is clear.”

    “And how would you know that, Ser?” Agatha asked, voice still easy, but her eyes searching. Rio turned to face her at last, and Agatha had not known a face more clearly in mourning. “Because I was taught to read what the Empire has forgotten.”

    Agatha looked back at the case.

    “You know,” she said lightly, “this isn’t of the Empire. But it does represent it.”

    Rio raised an eyebrow. “A stolen charm?”

    “Kept under glass,” Agatha corrected.

    They had barely returned to the outer edge of the ballroom when Lady Verin descended, doused in perfume. Flowers bloom from rot, but they cannot cover the stench.

    “Princess, what a delightful thing you’ve brought with you.”

    Agatha didn’t flinch. “She’s not a thing, Lady Verin. But I know you’re prone to such confusion.”

    Rio, beside her, remained perfectly still.

    Verin’s gaze slithered toward her. “And where was it again you trained, Ser? Your poise is impeccable. But your vowels betray you.”

    “I make no secret of my origins,” Rio said evenly.

    “No—but word is you make a rather careful effort not to name them.”

    Agatha felt the air shift. Eyes were on them now—casual observers pretending disinterest. Her smile sharpened.

    “Lady Verin,” she said smoothly, “I’ve always admired your talent for hearing the danger in accents and never in your own voice.”

    A flicker of tension coiled in Verin’s jaw. But she recovered quickly.

    “Oh, don’t get me wrong; I quite like your knight,” she said, turning back to Agatha. “So composed. So… obedient.”

    “She’s not a hound,” Agatha replied, “though I’m told you’ve a fondness for those, too.”

    A breathless silence stretched between them.

    And then Verin dipped her head. “Enjoy your evening, Princess. Do let me know if you’d like help selecting her inevitable replacement. I’ve always had an eye for strong bloodlines.” As she vanished back into the crowd, Rio exhaled.

    Agatha turned toward her. “She’s trying to get into your head.”

    “She’s welcome to try,” Rio murmured. “But I won’t dance for her amusement.”

    Agatha’s smile returned—real this time, wicked and gleaming. “Pity. I think I’d quite like to see you dance.”

    For a heartbeat, the space between them crackled with possibility.

    Then Agatha stepped back, smoothing her hands down the front of her gown. “Come, Ser.

    We shouldn’t linger here. My mother will notice.”

    They walked side by side now, not speaking, though their shoulders brushed more than once. Agatha kept her eyes forward, but her attention drifted sideways—drawn irresistibly to the steady presence at her side.

    She turned slightly, catching Rio in profile. The candlelight caught on the silver embroidery at her collar, on the long line of her neck, on the tension she carried so carefully in her jaw. “I can tell it’s your first time. You’re not very good at court,” Agatha said softly.

    Rio blinked, brow furrowing. “Is that… meant to be a compliment?”

    Agatha shrugged. “I mean it as one. The better someone is at this—the lying, the bowing—

    the more dangerous they are. They don’t fight fairly. You’re dangerous in other ways. Honest ones.”

    That earned a slow glance from Rio. “Other ways?”

    Agatha nodded, just once. “You look like you could break someone’s ribs with one hand and hold their heart together with the other.”

    Rio’s face took on a deeper shade. That pleased Agatha more than it should have.

    “And,” she added, voice dipping just slightly lower, “I do enjoy a woman in uniform. But I have to admit, this… is far more distracting than I anticipated.”

    Rio swallowed. “Your Highness—”

    Agatha’s grin turned feline and slightly feral. “Don’t worry, Ser. I won’t make a scene.” A pause. Then, a wicked glint in her eyes: “Unless you ask nicely.”

    “I will remind you,” Rio said quietly, “it is not my place to ask things of you.”

    Agatha’s smirk lingered for a heartbeat too long as the words sank in. Her gaze dipped, then flicked upward again, sharper now. She opened her mouth to reply, to challenge the notion, to say something she might regret…

    But then the tapping of a glass echoed across the room.

    The music slowed. Conversations quieted. Heads turned.

    From the raised dais, Lord Tarren of House Velor stood with a goblet lifted high, his expression a mask of solemn celebration.

    “Honored guests,” he called, voice carrying with the ease of someone who’d practiced their charm until it hardened into control. “If I may interrupt your merriment for but a moment—” Agatha’s stomach tightened.

    “I ask you to raise your glasses,” he continued, “to Her Highness, Princess Agatha Harkness. The thorn of our Empire’s crown, and the jewel we dare not misplace.”

    The room stirred. A few awkward chuckles. The clink of glass on glass.

    Agatha held her ground, glass untouched in her hand.

    “May her wisdom guide our future. May her silence preserve our peace. And may her legacy,” he added, with a too-smooth glance toward her, “endure.”

    Agatha huffed out a sound that might’ve been a laugh, if her throat hadn’t burned with it. She pressed the rim of the glass to her lips, pantomiming a sip. The wine wasn’t for comfort, but rather for show. She could not afford the luxury of being softened.

    Agatha turned her head slightly, just enough to meet Rio’s gaze through the soft hum of resumed conversation.

    “Tell me,” she murmured, voice low and silken, “do you always stand so still when someone bleeds in public?”

    Rio’s expression barely flickered—but Agatha saw it. The inhale that caught in her chest. The flick of her eyes to Agatha’s mouth before she masked it.

    “If I moved every time someone made a scene,” Rio said, “I’d never get any rest.”

    Agatha smiled, slow and deliberate. She lifted her glass—half salute, half challenge.

    “To the ones who never rest, then.”

    This time, she drank.

    Rio didn’t move. But her fingers curled faintly at her sides, and her mouth parted—just slightly, as if about to speak.

    She said nothing.

    But Agatha felt the heat of the words that almost followed her down her throat.

    The crowd was amused, loosened by wine. Blood warmed by insult and spectacle, the scene was ripe for something far more cruel.

    The laughter faded, but their focus did not.

    Agatha felt their gazes clinging like dew to her skin—too many glances quickly averted, too many smiles worn like masks, eyes hollow. The air in the ballroom had changed, the atmosphere thinner.

    She turned, slowly, sweeping her gaze across the floor.

    No one was speaking to her now. Not directly. But the murmurs had begun.

    It was always the same sort of performance, polite conversation masking sharpened speculation.

    She stepped to the edge of the gathering, letting Rio drift half a pace behind to grant the illusion of distance.

    And that was when she saw her.

    Evanora.

    Seated high above the crowd at the edge of the dais, her posture perfect, her hands folded loosely over the jeweled pommel of her scepter. She wasn’t speaking. She wasn’t smiling.

    She was watching.

    Not the room.

    Not the nobles.

    Them.

    It was that particular stillness her mother carried like a veil—the kind of silence that always came before the gesture: a punishment, or worse, a plan.

    Her wine suddenly tasted of metal.

    She turned her head slightly. “Rio.”

    “Yes, Your Highness?” The voice came soft. Steady.

    “If my mother stands,” Agatha murmured, “take me away.”

    There was a pause. “You’ll need to give me a reason.”

    Agatha glanced back at Evanora, who was still seated, still smiling that dangerous not-smile. “I imagine,” she said, “one will present itself soon enough.”

    The music shifted.

    Subtle. Strange. A slower tempo. A minor key threading through the chords.

    At the far end of the ballroom, velvet curtains were being drawn back. A stage was being revealed.

    The curtains parted with theatrical grace, revealing a raised platform draped in silks of imperial blue and ivory. At first glance, it looked like any other entertainment offering: a display of wealth, artistry, and novelty. The musicians fell silent, the room drawing inward as if caught in a net.

    Then Agatha saw the cage.

    Its construction was delicate—gilded vines twisted like filigree, curling over a domed top. It was almost ornamental, like a birdcage, except that no bird inside had ever carried such eyes. The creature crouched low, skin slick with an unnatural sheen, a being that shimmered like moonlight made tangible. The beast was made of limbs too fluid to be called animal, too grounded to be called air. Its “face” was the only corporeal thing about it. From the narrow fox-like jaws came a low, echoing hum.

    A silver collar encircled its throat, etched in runes Agatha couldn’t immediately read. Magic, yes—but cruelly bound.

    Its eyes shimmered with the glow of a thing not meant to be looked at under such harsh light. There was something wild behind them. Ancient.

    And then it moved.

    Not toward the bars, but toward the music.

    A soft, chime-like sound began to play. Delicate and strange. The spirit rose, thin fingers flexing, and began to sway.

    A performance.

    Agatha felt her stomach begin to churn.

    A few of the nobles clapped. Some laughed lightly, as if unsure whether they were meant to be amused or amazed. Agatha heard one voice murmur something about how marvelously it had been trained.

    Beside her, Rio had gone completely still.

    Not stiff, not alert— coiled. Like a bow drawn and waiting.

    Agatha turned her head, just enough to see the tightness in Rio’s jaw, the ghost-pale ring around her knuckles.

    The creature’s movements shifted, no longer elegant, but jerking now, as if it had lost the rhythm. Or was fighting it.

    A crack of energy snapped from the collar. The creature whimpered, fell forward, and immediately rose again, back into the pattern.

    Agatha’s hand clenched her fan so tightly the lacquer cracked.

    She looked again toward the dais.

    Evanora was watching.

    Not the spirit.

    Agatha.

    And she was smiling.

    The knight’s jaw was locked, lips pressed white. Not in anger, though Agatha mused that would have been easier. This was restraint. This was pain and fury being swallowed down like a mouthful of ash. Her eyes stayed fixed on the spirit.

    Her hand drifted and her smallest finger brushed against Rio’s.

    “You don’t have to stay,” she whispered.

    Rio did not answer. But her hand shifted beneath Agatha’s touch—tilting, only slightly, letting it rest against hers.

    Agatha did not do well with fragile things. But she allowed herself to cradle this moment. And then, like a thread snapped too tight, the hall shuddered with motion.

    Someone near the west balcony dropped a platter. The silver tray spun with a clanging crash that echoed far louder than it should have. In response to such a raucous sound, a cluster of minor nobles turned sharply. One of them bumped into a pedestal, sending a delicate tower of sugared fruits toppling to the floor in a sticky mess.

    Footmen scrambled. A matron shouted for more napkins. The musicians of the string quartet hesitated a note too long.

    It was enough to break her attention and draw her gaze away from Rio. With the spell broken, she resolved not to turn back too quickly.

    A gloved hand settled lightly on her shoulder, fingers resting with the softness of silk and the weight of shackles.

    “Ser,” Evanora said, too smoothly. “You looked… moved.”

    Rio bowed. “Your Majesty.”

    “I imagine it must be strange,” Evanora went on, her tone mock-gentle. “To see such a creature tamed. There was a time it might have frightened us, but now…” She smiled without warmth. “Now it is quite manageable.”

    Agatha’s throat tightened. Her hand remained poised around her goblet, its contents mostly untouched.

    Rio straightened slowly. “With your permission,” she said, voice carefully controlled, “I’d like to step away. Just for a moment.”

    Agatha hated the phrasing, that Rio had to ask.

    But worse, she hated that she had to say yes.

    “You may,” Agatha murmured, watching her knight retreat with measured grace.

    Evanora’s smile deepened. “Obedient. That’s rare, for one of them.”

    Agatha turned, meeting her mother’s gaze squarely. “Don’t.”

    Evanora arched an eyebrow, feigning innocence. “Don’t what, darling? I was merely admiring your taste.”

    She took a sip of her wine. “I wonder if Nicholas would like such a creature. They say children find comfort in simple things. Especially those already broken.”

    Agatha’s jaw clenched. “He is not yours to use.”

    “No,” Evanora said lightly. “We all have our own uses.”

    “You must excuse me.”

    “Leaving so soon?” her mother asked, cool as winter wind. “And miss the finale?”

    Agatha smiled, all teeth. “I’ve had enough performances for one night.”

    And she followed through the door Rio had exited.

    Beyond the hedges, the gala still hummed—laughter spilling from open doors, violins clawing their way through a waltz. But here, under the hanging vines and wrought iron lanterns, the world had narrowed to the beat of the pulse in her wrist.

    She knew where Rio would be before she turned the corner.

    There, near the farthest wall, backlit by the moon’s cold shine, stood her knight. Her back was to the palace, posture rigid, hands behind her—either folded or fisted.

    Agatha slowed her steps deliberately.

    “I don’t recall granting you leave to sulk,” she said lightly.

    Rio didn’t turn. “Then I hope Your Highness will forgive the infraction.”

    Agatha paused. “You walked out.”

    “You dismissed me.”

    “You asked first,” Agatha said, stepping closer, “and that’s what I hate.”

    That earned her a glance over the shoulder. “I’m not sure I follow.”

    “You shouldn't have to ask,” Agatha replied, voice quieter now. “And I shouldn't have to give permission to someone who threw herself through my door the first time I screamed.”

    Rio turned fully now, expression unreadable in the dark. “You seemed less fond of that at the time.”

    “I was undressed and halfway to the heavens, Ser,” Agatha said, managing a dry smile.

    “Forgive me for not applauding the dramatics.”

    A pause. The garden held its breath.

    “I apologize,” Rio admitted, “for failing you.”

    That brought Agatha up short. “Why?”

    Rio didn’t look away. “Because I couldn’t—stay in there. Not after that.”

    “The spirit.”

    Rio nodded once.

    Agatha crossed the final step between them.

    “They called it entertainment,” she said carefully. “But all I saw was a monstrous echo.” Rio's brow furrowed. “Of what?”

    Agatha smiled without humor. “A child in a silk cage. Told to smile. Told to sit. Told when and how to be loved.”

    Rio looked at her then, fully. The silence between them vibrated like a bowstring drawn taut. “If I could’ve stopped it, I would have,” Rio said, low and firm. “If it had been anyone else’s court—”

    “But it wasn’t,” Agatha interrupted. “It was mine. And I stood there. Pretty and silent and smiling.”

    “You didn’t smile.”

    Agatha blinked. “You noticed that?”

    Rio nodded once. “It is my duty to notice everything about you, Princess. I see you.”

    The air between them was still.

    And then Agatha, sharp-tongued thorn, born and bred of the Empire, spoke very softly.

    “If I ran, would you follow?”

    Rio stepped closer. “If you meant it, I’d lead.”

    Agatha’s lips parted.

    Then she caught herself.

    Her hand curled around the edge of the fountain beside them. She drew a breath, tasted the confession on her tongue, then folded it back into a teasing smirk.

    “Careful,” she murmured. “A girl could fall in love with that answer.”

    Agatha caught the shift in Rio’s shoulders, the way her spine straightened, the way her fingers curled slightly as if to stop herself from reaching out.

    Good.

    Agatha unfastened the buttons at her wrist with slow, unhurried precision. The silk glove slipped free from her hand, finger by finger, until it hung limp between her fingers like a shed skin.

    She let it fall to the stone ledge with the softest whisper of sound.

    Then she stepped closer.

    She laid her bare hand on the edge of the bannister, just beside Rio’s. The brush of skin was deliberate, the heat of it unmistakable.

    Rio tensed as if struck.

    “Your hand is cold,” she said, voice quiet and low.

    Agatha’s lips curved. “So warm me up, Ser.”

    Her pinky lingered, curling just enough to trace the line where Rio’s knuckles met the curve of her thumb. It was barely a touch. But the air between them crackled like something divine. Rio’s breath came slower now. Sharper. Her gaze locked on Agatha’s hand as though it were about to strike her.

    “You shouldn’t tease,” she murmured. “Not when I’m bound to obey.”

    “Oh, but that’s why it’s fun,” Agatha whispered, stepping in just slightly closer. “You’re the blade and the vow and the oath made human and still, I can make you shiver with a single finger.”

    Rio said nothing.

    But her eyes flared, and her fingers flexed, and Agatha could feel the restraint in her body like a rope pulled taut.

    “I wonder,” Agatha continued, voice dropping to a purr, “what you’d do if I touched more than the back of your hand.”

    Rio turned her head then. Slowly. Met her eyes fully.

    And looked at her like someone who could be dangerous in court too.

    “You’d best pray I never answer that.”

    Agatha grinned, all bite and promise.

    “I prefer all my prayers start with blasphemy.”

    She slipped her glove back on with slow care that belied the thundering pulse beneath it. “Come,” she said lightly, already turning toward the archway leading to the ballroom. “I don’t want this place, or this moment, sullied by any of them.”

    They didn’t quite make it back to the ballroom.

    Just as they crossed beneath the colonnade—the open archway yawning ahead, voices swelling behind the gold-trimmed doors— something shifted in the air.

    Not a breeze. Not a sound.

    Rio turned a half-second before it happened.

    A flash of steel caught the torchlight. A whistle through the dark. A dagger, black-handled and wickedly thin, flying straight toward Agatha’s ribs.

    Rio reacted instantly.

    She slammed her shoulder into Agatha, driving her sideways. The blade embedded itself into the stone wall behind them with a sound like a bone snapping, so close Agatha could feel the tremor through her spine.

    “Down!” Rio barked, shielding her with her body as chaos bloomed.

    Shouts erupted. Metal clashed. Someone screamed. The scent of smoke and perfume twisted in the air as footsteps thundered in all directions. The dagger still trembled in the wall, embedded deep just inches from where Agatha’s heart had been.

    She couldn’t stop staring at it.

    Her breath came shallow, her mind already moving—too fast, too far.

    No wound. No pain. She was still standing.

    “Are you hurt?” Rio’s voice cut through the din. Her hands swept over Agatha’s arms, her waist—checking, anchoring. Her eyes were wild and trained entirely on her.

    Agatha shook her head, but her lips were numb.

    “No. But—”

    The thought slammed into her chest before she could finish.

    Nicky.

    Her heart turned to ice.

    “The Prince.”

    Rio’s focus sharpened instantly. “Where is he?”

    “He wasn’t supposed to be here tonight,” Agatha said, her voice cracking. “He wasn’t supposed—”

    She grabbed Rio’s arm—clutched it like it might be the only thing keeping her upright.

    Her nails bit through fabric and leather. “You have to go . Now.”

    Rio hesitated—and that hesitation broke something in her.

    Because for the first time all night, Agatha saw the strain on her knight’s face. The war between instinct and obedience. Between staying and leaving.

    Agatha’s voice dropped into something cold and commanding, a monarch’s voice.

    “That’s an order.”

    The words startled both of them, Rio flinching like she’d been struck.

    Rio’s jaw clenched. Her hand flexed, almost reaching for Agatha before curling back into a fist, visibly reluctant to leave her. It was written into every line of her body.

    “I’ll find him,” Rio said, voice hoarse. “I swear it.”

    “North wing. Third floor. Red door with a willow carving. If he’s there, bring him to the West tower. If he’s not—” She would now allow herself to finish that thought, to give name to the unimaginable. “Find him, Rio.”

    For one last moment, their eyes locked. Agatha saw the current war behind Rio’s. Jaw clenching in resolve, Rio stepped back.

    And then she ran.

    Agatha stood frozen as her knight vanished into shadow, the echo of her footfalls swallowed by the rising din.
