    Look Away, Little One Chapter Summary Blades move before breath can catch. There is recognition and a silent vow.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes The first instruction, which felt like an order, came gently.

    “Close the door behind you, Lorcán.”

    Her mentor hadn’t looked up from the bundle of drying roots in their lap. The fire was warm. The wind outside was not. Rio turned and pushed the crooked wooden door shut, using both hands to make sure it latched.

    Some orders came like parables: Don’t touch the mushrooms with the blue caps. Carry the tea kettle with both hands. Never cross the stream without telling someone where you’re going.

    Those made her feel safe, like the world had edges and someone had drawn them out for her. So long as she stepped in the right places, she wouldn’t stumble.

    Later, there came orders with more gravity to them: Stand tall. Speak plainly. Keep your eyes up and look outward. Hold your staff here. Don’t draw a blade until you must.

    Try again.

    Again.

    Again.

    When she was twelve, her mentor gave Rio her first seemingly impossible task.

    “Let it go, Rio. You can’t keep every injured thing you find.”

    The command was about a fox kit who hadn’t made it through the night, but it was also about something else - something more significant. Rio did not cry in front of them, but rather took the kit to the edge of the woods and buried it herself, whispering her thanks into the dirt. She realized then that some orders weren’t commands.

    They were attempts to soften what life would demand of you anyway.

    She followed all that she received.

    Because following orders meant she was trusted.

    Because trust felt like being seen.

    And being seen felt like love.

    When she’d been called to the gathering house, summoned by prayers that carried the weight of her people’s hopes, she obeyed.

    Because before she accepted her orders, she had sworn an oath.

    To the Wilds. To the sacred.

    To balance, and the breath of the old gods still lingering in the soil.

    And for a time, that oath embedded itself in her breath and bones and lived naturally in her veins. Easy as breathing.

    Until along came a princess, stowed in a tower, tangled in the roots of a rotten empire.

    A princess who was not what she’d expected.

    Who teased and snarked and laughed with her teeth exposed. Who did not suffer fools and found delight in making them stammer.

    The woman was meant to hold a kingdom in her palm someday, but for now carried a specific heartache that could only be recognized by one who also held that pulsing wound outside of their chest.

    A princess who gave orders not like a commander, but like someone testing how far someone else would bend before they snapped.

    And now—

    Now she had said it.

    “That’s an order.”

    And Rio, so used to hearing those words from everyone else, flinched like it was a curse. Because for the first time in her life, the order didn’t feel like direction. It felt like betrayal. Perhaps Agatha hadn’t meant to say it like that. Not with that voice. Not with that look. But she had.

    And Rio had obeyed.

    Not because of the chain of command. Not because of the politics. Not even because of her oath. But because it was her.

    When Agatha told her to go, Rio went.

    And she didn’t know if she’d ever forgive herself for it.

    Rio ran.

    Not in the controlled, measured way she’d been trained to move through places that demanded quiet. Places like palaces.

    No. This was Wilds fast, like wind whipping through reeds and with all the force of the recirculating current beneath a waterfall. Each step echoed off the marble floor; every breath seemed to be a gasping roar. The scent of candle wax clung to the halls, growing more acrid with each turn.

    Third floor. Red door. Willow carving.

    She repeated it like a sacred chant in her head.

    Nobles had begun locking themselves behind gilded doors, guards clustered to protect the main halls, but no one else seemed to be running toward the true danger.

    They were not trained as she had been.

    She passed a handmaid weeping behind a column and ignored her. She rounded a corner; slid.

    Red door.

    There it was, at the end of a carpeted hallway.

    She sprinted harder until she was twisting at a knob that wouldn’t turn.

    The door didn’t budge, locked from the inside.

    She pressed her ear to the wood, hearing a soft whimper. Someone inside was trying to stay quiet, but failing to do so.

    Her heart sank. There was no time for pleasantries.

    She braced her shoulder and slammed into the wood.

    Once. Twice.

    On the third strike, it cracked open with a shriek of splinters.

    The prince was crouched behind the desk, a pale nursemaid clutching him to her chest. The moment she saw Rio, the woman screamed.

    “Stay back! I won’t let you hurt him—”

    “I’m here on the princess' orders,” Rio said sharply. “We don’t have time for panic. We have to move.”

    The young prince, Nicholas, peeked over the edge of the desk. His cheeks were tear-streaked, but dry now.

    Rio crouched down to his level and offered her hand. “Will you come with me? There’s a place we can go where no one will get close.”

    Nicholas looked at her hand, then back up to her eyes.

    “Did she send you? Or are you just trying to be brave?”

    Rio’s throat tightened. “Both.”

    He stared at her. “Why?”

    “Because she trusts me to keep you safe.”

    A beat. His lip wobbled, but he lifted his chin, brave, and Rio swore to every old god of the Wilds that he looked exactly like the princess when he narrowed his eyes at her.

    “Okay,” he whispered.

    She helped him to his feet and straightened his shirt. Rio ran her hand over his curls once— an instinctive gesture she barely registered.

    “You have been very brave,” she murmured. “But now I need you to do something harder. I need you to stay quiet. Stay close to me, and if I say run, you run. So fast - quick like a bunny.”

    The boy nodded.

    Rio looked at the nursemaid. “You too. Stay behind me.”

    They slipped back into the corridor, Rio reaching back to gently squeeze Nicholas’s hand. “Okay, time to show me how fast you are. Now!”

    And then they ran.

    They moved like shadows through the lesser halls—Rio leading, Nicholas tethered, holding hands between her and the nursemaid. In another place, the shadows they cast up on the walls could be three figures at play.

    Now it was just another risk, a liability.

    The stone beneath their feet was too loud. Everything was too loud.

    Halfway through a narrow passage between the council chambers and the old chapel wing, Rio’s steps faltered.

    Voices.

    Too low to be servants. Too measured to be guards.

    She pressed back suddenly, hand lifting in warning. “Wait.”

    The nursemaid halted. Nicholas jerked to a stop between the two of them, startled but silent. Rio dropped into a crouch and motioned for them to stay hidden beside a cluster of decorative armor stands. She crept forward, just enough to catch the sliver of light spilling from the adjacent hall.

    Three figures. Unfamiliar armor. Curved blades, not standard issue.

    Assassins? Infiltrators? Either way, unwelcome.

    Her jaw tightened. She could feel Nicholas’s eyes on her from behind—wide and unblinking, watching her every move.

    Rio turned her head slightly, catching sight of the boy over her shoulder. He wasn’t just a charge. Not just a child to be delivered from one hall to another.

    He was her child.

    There was no mistaking it—the sharp cleverness in his gaze, the slight uptilt of his mouth when he tried to hide how scared he was.

    He didn’t know. He couldn’t. Not yet.

    But Rio had realized the moment she saw him.

    Too small. Too soft. Still unblooded by the world.

    He couldn’t see what was coming. He couldn’t.

    Not this.

    She would not let his first memory of danger be a massacre. Not if she could help it.

    She turned back to him, crouching low.

    “Little brave one,” she said softly. “I need you to do something very important.”

    Nicholas blinked. “What?”

    She pulled a coin from her belt—a simple token, bronze and worn smooth. “Do you see the figure on this?”

    He peered closer to see a tiny fox etched in a leaping pose.

    “It’s a lucky charm,” Rio said. “Hold it for me. Keep it safe. Close your eyes and count to one hundred. Whisper quietly. I’ll be right back before you reach it.”

    Nicholas hesitated. “You promise?”

    Rio nodded. “On my honor.”

    He took the coin in both hands like it was sacred and sat against the wall, eyes squeezed shut. The nursemaid knelt beside him, clutching his shoulder.

    Rio leaned in close to her ear. “If you scream, it’s his blood. And yours. Do you understand?” The woman nodded.

    Rio unsheathed her ceremonial blade.

    It wasn’t built for killing. But she didn’t need it to be. Dull blades were often more dangerous than sharp ones.

    She slipped into the corridor, clinging to the edge of the wall.

    The first man never heard her. She drove the hilt into the soft of his temple and dragged him down in silence. The second turned in time to raise his weapon, but she was already inside his reach—knee, elbow, twist. The dagger slashed once, shallow, but against a primary artery, it was enough.

    The third tried to run. He didn’t make it.

    The bodies were left where they fell. She didn’t have time to move them, and was too focused on the next stretch to care.

    Blood darkened her sleeve.

    Rio wiped her hands once on the edge of her tunic and turned back to where the boy waited, still counting under his breath.

    “...seventy-three, seventy-four…”

    She knelt and touched his shoulder. “Told you I’d be back.”

    Nicholas’s eyes flew open. “I didn’t reach one hundred.”

    She smiled. “I’m fast too.”

    “Are we going to race again?”

    Rio leaned in, her voice low and conspiratorial. “No more racing. New game.”

    Nicholas tilted his head. “What kind of game?”

    “A hiding game,” she said, lifting him gently into her arms. “But just with your face.”

    He blinked at her, curious but trusting.

    “You hide it right here,” she murmured, guiding him to tuck his face into the crook of her neck. “Like you’re the world’s smallest spy, and you don’t want anyone to know where you are.”

    Nicholas giggled, but then grew serious, nestling closer. “Okay. How long?”

    “I will tell you,” Rio said, tightening her grip around him. “No peeking. Not even a little. If you don’t peek and are a good spy, I’ll give you a prize.”

    He nodded solemnly, arms wrapping around her shoulders.

    She stood again, eyes sweeping the hall. The nursemaid followed in a daze, one hand to her mouth, the other holding her skirts off the floor slick with blood.

    Rio stepped over the bodies without a word.

    Nicholas stayed quiet.

    His breath warmed her neck, steady and slow.

    And though she told herself it was to protect him, she knew it was also for herself.

    Because the only thing worse than dealing with the dead was seeing the way a child might look at them.

    They ran again. The final stretch was the worst—past a shattered alcove, through the long hall beneath the war gallery. She knew every blind corner, recognized every shadow.

    When they reached the final spiral leading to the West Tower, Rio paused and set the boy down.

    “Very good job,” she told him. “Just one last challenge. You’ve been brave, fast, and cunning. And now you’ll lead us the rest of the way up. Are you ready?”

    “Is my mother there?”

    Rio paused for a long moment, not sure how to answer. “She will be soon.”

    He nodded and took the nursemaid’s hand once more.

    Rio waited until they were safely ahead before glancing down at the blood on her sleeve. The fabric was dark enough that most wouldn’t notice. But Agatha would.

    She took a breath, wiped her blade clean with a scrap of torn tapestry, and followed them up. They reached the final landing in a blur of footsteps and half-held breaths. She ushered them inside in a controlled rush, her hand pressed gently to Nicholas’s back as he stepped into the familiar warmth of the princess’ chambers.

    Agatha was already there.

    She was still in her gown, though the beading had come undone near one shoulder. She turned sharply at the noise, her eyes wild—but the moment she saw them, saw him, she visibly relaxed.

    “Nicky.”

    He ran to her without hesitation.

    Rio moved with intent, circling the perimeter of the room. Her eyes scanned for shadows, her fingers still curled around the dagger’s handle tucked at her back. She threw open each wardrobe door, pulling each curtain aside.

    She didn’t care how safe the room seemed to be. Her blood hadn’t yet cooled enough to trust stillness.

    She searched under the chaise and behind the dressing screen.

    She checked the windows and the latch on the balcony.

    Behind her, the nursemaid sagged into the nearest chair and buried her face in her hands, crying softly.

    But Rio couldn’t stop moving.

    She had to be sure.

    Only when she’d finished her sweep and confirmed—twice—that they were alone did she finally turn.

    Agatha sat on the edge of her bed now, cradling Nicholas in her lap. One arm wrapped tightly around him, the other stroking through his hair.

    His cheek pressed against her collarbone, mumbling something about a knight with fast feet. Rio exhaled and leaned a hand against the doorway, allowing herself one long, grounding breath when she heard it: “Is she… your knight?” Nicholas whispered in a small, shy voice.

    Agatha’s voice was a murmur. “She is a knight. A very good one.”

    It felt like intruding to acknowledge the moment, so she busied herself with pulling the drapes back closed.

    “Does she have to go when the danger’s gone?”

    A pause. Then Agatha’s hand smoothed through his curls. “She will be here as long as we need her to be.”

    The bells rang out above them—three chimes, sharp and steady.

    The all-clear.

    The threat had passed.

    Rio exhaled slowly. She didn’t realize she’d been holding her breath until her shoulders finally eased.

    Behind her, the nursemaid wiped her face and stood shakily. She looked once more at the boy and his guardian. Satisfied by something she saw, she gave a shallow curtsy, and slipped from the chamber with soft words about informing the court that both the prince and princess were safe.

    The door closed gently behind her leaving the three of them alone.

    Agatha didn’t move at first. She only watched the flicker of the fire, her fingers still curled protectively over Nicholas’ back as he whispered something else against her ear.

    Then she spoke, calm but deliberate.

    “Would you like to ask her yourself? It’s alright.”

    Nicholas looked up, eyes wide and uncertain. “I can?”

    “Yes,” she said. “You’re allowed to be curious. And she is very kind, even if she looks like she could be scary.”

    Rio turned at that, finally stepping away from the window.

    She approached the bed slowly. When she reached them, she dropped to one knee.

    Not because of protocol, nor because of rank.

    But because she recognized the gravity of this moment. Agatha was holding the most important thing in the world in her arms—and Rio needed to show her that she saw it.

    Her gaze locked with Agatha’s for a moment, heavy with heat and clarity and something else: a vow not spoken aloud.

    Then her eyes lowered to the boy.

    Nicholas blinked at her, his small fingers twisting the edge of Agatha’s sleeve.

    “Do you have a sword?”

    “I do,” Rio said, her voice gentler than she knew it could be. “It’s very sharp.”

    “Did you stop the bad people?”

    “I did.”

    “Did you… hurt them?”

    She hesitated and glanced at Agatha, who gave a small nod of permission. “Only the ones who wanted to hurt others.”

    He thought about that, then nodded solemnly.

    “It’s not nice to hurt others.”

    “No. It is not,” Rio answered.

    “You were very brave tonight,” she said, directing it to Nicholas but letting the words echo toward Agatha. “And you earned your prize.”

    Nicholas tilted his head.

    “Do you remember the coin I gave you?” she asked. “Before we started our hiding game?”

    He nodded quickly and opened his tiny fist to reveal it. “I didn’t drop it.”

    “I’m glad,” Rio said with a smile. “You did exactly as I asked. You were a perfect spy.”

    Then she looked to Agatha. “If her Highness says it’s okay, you may keep it.”

    Agatha looked at the coin in his hand, then at Rio.

    “A token from a knight is a special gift, Nicky,” she said gently, eyes locked on Rio’s. “You may keep it, but we’ll store it with your special things here as a secret for the three of us.” There was a long silence after that—until the boy turned his head towards Rio, asking very quietly, “Will you stay here?”

    And though the question was addressed to her, it was Agatha’s gaze Rio met again.

    Her voice was steady.

    “If your mother asks me to,” she said, “I’ll never leave.”

    Nicholas blinked, the meaning lost on him.

    Agatha did not attempt to correct her, instead curling her hand tighter around his waist and pressing a kiss to the top of his head.

    Rio remained on her knees, the stone cool beneath her, her palms resting loosely on her thighs. In silence, she. watched as Nicholas shifted in Agatha’s lap, a small bundle of mussed curls safe for now in the crook of arms that were built to hold him.

    “I want to be a knight too,” Nicky said suddenly.

    Agatha’s brows lifted, just a fraction, but she didn’t smile. She glanced down at her son, brushing his hair back from his forehead. “Do you?”

    He nodded against her shoulder, small but certain. “I want to run fast and fight bad people!” Rio’s throat tightened.

    There it was, that simple, impossible purity: a child’s desire to be brave, to protect without awareness of the cost.

    She tried to smile, but failed.

    “There are other ways to be brave,” she said quietly.

    The boy pouted and wrinkled his nose. “But I want a sword.”

    Rio huffed softly, trying to clear the ache from her chest. “Maybe someday,” she said. “You’d have to train. Listen very well. Learn how not to use it.”

    “I could!” He insisted.

    “I don’t doubt it,” Rio said gently. “But there are better paths for someone like you, little prince.”

    Agatha interrupted then, “He should be allowed to be whatever he wants.”

    The way she said it wasn’t casual. It was fierce and fragile all at once.

    Nicholas looked between them. “Can I see your sword sometime?”

    Rio hesitated, then gave a short nod. “Maybe. When you’re taller.”

    He grinned, triumphant, curling closer into Agatha’s side. “I think you’re the best knight. The others don’t play with me like you did.”

    Agatha opened her mouth, lips parting -

    Wood slammed against stone with an echo sharp enough to startle the three of them as the door burst open.

    And there she was.

    Evanora, cloaked in shadow and silver thread, swept into the room with two of her personal guards trailing close behind. She didn’t so much as flinch at the intimate tableau before her— the prince curled in Agatha’s lap, the knight kneeling at their feet. She didn’t seem to acknowledge the tension, thick and warm as the blood spilled in the hallway.

    Her smile was thin. Surgical.

    “There you are,” she said, as if retrieving a misplaced object. “I’ve come to collect the prince.”

    Agatha stiffened, instinctively pulling him closer against her breast.

    Rio rose slowly to her feet, the motion smooth and deliberate, placing herself between the queen and the bed. “Your Majesty, we’ve only just arrived. Perhaps a moment would be allowed for them to catch their breath.”

    Evanora’s eyes flicked to her. “Step aside, Ser. The boy has had quite the fright. He needs his mother now.”

    Nicholas’s small voice cut through the tension.

    “But…” he whispered, his fingers curling into Agatha’s gown. “I want to stay with her. She kept me safe.”

    The room went still.

    Evanora’s smile faltered. Not gone, no—just thinned, tapered into something gleaming with practiced edge.

    For a heartbeat, it almost seemed she might indulge him. Her gaze flicked to the boy, then to Agatha, and back again. Her expression smoothed into something gentler.

    “Of course,” she said, voice light and melodic. “Of course, he wants to stay. It’s been such an exciting night.”

    Nicholas nodded solemnly, not understanding the shift.

    Evanora stepped closer to the bed, reaching to smooth a wrinkle from the coverlet.

    “You may keep him. Just for tonight.” Her honeyed tone turned cool, laced with something only the adults would catch.

    “Though I suppose we’ll need to cancel next week’s visit,” she added absently. “A pity. But surely we all understand—too much of a good thing spoils the appetite.”

    Agatha stiffened. Her hand tightened over Nicholas’ shoulder, but she didn’t protest.

    She couldn’t, Rio realized, not without giving everything away.

    Evanora’s attention shifted, the way a hawk might glance at a smaller bird caught mid-flight. “Ser,” she said, turning to Rio. “I’m told you abandoned your post during the attack. I’m sure you understand how… troubling that is.”

    Rio didn’t flinch, but her jaw grew tight enough to hurt.

    “With respect, Your Majesty, I was retrieving the prince and ensuring his safety.”

    “Ah. An admirable excuse,” Evanora murmured. “But I believe you were assigned to the princess.”

    A beat passed.

    “I was following her direct order,” Rio replied evenly.

    Evanora arched a brow. “Were you? And tell me, Ser… how many of your explicit orders do you deem flexible? You serve at the pleasure of the crown, not at the whims of my daughter.” There was no good answer for that.

    Agatha broke the silence for her, “Mother, you will forgive her flexibility, I hope. Given that she’s the reason your bloodline still breathes.”

    Evanora turned and, for the first time that evening, her smile cracked—only slightly.

    Agatha held her son a moment longer, long enough to press a kiss to his crown that lingered a fraction too long. She exhaled slowly, like something deflating inside her.

    “…It’s best you go,” she murmured. “Your nursemaid will worry. And I’ll see you again soon.”

    Nicholas turned in her lap, reluctant. “But I didn’t ask her yet!”

    He turned to Rio, eyes wide with earnest curiosity. “What should I call you?”

    Rio opened her mouth to answer, gentle and already half-smiling—

    But Evanora’s voice sliced in first.

    “There’s no need for titles,” she said smoothly, already stepping forward. “The knight is here to protect your sister, nothing more. You have no business with her.”

    She placed a hand on his shoulder and nudged him closer to her side. “Come along now, darling. We’ll talk about proper etiquette on the way.”

    Nicholas glanced back only once.

    And then the door closed behind them with a hush that felt too final.

    Agatha sat frozen, arms still curled as if her son remained in them.

    The air in the room seemed thinner now, like whatever warmth that had existed there had left with the boy.

    Rio could only watch the silence settle.

    She stood for a long moment, blood pulsing in her ears, limbs heavy with the ghost of motion. There was no enemy left to fight, no hallway left to sweep, and no child to usher into safety. Only this room—this stillness—and the woman sitting in the center of it, unraveled and folding herself around the ache of his sudden absence.

    She took a breath. Then another.

    Slowly, carefully, Rio crossed the room. Neither of them spoke.

    Rio lowered herself to one knee in front of where Agatha sat, again, not as a gesture of deference but rather one of proximity, of grounding.

    She rested one hand lightly on the edge of the bed, close enough that Agatha could pull away if she wanted to do so.

    Agatha did not.

    Rio didn’t know what she was supposed to say. Truthfully, she doubted any words she could muster would be welcome just yet.

    Her mind spun. She thought about her training: the long lectures on composure, on protocol. About how a knight must always be ready to follow the next command.

    But how none of that meant anything now.

    Even poachers, she thought, didn’t separate a mother from her young.

    She stayed silent, watching Agatha in profile.

    Agatha did not speak.

    "I can leave, if you’d rather—"

    Agatha did not look at her, but she also did not move away.

    "Stay," she said, the word half a breath.

    So Rio stayed.

    When Agatha leaned forward, Rio thought it was to stand. She rose slightly to help—reflexes ahead of reason—just as Agatha noticed the blood.

    "It’s not mine, or his," Rio said quickly.

    "I know," Agatha replied. Her voice was too calm. "If it were yours, you wouldn’t be kneeling. And if it were his, you would know better than to face me."

    She reached forward and, rather than pull away, tipped forward. As she did, her forehead brushed Rio’s.

    Rio’s hand moved on instinct again. It hovered, then settled lightly at Agatha’s hip. Not pulling, just resting, anchoring.

    Their breaths were nearly in sync now.

    Agatha didn’t pull back. Neither did Rio.

    But just before the moment could tip, before gravity could draw them the final inch, Agatha exhaled a shaky breath and turned her face slightly. She rested her brow against Rio’s temple instead.

    It wasn’t a kiss.

    But it was closer than anything had ever been, closer than they ever should be.

    And Rio, jaw tight and hands steady, closed her eyes. And let it break her open.

    Neither moved for a long time.

    Agatha’s breath, steady now, brushed warm against Rio’s cheek. Her voice, when it came again, was barely a whisper.

    “Tell me something about you that is real.”

    Rio’s brow worked into a furrow as she remembered her place. “This is not—”

    “Not proper?” Agatha interrupted, pulling back just enough to look at her. “Nothing about this is proper. I am barefoot and aching. You are bleeding and kneeling. Spare me the lesson in etiquette. You can visit my mother for that. Tell me, what is real?”

    Rio’s throat worked around an answer she wasn’t sure she should give. But the way Agatha looked at her seemed to compel a truth.

    “There’s a field near the edge of the Wilds covered in marigold yellow grass. Every spring, it catches fire from the lightning storms and grows back twice as tall. I buried something there once.”

    Agatha tilted her head. “What?”

    Rio shook her head. “Another time, perhaps. For now, that story is just mine.”

    Agatha didn’t press.

    Instead, she said, “Then I hope it stays standing. Whatever it is.”

    Rio blinked, surprised by the sentiment, which loosened something in her chest. Before she could think to stop herself, she said, “It will never fall. It is a sacred place.”

    The silence after that was different. Not strained, not expectant. Just still.

    And then Agatha, her voice barely above breath, whispered, “Ask me now for something real, Ser.”

    Rio looked up, startled. “Your Highness, I do not—”

    “Rio.” The name left Agatha’s lips like a secret coaxed from hiding. “Please.”

    A single word. And yet Rio knew it carried the weight of every time she hadn’t been asked. Every time something had been taken. Every time she’d had to steel herself because softness was not afforded to her.

    A breath passed. And she allowed herself two questions, even though Agatha had only requested one.

    “When you look at me… is it real? Or is it just something to pass the time?”

    Agatha blinked, visibly taken aback. “What?”

    “You’ve crafted your own language, one that I have not studied,” Rio said, voice steady. “But I need to know if I am your entertainment. A distraction for a clever girl who knows how to twist minds and hearts to her liking.”

    Agatha stared at her. The glint of mischief that usually curved her mouth was absent. This was something else, bare and unscripted.

    She didn’t laugh or deflect.

    Instead, she reached out, brushing her thumb along the curve of Rio’s jaw. And her voice, when it came, was raw.

    “You see too much,” Agatha whispered. “But still, you look at me like I’m something soft. I don’t know how to be tender and survive this place.”

    Rio didn’t pull away. “I was taught never to look away.”

    Agatha’s hand slid up, fingers ghosting along the curve of Rio’s jaw. Her thumb brushed just below her lip, and for a moment, Rio thought—hoped—she might close the space between them.

    But then Agatha stopped.

    She pulled back half an inch, eyes searching, sharp with something like fear.

    “If I kiss you,” she said quietly, “I’ll stop pretending I don’t care what happens to you.” Rio’s breath caught.

    “And if you don’t kiss me?” she asked.

    “I’ll still care,” Agatha said. “But I’ll keep lying about it.”

    Neither of them moved.

    Rio swallowed. “Then lie, if you need to. But don’t ask me to believe it.”

    Agatha exhaled, shaky. Her forehead lowered again to rest against Rio’s. One hand tangled gently in the fabric near her shoulder—not to hold her closer, but to keep herself from falling further apart.

    And Rio allowed her this touch.

    Chapter End Notes Up next, Agatha in the aftermath. An investigation. A journey begins.

    Temptation Rides Ahead Chapter Summary A moment alone, then a journey without a chaperone.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes Agatha had made a life of dressing up truths until they glittered brightly enough that no one would realize how they cut. She could spin cruelty into silk, folding even the most damning sentiment into a sly smile, and had survived by knowing both when to strike and when to hide her fangs.

    But this intimacy wasn’t something she knew how to handle.

    She could feel the truth, raw and trembling, lodged just beneath her ribs. It didn’t want to be hidden, screaming to be named.

    “I’m tired,” she whispered.

    Rio shifted slightly, not pulling away, just adjusting.

    “I know,” came the soft reply.

    “No, you don’t,” Agatha said, too fast, too harsh. She tried to make herself soft. “This is not the kind of tiredness that sleep fixes.”

    Rio didn’t respond. She didn’t need to.

    Agatha felt the weight of her hand settle lightly over her own. No grip. No command. Just presence.

    Steady, and impossible to ignore.

    “I won’t ask you to promise me anything. I will not order you to stay,” Agatha said. “I just needed one moment in which I did not have to be careful.”

    Rio’s fingers brushed over hers once, then stilled again.

    And Agatha—finally, slowly—closed her eyes.

    The kiss didn’t come.

    But something deeper did: a fragile peace, the kind that only exists between people who know how close they are to destruction, but choose gentleness anyway.

    Agatha’s breath had just begun to steady when she felt Rio shift. Not away, not quite. But something in her posture braced again.

    “I should check the halls,” Rio murmured. Her voice was low, close, still exceedingly gentle. “Make sure nothing else has slipped past the chaos.”

    Agatha didn’t answer right away. Her hand curled tighter into the fabric at Rio’s shoulder, just for a moment. Then she nodded, barely.

    Rio lingered there for a heartbeat longer before standing.

    Agatha didn’t watch her go.

    But she listened for every step.

    She didn’t move for a long while after the door had shut behind Rio. The click of it echoed like the end of something, though she wasn’t sure what.

    The silence crept in again - not oppressive this time, but simply present, real, and impossible to ignore.

    Agatha was alone again.

    Letting her fingers drift down to her lap, Agatha contemplated Nicholas’ presence only moments before. The warmth of him still lingered faintly in the fabric of her skirts, like sunlight caught in wool. Her hand brushed against something small.

    The coin.

    She turned it over in her palm, letting her thumb run along the etched curve of the fox’s face: clever, sharp, watching - a little like its owner.

    Of course Rio had known.

    She hadn’t said anything; hadn’t flinched, hadn’t asked. But the moment Nicky had curled into her side, there had been a softness in Rio’s gaze that told Agatha she’d seen all she needed to.

    It hadn’t surprised her, not really.

    Agatha flipped the coin over. The other side bore a creature she didn’t recognize—long-

    limbed and antlered, elegant and eerie. Some forest god, maybe, or some half-remembered legend from a land she’d never been permitted to know.

    Agatha flicked the coin into the air.

    It spun, one flash of light, then another, and she caught it easily.

    The fox looked back at her.

    She narrowed her eyes and flipped it again.

    Fox.

    A small laugh escaped her.

    She spun it once more, this time letting it whirl on the smooth surface of her bedside table. It spun fast, clattering gently until it slowed, tipped, and fell.

    Fox.

    “Of course,” she murmured.

    A trick coin.

    Not weighted, not obvious. Just enough of a cheat to make its own luck, just like her knight. It would not do for her son to carry a token bearing an image from The Wilds in his pocket, as it would raise too many questions. Even if Agatha could do nothing else, she could protect him from scrutiny.

    It was a gift for a child to get to stay a child.

    Agatha stared down at the coin a moment longer. Then, with careful fingers, she reached for the drawer at her bedside and slid it open. She nudged aside a half-burned taper candle, a broken brooch, and a few folded letters stored for safekeeping.

    The coin slipped in, and she closed the drawer.

    Then she stood, her hands moving automatically to the buttons at the back of her gown. The silence returned—not heavy now, just vast. It was the kind that echoed in the hollows after something meaningful had nearly been given name.

    She stepped out of the gown, folding it without thought, and reached for the sleeping robe laid out on the nearby chair. Her fingers caught in the sleeve, and for a brief moment, the fabric tangled. Drained as she was, Agatha found herself stilling rather than feeling her ire rise.

    Then she let the robe fall to the floor.

    She sat on the edge of the bed in her shift, hands braced on either side of her thighs. She didn’t move. Not for a long time.

    The events of the day played behind her eyes in fragments—the glint of the dagger, the way Rio’s body had collided with hers, the crack of the blade burying in stone. Nicky’s voice. Evanora’s hand curling around his shoulder. The hum of the spirit’s forced dance, the ring of its collar sounding like the scream she hadn’t allowed herself to free.

    And Rio.

    Kneeling, but not submissive. Just… present. There.

    Agatha touched her brow, still faintly warm from where Rio’s had pressed to it.

    She curled into the bed at last, dragging the heavy blanket over her legs.

    Sleep did not come quickly.

    It paced along the edges of her thoughts like a cautious animal, waiting until her breathing slowed, until her grip on her own restraint loosened.

    And then, at last, it found her.

    Agatha drifted off with one arm stretched across the empty space beside her, palm faintly open as if, in sleep, she might still be waiting for someone to take it.

    Agatha found herself blinded - not by the sun itself, but by its light glinting off the polished steel in front of her, sharp and dazzling.

    Rio stood with her back turned, fully armored. Gleaming plates curved over her form, each etched with intricate scrollwork of vines, thorns, and unfamiliar blooms. In one hand, she held her falchion, its blade slick with blood, steaming into the cool air.

    Though her stance was wide and steady, her body lurched with each ragged breath, as if she’d come from battle.

    Agatha took a half step forward, but Rio’s voice stopped her, firm and unwavering. It was still her voice, but tempered with command that Agatha had no choice but to obey.

    “Wait.”

    And she did. Not out of fear. Not even out of loyalty.

    But because there was no softness in that word - only steel, only need - spoken by someone who could not afford to ask twice.

    Rio wiped the blade clean on a strip of linen she pulled from her belt. It was clearly an efficient, practiced ritual.

    She sheathed the blade before finally turning.

    The blood was clearly not hers, yet she wore it like it belonged on her. Dark splotches splattered across the silver of her pauldron, streaked down her greaves, drying in dark arcs across her collar. Her eyes found Agatha’s through the helm for a moment.

    Then the helmet came off.

    The look she gave her was the kind that should’ve required a vow to witness, heat and hunger and possession barely contained.

    “You’re safe now,” she said.

    Agatha took one step forward, then another. “You came for me.”

    Rio’s gauntlet dropped to the grass with a heavy thud. It was followed by the other, her hands now bare, blood visible on her knuckles.

    “I’d come for you a thousand times,” she said.

    Again, Agatha reached for her, but this time Rio met her first.

    She gripped Agatha by the hips and pulled her forward, armor clinking, plates shifting, and kissed her like the great war had never ended.

    Like she needed to claim her before another enemy rose.

    Agatha didn’t resist, instead leaning in, fingers curling around the edges of Rio’s pauldrons. The kiss deepened - metal against silk, fierce and consuming.

    Rio's breath was rough in her ear. “Say my name, your Highness.”

    Agatha did. Between kisses, between sighs, like she meant to keep it on her tongue forever. And when Rio lowered her to the grass it wasn’t gentle.

    It was reverent.

    It was ruin.

    Not a single buckle had been loosened, not a single piece of steel removed, and still it felt like she was being laid bare under that gaze.

    Rio braced herself on either side of her, knuckles leaving faint red smears on the silk at Agatha’s hips. The scent of iron clung to her, not unpleasant, but real. Raw. A reminder of what she had just done. For her.

    "You mustn’t look at me like that," Agatha whispered, voice trembling despite herself.

    "Like what?"

    "Like you already know how this ends."

    Rio leaned down, brushing her lips once, then twice. She traced over Agatha's jaw, just shy of her lips. "But I do. There isn’t a world in which this ends any other way."

    The words cut deeper than the almost-kiss. Without thinking, Agatha arched into her, fingers tightening around the edges of the breastplate, as if sheer will could draw her closer through the steel.

    Rio's mouth found hers again, slower now.

    The way she held Agatha wouldn’t be called gentle, but it was careful, precise as if every move she made had already been rehearsed in her mind a thousand times.

    And Agatha let herself be kissed until she forgot every lie she’d ever told to keep herself untouchable.

    She didn’t want softness. She wanted to be carved into.

    Rio pressed her palm to the curve of her hip. “Say it.”

    Agatha shuddered. “Say what?”

    “That I can have you.”

    Agatha’s fingers slid up into the collar of her armor, dragging her closer. “You’re damning me.”

    Rio’s eyes searched hers, dark and burning.

    “I can’t damn what’s sacred,” she whispered. “You’re not my ruin, Agatha. You’re my oath.” Again, she kissed her.

    “If this is heresy,” Rio murmured, voice rough now, reverent, “then let me be the first to fall for it. I’d march a thousand miles in penance, burn temples to ash, and build new ones from the wreckage, all to kneel at your altar.”

    Agatha’s head tilted back as Rio kissed the hollow of her throat.

    “I’d crusade for your whims,” she continued. “And offer prayers to the curve of your mouth.” Her hands cupped Agatha’s face, thumbs brushing the corners of her lips.

    Agatha’s voice trembled like an invocation. “Worship me then.”

    And in that sun-drenched field, Rio obeyed.

    For a moment, she didn’t remember where she was when she woke. Her body ached in places that dreamt too vividly, and her skin was damp with sweat.

    A square of early light painted her bedsheets gold, mocking the color of the dream.

    Agatha blinked, swallowing and lifted a hand to her lips.

    The warmth was already fading.

    She curled her knees up to her chest, burying her face in the crook of her arm.

    Her body still felt foreign, like it belonged to someone else - someone who could be held like that, kissed like that.

    Agatha ran her fingers down her side, invoking the sensation of Rio’s hands holding her.

    She half expected to find blood.

    But there was nothing beyond the pounding of her heart.

    Suddenly, a swift knock at the door shattered the quiet.

    Too sharp. Too early.

    She pulled on a dressing robe, the silk brushing against her skin like a reminder that she was still here, in this place, this cage, this palace. The door creaked open without her bidding. It was one of her mother’s attendants, all neat posture and blank expression.

    “Her Majesty requests your presence. Immediately.”

    The sun had not yet cleared the terrace windows, but Evanora already had a goblet of wine in hand. She was dressed in pale blue and silver, soft tones that made her look gentler than Agatha had ever known her to be.

    “You look flushed,” she said, without looking up. “Bad dreams?”

    Agatha opted not to answer, instead standing and waiting, still barefoot.

    Evanora finally glanced up from her desk. “Sit. There’s much to discuss.”

    Agatha remained standing, the cold from the stone seeping into her calves.

    A thin smile pulled at the queen’s lips. “Very well. This won’t be long.”

    She folded a piece of parchment and set it aside.

    “There’s been rumblings of unrest in the border provinces,” she began, tone light as a garden breeze. “Nothing dramatic. But enough to require… a symbol. A reassurance. A reminder of the Empire’s grace.”

    Agatha said nothing.

    “I’ve decided to send you.”

    Evanora turned to face her more fully, wine glass in hand. “You’ll leave today. You will be well dressed, charming, and dutiful: everything they expect of a princess who seems to have captured the people’s sympathy.”

    Agatha’s voice came out flat. “And what am I to say to them?”

    Evanora’s smile returned, razor-thin. “That this peace is a gift, one which they must not squander.”

    There was a long pause. Agatha’s fingers tightened into fists, bunching the fabric of her robe. “This is not just about diplomacy,” she observed.

    “No,” Evanora agreed. “It’s about loyalty - and visibility. You’ve been quite visible lately, have you not? One could say preening. Do not tell me you want to shy away from attention now.”

    Evanora stood and stepped closer, her presence bringing an even cooler chill to the room. “You will go. You will smile. You can even keep that lovely knight of yours close, if it helps keep you agreeable.”

    Her voice remained pleasant and detached.

    “And with the origin of that dagger traced back to the Wilds…” she began, almost as if commenting on the weather, “your presence becomes a message, a reminder that the Empire does not flinch when threatened.”

    Agatha’s breath hitched. “You want me paraded as what? Bait?”

    “I want you to be seen,” Evanora replied smoothly. “Alive. Unshaken. You survived an attack, and still, in a show of the Empire’s grace, you extend peace with an open hand. What better way to show who holds the reins?”

    “And if they refuse the gesture?”

    Evanora finally turned to her again. Something far colder than calculation masked her face. “Then you smile, and you leave them with the weight of what could have been.” She set her glass down with a soft click. “There is power in mercy, Agatha, but only when people know what would happen if it were withdrawn.”

    Agatha’s voice was quieter now, but no less sharp. “You’re not interested in peace. You’re interested in performance.”

    “I’m interested in control,” Evanora said. “Which, as you’ve proven, requires more delicate handling than I’d hoped.”

    She moved forward again, close enough now that Agatha could smell the wine on her breath. “You’ve charmed the court. You’ve even found yourself a knight willing to bleed for you. So use it. Use her. Give them something to believe in.”

    A beat passed. Evanora tilted her head. “Unless, of course, she’s become a distraction.”

    Agatha’s chin lifted. “She is not.”

    “No,” Evanora said, turning back to her desk, “she’s your shadow. Do make sure she knows how to walk in step.”

    There was a long silence. Then Agatha spoke, her voice carefully measured. “Is that all?” “For now.” Evanora waved her hand dismissively. “You’ll want to dress for the journey. The people you’re visiting have a fondness for ceremony. And sentimentality.”

    Agatha turned to leave.

    “And Agatha?” her mother added, voice once again airy.

    She paused.

    “Take care not to bleed in public again. Once is sympathy. Twice is weakness.”

    The courtyard smelled of fresh hay and saddle oil, the morning fog still clinging low to the cobblestones, swirling like breath around the hooves of waiting horses.

    Agatha moved through it slowly, her long purple cloak trailing behind her like pooling ink. Her eyes were fixed on the stable yard, where Rio stood in half plate, checking the straps of a chestnut mare with practiced ease. Beside her stood a boy, animated and flushed, clearly in the middle of some emphatic argument.

    “Please, Ser, let me ride at the rear! You’ll need someone to handle your kit. If there’s trouble and you’re in full gear, you’ll want your plates fastened right, and you know it—”

    “I’ve saddled my own horse since I was old enough to climb it,” Rio replied patiently. “And if there’s trouble, I’d rather not worry about who’s riding too close behind me.”

    “But I’m fast, ” the boy pleaded. “And quiet! Mostly.”

    Agatha paused at the edge of the courtyard, amused. She hadn’t caught his name yet, though he clearly belonged to Rio, like a particularly stubborn lost puppy nipping at her heels. “I wouldn’t worry,” she drawled as she approached, just loud enough to make both heads snap in her direction. “I imagine there’ll be no shortage of hands eager to assist Ser Rio if she finds herself in need of help… undressing.”

    Rio went still. Then her head dipped in a deep bow so smooth it might’ve passed for polite etiquette if not for the unmistakable pink blooming just beneath the collar of her tunic. The boy gawked and Rio, without lifting her head, reached out and smacked the back of his skull with a flat palm.

    He yelped. “Oh! Uh, Your Highness!” He scrambled into a bow so low it nearly toppled him forward.

    Agatha’s lips twitched. “Who might you be?” she asked.

    “Billy, Your Highness,” he said in a rush. “Her squire. Sometimes.”

    “A title we’re still working on earning,” Rio muttered, straightening now, the pink color on her face only slightly faded.

    Agatha stepped closer, hands folded in front of her.

    “Well, William,” she said, drawing out his name, “if you’re so desperate to escape the stables, perhaps they are not the right place for you. Soft hands like yours might be better suited to shadowing someone like Lady Jen while we’re away.”

    Billy paled. “Lady Jen?”

    “She could use a clever shadow,” Agatha continued, her tone smooth and deliberate.

    “Someone eager to learn, quick to listen. And I imagine she’d be delighted to have someone underfoot who my mother did not personally hire.”

    Billy turned helplessly to Rio, caught somewhere between terror and flattery. “Ser?”

    Rio didn’t move. Her voice, low and warning, slipped between her teeth. “The princess has made a request, Billy.”

    That did it.

    He jolted upright, knocking his heel into the saddle of a nearby horse in his haste. “Of course! Yes, Your Highness, thank you for the opportunity! I won’t let her down—I mean, you down—I mean—”

    He nearly tripped over his own feet backing away, then turned and scurried toward the palace with all the urgency of a man fleeing a doomed duel.

    Agatha waited until he disappeared around the corner before turning fully to Rio.

    “A squire spooked by his own shadow,” she said, her smile not entirely innocent. “Another unconventional choice, good Ser.”

    Rio exhaled like she was biting back a groan. “He’s young. His life has been softer than some. What he lacks in expertise, he makes up for in enthusiasm. The rest he will learn.” “Mm,” Agatha mused. “Well, I do hope he learns to bow before his voice cracks next time.

    It’s very hard to maintain mystique with those startled doe eyes.”

    Rio offered her a hand then, palm up, a gesture that was quietly deferential. “May I assist you, Your Highness?”

    Agatha glanced at the offered hand, then at the gleaming saddle. Gathering the reins, she hoisted herself up in one graceful motion.

    “Tempting,” she said once seated, peering down at Rio. “But I do pride myself on knowing how to mount a beast or two.”

    She only saw the flicker of Rio’s smirk after the comment had already landed. But it was there, half hidden behind the careful turn of her face as she moved to her own mount.

    Agatha’s horse sidestepped a little under her, and she settled its gait with a gentle nudge. Her gaze lingered on Rio’s back, following the precise way the chain shirt she wore in lieu of armor rippled.

    Once they had reached the gates and passed outside them, beyond the eyes of the court and the reach of her mother’s suffocating surveillance, Agatha shifted.

    With a quick glance back at the palace, she swung her leg fully over the saddle, shifting from a demure side-saddle posture into a full straddle.

    Rio looked over, clearly having noticed the change in position from the corner of her eye. “Comfortable?” she asked dryly.

    “Immensely,” Agatha replied. “If I’m going to be jostled for the next day or so, I would rather not do it in a half seat. Are you offended? Will you tell my mother that I ride without consideration for what is lady-like?”

    “I believe the word you’re looking for is decorum.”

    Agatha arched her eyebrow. “Oh? Is that what you call it when you stare at my ankles?”

    Rio didn’t answer immediately, but the brief hitch in her breath and the pointed way she kept her eyes straight ahead was enough of an answer.

    Agatha smiled, slow and triumphant. “Caught.”

    “You’re imagining things,” Rio said, a touch too flat.

    “Am I?” She shifted again, letting the hem of her cloak draw back just enough to expose a few inches more of her riding boots.

    Rio’s jaw twitched. “You’re incorrigible, princess.”

    “And you are not as subtle as you think, Ser.” Agatha shot back, the tease curling off her tongue. “It’s almost sweet, the way you try not to look.”

    Rio gave a soft snort, but the corner of her mouth betrayed her with a twitch. “It’s not you I’m watching, it’s the road.”

    “Mmm,” Agatha hummed, tilting her head just slightly as if considering. “I have been meaning to ask. Is celibacy among the oaths you carry?”

    Rio didn’t rise to the bait, but her nostrils flared ever so slightly, and she adjusted the fit of her gloves with more attention than was necessary. “I was taught to be vigilant.”

    Agatha led her horse to match Rio’s pace. “Vigilant to danger, or to temptation?”

    Rio clicked her tongue. “Temptation rarely announces itself with this much noise.”

    She couldn’t hide the delight as she laughed. “You’ve noticed my volume, have you?”

    “I notice everything, your Highness,” Rio said smoothly. “Including the rather sharp bend coming up.”

    “You are deflecting.”

    “I am riding. And keeping you alive, which, as I understand it, remains my primary assignment.”

    Agatha sighed, but it was the theatrical kind, rich with amusement. “You’re lucky I like a challenge.”

    Rio gave nothing away, but her voice was quieter when she replied: “So I am.”

    The way Rio said it carried no venom, only weight - as if Agatha were not a nuisance, but rather an inevitability.

    She felt the pulse in her neck thrum a little faster.

    They rode in silence for a while, the landscape beginning to change, slowly shifting to loamy fields dappled with wildflowers. The road narrowed and meandered toward the forested edges of the province. The trees would thicken soon, and they would need to dismount and walk some of it.

    Agatha breathed in the early morning air, the scent of pine and dew-soaked earth: no guards. No watchful eyes. No eyes but Rio’s.

    She glanced sidelong again.

    “I wonder,” she said, voice lilting and lazy. “If you would have stared like that if we’d met under different circumstances, if I were just some girl in a tavern, hiking her skirt to warm herself by the fire.”

    Rio didn’t respond at first. She shifted slightly in her saddle, the leather creaking under her. Then, “No.”

    Agatha blinked, surprised. “No?”

    “If you were just some farm girl, I wouldn’t have stood a chance of resisting you.”

    Agatha’s eyes sparked. “That’s not an answer.”

    “It’s the only one you’re getting.”

    “I think I’ll take that as a yes.”

    Rio looked forward again, but her ears were pink. “Take it however you like.”

    Agatha’s smile deepened. “I usually do.”

    They rode in companionable silence for a stretch after that, the path continuing to narrow as the trees grew denser. The last of the afternoon sun cut through the canopy in fractured gold, the light flickering like fire over Rio’s shoulders.

    The road gave way to thick roots and moss-laced stone. Ferns crept up from the underbrush, brushing against her boots with every sway of the saddle. The air smelled richer here.

    Rio pulled on the reins and dismounted in one smooth motion. She began to lead her horse forward by the bridle, boots crunching through the underbrush.

    Agatha cleared her throat. “My, my. Has the gallant knight forgotten herself?”

    Rio glanced back with a confused look, as if she had misremembered Agatha stubbornly refusing her help just hours before.

    Agatha smiled, keeping her voice light and just shy of mocking. “No hand offered? No assistance? Has chivalry died here on the edge of the woods?”

    Rio said nothing, but turned back, stepped close, and placed both hands at Agatha’s waist. Once her boots kissed the ground, Agatha didn’t step back.

    Neither did Rio.

    Agatha felt every point of contact: the way Rio’s hands settled, fingers splayed against the fabric of her riding coat, thumbs hovering just above the curve of her hips.

    Up close, she could see the tension in Rio’s jaw, the flicker of restraint in her throat as she swallowed. Her eyes didn’t move - not down, not away. They locked on Agatha in a way that nearly robbed her of words.

    Nearly. But Agatha was not known to be speechless.

    “Careful,” she said softly. “You hold me like you want to make a habit of it.”

    Rio’s eyes darkened, though her expression didn’t shift. “And you have a habit of… making things difficult.”

    Still, Rio remained, fingers lingering on Agatha’s waist. She was close enough that Agatha could feel her breath when she finally exhaled, slow and steady.

    Just as Agatha leaned forward half an inch, her fingers sliding in invitation against the front of Rio’s cloak, Rio stepped back.

    Agatha’s hands dropped, but her gaze did not.

    She stood where she’d been left, jaw tight, heat crawling up her spine in a slow and burning tide.

    She watched Rio turn away, guiding her horse toward the thickening woods like nothing had passed between them.

    And that infuriated her.

    If the knight would not be tempted by proximity, then she’d find another weakness.

    Every warrior had one - a blind spot, a vulnerable seam beneath the armor.

    She fell into step beside Rio, pretending to admire the mossy ridgeline of a fallen tree as she plotted.

    Perhaps if she tripped, just enough to warrant a steadying hand at her waist. Or maybe she could feign cold and ask for her cloak to be drawn tighter, her hair to be adjusted, her boots to be checked for a stone.

    Or better yet, she could ask her to look at a scratch just behind her ear - one she didn’t have, of course, but how could Rio possibly know that until her fingers were brushing against Agatha’s bare neck?

    Her thoughts had just begun to sharpen into something wickedly viable when she heard it.

    It was a soft trickling sound, half-hidden by birdsong, but unmistakable: water. The hush and hiss of a spring moving over stone.

    Her steps slowed. Her head tilted.

    Perfect.

    She pinched her cheeks harshly until color bloomed high beneath each cheekbone, mimicking exertion.

    Then, with the most practiced hint of fragility in her voice, she called out, “Ser…”

    Rio stopped instantly. “Your Highness, are you unwell?”

    Agatha pressed the back of her hand to her brow, eyelids fluttering just enough to sell the image. “It’s nothing. Just the heat. I didn’t realize how close the air would get once we left the breeze of the road…”

    Rio’s gaze swept over her, brow furrowed. Then her head tilted.

    “I hear water,” she said reluctantly. “A spring, maybe. If you splash some across your face, it might help.”

    Agatha blinked up at her, wide-eyed and innocent. “Do you think we have time for a detour?” Rio hesitated, too long, too telling, and Agatha watched the internal war flash across her face. “We will make time,” Rio said at last.

    Agatha smiled, letting it curl up slow and grateful. “How good of you. Always prioritizing my comfort.”

    She followed a step behind as Rio adjusted course toward the sound of water, allowing her expression to shift the moment her back was turned.

    Wicked satisfaction took over her face.

    A dip in the spring would suit her needs very well.

    Rest and Ruin Chapter Summary Agatha takes a dip. Rio offers a helping hand.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes Rio wasn't fooled by Agatha’s newly claimed sensitivity to the heat of the day.

    There was a specific tone Agatha took when she was lying in a way that was meant to be noticed - a practiced breathlessness, her hand pressing to her collarbone like it might precede some extraordinary swoon.

    Rio could catch her easily, no fainting couch required. She could easily lift her into her arms and lay her down on a chaise to be immortalized by some painter. If Agatha willed it, she would never need to let her feet touch the earth again.

    But that would be foolish.

    "You look flushed," Rio said flatly, not looking up from her work removing the horse’s bridle.

    "Do I?" Agatha asked, fluttering her lashes in a way that would’ve fooled someone who hadn’t spent weeks cataloging every variation of her expressions.

    Rio bit back a sigh. "Perhaps a splash to the face will help."

    Agatha was already unfastening her cloak. "Perhaps."

    If Agatha expected Rio to be a fool, following her immediately would certainly confirm it. Instead, Rio busied herself with attending to the saddlebags. She checked the perimeter.

    There was a spot on her greaves that didn’t need polishing, but she rubbed at it until it shone anyway.

    With the horses settling, she inhaled as deeply as she could before turning the bend.

    Each step felt heavier than it should have, like she was trudging through mud instead of walking over grass that sprang back up after each step.

    It reminded her of something an old warrior had once said - one of those grim sayings meant to make squires flinch and soldiers somber. A rumor about how the Empire conducted their business, frightening enough to be true.

    “Prisoners destined for death are granted a final walk. It isn’t mercy. It’s time - to consider their last words.”

    Rio had never understood the point of that. Why waste breath on a truth that wouldn’t outlive you?

    But now—now she wondered what hers would be. And if she should say them, just in case.

    Because this, she was sure, was her execution: not with rope nor blade, but with glances too long and sighs too soft, with skin bared beneath sunlight and laughter that echoed like a dare. If Agatha Harkness was going to be her end, maybe it was right to consider the words she’d offer before she went.

    Rio stepped around the rocks and saw her.

    Agatha stood waist-deep in the water, her shift translucent in places, clinging like a second skin.

    The sunlight filtered through the canopy in slanted beams, catching on the droplets rolling down Agatha’s shoulders, her throat, the hollow between her collarbones. Her arms were raised, fingers weaving her wet hair up into a knot atop her head.

    And the only thought that filled her mind, that hummed loudly enough to drown out every order she'd ever obeyed, was that any instinct or bloodbound duty that commanded her to avert her gaze was wrong.

    Maybe she ought to speak those last words now, be they truth, prophecy, or something in between.

    I’d let you ruin me.

    She felt them, heavy and hot, lodged at the back of her throat.

    And worse still, she meant them.

    It was impossible for someone trained to observe not to look.

    Rio’s gaze followed the line of Agatha’s wrist to her elbow, where water beaded and caught like dew. Her skin was flushed pink from the heat as she twisted her hair high, away from her face and off her neck.

    Rio’s eyes drifted lower -

    To the curve of her underarm, dark with hair that curled softly, not yet wet and matted. To the slick sheen at the hollow of her throat, where it met the rise of her collarbone. A single droplet lingered there, as if it had forgotten how to fall. Or perhaps it couldn't find a reason to make a home anywhere else.

    She caught the faint shadow of her nipples visible through the damp linen, pebbled from the water. And when her eyes met Agatha’s again, her gaze was unbothered and unashamed.

    Rio allowed herself one last look…

    To the length of her thighs beneath the surface, one shifting against the other, sending small ripples through the water with each idle motion.

    She’d been warned about creatures like this: Dryads, with smiles full of sharp wicked teeth and nails shifting into claws.

    Sirens who sang sailors to their deaths and shed not a single tear afterward.

    Nymphs who lured mortals into wild things just to watch them burn.

    Fae queens who wore mortal skins like gowns and feasted on their longing.

    She understood now. Maybe not the danger. But the pull.

    Every man in every tale had walked willingly toward his ruin, with a smile on his face and no regrets in his chest.

    And Rio… would not have blamed them.

    Not one of them.

    She closed her eyes for a moment.

    It didn’t help.

    Because she could still see her, and worse, she could still want.

    This wasn’t a new experience - not the wanting; she’d felt that pull before. She remembered a girl with a freckled nose and a quick laugh, from a village near the sea. They’d dared each other into a lake at dusk, giggling among the reeds.

    The girl had made her feel shy, a hand on her thigh under the water, warm lips brushing her cheek as secrets passed from breath to breath.

    Rio had stammered. She’d flushed red to her ears. But she’d wanted it.

    This was not that.

    Agatha did not make her feel shy. To the contrary, Agatha made her want to walk into the spring fully armored and let its weight drag her to the bottom. Perhaps if Agatha were feeling merciful, she’d join her there and end her suffering by pulling the last bubbles of air from her lungs.

    That was her way.

    Agatha didn’t offer halting touches or whispered nothings. She pointed Rio to the edge of something she wouldn’t return from - a cliff with no landing, only the thrill of the fall. It was tempting.

    Rio had been built of orders and forged by oaths layered like armor, until she wasn’t sure where the command ended and where she began. She had been taught to anticipate needs, to guard others’ peace at the cost of her own. Wanting was indulgence. Reaching was risk.

    And yet…

    She wanted to reach for Agatha.

    To be known. To be seen without the armor. To be touched like a person, not a protector.

    She wanted not to be the blade that ended a line, but rather the shield that preserved it and allowed it to continue.

    Clenching her jaw, Rio shook her head slightly. That still wasn’t right, because she didn’t want to be anything resembling a weapon at all.

    Regretfully, she turned her gaze away, fixing it on the trembling branches above.

    “Are you going to watch me all day,” Agatha called, her voice wickedly sweet, “or are you coming in?”

    Rio exhaled through her nose—slow, measured, as if control could be exhaled too. She quickly wondered how long she’d gone silent beyond clearly long enough for a princess to notice. That was dangerous.

    She kept her gaze fixed upward. It was better to stare down the sun than look at the water and see how easily she could be undone. “Someone has to keep watch.”

    “From that distance?” Agatha called sweetly. “You must have hawk’s eyes, Ser.”

    There was a soft splash, just enough to draw attention. Rio bit the inside of her cheek—too hard—and soothed it with her tongue. She tasted the tang of blood.

    “I could help encourage you,” Agatha murmured, voice slick with mirth. “Say the word, and I’ll splash you. I’ve always wondered if wet leather improves a knight’s mood. It would certainly be something to look at.”

    Rio didn’t look, didn’t blink. “If you soak my armor, you’ll have to be patient as you watch me spend the next hour oiling it.”

    Agatha’s laugh rippled like the sunlight across water. “Oh, Ser. You’re going to have to try harder if you want me to behave.”

    Silence followed—but it wasn’t empty. It shimmered between them, warm and teasing and far too charged.

    “You won’t enjoy the ride ahead if you seize up from rust,” Agatha said, softer now. “Come sit. Just your feet. Unless, of course… the nearness of me is what’s truly unbearable.”

    Rio swore under her breath.

    With practiced efficiency, she unbuckled her greaves and set them aside, the sound of metal striking earth more aggressive than necessary. She rolled the cuffs of her trousers to mid-calf, stalked toward the edge of the spring, and sat heavily on the mossy stone, boots off, legs submerged.

    The water was hot. Too hot. Perfect.

    Agatha smirked.

    Rio did not look at her.

    “Feeling better?” Agatha asked, her tone coy.

    “I’m tolerating the moment,” Rio replied flatly.

    And then, because the gods had clearly abandoned her, there was a splash of movement.

    Rio turned just as Agatha had pulled her shift over her head and tossed it toward the rocks. “What,” Rio asked, too sharply, “are you doing?”

    Agatha raised her arms overhead in a languid stretch, unbothered. “Drying my clothes.

    Would you mind hanging that from a branch for me? It wrinkles terribly if left in a heap.” Rio’s ears burned.

    She reached out blindly, snatched the wet shift from where it had landed, and stood without looking. Not directly. Not yet.

    “You’re impossible,” she muttered, walking stiffly to a low branch and draping the garment carefully over it.

    “So I’ve been told. I thought you had caught on by now,” Agatha called back, voice light, lips curved in a satisfied little smile.

    Rio returned to her seat on the stone and planted her hands on either side of her thighs. She kept her focus locked on the far bank, her breath shallow, her posture rigid.

    If Agatha noticed, she didn’t say a word.

    A soft sigh broke the silence, light as the ripple of water.

    Rio did not turn.

    Another breath from Agatha, this one heavier. Deliberate.

    Rio ground her palms into the stone. Her gauntlets were off, but she could still feel the tension buzzing in her wrists, her spine, her jaw. She tried to focus on the flicker of light through the canopy, the faint chirr of insects, the wind stirring leaves high above.

    She heard the shift of movement in the water—subtle but unmistakable. The drag of skin against stone. A quiet intake of breath that didn’t quite sound like discomfort.

    Rio's neck locked tight.

    She didn’t look - couldn’t. But her ears caught everything.

    A gentle splash. A shift in breathing. A pause that lingered just a moment too long before the exhale followed.

    Her fingernails threatened to cut into the calluses on her palms.

    She recognized that sound.

    Rio squeezed her eyes shut, her mind racing. Should she say something? Demand she stop?

    Stand and walk away? But if she left, she’d be giving Agatha what she wanted—proof that Rio could be shaken, undone, driven from her post by something as simple and sinful as a sigh.

    She gritted her teeth. Her voice, when it came, was low and rough.

    “You should be resting.”

    There was a small splash in reply, the kind that came not from movement, but from a careless flick of water.

    “I am resting,” Agatha said, sweetly. “This is very soothing.”

    Another sigh. And gods help her, it trembled into something close to a whine.

    Rio stared hard at the far bank, her jaw tight enough to ache.

    “Is something the matter, Ser?” Agatha purred. “You’re so tense. I could show you how to relax.”

    She didn’t know how to release the tension in her body without standing abruptly. “I’ll check the perimeter.”

    “If I cry your name, will you come running back? You said you would.”

    Rio froze halfway down the narrow path, her shoulders locked and unmoving. She didn’t turn, but the muscle in her jaw twitched.

    “That was different,” she said. The words came out clipped, too precise.

    Agatha hummed behind her, the sound sultry and unhurried. “Was it? I’m not so sure. I think you just like me better helpless.”

    Rio clenched her fists at her sides. “You are many things, Princess. Helpless is not one of them.”

    Agatha sighed dramatically behind her. “Alright then,” she called. “I’ll make it easier for you.”

    The water shifted.

    “I’ll turn away. You won’t even have to see me. Maybe then you’ll remember how to breathe.”

    There was the sound of Agatha pushing through the water, repositioning herself deeper toward the edge of the spring.

    Rio turned. Just to ensure she wasn’t about to crack her head on a rock - just to be sure. Agatha had her back to Rio now, arms arched above her in an exaggerated stretch, head tilted to one side in a show of mock innocence.

    But Rio didn’t see that.

    She saw the wound.

    Just below her left shoulder blade, cutting jagged toward her spine—an awful, caustic burn. It curved like something unnatural, as if etched into her skin by fire or worse. The flesh was still raw in places, the surrounding skin pink and glossy with half-healed scar tissue.

    It was the kind of wound that should have festered. The kind that should have ruined someone. It hadn’t.

    But it looked like it had tried.

    Rio stepped forward before she realized she was moving. Her mouth parted, but no sound came.

    “Agatha,” she breathed, horror seeping into her tone.

    Agatha’s posture grew taut.

    “Is that what it takes?” she asked, still not facing her. “A little damage? You’re more interested now, Ser?”

    Rio did not answer.

    She was too busy looking at something no one had warned her about, because no one thought she’d be allowed to see Agatha like this.

    The scar wasn’t old. The rawness hadn’t faded. It was a wound that had been managed, not healed. Kept hidden, restrained. Like it had been treated with just enough care to keep it from killing her, but not enough to spare her from its memory.

    And still, Agatha stood there, half turned, chin lifted like she was daring Rio to flinch. Rio didn’t flinch.

    But her hands formed into fists at her sides, bruising the grass underneath..

    It wasn’t just the wound that hurt to look at.

    It was the way Agatha spoke about it. Like Rio saw it as a pretty blemish to admire in the soft hush of evening.

    “What an awful thing,” Rio said quietly. Her voice felt hoarse. “To think that would be alluring to me.”

    Agatha froze.

    Rio’s breath came hard. “To think I could see something like that and find it appealing.

    Something to want.”

    “I would cleave the world open to undo that,” Rio said, teeth bared now. “I would kill for the chance to erase what left that mark on you.”

    She was shaking.

    Not from restraint.

    But from rage.

    Not the rage of a knight, trained and honed and weaponized, but that of a woman who had allowed herself to care.

    Rio reached out, not to touch the wound yet, but to be near it. “You are not something to survive and show off. You are not a wound made beautiful.”

    Agatha turned her head slightly, eyes darting to hers.

    And Rio’s voice dropped into something low, something carved from the stone of her chest. “You are something to be fought for.”

    She stepped to the edge of the spring and crouched, still not touching her.

    “Who did that to you?”

    Agatha met her eyes, unreadable. “Does it matter?”

    “Yes.”

    A pause stretched between them.

    Rio’s hands curled into fists at her sides.

    "How bad is it?" Agatha asked quietly, without turning. Her voice didn’t tremble—but it wasn’t steady either. "I’ve never seen it."

    Rio stepped forward into the spring, the water sloshing around her calves. "It’s bad. Will you let me see closer?"

    Agatha let out a soft, breathy laugh but took a few steps back closer to Rio. "Well, that’s unfortunate. I was hoping it was more of a charming blemish."

    Rio didn’t smile.

    Her hand hovered near the wound, her breath tight. "Again. Who did this?"

    Agatha’s voice was quiet now. “My mother. To protect me, she said. To hide what I am. From the world. From myself."

    Something inside Rio broke open. A sharp, burning ache bloomed in her chest.

    “And then I did,” Agatha continued, “so that it was one less thing to be wielded over me. If I cause the pain, she doesn’t get to control it.”

    Rio raised her hand. As her fingertips brushed the edge of the scar, a flicker of violet light sparked from beneath Agatha’s skin.

    "Never again," Rio said, low and fierce.

    Agatha turned slightly, startled by the raw edge in her tone. But before she could speak, the light surged.

    Tendrils bloomed.

    Not fire or flame. But something older—veins of magic pulsing out from beneath the ruin, soft and slow and alive . They threaded like roots through the wounded flesh, illuminating the twisted edges of the scar in hues of lilac and indigo. The skin shimmered beneath her touch, the scar tissue rising slightly, then smoothing.

    As though magic was unknotting what pain had once made permanent.

    Rio froze.

    She felt it before she understood it: an ache under her ribs, like something being pulled from her and offered freely, a piece of her magic she hadn’t summoned, now moving with its own purpose.

    She made a small sound of surprise, more breath than voice.

    And then she pressed her palm flat over the wound, instinct overriding reason.

    Agatha gasped.

    Her body bowed forward with the force of it, knees bending just slightly, head dropping as if something had been yanked from her. Or given back to her. The purple light surged again, brighter this time, before dimming to a steady glow, not unlike the colors changing as a bruise healed. The jagged edges of the scar softened further, until the mark was no longer a wound at all.

    The rune revealed itself in full.

    Not a burn, not a brand. A symbol of birthright.

    Rio’s other hand flew to Agatha’s waist, steadying her. Her fingers curled against her stomach, arm wrapping firmly around her middle, first holding her up, then holding her close. “Easy,” Rio murmured. “I’ve got you.”

    Agatha’s breath stuttered—caught somewhere between a sob and a moan. Her hand reached instinctively for Rio’s forearm, clutching it more firmly to herself.

    The spring around them went utterly still. No birdsong. No breeze. Just the low hum of magic settling into a new shape, transforming into truth.

    Something so sacred that the world at large stopped to listen to it.

    Rio didn’t let go. Couldn’t.

    She wasn’t ready.

    And neither, she suspected, was Agatha.

    Because the scar wasn’t replaced by pale freckled skin.

    It revealed something older, deliberate.

    The lines of the rune beneath glowed faintly, now fully visible in the wake of her touch— etched deep in a script Rio had only seen once before, in a half-burned scroll buried in the ruins beyond the Western Wilds.

    Agatha Harkness, daughter of the Empire, Princess and heir, was a runechild.

    Not just marked, but chosen, claimed by something older than any crown.

    Agatha’s voice drifted backward, soft as a confession. “You saw it, didn’t you?”

    “Yes,” Rio murmured. “I did.”

    “And you healed me anyway.”

    Rio’s arm tightened slightly. “Because no one should have to carry pain like that. Not when I can stop it.”

    A pause.

    Then, quieter still, she breathed, “Not you.”

    Agatha exhaled, the sound suspended somewhere between laugh and sob..

    “Rio…” she exhaled, her voice trembling.

    “I don’t know what it means,” Rio admitted, her lips near her ear. “But I know what I’ll do if anyone tries to brand you again.”

    Agatha tilted her head, eyes fluttering shut. “What?”

    “I’ll show them what it feels like.”

    Agatha leaned into her for a moment longer, her breathing still uneven. Rio held her steady, not pressing, not urging. Simply present.

    After a minute, Rio carefully lifted her hand from Agatha’s back, the skin now smooth beneath her palm. The rune remained, gleaming like something reborn. It pulsed once before quieting, settling into the natural pattern of breath and skin.

    Agatha turned in the circle of her arms, her expression having shifted from the flirtatious, teasing deflection to something more raw, almost solemn.

    Rio didn’t know what to say.

    So she said nothing at all.

    Agatha reached up slowly and tucked a damp strand of hair behind Rio’s ear, her fingers brushing her temple in a touch so gentle it almost undid her.

    “You should have let it be,” Agatha said, not unkindly. “You didn’t have to take it from me.” Rio searched her eyes. “I didn’t take anything from you. I uncovered what was already yours.”
