    They stood like that, the warmth of the spring lapping softly at their legs, the trees swaying above, a breeze threading through branches like breath held too long.

    Agatha looked at her for a long time.

    Then, without a word, she stepped back.

    “Thank you,” Agatha said finally. And the words, spoken so simply, held more weight than anything else could have.

    Rio nodded, her throat too tight to respond.

    The rune still glowed faintly behind her eyes as she turned away.

    Chapter End Notes Oh, these fools.

    Stay Close Chapter Summary Hospitality, a hearth, and only one bed.

    Chapter Notes Hey folks, adding a note to make clear that some of the phrases used by Rio/Her mentor in this work are of the Irish language and not a fantasy language. While this is a fantasy setting and many languages are spoken in many places, I never want to intentionally erase another’s cultural history and heritage!

    See the end of the chapter for more notes Rio tried to be subtle in the way she backed away. But the woods did not allow her this; the moss squelched under her boots, and a rock tumbled down into the spring.

    She didn’t expect for Agatha to suddenly care about keeping her modesty, but Rio gave her the privacy to dress for her own sake.

    She needed space, to put an allowable amount of distance between them, needed time to collect the sharp-edged thoughts rattling in her chest.

    Agatha was a runechild.

    There was no other explanation, not after what she had seen in vivid detail and had felt thrum beneath her fingertips.

    Ruined flesh had wiped away like accumulated dust, leaving the mark beneath whole and untouched. A spiral of deep violet, coiled tight just along the edge of Agatha’s shoulder blade.

    Not just a mark. A sigil. Magic made living.

    Rio’s own rune itched in phantom sympathy just beneath her left collarbone, a thicket of thorns tangled around a central bloom.

    Magic, in this kingdom, was old. Elemental. Wild and inherited.

    No one was born with magic who didn’t also carry a rune.

    Most were small, simple shapes that flickered across the skin when called upon: an arrow for strength, a flame for fire. Some faded with time, while others grew darker or spread as these powers were embraced.

    They were seen as gifts in the Wilds. Curses to the Empire. Everywhere they were known as promises written into the body by something older than gods and men.

    Agatha’s didn’t appear to belong to any font of power Rio was familiar with, instead spiraling in on itself almost like an ouroboros.

    She’d felt the magic when she had touched her.

    Not just pulsing beneath the skin, but responding to her touch. Flaring in recognition.

    Rio didn’t know what to make of that, nor did she know what to do with the way it left her feeling untethered.

    She tugged the collar of her shirt aside.

    A new tendril had appeared. A single curling vine stretched out from the thicket, reaching inward toward the hollow of her chest.

    She hadn’t spoken a spell, hadn’t willfully called the power to her hands, but it had answered anyway.

    The last time her magic had stirred without warning, she’d been a child trying to save a wounded rabbit by willing it still and had sworn never again.

    But this time…

    Agatha had been restored, not only made whole, but her truth also revealed.

    And Rio’s mark had grown in answer.

    She chewed her lip, unsteady, and knelt in the mossy grass beside her mare.

    Only fools would try to find certainty in things that drew their power from unproven hope. Still, she needed to see it again.

    Rio spread her fingers against the earth, slow and deliberate, pressing her palm flat against the damp soil.

    Beneath her hand, the ground was quiet.

    Then she felt a shift, betrayed by the smallest tremor in her hand.

    She lifted it away.

    A dandelion rose with the motion, blooming where her palm had rested. Wisps of white seeds clustered, full and impossibly out of season.

    Rio closed her fingers around the flower’s stem.

    She hadn’t meant to reach for Agatha, hadn’t meant to cast anything. She hadn’t meant—

    But she had.

    And something had answered her back.

    "Are you making a wish?"

    Rio startled at the sound of Agatha’s voice.

    She turned to find her standing a few paces away, clad again in traveling clothes, hair still damp and half-loose around her shoulders. A smirk curled her lips, but Agatha’s expression was softer around the corners of her eyes, in curiosity, maybe. Or perhaps it was the afterglow of being seen and finding Rio still standing there.

    Rio looked down at the dandelion resting in her palm. "No."

    Agatha arched a brow. "Missed opportunity."

    "It is dangerous to make a wish in the woods," Rio said, voice low. "Especially when you don’t know what is listening."

    It made Agatha pause. Her smile didn’t vanish, but it faltered just enough to reveal the edge behind it.

    "You’re serious," she said.

    Rio met her eyes. "Yes."

    Agatha looked around then, as if reassessing the trees themselves. The sun was low now, the light slanting strange and long through the branches.

    "Have you spent much time in the woods?" she asked, quieter now.

    Rio shook her head. "Not these."

    Agatha hummed. She walked a few steps further, trailing her fingers over a low-hanging branch as if testing its strength. "I tried to run away into them once. I was young—seven, maybe eight. I didn’t get far."

    Rio tilted her head. "What stopped you?"

    "I tripped," Agatha said simply. "There were brambles. They caught in my skirt and my hair. I like to think the woods wanted to keep me."

    Her hand drifted absently toward her scalp, fingers brushing a strand of damp hair behind her ear.

    Rio rose to her feet slowly. "Did it hurt?"

    "Not at first. Not until my mother brushed them out."

    The air went still between them.

    Something flared hot behind Rio’s ribs - not her magic this time, but something more visceral. Protective. Furious. Raw.

    She glanced down at the dandelion still in her hand, its fluff catching the light. Then, slowly, she loosened her grip and let it go.

    The seeds scattered, no wish made.

    The pair rode for a while after that, no words passing between them—just the steady rhythm of hooves sinking into loamy earth and the hush of the woods closing in behind them.

    No birds sang, and neither person spoke.

    The sun continued its slow descent, casting everything in the pale glow of waning evening. The silence gradually thickened, not uncomfortable exactly, but charged—like the world itself was waiting to see who would break it.

    Rio bent first. “Is the prince marked?”

    Agatha didn’t turn to look at her. For a moment, Rio thought she might not answer.

    Then, softly, she sighed. “No. He isn’t.”

    Rio just nodded, not pressing further, understanding the weight behind the words too well. To be unmarked meant to be safe in a place that would find it an ill omen instead of a blessing.

    After another half hour, the woods began to thin, heavy boughs giving way to fields and open sky. A small homestead came into view up ahead, nestled between two hills. Smoke curled from its chimney, and she could just make out the distant shape of a laundry line weighted with fresh linen fluttering like white flags in the breeze.

    Rio reined in her horse slightly, nodding toward it. “Up there. Might be worth asking for lodging. Unless you'd prefer we camp.”

    “We need not ask. They ought to be honored to host us.” Agatha replied archly.

    She smiled and shrugged, growing confident in a land that was more familiar to her own.

    “This is true, your Highness. Still, it might be nice to give them the option. People out here aren’t like those at court. They offer things because they want to, not because they’re afraid of what will become of them if they don’t.”

    Agatha tilted her head, unconvinced but clearly amused. “You think I should stoop to begging for a bed?”

    “I would not ask you to stoop. But I do think,” Rio said, kicking her horse into an easy trot, “you might be surprised by what kindness looks like when it is given freely.”

    Agatha rolled her eyes. “Go along then. Win hearts and invitations.”

    Rio grinned, already shifting in the saddle. “Gladly.”

    The cottage was built from thick stone and dark timber, the door sun-bleached and worn smooth by weather and years and many hands pushing it in. Rio dismounted just outside the gate and made sure to leave her sword belted, but her hands open and visible.

    She approached the door and knocked twice.

    A moment passed. Then a pair of wide eyes peered out from the cracked wood.

    A young girl, no older than seven, braids looped with bright ribbon greeted her with a curious smile.

    Rio knelt without hesitation, lowering herself to the child’s level.

    “Well met,” she said, voice gentle. “Is your mother home?”

    The girl blinked, then gave a solemn nod and disappeared through the tall doorway.

    Rio stayed kneeling.

    When the door opened again, a woman stepped out. She was tall and dark-skinned, with a linen wrap binding a sleeping baby to her hip and sleeves rolled to her elbows.

    Her eyes swept over Rio in a single, assessing pass. Her eyes held neither fear nor suspicion, but rather demonstrated the quiet readiness of someone who’d had to decide too many times, too quickly, who was worth trusting.

    The woman didn’t flinch, but she didn’t smile, either.

    Rio dipped her head in respectful greeting. “Good evening, I apologize for intruding. I seek shelter for the night for myself and one other. We can offer coin… or trade for help, if there’s work to be done, máthair an tí.”

    The woman’s gaze narrowed just slightly, her head tilting. “You call me mother of the house in the Wilds tongue. You hail from the border?”

    “Not for some time,” Rio said. “But yes.”

    The woman’s eyes softened barely before hardening once again as she peered around Rio to where Agatha remained mounted. “Not many knights pass through these fields. Who is the other that travels with you?”

    “A noble. But not one who is useless,” Rio said with a small smile.

    That earned a chuckle in return.

    “There’s room in the loft,” the woman said. “Water for your horses, stew on the fire. You can help with collecting wood.”

    “Gladly,” Rio replied. She stood, but the woman offered her hand before she could extend her own.

    It was rough-palmed, honest from years of repetitive work.

    Rio took it, feeling the calluses along the base of the woman’s fingers, the strength in her grip. These were not the hands of someone who’d been spared hardship, but rather of someone who had shaped her life with them. They were the hands of someone who had carved safety from stubborn earth and withstood many long winter nights. There was resilience in them.

    They reminded her of her own, of her mentor’s, of generations of elders who seemed to hold the world together through brute will. They were nothing like Agatha’s.

    Agatha’s hands were smooth: the hands of someone who had been told that they were made to gesture, not grip.

    Rio released the woman’s hand with a nod of gratitude, her thoughts a half-step behind her. “Thank you,” she said.

    “Leyna,” the woman replied.

    “Rio.”

    Leyna’s eyes cut once more to Agatha’s silhouette in the distance, framed by the fading sun like an Impressionist painting: regal even when idle, a creature carved from courtly expectation, yet somehow always slightly misaligned with it.

    “She is of the Empire?” Leyna asked, not accusing—just careful.

    Rio’s jaw flexed as she considered how to answer. There wasn’t a word that fit. Princess was dangerous, and diplomat was only a half truth.

    And runechild didn’t belong to anyone but Agatha herself, and Rio wouldn’t tell her secret — not like that.

    “She was born to it,” Rio said finally, voice measured. “But she is not one of them.”

    It was a delicate line, a truth that left just enough unsaid. Perhaps Agatha would find the humor in Rio finally adhering to the courtly game.

    Leyna studied her for a long moment, and then nodded once. A weighty kind of understanding passed between them, a woman recognizing the difference between blood and choice.

    “Then she is welcome in my home.”

    Leyna glanced down at the child who had returned, watching wide-eyed from the doorway.

    “Boots off at the door. I won’t have mud tracked where my children sleep.”

    Rio smiled. “Understood.”

    She turned back toward the road where Agatha still sat atop her horse, arms folded, watching with an expression between curiosity and amusement.

    As Rio approached, Agatha lifted a brow. “You took your time. It would have been quicker if I had asked.”

    “Perhaps, but the result was the same,” Rio said. “The lady of the house requests boots off inside. She asked for some work to be done, but do not worry. I did not promise you would chop any wood, just that it would be done.”

    Agatha scoffed. “I should hope not. My hands are not meant for splinters.”

    Rio offered both hands to guide her down from the saddle.

    She tried not to think about how soft Agatha’s hands felt where they met hers. She pushed harder not to wish they’d linger.

    Everything about the way Leyna moved around her home was lived-in, sure-footed, and deeply unbothered by the expectations of the outside world. She stirred stew one-handed while balancing the baby on her hip and directed the older child with a single glance that needed no raising of her voice. Rio caught Agatha watching her.

    She stood by the hearth, half-shadowed by the low beams of the cottage, one hand resting on the back of a chair. Her traveling cloak had been shed, sleeves rolled delicately to just above the elbow. Her hair, still damp, formed into curling waves that framed her face, not like a crown but rather something untamed and wild.

    Agatha laughed at something the child said—an earnest, babbled story about a troublesome goat that liked to eat boots left outside. The sound rang clear and real in the warm air. And then she went still.

    Rio saw the exact moment the joy caught in her throat like a swallowed stone.

    Agatha's smile faltered—not dramatically, not enough for anyone else to notice. But Rio did, as it was her duty to do so.

    The small girl giggled and tugged at Agatha’s hand, oblivious to the change. Agatha bent to humor her, tucking a braid behind her ear and murmuring something soft in return. But the light behind her eyes had gone distant - strange and far away.

    Rio didn’t need to ask why that spark had been snuffed.

    This was a home built of wood and warmth, but it may as well have been plucked from an idyllic storybook: a mother who soothed, not scorched; a child who played freely without the weight of dynasty or dread. There was no lineage here, no legacy to weaponize - just safety. Only love given freely.

    Rio watched Agatha linger at the threshold of it all like someone looking in through a glass pane.

    When the stew was passed around, Leyna asked, simply and kindly, “Would you like another bowl, dear?” Rio saw the flinch that Agatha surely didn’t intend to show.

    Like the word had hit her harder than it should have.

    After the meal, the rhythm of the homestead settled into something familiar to its occupants. Leyna handed out tasks without ceremony, her child darting off for kindling, the baby curled quiet against her shoulder. It was the kind of routine that spoke of habit, of comfort, of people who trusted one another to get things done.

    Rio stood before she was asked, already loosening the ties of her tunic and rolling her sleeves to the elbow.

    She didn’t look to see if Agatha had followed, but heard the door creak behind her. She could feel eyes on her, Agatha, leaning on the threshold, one shoulder resting against the frame, watching.

    The axe was easy in her grip - a good weight, a well-made tool. The chopping block had seen years of use, and the wood stacked beside it was ready and waiting.

    She braced a log and brought the blade down. Clean. Straight through.

    No flourish. No wasted motion.

    Again.

    And again.

    Work like this didn’t require thought, which was precisely why she needed it. The rhythm steadied the tumult she couldn’t yet give a name, images of scars and blooming runes and dandelions springing to life unprompted.

    And Agatha’s back. That mark. The magic that knew hers.

    Crack.

    Another piece split. The scent of fresh-cut pine caught on the air.

    Leyna passed beside her, close but never crowding. She asked questions—about wolves, about routes she might take to town, about the quiet threats hiding in the thinning forests along the southern roads. Rio answered plainly, never raising her voice, offering facts and truth.

    These were easy things to trade for the hospitality.

    Leyna gave her a long measured and thoughtful look after a while.

    “You’re good with your hands,” she said, nodding to the growing pile of firewood.

    Rio gave a quiet grunt of acknowledgment. “I like to keep them busy.”

    Leyna smiled, then teased almost absently, “You would make a good father.”

    The axe didn’t falter, but Rio’s breath did.

    It wasn’t the first time someone had said something like that to her. But it was the first time she actively considered the idea.

    Parenthood was never a role she’d imagined for herself—not because she hadn’t wanted it, but because she’d been taught early not to.

    Knights didn’t have children - certainly not of their blood. They took in strays, squires, sons of other men lost to war or pride or duty. That was the shape of it. Knights raised them like their own, even if they weren’t.

    Especially if they weren’t.

    Billy’s voice echoed in her memory—persistent, eager, full of questions. Her stray.

    And Nicky.

    Nicky who she would not dare to claim. The prince who carried Agatha’s sharp smile and too weary eyes, a boy whose existence had changed something inside her the moment she learned of him.

    She drove the axe down again. The wood split with a satisfying crack.

    She wasn’t sure what finally made her speak.

    “Parents,” she said slowly, “do not make for very good knights.”

    Leyna tilted her head. “Because of the danger?”

    Rio shook her head. “Because if I were a parent, any other duty would be… secondary.

    Always.”

    Leyna didn’t answer right away. But something in her eyes shifted—something soft, and a little sad.

    “You say that like it’s a flaw.”

    Rio shrugged, wiping sweat from her brow with the back of her hand. “In my profession, it is.”

    But even as she said it, something deep inside her pushed back - fiercely.

    She didn’t know whether she was mourning something she never had or something she still might. And that uncertainty gnawed at her in a way no impending battlefield ever had.

    In the periphery of her vision, Agatha shifted like she’d been struck.

    Then she turned away from the door and disappeared into the house, as if the words had opened a wound too raw to name.

    She didn’t know what expression had crossed Agatha’s face, but she recognized what lived behind it because she felt it too: an ache. A longing that crept in when no one was looking. The kind a person didn’t know they were allowed to feel until they saw it alive in someone else.

    Rio braced another log.

    She swung.

    The axe hit true.

    Again.

    Always again.

    By the time the sun dipped fully behind the hills, the cottage had absorbed its rays, the cheery fire in the hearth providing a suitable afterglow. The scent of dinner still lingered, mingling with smoke and the herbs strung above the door to reinforce the warm, cozy atmosphere.

    Rio stacked the last of the firewood under the eaves and rolled her sleeves back down, the bite of evening wind just enough to stir the sweat on her neck. Pushing the worn door open slightly, Rio observed the scene inside. The child had fallen asleep in a nest of blankets in the corner, cradling a hand-stitched doll tight to her chest. Leyna moved quietly around the room with a mother’s precision, never rushing, never wasting a step.

    Agatha sat near the hearth, hair still half-damp and curling in unruly waves as she tried to twist it into a single braid. She wasn't especially skilled and kept muttering under her breath at lost strands, but she kept trying anyway, expression mulish and stubborn.

    Rio swallowed hard.

    There was something about seeing her like this - dressed not in silks or fine things but in simple, borrowed clothes. Silhouetted by firelight, her hair frizzing from the steam and cheeks flushed from the fire’s proximity, knees tucked beneath her, Agatha glowed.

    Rio wanted to say something. It would be far too easy to part her lips and allow something true to tumble out.

    Something she shouldn’t voice.

    Instead, she stepped inside quietly, brushing her shoulder against the low doorframe and taking a seat across from her. Agatha glanced up but didn’t comment on her arrival.

    The firelight flickered, and in that shift of light, Rio saw it again.

    The spiral.

    Faint, but unmistakable—glowing beneath the skin at the back of Agatha’s shoulder as she shifted to pin a wayward lock of hair behind her ear. It pulsed once. Like breath.

    Agatha didn’t notice.

    But Rio did and her hand moved before her thoughts could catch up. Half-lifted, not quite reaching, not quite daring.

    Agatha turned just then, catching her in the act.

    She said nothing. Didn’t flinch. Didn’t smile. But her gaze landed squarely on Rio’s fingers, suspended mid-air, and then climbed to meet her eyes.

    Agatha knew .

    She didn’t speak it, didn’t call it out, but the fire reflected in her eyes like a dare.

    Rio dropped her hand.

    It was too much. Too soon.

    Agatha turned back to her braid. Rio watched her lashes lower. Watched her breathe.

    A soft creak of wood made her glance toward the door.

    A charm hung there—woven from thin strips of bark, feathers, and stones. Leyna had mentioned it in passing, said it was to keep out sickness and ill omens. It had been still all night.

    But now… it was humming.

    A low, near-imperceptible sound, like a stringed instrument stretched. The feathers swung gently, though no breeze touched them.

    Agatha rose to rinse her plate in the basin—and as she walked past the charm, it glowed faintly violet.

    Just for a moment.

    Just long enough for Leyna to notice.

    She stood from where she’d leaned near the back wall, the baby still sleeping soundly in its woven sling. She watched Agatha’s retreating figure, then turned her gaze on Rio.

    “The woods remember their own,” she said quietly.

    Rio’s breath hitched.

    Outside, beyond the glow of the hearth and the reach of the light, the trees rustled in a way that felt like more than wind.

    The ladder creaked as Agatha climbed to the loft, the hem of her borrowed night tunic brushing her calves. Rio followed close behind, a careful hand under her elbow. The ladder was strong and there was no need to steady her. But just in case.

    The loft was simple with low beams and a mattress of thick wool blankets tucked near the far wall. Faded quilts and a few hanging herbs gave the space a lived-in scent—lavender, smoke, the faintest hint of citrus.

    Rio helped Agatha settle, then turned to descend.

    “Where are you going?” Agatha asked, voice low. She shifted onto her side, bracing herself on one elbow.

    Rio glanced up at her. “I’ll take watch. Sleep by the fire.”

    The idea of lying in the same bed, sharing breath and silence alike, was a temptation she wasn’t sure she’d weather. It wasn’t propriety that urged her back down the ladder; it was self-preservation.

    And it was something else entirely that forced her to linger.

    Agatha sat up straighter. “You don’t think it’s safe here?”

    “It’s safe.” Rio paused, then added, “You may wake with a spider or two for company, but they’re harmless.”

    Agatha gave her a flat, unimpressed look.

    Rio cracked half a smile, hoping that she’d be allowed to disengage.

    “Stay.”

    It wasn’t a command, and not quite a plea either.

    For a moment, Rio didn’t move. She stood on the edge of that choice, heart knocking against her ribs in an unfamiliar rhythm. Then, slowly, she nodded.

    “If it pleases you, I will.”

    She crossed the loft and lowered herself onto the blanket beside Agatha, careful to leave a respectful space between them.

    The fire crackled below them and the rafters groaned once before quieting.

    Agatha didn’t speak again. She rolled to face the wall, tucking her knees to her chest, curls spilling across the pillow behind her.

    Eventually, her breathing slowed.

    Rio waited until she was sure.

    If she moved, she might wake her. If she didn’t, she might never breathe again.

    She wasn’t sure which would be worse.

    Cautiously, she reached out. Her fingers brushed the back of Agatha’s shoulder, just over the place where the rune pulsed beneath her skin.

    Agatha sighed in her sleep and shifted.

    She rolled toward Rio without waking, her face nestling instinctively into the curve of Rio’s ribs, one hand curling just beside her sternum. She inhaled, deep and trusting, her breath warming the thin fabric between them.

    And then—barely audible, more breath than voice—Agatha murmured something against her shirt.

    “Stay.”

    The word was slurred, unconscious, unmeant for waking ears. But it landed square in Rio’s chest like a brand.

    Her throat tightened. She didn’t move. Didn’t exhale. Just watched the firelight dance across the ceiling beams and counted every slow second as if it might hold.

    Agatha drifted in and out of sleep, easy as breathing. And Rio counted every heartbeat she spent touching her.

    On that same night, in a distant castle, Billy dreamed of fishing.

    He and Rio sat side by side at the edge of a lazy river, boots kicked off, their poles resting in the crooks of their elbows. The sun was warm. The water was slow. In the dream, she smiled at him genuine and proud. Like he’d done something worthwhile. As if he was worthy.

    Then came the crack.

    Not of a fish biting the line, but of wood splintering.

    He jolted upright just as the door to his chambers slammed open, ricocheting off the wall with enough force to rattle the hinges.

    The hallway beyond was completely eclipsed. A wall of armored men filled the threshold, their torchlight casting jagged shadows against the floor.

    “What—” Billy didn’t get the rest out before they were on him.

    Rough hands tore back the covers. Another pair yanked him bodily from the mattress, one wrist twisted behind his back, the other gripping the front of his nightshirt hard enough to choke.

    “What’s going on? Where are you taking me?” His voice cracked.

    “Silence,” growled one of the guards.

    He stumbled as they hauled him into the hall. The marble floors were cold on his bare feet, and the breath was already gone from his lungs by the time they reached the first set of descending stairs.

    “Why—why are you—”

    A voice cut him off. Sharp, dry, cruel.

    “Shame we do not take the tongues from traitors anymore.”

    “They will after he talks.”

    Billy froze, but his hesitation earned him a hard shove. The next blow came quicker, a brutal knock to the back of his skull. The corridor tilted sideways.

    Then darkness took him.

    Chapter End Notes It's a good thing Agatha is sleeping well because no one else is...

    Heart's Pyre Chapter Summary Agatha is caught and cast in a performance. A marionette loses its strings.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes In the dark of the loft, Agatha dreamed of sunlight.

    Dappled and warm, it filtered through trees overhead, flickering across her skin as she lay back in the grass, one hand lazily combing through clover.

    Somewhere nearby, Nicky laughed so loudly it startled a pair of magpies into flight.

    She sat up to search for him and found him ankle-deep in a creek, chasing minnows with a long stick. There was no chance of him catching anything at all. It was just child’s play. His trousers were soaked to the knee, and his curls were plastered to his forehead, cheeks reddened both by excitement and the kiss of the sun. He was radiant.

    A basket lay beside her, half-filled with wild herbs and blackberries. A water skein lay nearby. Her hands were smudged with dirt. There was no crown upon her head. No guards.

    No weight on her shoulders.

    Just birdsong. Just grass. Just her son, happy and free.

    And then Rio stepped into the clearing.

    She looked like she belonged there—rolled sleeves, sun-warmed skin. A satchel was slung casually over her shoulder, a half-split bundle of firewood tucked under one arm. There was a smear of pollen on her jaw and a smile curling at the edges of her mouth that made Agatha’s breath catch in her chest.

    Nicky called to her.

    “Rio! Come see the fish!”

    Rio didn’t flinch. She laughed and set the wood down, crouching beside him with one hand steady on his back as she peered into the water.

    Agatha watched them from the hill, her heart aching in a way that threatened to spill over into hurt.

    “Watch close, little fox. If you hold your hand so still like this…”

    “They tickle!” He squealed.

    This wasn’t her life.

    It wasn’t real.

    But the part of her that had fallen asleep pressed against Rio’s side wanted to believe it could be.

    That she could live in a world where her son didn’t have to carry a title or ceremonial blade. That she could find refuge in a place where Rio could walk freely and be greeted by a child unaware of the meaning of exile or shame.

    Where she could love both of them without consequence.

    Rio looked up suddenly, eyes catching hers across the glade like she knew, like she’d felt Agatha’s gaze all along and had only pretended to be too preoccupied with Nicky to notice. And with the faintest smile, she beckoned, “Agatha, come down. The water’s warm.”

    Agatha woke to the scent of smoke and fresh bread, and to the sound of wood creaking below her, the murmur of quiet voices in the house beneath.

    For a moment, she didn’t open her eyes. She lay curled beneath the quilt, a thread of warmth still lingering along her spine where Rio had slept, just barely not-touching her all night. She could still feel the phantom weight of Nicky’s laugh in her chest, the softness in Rio’s voice. “Come down. The water’s warm.”

    She didn’t want to let it go.

    “Agatha.”

    The voice was low, not urgent, but firm enough to tug her gently to the surface.

    She blinked and turned her face toward the edge of the loft where Rio stood, half-lit by the morning light from the narrow window.

    “We need to leave soon,” Rio said, her tone softened by what might’ve been an apology.

    “Come down,” she said quietly. “The bread is still warm.”

    Beneath the shade of high boughs, the path to Fenhaven curved and cut through the shrub.

    Spinner’s webs bejeweled with dew swayed as they passed. Everywhere, the scent of pine and wet earth clung to the morning air.

    Rio led the way, reins loose in one hand, her other resting on the pommel of her sword.

    Agatha rode a few paces behind, the borrowed cloak Leyna had gifted her still draped around her shoulders, smelling faintly of the same lavender and smoke that filled the cottage.

    They hadn't spoken much since leaving the homestead.

    But Agatha kept replaying her dream: Nicky’s laugh, the way Rio had spoken to him with such easy affection, how when their eyes had met across the tall grass something in her chest had cracked open like ripe fruit.

    She wished she could live forever like that. Just a woman. Just a mother. Just someone Rio might choose, if things were different.

    But they weren’t.

    So instead, she cleared her throat and asked lightly, “Did you sleep well?”

    Rio looked over her shoulder with a crooked grin. “You snore, your Highness.”

    Agatha choked on her breath. “I do not.”

    “You do,” Rio said, turning back around. “Like a very dignified boar.”

    “That is slander. I could have you executed.”

    “I only speak the truth,” Rio replied, her tone all mocking solemnity. “The forest spirits themselves trembled.”

    Agatha urged her horse to catch up. “You were dreaming, surely.”

    “You kicked me in your sleep too, for the record. Right in the thigh. Pretty sure it bruised.” Agatha narrowed her eyes. “I’ll believe it when I see it.”

    Rio chuckled low in her throat, easing something in Agatha at the sound. She tried not to smile, but failed.

    The trail sloped downward, curving through low grasses and thickets of wildflowers, and then widened, the valley opening before them.

    Agatha slowed her horse to take it in.

    Below, the village spilled out in a burst of color. Every rooftop was draped with fluttering pennants and hand-painted banners, none bearing the seal of the Empire.

    Instead, symbols of flowers, birds, suns, and strange looping runes danced on the wind.

    Bright cloth streamed from windows and balconies. Even from this distance, Agatha could hear music, lilting and uneven, buoyed by the chorus of laughter and the soft clatter and rhythm of market stalls.

    Agatha smiled, “Well. I didn’t expect my mother to send me somewhere joy existed.”

    Beside her, Rio was quiet for a beat too long. “Me neither,” she said at last, though her tone held a wary edge.

    Agatha glanced sideways. Rio’s hand had settled on the hilt of her blade, a reminder that beauty could still be a trap.

    Still, Agatha reached for the lightness. She glanced down at her simple linen tunic and travel- worn trousers, far from her usual court finery, and was suddenly grateful she'd accepted Leyna’s gentle suggestion to leave the silks in the pack for the day.

    She leaned toward Rio, coaxing. “Before we announce ourselves, can we walk around the village?”

    Rio arched an eyebrow. “We’re expected.”

    “I know,” Agatha said, brushing an imaginary speck from her sleeve. “But come on, good Ser, just a little time. No titles, no obligations. Just two travelers on Festival day. I’ll even buy you a ribbon if you behave.”

    Rio gave her a long, considering look. “And if I don’t behave?”

    Agatha grinned. “Then I’ll buy you two.”

    The festival buzzed around them, drums pounding somewhere near the center square and streamers twining between wooden beams like vines reaching for the sun. Children darted past with sugar-stained mouths, every corner of the village seeming to hum with life.

    Rio walked half a pace behind Agatha, hand never straying far from the hilt of her sword. Her shoulders were tight, eyes scanning from cobblestone to balcony.

    Agatha moved like someone tasting sweetness for the first time. She drifted from stall to stall, fingers brushing bolts of dyed linen and delicate, wood-carved charms.

    Then came the blast.

    A sudden, thunderous crack ripped through the air.

    Agatha didn’t see what happened. One moment she was reaching for a ribbon braided in the soft pinks and purples of the dawn, and the next her back hit the cobblestone hard, breath punched from her lungs. The weight of a body pressed over hers, shielding her.

    Rio.

    Agatha blinked up, heart thudding, ears ringing. Around them, people screamed, but not in fear. The crowd erupted in laughter, applause, and whooping cheers. Multicolored sparks burst in the sky above the square: a firework. Another followed, trailing glittering gold into the blue.

    “I…” Agatha blinked again. “That was—”

    “Fireworks,” Rio muttered, frowning as she slowly lifted her head. Her voice was gravelly and cautious. “Of course.”

    Still, she didn’t move.

    Agatha became acutely aware of the press of Rio’s thigh between hers. Of the tight circle of her arms. The way Rio’s breath grazed her cheek with every exhale. Rio’s eyes flicked down and found hers.

    And stayed.

    “You’re heavy,” Agatha murmured, breathless and pink-cheeked, not trying very hard to sound delighted.

    Rio scrambled up like she’d touched fire, muttering an apology as she extended a hand.

    Agatha took it, rising slower than she needed to, lingering in the moment where their fingers met.

    The crowd was thick now, pressing from all sides as the next wave of festivities began to parade through the street, musicians, dancers, and something tall and masked swaying at the center. A banner unfurled ahead, painted in a strange looping script she couldn’t quite read. Agatha leaned close, her lips near Rio’s ear. “Don’t let go,” she said softly. “Keep a hand on me. We might get separated.”

    Rio’s hand found the small of her back at once.

    “As you wish, your Highness,” she said.

    The music began as a low thrum, drums beating a rhythm that echoed through the ribcage. A bell chimed in time. Then came the shrill song of flutes, a whistle like birdsong.

    The crowd stirred with anticipation.

    Agatha turned her head toward the sound just as the first puppet emerged from the narrow street ahead.

    It was enormous—seven feet tall at least, held aloft by four cloaked puppeteers who moved so fluidly it was hard to notice them in the shadow of the beast.

    Its body was a patchwork of painted bark and pale linen. Twigs sprouted from its fingertips, wildflowers trailing from its joints. Its carved face was serene, genderless, crowned with a tangle of antlers and ivy. A spirit of the forest, Agatha guessed, though it didn’t match any imperial sigil she’d ever seen.

    More followed in procession.

    A great crane creature with wings like stained glass, casting jeweled colors down onto the crowd.

    A faceless nude woman with flowing red hair that wrapped around her body like a gown, her painted hands cradling a bundle of fabric shaped like a child.

    A magnificent lumbering bear with mirrored eyes which caught the sun and flared it back toward the crowd in blinding sparks.

    Each puppet was accompanied by a small group of dancers and musicians moving with each new song.

    None of it bore the hallmarks of the Empire. No gold leaf. No martial pageantry. These weren’t state-sanctioned symbols. They were older. Wilder.

    Agatha was captivated.

    She moved instinctively closer, weaving through the crowd with Rio shadowing her like a second heartbeat.

    The final puppet turned the corner, and the crowd inhaled as one.

    The dragon was magnificent.

    It spanned the entire width of the street, nearly ten feet tall and easily thirty feet long from painted snout to rippling tail. Its body was a mosaic of sun-faded silks in reds, coppers, and greens, stitched together in overlapping scales.

    Its limbs, controlled by puppeteers through intricate pole riggings, moved with surprising grace. Its head bobbed and twisted with lifelike menace, nostrils flaring and golden eyes fixed forward.

    It moved like it remembered being real.

    And held delicately in its open jaws, suspended by fine chains, was a much smaller puppet— a queen.

    Not just any queen.

    Agatha gasped and then laughed, a short, delighted burst. The puppet was a caricature of her mother. Sharp-cheeked, crowned, dressed in imperial violet. Her painted mouth was set in an unmistakable sneer.

    From this angle, it looked like the dragon was deciding whether to devour her or simply carry her off as tribute.

    “Well,” Agatha said, eyes alight with mischief as she turned to Rio. “This is a good show. You bring me to the nicest places, Ser.”

    Rio didn’t laugh.

    Her hand, which had drifted from Agatha’s back, returned - a firm pressure just above her hip now. Not intimate. Anchoring.

    Rio’s eyes were locked on the dragon. Her whole body had gone still, the coiled silence of a predator scenting something wrong. Her jaw clenched tight; the tendon in her neck twitched once, then twice.

    “Come on,” Agatha said softly, teasing, trying to lighten the air. “I never took you for a loyalist.”

    Rio didn’t smile. “That puppet is… a message.”

    Agatha’s gaze slid back to the queen puppet, which now bobbed gently between the dragon’s jaws as the creature coiled sinuously around a street corner. The music had shifted to something dissonant now—still rhythmic, still celebratory, but with a strange undercurrent that prickled the hairs at the back of her neck.

    “It’s just a joke,” Agatha said, though less certainly this time. “Mockery, not message.” Rio said nothing.

    The puppet queen’s painted face turned slightly as it passed, and for a heartbeat, Agatha could’ve sworn it looked straight at her. Eyes dark. Expression unchanged.

    A child ran past, shrieking with laughter. A vendor nearby handed out honey cakes. The crowd surged forward again, delighted and dazzled.

    But something in Agatha had shifted.

    She looked again at Rio - at the line of tension in her shoulders, at the way her fingers had flexed as if preparing for a fight - and knew that she wasn’t just reacting to a puppet.

    She was reacting to what it meant. Or what it might precede.

    The dragon disappeared around the bend, its long tail flicking silk ribbons in its wake and the music faded to a distant hum, replaced by the thrum of blood in her ears.

    Agatha ducked into the alley first, pulling Rio by the hand behind her.

    The passage was narrow and half-shadowed, lined with stone and brick still warm from the day’s sun. Flowering vines spilled from a windowsill overhead, brushing Agatha’s shoulder as she leaned against the wall.

    “That dragon was magnificent,” she said, flushed with delight. “And did you see her face? That painted scowl? I swear, if someone had carved her out of marble it wouldn't have captured her half as well.”

    Rio still didn’t smile.

    Her eyes were scanning up toward the rooftops, down to the alley’s far exit, back to the entrance they’d just come through.

    “Rio,” Agatha said, tilting her head. “You can’t possibly think someone’s going to ambush us here. Please, just be here with me. One more moment.”

    Rio didn’t answer. She was too focused, too taut.

    Letting her body lean fully into the brick behind her, Agatha huffed as she crossed her arms. “You promised me a little fun.”

    Rio’s attention snapped back to her at that. “Your Highness, I don’t mean to cut our day short, but perhaps, your business here-”

    “Tell me something, Ser. Have you ever kissed a village girl in an alley before?”

    There was no response.

    Agatha took a step closer, letting her voice soften and slide into a low purr. “I’d wager a full satchel of imperial coin you have.”

    Still no answer beyond the tightening of the taut line of Rio’s jaw.

    Agatha stepped forward again, until it was Rio pressed back against the wall, chin tilted with defiance and challenge. She let her fingers trail down the front of her tunic like an afterthought, as if brushing away dust.

    “You’ve been acting like I’m going to break,” Agatha said, barely above a whisper now.

    “Like the world’s going to end if you stop looking over your shoulder for one minute and turn your eyes on me.”

    “It might,” Rio muttered, gaze dropping to Agatha’s mouth for just one breath too long.

    Agatha grinned. “So you have kissed a village girl in an alley.”

    Rio’s voice came rough. “She didn’t look like you.”

    A beat passed. The heat between them coiled and snapped.

    Agatha reached out, fingers grazing the edge of Rio’s belt, tugging her forward just enough. “Well, lucky me,” she whispered.

    Rio leaned in, slow and agonized, her hand braced against the wall just beside Agatha’s head. For a moment, Agatha thought she might finally close the distance.

    But Rio stopped, their noses nearly brushing.

    “I can’t,” she said, breath ghosting against Agatha’s lips. “Not here. Not now.”

    Agatha’s voice was barely audible. “Because of your duty?”

    “Because of you.”

    Rio’s gaze dropped to Agatha’s mouth, then rose again, aching and unguarded. Agatha had never seen brown eyes look so golden.

    “The moment I stop being careful,” Rio murmured, “I will lose myself to you.”

    She leaned in closer until her breath warmed Agatha’s cheek, her forehead nearly resting against hers.

    “Would that be so terrible?” Agatha whispered, her voice trembling with the weight of hope and heat and something deeper she dared not name.

    Rio’s eyes fluttered shut for a moment, her hand rising instinctively toward Agatha’s jaw but stopping short, suspended a breath away.

    “No. It would not be terrible at all… It would be the greatest thing to happen in my life.” Her thumb hovered like it might brush her cheek, her lips parting like she might say something else, but no words came.

    One breath more and they would’ve touched. One heartbeat more and they would’ve fallen.

    Then, from somewhere in the distance, a bell tolled.

    A heavy, echoing sound that rolled through the alley like a summons.

    Rio’s breath caught. She pulled back—not abruptly, not harshly, but as if it cost her something.

    Agatha’s hand lingered on the wall behind her, as though steadying herself.

    Rio looked at her once more, eyes full of everything she hadn’t said, and then turned toward the sound of the bell with her shoulders squared and her body suddenly a soldier’s again. “We need to move. Something’s wrong.”

    Agatha nodded, though her legs felt boneless. Her pulse was still caught somewhere between her ribs and Rio’s lips.

    The bell tolled again. An alarm.

    They slipped out of the alley and into the cobbled main road, where festival-goers were already murmuring in uncertain waves. The music had stopped entirely now. What had once felt celebratory now trembled with something else: curiosity edged in fear.

    People were moving toward the square, drawn like moths.

    Rio stayed close to Agatha’s side, one hand ready at her hip, the other brushing the small of Agatha’s back every few steps.

    It was a short walk, but the air seemed to change with every stride. Thicker. Tighter. Like stepping into a snare one could feel, but not yet see.

    They were just approaching the edge of the crowd when a figure stepped directly into their path.

    “Ah,” said a voice as smooth as polished stone, “there you are.”

    Agatha stopped cold.

    Vizier Calmet stood before them, regal and unhurried, as though they’d simply stumbled into a garden party. He wore imperial violet with fine silver embroidery, his circlet gleaming dully beneath the fading light.

    “Princess Agatha,” he said, folding his hands together. “How... delightful to find you among the common revelry.”

    Agatha’s mouth went dry.

    Behind her, she felt Rio shift—imperceptible to most, but Agatha felt it like a chord pulled taut.

    “Vizier,” Agatha said, tone cool. “I wasn’t aware my mother had begun assigning court dignitaries to monitor puppet shows.”

    His smile didn’t reach his eyes - his teeth bared in more of a snarl. “Oh, Her Majesty does so enjoy theater.”

    He took a single, pointed step closer. “And she felt that you might benefit from seeing a story play out with your own eyes. One in which the line between loyalty and treason becomes very... personal.”

    Agatha’s stomach turned.

    “Come,” Calmet said, gesturing toward the square behind him. “It’s time for the finale.”

    They followed.

    Gone was the laughter, the festival color. The banners still fluttered, but here they hung more stiffly, shadows cast long by the midday sun. A ring of imperial soldiers stood at attention in polished armor, their expressions blank as stone.

    At the center of the square stood a wooden post.

    Tied to it was a woman with wild, gray-streaked hair. Her hands were bound tightly behind her back, wrists already raw. Strips of her festival costume clung to her in rags, clearly torn from struggling. Paint stained her sleeves. Her chin was lifted in defiance, even as a bruise bloomed along her cheekbone.

    Her eyes locked on Agatha the moment she stepped into view.

    And she spat at her feet.

    Agatha flinched. Not from the spit, but from the fury in the woman’s eyes—a fury that made perfect sense. It was a look she had worn herself many times.

    She couldn’t blame her.

    Calmet stepped ahead with courtly grace, voice smooth as lacquer. “My lady,” he said with a shallow bow, “you honor us with your presence. Her Majesty sends her warmest regards and is quite… eager to see how you conduct yourself.”

    The torches were already lit.

    Four soldiers stood ready.

    But only one torch was offered.

    To her.

    Agatha stared at it.

    The flame danced in the breeze like a living thing, gold and hungry. It cast a flickering light onto her hands, as if tasting the idea of her touch.

    “She’s a traitor,” Calmet said pleasantly, as if discussing the weather. “She mocked the Crown. Displayed a grotesque likeness in the jaws of a dragon—hardly subtle, even for Wilds-born dramatics.”

    He turned the torch slowly, so that the flame leaned toward her.

    “The Queen, in her mercy, offers you this opportunity to deliver judgment. A public act of strength.”

    Agatha’s throat tightened. Her heart pounded with something more profound than panic.

    Rage. Shame. Recognition.

    The crowd said nothing, but their silence was thunderous. Some villagers looked away. Some stared. A child wept quietly behind a market stall. A dog barked once, then stopped, as though even it sensed something had shifted.

    Calmet’s voice curled in the air like incense. “Well?”

    He offered the torch again.

    Agatha didn’t move.

    Behind her, she felt Rio shift.

    A subtle adjustment in stance. One step closer. Her body not touching, but present. Agatha didn’t look at her. She didn’t dare.

    The woman on the post was breathing heavily now, watching with narrowed eyes. Waiting to see what kind of monster Agatha might be. Or what kind of coward.

    The flame crackled softly.

    Agatha felt the rune, hidden beneath her collar, burn as if the magic within her could sense the weight of the moment. And was waiting, too.

    The torch crackled, its flame snapping in the breeze, casting stray sparks and embers across the stone square.

    Agatha’s world had narrowed to this one object, this gold-tipped brand of power, of complicity, of her mother’s cruelty. Vizier Calmet held it like a scepter, offering it to her. Then Rio stepped forward.

    Silent. Controlled. Her expression unreadable, but her movements unmistakably protective. She didn’t speak. She simply raised her arm, hand open, palm steady, placing herself between Agatha and the fire.

    Agatha froze.

    The realization struck like lightning: Rio was offering to do it for her.

    To bear the stain. To shoulder the shame. For her .

    A soldier lunged.

    Steel-armored, sharp and sudden, he seized Rio by the shoulder and slammed her down. Her knees hit the flagstone with a brutal crack, her sword still sheathed, her head forced low. A gauntlet twisted her arm up and behind until her breath hissed through her teeth.

    Agatha’s body reacted before her mind could catch up.

    A scream tore out of her throat, not born of fear but of fury.

    Her hand shot forward, snatching the torch from Calmet’s grasp. The flame roared higher, catching a gust of wind, as if it too had been waiting for violence.

    Calmet turned just in time to see her coming.

    With a cry, Agatha swung the torch in a wide, arcing strike and slammed the burning end into his face.

    There was a wet crack of bone against wood and then the hiss and shriek of fire meeting flesh.

    Calmet screamed the raw, animal howl of a man who had never once imagined he too could bleed.

    He dropped to the ground, clutching his scorched face. His circlet skidded across the stone, bouncing once before landing in a pool of muck.

    For a heartbeat, the world held its breath.

    Then the square erupted.

    Guards surged forward like wolves scenting blood, blades drawn, armor clanging, shouts echoing off stone. Villagers screamed. Some ran. Others were frozen in place. A market cart overturned. A trail of fruit spilled across the cobblestones.

    Hands grabbed for Agatha, too many, too strong.

    She twisted, broke free, but others came. She ducked and staggered back, the broken half of the torch still burning in her grip.

    And then Rio.

    She was back up, blade drawn quicker than a breath. She drove her elbow into the soldier behind her, spun, and sliced clean through the strap of his breastplate. He fell, gasping. Another rushed her—she turned and caught his blade on her own, sparks flaring.

    Agatha had seen her fight before in the tournament.

    She’d been like a dancer then. Nothing like this.

    There was no precision now. No restraint.

    Just fire and fury.

    She moved like a feral creature, and Agatha caught sight of a bright green glow pulsing faintly beneath her torn tunic as she cut a path through the chaos.

    A soldier lunged at Agatha from behind. She turned, too slow, and two more grabbed her arms, pinning her.

    Her back hit the wall hard. A gauntlet clamped around her throat.

    Her vision blurred.

    Panic, hot and sharp, rose in her throat.

    No. She would not be bound.

    She screamed, and the world flinched at the sound.

    A blast of violet magic surged from her palms, searing through the air with a deafening crack. The guards were thrown back like rag dolls, bodies slamming against the cobblestones and skidding. One hit a pillar and didn’t rise. Another twitched once before falling still. The third tried to stand, but his armor smoked where it touched his skin, and his last service to the crown was to fall and writhe at the princess’s feet.

    Agatha stood in the center of it, her hands trembling, steam curling off her fingertips. Her chest rose and fell in sharp, frantic bursts.

    She felt the prickle race across her body like thousands of needles, the mark on her back shifting, unfurling like a serpent waking from sleep. It coiled across her shoulder and up her neck. It pulsed with each beat of her heart.

    Gasps rose from the crowd.

    Rio turned, eyes wide, not with fear, but with the reverence of someone looking upon something ancient and holy.

    Agatha didn’t notice.

    She turned and ran through fire and smoke, toward the stake.

    Toward the woman still bound, still defiant, still alive.

    The woman’s eyes widened as Agatha reached her.

    “You’ve done nothing wrong,” Agatha panted, reaching for the ropes.

    A soldier raised his sword to stop her, and was stopped in turn by Rio’s blade passing clean through his throat.

    Together, they tore the bonds loose. The woman sagged, legs buckling, but Rio caught her. Rio lowered her gently to the stones, one arm still braced around her ribs. Agatha knelt beside them, touching the woman's cheek, brushing soot from her brow. The world was still chaos—shouts, steel, smoke, screams—but for one fragile heartbeat, it was just the three of them in the eye of the storm.

    Agatha’s violet-lit rune still shimmered across her throat and shoulder like something alive. Her chest heaved, eyes burning—not just from smoke, but also. fFrom the enormity of what she’d just done. From everything that had come unleashed.

    Rio’s gaze held hers.

    And in it, there was no caution. No fear. Only awe.

    “Get her out,” Rio said, voice low, urgent, commanding.

    “What? Rio-”

    “Get her to safety.”

    A soldier charged at them. Rio turned without hesitation and met him head-on, sword rising. She struck once, clean. The man collapsed in a sprawl of blood and armor.

    Around them, the villagers had begun to fight back.

    It started with one boy throwing a rock.

    Then an old man wielding a pitchfork.

    Then two more, then five - then dozens of people poured out of homes and shops with farming tools, broken chairs, whatever they could find. A woman with a scarred face smashed a guard’s helm with a hammer. A child stabbed at a boot with a sharpened bone.

    Rio gave Agatha one last look and joined the fray.

    Her movements were sharp, decisive. Not beautiful, not elegant but effective. Her hair whipped as she turned, her body soaked with sweat.

    Agatha stood frozen for a half-second, unable to move, unable to look away.

    Then the woman at her side whimpered in pain, slumping against her, and Agatha snapped back into herself.

    She hauled the woman upright and slipped an arm around her back.

    “I’ve got you,” she whispered. “Come on. Come on.”

    She turned away from the fight, forcing herself not to look back. She could feel her rune still pulsing with magic under her skin, still stretching and shifting, like it wanted to turn her back into the fire.

    But she didn’t.

    She pushed through the crowd, shoulder-first, dodging sparks and elbows.

    Smoke stung her eyes.

    Someone shoved past her, bleeding and shouting.

    A flash of red. A scream.

    A hand reaching out and disappearing again.

    She made it to the edge of the square.

    Agatha turned, just once, to look back.

    For a moment, framed in smoke and fire and glory, Rio was still standing. Still fighting. Still brilliant.

    Their eyes met, and Rio smiled.

    Then someone ran between them.

    And when Agatha looked again, Rio was gone.

    Swallowed by the chaos.

    Agatha didn’t stop running until the woman was safe, taken in by a pair of villagers who ushered her into a house still untouched by fire. Agatha barely heard their reassurances. She turned before they’d even finished speaking.

    Her boots struck the dirt hard, skidding as she rounded a corner back toward the square.

    Smoke still hung low over the stones, coiling like fingers too stubborn to release what had been claimed.

    The battle was over.

    The ringing of steel was gone now, replaced by a different kind of sound: low murmurs, soft cries, the dull scrape of bodies being moved. Villagers moved slowly through the aftermath. Some carried the wounded. Others knelt beside the dead. A woman wailed into her apron. A child sat quietly beside a broken cart, blood drying on his cheek.

    Agatha’s chest ached.

    She pushed through them, eyes scanning every face.

    “Rio?” she called. “Rio! ”

    No answer.

    Her voice cracked.

    She stumbled past a pair of villagers tending to a man with a bandaged eye, then over a splintered table that had been used as a barricade. She turned in circles. Her throat burned. “Rio!”

    Then she saw her.

    Slumped against the stone lip of a fountain at the far edge of the square, legs sprawled, one arm limp at her side, the other braced against the rim. Her head tilted back, her eyes half- lidded. She looked like she was dozing.

    Agatha’s heart nearly stopped.

    She ran.

    She dropped to her knees beside her, hands reaching instinctively to cup her face. “Rio, gods, I’m here, are you hurt?”

    Rio blinked slowly as her eyes focused on Agatha.

    And then she leaned in.

    No hesitation. No clever remark.

    She simply pressed her lips to Agatha’s, soft and searching and so full of feeling it knocked what little breath she had regained from her lungs.

    Agatha stilled.

    Then kissed her back.

    For one beautiful moment, there was only that. That impossible heat between them, no longer withheld. The taste of ash and iron, the tremble of Rio’s fingers in her hair.

    And then Rio pulled back.

    Her mouth parted, as if to say something. Something quiet and tender, maybe even something foolish.

    But what came out instead was a wet, ragged cough.

    Blood sprayed between them.

    It splattered onto Agatha’s cheek, warm and thick.

    She froze.

    Rio’s head bobbed twice, and that’s when Agatha saw the dark bloom spreading across her side. The torn fabric of her tunic soaked through, the red trailing down from her ribs and pooling at her hip.

    “Oh no. No, no, no—” Agatha’s hands moved automatically, pressing against the wound, trying to stop the flow, trying to do something.

    Rio gave a choked laugh. “Sorry, your Highness, I did not wish to ruin the moment,” she whispered.

    Then her body went slack.

    And Agatha, blood on her hands and fire still smoking behind her, let out a cry that echoed through the square like the toll of a bell.

    She gathered Rio into her arms, heedless of the blood soaking into her own clothes. Her hands were useless, slippery with red, trembling.

    “Stay with me,” she whispered, over and over, her voice cracking. “Please, Rio. Please.”

    But Rio didn’t answer.

    Not once in her life had Agatha been allowed to hold what she loved without the stain of her mother’s cruelty seeping in.

    Somewhere, a bell rang not in celebration this time, but in mourning.

    As the sound echoed outward, Agatha held her knight in the center of it all, her rune still glowing faintly beneath the blood, her breath caught between a scream and a sob.

    Chapter End Notes Happy to be back in this realm after all the fun of AAA week! Promise not to leave you here for long.

    Bleed and Bloom Chapter Summary A memory turned nightmare. Truth and tethers.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes Rio was twelve years old, and everything ached.

    Her legs cramped from crouching too long. Her eyes burned from being forced open, staring into the dark for hours.

    They were keeping watch near the border. Looking out into the pitch black and praying not to catch something staring back. A very human task.

    Spirits didn’t need to see things to be certain they were there.

    But Rio was not a spirit. She was a girl.

    And the longer she looked, the more certain she felt it — something else was too.

    Somewhere out there, beneath the hush of the grass and the low cast stars, beneath the patient weight of night, something watched.

    The bow across her lap felt heavier with every hour. The string pressed into her fingers, numbing them. Rio wondered if she would even have the strength to pull it when the moment came - if it came.

    A voice beside her, low and steady, broke the spiral.

    “Most folk,” her mentor said, not looking away from their own vigil, “think they’re afraid of the dark.”

    Rio glanced over, mouth dry. “They’re not?”

    Her mentor smiled faintly, eyes catching the starlight for a moment. “They’re not. No one is afraid of the dark, not really. They’re afraid of what waits inside it; afraid of what will move through it when they can’t see.”

    Rio swallowed and nodded.

    Her mentor’s gaze returned to the grass, endless and black. “And the longer you watch, the more your mind starts filling the dark in for you.”

    The wind picked up then, just for a moment, and Rio shivered.

    Without a word, her mentor shifted beside her, unfastened their dark cloak, and draped it over Rio’s shoulders. The wool was rough and smelled faintly of smoke and pine.

    Rio stiffened. “I’m not a child afraid of the dark,” she muttered.

    Her mentor’s smile spread wider.

    “I know,” they said.

    Their hand rested briefly on her shoulder, solid and warm. “But if you don’t want to get cold so easily, you’ll need to pick up some weight and muscle.”

    Rio’s cheeks burned, but she stayed still beneath the cloak.

    The wind faded again, leaving them in the hush of the grass, the hush of the waiting.

    “So,” they said softly, “the trick is to breathe. Not to fight the dark. Just to keep your place inside it.”

    Rio tried. Gods, she tried. But her heart thudded louder with each breath, as though the dark might hear it and come closer. Her muscles cramped and twitched with exhaustion and dread. And still, she could feel it out there: the weight of unseen eyes; the hum of something older than her bones, older than fear itself.

    Waiting.

    The stars above blurred.

    Minutes passed - or hours. She couldn’t tell anymore.

    At some point, her voice slipped out in a whisper. “Why do we wait here?”

    She hated how thin it sounded, like a child’s question. But her mentor only glanced down with a tired kind of kindness.

    “Because we’re near the end of it.”

    “The war?” Rio asked.

    Her mentor shifted, adjusting the worn leather strap at their shoulder, gaze fixed outward as though seeing more than field and grass.

    Rio looked too, but only saw the same expanse of nothing.

    “They say,” they started, voice soft, “that the Queen is carrying a child. A daughter.”

    Rio blinked. “Then she’ll be stronger.”

    “No.” A slight shake of the head. “Weaker.”

    They looked down at Rio fully now, eyes dark as the sky.

    “There was an oracle, a woman the Queen couldn’t burn fast enough to stop the words from spreading.” A beat. “When the child is born, the Queen’s shadow will begin to end. The Wilds will awaken.”

    Rio’s skin prickled with a question she wasn’t sure how to ask.

    ”This princess, she matters that much?”

    ”More than any sword or crown.”

    Her mentor smiled at her again patiently, steady and sure. It was the sort of expression soldiers would fall for in battle, and lovers would chase to their doom, all because it made the dark feel a little brighter.

    The kind Rio would do anything for. So here she listened.

    “You think that sounds like a story. But stories,” her mentor said, turning their gaze back to the black horizon, “are what end queens.”

    The field breathed in the silence between them. The dark seemed to lean closer again.

    And for the first time, Rio understood that her place in this war was not just to wait. It was to endure the waiting, to hold the breath of this moment until the world tipped and let it out. Then the dawn would come. They would walk in the light. The sun would rise, and the Wilds would grow to reach it.

    She didn’t know this child’s name, didn’t know when or how the collapse of the Empire would happen.

    Only that the Queen would fall, and the dark they sat in would not last forever.

    Because her mentor said this was true. And Rio believed them.

    Her mentor reached for the canteens, rising with a quiet grunt. “Stay. Don’t move.”

    Rio watched them go, their figure swallowed by grass and night.

    And then…

    A sound.

    Not her mentor’s voice. Not her own. Not human.

    The snap of a bowstring.

    Then another.

    A scream after the thud of impact.

    It tore through the stillness, through Rio’s chest, through bone and breath alike.

    She was running before she knew it.

    The blind in which they sat, the bow she held, the lesson: all forgotten.

    She raced through the brush, but her thoughts were quicker.

    Not them. Not now.

    Not alone. Not in the dark.

    Reeds slapped her arms. A crow wheeled above, cawing once before vanishing behind a black cloud.

    She cut a fast path through the glen, rounding to the riverbank.

    Suddenly, she saw a body, her brain refusing to process it for a moment.

    They lay crumpled at the stream’s edge, limbs twisted, tunic soaked dark, canteens still clutched in one hand. Arrows jutted from their back like broken branches.

    Rio’s feet stumbled to a halt.

    Her breath caught.

    She nearly choked out their name, but it crumbled like ash placed on her tongue.

    Because when she looked again, it wasn’t her mentor lying there.

    It was Agatha.

    Her hair spilled like dark ink across the moss. Her skin was too pale, blood soaking through the front of her dress in an impossibly vivid bloom of red. One hand lay curled against her chest, her rune now stretched to her fingertips and faintly glowing even in death.

    “No,” Rio whispered, dropping to her knees. “No, no this isn’t—”

    She reached for her, and Agatha’s eyes snapped open.

    But they weren’t her eyes.

    Until Agatha, Rio had never met anyone with blue eyes.

    Not in the Wilds. Not in the camps. Not under the bone-gray skies of battlefields.

    Blue eyes belonged to myth. It was said the force of tides, and stream, and sky could be caught in their glance if the gods so willed it.

    Now, though, those eyes - Agatha’s eyes - weren’t blue at all.

    They burned violet, deep as twilight, as if the sea and sky had bled out and left behind only the heart of the magic itself.

    “I will not be another name fate steals from your heart. And you will not be left loving a shadow of me.”

    The voice didn’t come from her lips; instead, it came from everywhere.

    “Because if I lose you to the heavens, I will tear the stars down to bring you back.”

    Air ripped from her lungs, Rio fell backwards, the world collapsing around her in streaks of violent violet light.

    The smell of smoke and moss. A blanket that scratched against her skin. The sharp, metallic tang of dried blood at the back of her throat. A hand curled gently around hers.

    Agatha.

    She was seated beside her, hair clinging to her sweat-slick face, lips moving in a low, steady murmur.

    Her throat burned, and her body felt heavy and wrong.

    Then, with effort that sent a ripple of pain through her chest, she forced her throat to shape words.

    “You stayed,” Rio rasped.

    Agatha’s head jerked up. Her face was drawn with lines from exhaustion, but Rio saw her hope flickering behind her grief.

    “Rio,” she breathed. “Of course I stayed. Don’t you dare… don’t you dare go where I can’t follow. Please.”

    Rio tried for a smile. “I did not intend to, Princess.”

    Agatha reached out, brushing a stray lock of hair from her cheek. The touch nearly undid her. “You almost—” Agatha’s voice broke, and she swallowed roughly to cover it. “Don’t do that again.”

    Rio wanted to promise she never would, but her body disagreed.

    Pain lanced through her side as she shifted.

    Sickly green light flared beneath the bandages wrapped around her torso in lieu of a tunic. Rio tried to speak, but as the breath caught in her throat, something else caught too.

    A sharp sting. A tearing warmth.

    Small, pale thorns burst from her wound, coiling through the cloth. She gasped, eyes wide in panic.

    Agatha’s breath caught. “It’s alright,” she whispered, though her eyes betrayed her soothing tone. “Just… don’t move.”

    Her hands trembled as she reached for the cloth. The bandages were already dark with blood, but the dark color was now broken up by thin green tendrils snaking through the weave.

    “I need to see the wound,” Agatha murmured. “I can’t let you keep hurting. Not for me. Not because of me.”

    Rio could barely nod. Her throat burned too badly for words.

    Agatha hesitated for a heartbeat, then began to unbind her.

    The bandages were stiff, clinging to the wound and to Rio’s sweat-damp skin. As Agatha peeled them back, her breath came slower, steadier, fingers careful and deliberate.

    The fabric unwrapped across Rio’s ribs, then higher.

    Rio flinched as she realized Agatha’s hands had reached the top of the dressing, and now the bandage would have to be unwound entirely.

    Across her chest.

    Her skin burned hotter than any fever.

    She opened her mouth to protest? Deflect? She wasn’t sure.

    Agatha’s gaze flicked up to hers, searching.

    “You’re not worried about modesty now, are you?” she asked, voice gentle, lips tugging toward a smile that didn’t quite reach her eyes. “You’ve seen me more vulnerable, surely. Or have you forgotten?”

    Rio exhaled a rough, huffing breath. “I am too tired to be vain.”

    Agatha’s smile deepened, a hint of real warmth breaking through. “Good.”

    She unwound the rest of the cloth in slow, careful motions, the last strip falling away.

    And then they both saw it.

    The vine.

    It coiled across the plane of Rio’s chest, creeping over her collarbone, etched in deep green light. It moved now, slow, pulsing, as though drawing breath from the air around it.

    The old lines of the rune had always been faint, dormant against her collar. But now it swirled, tendrils unfurling in intricate spirals that seemed to reach toward her wounded side. They both stared at it.

    “I thought you might,” Agatha whispered, eyes wide and reverent. “I suspected. But I wasn’t sure. Not until now… It’s awake now.”

    Her gaze flicked up to Rio’s face, uncertain. “I don’t know whether that was my doing or yours.”

    Rio tried to process the words, but her thoughts tangled like the vine now blooming beneath her skin.

    Before she could speak, Agatha drew in a sharp breath.

    “Your side. We need to do something about that.”

    Rio followed her gaze.

    The thorns were growing.

    Pale green, almost translucent, they twisted out of her wounded side, curling along the skin as if trying to root there, an invasive growth taking her body as its host.

    Rio stiffened, panic starting to claw at her throat, and Agatha caught the shift.

    Her voice stayed soft, steady. “Rio. May I touch you? I need to try to remove them.”

    Sweat beaded at her brow and a drop spilled down her face as Rio nodded. “Do it.”

    Agatha’s eyes flicked to hers again: offering, not demanding. “You can hold my hand, if you would like?”

    For a heartbeat, Rio hesitated.

    Then she reached out, fingers trembling, and took Agatha’s hand. It was warm, stronger than it looked, and Rio gripped it harder than she meant to.

    Agatha’s other hand hovered over the nearest thorn. “I’ll be gentle.”

    She took hold of it carefully between two fingers and gave the faintest pull.

    Pain.

    Not like a blade. Not like the crack of bone or the sear of fire—those she knew. Those sensations had edges she could grit her teeth against. Experience had taught her how to breathe through pain and survive it.

    This was raw and alive. It sang through her nerves like a thousand needles blooming beneath her skin, writhing deeper with every breath.

    The thorn burrowed more deeply into her side.

    Rio gasped, a ragged, broken sound that tore from her throat before she could stop it. The increasing force of her grip on Agatha’s hand whitened both their knuckles.

    “Stop!” she managed, voice raw with panic.

    Agatha froze. “I’m sorry,” she whispered. Her breath was shaking now. “I’m sorry.”

    She pressed her palm flat against Rio’s chest, against the swirling vine of her rune, as if to ground her. To soothe.

    Magic flared, a pulse of violet through green, inducing a shiver beneath Rio’s skin.

    Both women stilled.

    Agatha looked down, wide-eyed, as if realizing only now what she’d done.

    And under her hand, the creeping vine stilled too.

    Waiting.

    Perhaps it recognized Agatha as the sun around which Rio now orbited, the light toward which she would turn.

    For a long moment, neither of them moved. The world beyond the bed, their breath, and the fire in the hearth faded into quiet.

    It was quiet, it was warm. Rio caught herself wishing Agatha would press harder, that her hand would stay in this position, that they could both linger connected here.

    The green light beneath Agatha’s palm deepened, pulsing once. And something opened.

    Rio sucked in a breath as her mind flooded not with the echoes of her own experiences and emotions, but Agatha’s.

    Not the shape of them, not the words that would describe them. The heart of them.

    Distress. Terror. Worry. Each fierce and burning. The flavor of a thousand unsaid prayers on her tongue.

    Agatha seated by her side, brushing damp hair from her brow with trembling fingers.

    Agatha snapping at a healer who dared to step too close: “She needs quiet. And space. I’m giving her both, and you’re in the way.”

    Agatha, voice hoarse, soothing her in the dead of night as her body writhed with fever, “You’re safe, Rio. You’re with me”

    A kiss.

    A press of lips to her brow, desperate and tender and unguarded.

    Not meant for waking eyes. Not meant for knowing.

    Rio’s breath shook as her vision cleared.

    She blinked, heart thundering now for reasons that had nothing to do with the pain.

    Agatha’s gaze was locked on hers—wide, startled, pink already blooming across her cheeks. And Rio knew, with utter certainty, that Agatha had felt the tether pull both ways.

    Had seen what Rio had seen.

    Whatever tether had formed meant it wasn’t necessary to give these shared visions a name. But Rio couldn’t stop herself from acknowledging it or hide her surprise as she murmured, “You… kissed me.”

    Agatha’s lips parted and Rio could feel her indecision, whatever reply came next was currently caught between haughty and earnest.

    Her cheeks deepened to a near-crimson and she chewed her lip for a moment longer. “Well, technically you kissed me first and then had the gall to nearly die and leave me with that.” Rio’s fingers twitched around hers. She swallowed, the words thick in her throat.

    “I… I wasn’t thinking.” Her voice cracked slightly. “I thought I was…” She faltered, gaze flicking to the side. “I thought I was dying.”

    Agatha’s breath hitched.

    Rio pressed on, words rushing now, uneven. “All I could think about was how I was dying a coward.”

    Another flush rose in her cheeks, but not from fever this time - shame burning hotter than any pain. She couldn’t meet Agatha’s eyes.

    “I wasn’t trying to make it harder for you,” she added, softer now, voice barely above a whisper. “I didn’t mean to… to leave you with that. But selfishly, I thought if I couldn’t keep protecting you, if my duty was lost, at least then I could be true to my heart. So I kissed you. I had to.”

    Agatha hadn’t moved her hand.

    Her palm still rested over the heart of Rio’s rune, where green and violet light still pulsed softly, together.

    Rio felt her. Not just the touch of her hand. The pull of her magic. It soothed and ached at the same time, like a balm applied to a burn.

    For a breath, she said nothing, the silence stretching on.

    Then, quietly, Agatha spoke.

    “I’m glad you did.”

    Agatha’s thumb brushed, without thought, a small circle over the glowing rune beneath her palm.

    Rio dropped her eyes to where their hands now rested, intertwined over the living pulse of magic between them.

    Agatha swallowed hard. Her cheeks were still flushed, but her voice was steady now, stripped bare.

    “I think I would have kissed you in that square even if there were no battle. Even if we had all the time in the world.”

    And in that moment, pulse for pulse, skin to skin, Rio felt the truth of it through the tether. Neither a stray thought, nor pity, but want. Deep, aching, unraveled desire.

    Before Agatha could move away, Rio raised her free hand and pressed down over Agatha’s on her chest.

    Held it there.
