    The magic answered as if it had been waiting, a third pulse thrumming beneath their joined hands.

    Green and violet light shimmered beneath Agatha’s palm, spreading outward in slow waves.

    The vine beneath her hand warmed, almost seeming to breathe.

    And the thorns stilled.

    Like frost melting beneath dawn, they began to change.

    Sharp points softened, rounded, dissolved. The tendrils unfurled in elegant spirals, and from their tips, new growth emerged: thin, fragile stems that thickened and split.

    Petals.

    Pale and delicate, kissed with a faint blush of violet at their edges, they opened one by one. Small clover-shaped blooms spilled across the torn skin.

    It wasn’t fast. It wasn’t violent.

    It was gentle. Patient. A living bandage.

    The flowers spread in slow waves, weaving a soft, intricate covering over the raw wound until no broken flesh remained—only a field of pale blooms swaying faintly with each breath.

    Rio’s breath caught, not from pain now, but wonder.

    The ache in her side faded as if the flowers carried it away with each unfurling petal.

    Agatha let out a shaky breath of her own.

    Her gaze flicked from the sea of pale blossoms across Rio’s ribs to where their hands remained joined, light still pulsing beneath their skin.

    Rio moved first, tracing her thumb in a small, trembling stroke across the back of Agatha’s hand, as if to anchor both of them there.

    When she found her voice, it was low. “Don’t let go. Please.”

    “You neither.”

    The air between them hummed with something fragile and wanting, stretched near to breaking.

    Agatha’s gaze lingered on Rio’s face, on parted lips, on the flush high on her cheeks not born of fever.

    And Rio felt it all through the tether. The want. The hesitation. The unbearable pull.

    She couldn’t stand the distance of it. Not now, not when every part of her was singing with the memory of their kiss in the square.

    Her thumb brushed up along Agatha’s jaw.

    Rio’s voice was rough with disuse, but heat threaded through it. “You know…” she murmured, eyes half-lidded, “you could allow me to kiss you again?”

    A soft, strangled laugh escaped Agatha—equal parts longing and disbelief.

    Rio pressed on, a small smile tugging at her lips. “And still get away with telling me it was a dream later. If you change your mind.”

    Agatha leaned in close enough that Rio could feel the shudder in her breath.

    But she didn’t close the last space between them.

    “There is so much we need to talk about,” Agatha whispered, voice unsteady.

    Rio’s gaze flicked to hers. “Later.”

    Agatha swallowed. “When you’re healed.”

    “When I’m healed.”

    Agatha closed the remaining distance between them. Her nose brushed gently against Rio’s. Her hand, still cradled in her grip, pressed more fully to her rune, their joined pulse echoing between skin and spirit.

    And then she kissed her.

    Soft, steady, sinking like sunlight into deep water.

    Rio inhaled sharply, her fingers tightening around Agatha’s. The tether pulsed in rhythm with the deep ache blooming in her chest.

    This kiss was nothing like the one in the square. Not a stolen moment. Not a last request. This one was a promise.

    Agatha’s mouth moved slowly against hers, as if learning the shape of her. As if memorizing it. As if she had all the time in the world and wanted to taste every second.

    Rio answered with what strength she had left, a low hum caught in her throat, equal parts relief and surrender.

    When they parted, neither moved far.

    Their foreheads rested together, breath warm between them, hearts still tangled in the tether’s quiet hum.

    For a moment, there was only silence.

    “Where are we?”

    Agatha exhaled, a soft shudder. Her hand still pressed lightly to Rio’s chest, reluctant to pull away.

    “A safehouse,” she murmured. “Somewhere deep in the Wilds. I couldn’t tell you exactly where. Some of the villagers brought us here after… after the square.”

    Rio swallowed, her throat raw. “How long?”

    “You wouldn’t wake for seven days. It was seven days they said to let you go.”

    Rio closed her eyes, fighting the surge of panic. She could feel the gap in Agatha’s words. The weight of things left unsaid.

    “Agatha.”

    She waited until their eyes met.

    “I can only help us if I know the whole truth.”

    Agatha swallowed hard. “I don’t know everything,” she admitted.

    “The people here—” she glanced toward the doorway, voice lowering, “—they’re suspicious of me. I can’t imagine why. I have such a winning personality.”

    Rio let out a soft, hoarse laugh. It hurt, but she welcomed the sound.

    “I can only imagine,” she said. “You’ve likely been a terror.”

    Agatha’s eyes warmed, a flicker of mischief peeking through the weariness. “Oh, you have no idea.”

    “I can guess.”

    Rio gave Agatha’s hand a faint squeeze. “And I will ask them myself soon enough.”

    Agatha’s smile thinned. “You will… but not today.”

    “Why not?”

    Agatha hesitated, then exhaled slowly. “There was a healer. She came two days ago.”

    Her thumb traced an absent line along the edge of Rio’s rune, still resting beneath her palm. “She knew your name.”

    That caught Rio’s full attention. “Knew me?”

    Agatha nodded.

    Rio’s pulse stirred. “And she’s returning?”

    “Tomorrow,” Agatha said softly. Her gaze flicked again toward the doorway, wary. “She didn’t linger. Left orders and herbs.”

    Rio narrowed her eyes slightly. “Did you ask her name?”

    Agatha huffed in the petulant way perfected by many a princess. “No.”

    Rio blinked, amused despite the weariness pulling at her limbs. “No?”

    “I didn’t care for her.”

    “No?” Rio echoed again, smile growing with understanding.

    Agatha’s cheeks colored faintly. “I didn’t like the way she looked at you.”

    Rio laughed. “That I believe.”

    Before another retort could sound, a sharp knock split the quiet of the cottage.

    Not the careful knock of a villager. Not the heavy-handed rap of a soldier.

    Three crisp taps. Measured. Certain.

    What the Moon Cannot Hold Chapter Summary Three wait beneath the moon: a boy, a child, a knight. And one walks into the dark, not knowing what she’ll find.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes There was a fox of the singing spring who fell in love with the moon.

    He saw her high in the hush of sky a waning, lonely, bloom.

    They say a fox can love a star, through each and every phase.

    Agatha sat beside the cot, knees drawn up, hands slack in her lap. Her body ached from a week of nights with little sleep, too many hours braced for something worse.

    Next to her, Rio lay still.

    Her breathing shallow, color barely returning to her cheeks. The bloom of flowers across her side, cloverlike petals that replaced the ugly wound, rose and fell with each breath.

    Then she’d faded again. Slipped back into dreams or the dark. Agatha wasn’t sure which.

    But Rio hadn’t noticed the paper.

    She hadn’t seen the faint marks pressed into the wrapping. Creases where someone had written, then hidden the letters by placing them to face the stems. A note hidden in plain sight.

    Agatha hadn’t opened it.

    She’d told herself there hadn’t been time. That it wasn’t important. But the truth lived lower in her chest, where it knotted behind her ribs and refused to let go.

    She wasn’t ready.

    The light had long since dipped below the treeline. A pale sliver of moon broke through the curtain, stretching silver over Rio’s cheekbone.

    She looked peaceful like this, which was cruel, really.

    Peace was something knights were meant to fight for—a vow carried like a banner across every battlefield. A noble promise. A necessary one.

    But it asked too much of the ones who made it. It demanded their edges. Scraped them hollow. Left the inside thin and cracking.

    How many lives would be traded for the dream of none lost again? How much blood must be spent to buy silence?

    And was it ever real?

    Or just another myth whispered to children—like a princess who ran from a tower and wasn’t caught, like a knight called down by a wish on a star who fell out of the sky and survived the landing.

    Knights were meant to be protectors. That was the word everyone liked to use. Not defenders, not warriors. Protectors. Like they could shield the world with their bodies and still sleep at night. As if any of them ever truly were at rest.

    She wondered if in all of history a knight had ever known peace.

    Agatha’s gaze softened. She reached out, brushing her knuckles just barely along Rio’s arm. She looked so still. Like she’d finally found a moment to rest, even if she had to nearly die to earn it.

    The center of the rune wasn’t like the rest. It sat just above her heart, perfectly flat, shaped like a flower Agatha didn’t recognize. Not native to the Empire. Not anything from her childhood books.

    Not randomly. Not passively. But curling more tightly, more deliberately—tendrils stretching in Agatha’s direction before falling still again, reaching for something just beyond their grasp.

    Was this what it meant for a rune to awaken? Was it a gift or a curse? A tether? A trap?

    She didn’t know. But the flower at its center looked alot like an offering. And the vines seemed to be reaching much like a plea.

    For what, Agatha wasn’t ready to name.

    She stayed still there, watching and waiting, for a very long time.

    And when she finally moved, it was only to lower her head to Rio’s uninjured side. Just enough to feel the warmth of her. To listen to the proof of her breath, her pulse.

    It grounded her. It undid her.

    But now she reached for it.

    The paper crinkled under her fingers, still damp along one edge from the dew. As she unwrapped it, the flowers dropped away with a faint rustle, their purpose already served. Two slips of parchment fell loose from the bundle.

    One on paper embossed with her family crest.

    Agatha unfolded that piece first and recognized the hand in an instant.

    To Princess Agatha of the First House, Your absence, while noted, is temporary.

    The Empire endures, as do your duties to it. The child is well. His future remains—

    unlike yours—untarnished.

    You are to return to court by the next full moon.

    Failure to do so will be considered treason.

    Your mother, in her mercy, has left you enough rope to climb—or to hang.

    Use it wisely.

    Agatha let the paper curl in her hand. She had expected cruelty, that wasn’t a surprise.

    Then her eyes dropped to the second note. The smaller one. No seal, but something more personal: a tiny symbol drawn in the upper right corner.

    A quill and bloom, their childhood shorthand for secrets passed in scribbled notes.

    Her throat tightened as she unfolded the paper carefully.

    The page looked blank.

    For a moment, she thought it might be a decoy—until her fingers brushed the paper’s edge and she felt the faint warmth beneath her skin. Not ink. Not invisible dye.

    They used to call it “petal writing.” You pressed your thumb to the page and whispered the name of the one it was meant for. If it was yours to read, the words would bloom.

    Jen never told Agatha who had taught it to her. But they’d shared the secret between them. Back when they were friends.

    Real friends.

    Before Jen had taken the sigil of the First House and sworn herself to the Queen’s left hand. Before secrets became currency and every look carried extra weight. Before Agatha had to start wondering whether the girl she once whispered stories to in the greenhouse was gone for good.

    They’d fought about it once. It went so terribly that there was only ever one fight about it. Jen had stood stiff-backed in Agatha’s room, eyes flaring with disbelief. “You think I’d ever betray you?”

    And Agatha, already anticipating the cold blade of grief, had said: “You already did. You chose her.”

    Jen hadn’t denied it. She’d just looked at her like it hurt.

    That was the last time they’d spoken plainly.

    Agatha glanced toward Rio, still sleeping. Then lowered her thumb to the paper’s margin and whispered, “Agatha.”

    A slow shimmer curled across the parchment—first pale violet, then deepening into legible lines of ink. The words unfurled like vines growing across the page.

    You are not as hidden as you think.

    Your knight still draws breath—but the noose tightens with every dusk.

    The boy lives. But his judgment comes soon. I need not tell you what it will be.

    Squires need knights, and boys need their mothers.

    I remember the garden oath we whispered beneath the pear tree.

    If you do too, burn this. And move quickly.

    Agatha read the message once. She remembered.

    Her fingers hovered over the edge of the parchment for a long while. Long enough that the candle beside her burned lower, the wax beginning to pool.

    She reached for the flame.

    The paper caught quickly. Agatha didn’t flinch.

    She watched it curl and blacken, petals of ash falling into the basin below.

    No tears came. Not yet. Just the throb of knowing the soft, stolen hours were running dry. She stayed seated long after the fire had died, fingers ink-smudged and soot-tipped, as if she'd pulled the words of warning from the page with her bare hands and meant to carry them on her skin.

    Outside, the moon had risen higher.

    Agatha tilted her head to the window, her voice too quiet for anyone but the night to hear. “Keep her company,” she whispered.

    She didn’t expect an answer. But for one breathless moment, the wind shifted—gentler than before. As if the moon had heard.

    As if it would try.

    She stood slowly, mindful not to wake her.

    Rio slept on, brow faintly creased in dreaming. Her lips parted just enough to whisper a name to the night.

    Agatha’s hand hovered over her for a heartbeat longer.

    Then she leaned in, and pressed a barely there kiss to Rio’s temple.

    It was a thief’s kiss. One of a coward. A promise in reverse.

    “Please, my love… be safe,” she breathed, too soft for the room to echo.

    When she left, the door shut behind her without sound.

    There was a fox of the singing spring who fell in love with the moon.

    He saw her high in the hush of sky a waning, lonely, bloom.

    “I’ll keep you company,” he called one night, but to his howl she made no reply.

    For the moon was bound to the sky and tide, and the fox to roots and running.

    Still he returned to the hill each night, with stories tucked in his whiskers—

    He told her of stolen berries, warm dens, and the mischief caused by chasing hens.

    The moon said little, but she glowed more brightly.

    And the little fox grew bold.

    “Come down,” he said, “my lady—run with me.”

    And for the first time, she replied: “I cannot play,” said the moon. “Only watch.”

    “I cannot hold,” she whispered, “only shine.”

    “I cannot stay,” she warned. “I can only give you time.”

    “But I’ll keep your shadow company.”

    The fox still waits in the hallowed glen, where nightblooms wake to silver light.

    He leaves his tracks in pacing paths, and whispers her name to night.

    They say the moon turns soft for him, That her light bends low to meet his gaze.

    They say a fox can love a star, through each and every phase.

    They say the fox, when old and worn, climbed high and did not stop.

    That moonlight kissed his silver brow upon the mountain top.

    And when he lay beneath the stars, his breath a thin fraught thread, the moon came down without a word and rested by his head.

    Promise and Regret Chapter Summary A vow once whispered in blood and stone rises to collect its due.

    Agatha walks into the Wilds, and the past follows close behind.

    A stranger is met.

    Chapter Notes This chapter includes fairly detailed descriptions of childhood abuse. Please read with care.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes Briars clung to the hem of her cloak like desperate hands, thorns catching and tugging until the fabric frayed. Roots jutted from the earth like broken bones, slick with moss, ready to trip her—ready to remind her she didn’t belong here. Branches reached down, scraping her arms, her cheek, as if the forest itself meant to scar her for leaving.

    To leave a mark on her, to claim her, to prove she was here. To shame her for leaving.

    Every step was a struggle.

    And still Agatha walked.

    The sweetness hadn’t been enough to make her stay. Not the quiet promise of the spring. Not the warmth of the fire or the scent of Rio’s hair when she leaned too close. Not even the feel of her heartbeat steadying beneath Agatha’s palm.

    So the world turned on her, as it always did. The trees bent with malice. The wind howled through gaps in the canopy like vindictive laughter. That was the way of things, wherever Agatha moved, rot bloomed in her wake.

    Agatha knew well this kind of turning.

    She recognized it from the court, from her childhood, from the polished smiles of those who offered affection like a trap. Sweet when they thought she could be cajoled. Vicious when she had to be controlled.

    The Wilds were no different.

    Or maybe they were.

    Because this place didn’t pretend.

    And she—a stupid girl, a selfish one too, sick with hope better spent on better people, good and kind people with warm hands and gaps between their teeth with which to whistle—had dared to taste something gentle. Had let herself rest. Had let herself fall in love with something soft.

    She reached for the light, expecting it to burn, not realizing she’d be the one to snuff it out. Now the whole forest seemed to rise against her in protest.

    She did not cry. But her breath came fast, and her heart beat like it wanted to break free of her chest and race back the way she’d come.

    Back to where Rio slept.

    Where she should have stayed.

    But there was no turning around.

    Not now.

    Being stabbed hurt less than being cut. A cut sliced the nerve endings, leaving each raw and screaming. A stab wound didn’t hurt until one realized what had happened. There were a few seconds between the knife sinking in and the blood seeping out.

    She had been Nicky’s age when she was encouraged to learn the particulars of that lesson. There were more lessons after that.

    Not ones taught with ink and paper, but the ones etched into skin. Lessons of silence. The ones that taught her to smile through pain. Those that trained her to flinch inward instead of out. To hide the bruises. To never cry in front of the servants. To say thank you.

    To never ever run, because running only made her mother angrier.

    And she was always caught.

    She had learned to keep her voice soft. Her face pleasant enough. Her spine straight no matter what was aching inside her.

    The world didn’t mourn the breaking of little girls. Not even daughters of queens.

    She remembered being small, standing before Evanora’s towering mirror, reciting titles she didn’t yet understand. Her voice had trembled, but she’d kept it steady. She had to.

    Behind her, her mother stood like a shadow stitched to her spine, cold hands resting on her shoulders, nails digging in just enough to bruise.

    Correcting every waver. Repeating each mistake until it bled out of her.

    Smiling—only when Agatha got it right.

    And that smile had always frightened her more than the snarl that came before punishment. Because it meant the training was working. It meant she was becoming the thing her mother wanted.

    Evanora had never wanted a daughter.

    But she had tolerated one.

    She’d made that clear—first with indifference, then mockery, and finally the meticulous cruelty of reformation.

    And in the end, she’d succeeded.

    She’d carved Agatha into something sharp, obedient, and unnatural.

    Something the Empire could crown.

    Agatha had never been loved. Only crafted.

    She would not let that happen to her son.

    Her boot slipped in the mud, and she caught herself on a gnarled branch, splinters biting into her palm.

    Agatha did not stop.

    Because if Evanora thought she could wield her child as leverage, then she had forgotten the most dangerous truth of all.

    She had made Agatha.

    Shaped her in fire and silence, forged her in shame and obedience. Evanore had taught her to endure, to outlast, to turn pain into a weapon.

    Agatha was her mother’s daughter. She knew well the taste of blood.

    Her foot caught on a raised root, nearly sending her tumbling. She hissed and caught herself again, sharp pain sparking up her ankle. But it wasn’t the ache that stopped her.

    It was the scent.

    Subtle. So faint she might’ve imagined it. A trace of something cloying on the air, bitter- sweet and slick.

    Wormwood.

    She recognized the note from the oil prepared for the cleansing rituals and the scent drew a memory that struck like a blade between her ribs.

    She was fifteen then.

    The fire had burned low in the great hearth, casting the walls in flickering amber. She’d sat still, her knees sore from the stone floor. Evanora had anointed her in silence, fingers precise and slow, tracing the searing oils over her back.

    “Impurities must be drawn out,” her mother had said, tone clipped and clinical. “And resistance is the root of rot.”

    But Agatha had been burned with more than just the oil. She’d felt it boiling in her chest all day; the resentment, the anger, the unbearable pressure of never being allowed to speak, to choose, to be.

    When her mother turned for just a moment to gather another cloth, Agatha made her choice. She lunged and took the pot with shaking hands and flung it toward her mother’s face.

    It hit her shoulder instead—hot liquid splashing over her silks.

    For a moment, there had been only silence.

    Then Evanora smiled.

    A thin, terrible, deadly thing.

    “I see,” she had murmured. “You’ve learned nothing.”

    She was sent reeling by the cold slap of her mother’s hand. A sharp crack against Agatha’s cheek, swift and practiced.

    It was followed by the soft, deliberate sound of willow switches being chosen. Tested. Bent. Each one slower than the last. As if her mother was savoring the decision.

    “You will not destroy yourself trying to destroy me,” Evanora said, her voice low and dangerous. It was not raised, it was never raised. She didn’t need to shout.

    She struck Agatha once for the oil. Twice for the defiance. A third time, the hardest yet. “You are mine,” she hissed between blows. “Your blood. Your body. Your future. You do not get to waste any of it.”

    Agatha didn’t cry. Not then. Not until it was over.

    She stayed upright for as long as she could, knuckles white, biting down so hard she tasted iron.

    Because she knew what came next would be worse if she screamed.

    It always was.

    But as she lost count of the strikes, she also lost her hold on her tongue.

    As Agatha wailed, the room echoed it back to her—an awful chorus that stretched too long, too deep. A sound not entirely hers.

    The shadows of the ritual chamber flickered. The slick on her skin burned colder now, and she became aware of the cuts on her lips that she had caused, trying to hold back the inevitable.

    The final strike fell slower than the rest, more theatrical than punishing. A final cruel flourish.

    Then silence.

    Evanora stepped back, breath steady, not a single hair out of place. She wiped her hand with a cloth and tossed it onto the floor.

    Agatha was still trembling, bloodied and shivering, when her mother’s voice cut through the heavy quiet—calm, precise, unbothered.

    “I expect your apology in the morning.”

    Evanora waited a moment, as if daring Agatha to speak.

    “See that you make yourself presentable before you return to your chambers. I won’t have you dripping filth on the carpets.”

    She didn’t remember her mother leaving.

    Only the weight of her own heartbeat, loud and ragged, as she crawled into the corner of the room where the light didn’t reach.

    There, with her forehead pressed to stone, blood in her mouth, and shame coiling in her belly, she whispered the words.

    She spoke no incantation. She cast no spell. Not that she knew of.

    Agatha simply made a true promise.

    “I will never do to a child what was done to me.”

    Her voice shook.

    “I will love what comes from me. Fiercely. Entirely.”

    Another breath, and then whispered a final refrain.

    “No matter what it costs me.”

    The walls didn’t answer her. But the air changed. It stilled. Heavy and listening.

    If she'd had any training in deep magic, she might have felt it gather in the mortar and marrow of the room. She might have known better than to speak such things aloud, in a place steeped with blood and bargains.

    But she was only a girl.

    Outside, the wind howled.

    She hadn’t meant to cause any more trouble for herself. But something had moved in the dark that night and had heard her vow.

    And it had taken her at her word.

    Her body began to change before the season did. Subtly at first—her appetite stronger, her mornings slower. Her limbs ached with a new heaviness, her chest tender and sore.

    Evanora noticed, of course. She always did.

    But instead of the usual jabs— You look like a wisp, girl. Eat something or be mistaken for a ghost —there was a new note in her tone. Approval. Amusement, even.

    “You’re finally starting to look like something capable of carrying the crown,” she said one morning, brushing Agatha’s hair too roughly. “No one respects a waif of an heir. What are they meant to represent? Famine?”

    She laughed then, and Agatha had wanted to shrink her flesh down to bone.

    It wasn’t until her sickness began—persistent, morning after morning—that Evanora’s satisfaction sharpened into delight.

    “Oh,” she said, like the last piece of a puzzle had been set into place. “Now this is a miracle indeed.”

    Agatha had held perfectly still.

    Evanora didn’t ask who. Didn’t demand a name. There was no interrogation.

    Just a slow, calculating smile.

    She was thrilled.

    Agatha was terrified.

    Not because she’d been caught—she hadn’t. No one could be named. There had been no father. No dalliance. No fall from grace.

    Just the quiet promise of a hurting girl in a place that had not once listened to her cries or pleas but had chosen to accept her vow.

    And now she carried proof of it in her blood.

    She didn’t know what she’d summoned.

    But she knew her mother meant to make it a weapon. Or worse, mold it to bear a crown.

    Agatha’s boot caught on a vine, jolting her back to the present with a curse under her breath. The forest had grown darker while she’d wandered through memory, and the chill of the evening was beginning to bite more fiercely.

    She adjusted her cloak, arms crossed tightly as if that could hold the past at bay. As if it could shield her from what was to come.

    It was then that she noticed the mushrooms.

    Pale as milk teeth, clustered in a near-perfect ring. Agatha went still.

    It was the second time she’d passed this landmark. There was the same leaning birch nearby with a twist of ivy around its trunk.

    A circle walked twice was a warning.

    She hadn’t stepped into it. She knew better than that. Knew the stories, the old rules whispered by children and scoffed at by those in court.

    Step into a fairy ring, and one might not come back the same.

    But gods, she was tired. Her legs ached. Her chest hurt.

    And for one strange moment, she considered it.

    What if she stepped into the ring and disappeared? What if the woods swallowed her whole? What if she could just vanish?

    Agatha’s foot hovered over the line.

    Then she drew it back with a ragged breath and turned away.

    There was no peace waiting inside a circle like that. No answers. No Rio.

    Only more things waiting to take her name and remake her into something they could use.

    So she kept walking. Not because she was brave, but because somewhere ahead, a boy needed her.

    That was reason enough to keep going.

    She continued to trudge until a hush fell that was deeper than silence.

    Even the insects stopped singing.

    She stepped through a gap in the bramble and into a clearing slick with moonlight. A narrow pool lay at its center, still and dark as ink, ringed with pale blossoms that pulsed faintly with some inner light.

    Slender stalks that bowed low as she passed, as if in mourning.

    She didn’t recognize the species. But that didn’t matter. Her eyes had brushed over them, already drawn to the surface of the water.

    It did not reflect the canopy nor the stars in the sky.

    It showed a throne room—familiar and wrong. The banners of her house faded to bone-white. The windows were shrouded. Her mother’s throne sat empty, draped in creeping vines. And there, seated at its base, Nicholas.

    He was older. Barefoot, draped in coarse linen, his hands resting in his lap. He didn’t look up. His eyes were dim, like he’d stopped looking for a way out long ago.

    No crown. Just the same blue cloak he used to wear while playing knight.

    And beside him, a woman rose from the shadow of the throne.

    She wore Agatha’s face.

    But her hair was pinned with iron spikes. Her mouth was a cruel slash of red. And her voice —when she spoke—was her mother’s.

    Agatha stumbled back, heart thundering.

    “No,” she rasped, stumbling another step. “No, this isn’t—”

    The rocks caught her heel and she lost her footing.

    There was no time to catch herself, no time to scream. The surface of the pool parted and she tumbled through it.

    There was no splash.

    Only icy cold.

    It wrapped her instantly, curling tight around her chest like a vice. Light vanished. There was no up or down. No sound but her pulse hammering in her ears.

    She thrashed, lungs already burning.

    Then everything went bright and warm. Her boots hit grass. She could breathe.

    The first thing Agatha noticed was the color of the landscape. Not gray stone or crimson velvet, but gold.

    Wheatgrass rolled in every direction, brighter than the sun itself. The sky above stretched vast and endless, streaked with wisps of white clouds. She heard a whistle on the air, not birdsong, something made by a human.

    She turned toward it instinctively.

    Rio stood a short distance ahead, her boots half-sunk in the tall grass, a low hum on her lips and her hair swept back into a half-knot. She wasn’t armored.

    She was dressed in loose linen trousers and a shirt open at the collar, the sleeves rolled up to her elbows. At her hip, a belt with a small dagger, but no sword. No shield. Nothing to weigh her down.

    Agatha called her name—not loudly, just enough to carry. “Rio.”

    Rio turned. Her smile bloomed slow and warm. She didn’t answer right away. She just walked, the grass parting for her, and came to a stop before Agatha, looking at her like they had all the time in the world.

    Rio tilted her head. “You called?”

    Agatha reached out to tuck one of the loose strands behind Rio’s ear, but Rio caught her hand and brought it to her lips.

    She kissed the inside of her wrist. Then her palm. Then, without a word, she leaned in.

    The kiss was slow at first, sweet and warm and sure, as if it had happened many times before. Rio’s hands shifted—one to Agatha’s lower back, the other behind her shoulders—and she dipped her, a sweeping motion that made Agatha laugh against her mouth.

    “I should’ve known you were a show-off,” Agatha whispered.

    Rio grinned against her cheek. “I’m indulging the princess.”

    And then, with effortless strength, Rio adjusted her grip and lifted her off the ground entirely. Agatha’s legs wrapped around her waist without thinking, arms looped around her neck, their mouths never parting. The sun filtered down like a blessing, a celebration of heat, light, and salt on the skin.

    There was a gasp against her lips. Agatha smiled, drunk on sun and the shape of Rio in her arms.

    She could live a lifetime in this moment.

    Something sharp pressed against her stomach, and Rio’s hands faltered.

    Agatha was dropped—not roughly, but with a suddenness that broke the spell. Her feet hit the earth, and she swayed slightly, disoriented.

    She blinked and looked down.

    A red splotch bloomed across the front of her dress, vivid and wrong against the dream-spun silk. She pressed her fingers to it, but there was no pain. No wound.

    Agatha looked up.

    Rio was still standing, but only barely. Her eyes were wide and her mouth had gone slack. An arrowhead protruded from her chest, the shaft trembling with the weight of its sudden arrival. It had gone clean through.

    There was a sound—a whistle through the air—and then three more. Each one thudded into her back.

    Rio gasped again. Her knees gave out.

    Agatha lunged to catch her, hands gripping whatever they could as they both collapsed into the tall grass.

    “Rio?” she said, but the name came out cracked and wrong. “No, no, no—”

    They fell together, a tangle of limbs and breath and disbelief. The golden grass cradled them like a shroud, its sunlit blades curling and browning at the tips, turning brittle beneath her knees.

    Agatha’s hands moved in a frenzy, desperate to find the wound, to press, to hold, to mend— but there was too much of it. Or not enough. Her palms came away dry, but she could smell the iron everywhere.

    She looked at Rio’s face, pale now, drained of the warmth it had just held. Her lashes fluttered, mouth parted as if still tasting Agatha’s name on her tongue.

    “I have you,” Agatha whispered. “I have you, I have you—”

    But her voice had no weight here. No gravity. The world was shifting around her, slipping sideways.

    Above them, the sky was no longer gold but the color of old sickly yellow bruises. The sun, once soft as honey, flickered like a dying candle, swallowed by clouds that moved too fast. The light fractured. The shadows reached them.

    The tall grass began to bow. Not with the wind. With mourning.

    Agatha clutched Rio tighter, forehead pressed to hers. “Stay. Please, stay.”

    Rio’s eyes opened briefly.

    “You… promised,” Agatha choked.

    Rio’s lips moved. No sound came. But Agatha read the shape of it.

    Run.

    The ground beneath them cracked.

    From the fissures came pale vines, sinewy and seeking. They reached not for her, but for Rio. Curling around her ankles, brushing up her calves, as if the earth itself wanted to reclaim her. To take her back.

    “No!” Agatha shouted, rising to her knees, clawing at the vines. They turned to ash in her grip, but more sprouted in their place.

    Rio was slipping from her, eyes closing again.

    And suddenly, the arrow shafts in her back were no longer wood but bone—long and brittle and ancient. Fossilized things that belonged to the dead.

    Agatha screamed, and the sound finally tore through the vision.

    She was still underwater.

    The last of her air fled her lungs, and the cold clamped down like a coffin lid.

    Her limbs flailed, desperate, but the dark held her fast.

    Then she felt hands upon her.

    Fingers gripped her shoulders, firm and unrelenting. For a split second, she thought it was the vines again, come to drag her down. But no, they were warm. Real. A sharp pain bloomed as her shoulder was yanked sharply upward.

    Agatha broke the surface with a ragged gasp. Her body convulsed, choking on breath and water all at once. The sky above spun wildly. She registered leaves. Bark. The glitter of the pool's edge.

    And then the hands again, hauling her fully out, dragging her onto solid ground. She collapsed onto the bank, chest heaving. Wet earth smeared her cheek. Her fingers clawed at moss as if to anchor herself to this world.

    She tried to speak, but her vision had already begun to tunnel.

    The last thing she saw was a face above her. Blurred, backlit by moonlight. A woman’s face, dark eyes narrowed in urgency.

    “You’re alright,” the figure said, breathless. “You’re alright. I’ve got you.”

    Agatha tried to ask who had her.

    But the light was snuffed out.

    She woke to darkness.

    Not the kind that could be seen through once the eyes adjusted. Not the comforting dark of a safe room or a moonless night.

    This was blindfolded, bound darkness. A weight pressed against her eyes, and something rough dug between her teeth. She tried to speak—only to gag.

    Panic surged. Her wrists jerked, but they were tied. Her legs too, cinched at the ankles. She shifted and felt the lurch beneath her, the uneven rhythm of motion.

    Her breathing quickened. She forced herself still, ears straining. Hoofbeats kept time, two horses, maybe three.

    There was a rustle of movement next to her. She flinched instinctively, but couldn’t retreat. “Good, you’re awake,” a woman’s voice said.

    The blindfold stayed. But fingers tugged the gag loose. Rough twine scraped her lips as it fell away, and she gasped, mouth flooded with stale air.

    “Water,” the voice added.

    A canteen was pressed to her lips without warning. She hesitated, then drank greedily, the metal lip clinking against her teeth. It tasted like rust, but she didn’t care. She swallowed until it was pulled back.

    Only then did she realize how dry her mouth had been. How badly her body ached from the cold. The bruises. The strain.

    The voice returned. Closer this time. Calm, unreadable.

    “No screaming,” it said.

    Not a threat. Not a plea. Just a rule.

    Agatha nodded once, sharp and tight. She did not speak.

    Silence followed for the length of one breath, then another.

    Then the voice, gentler this time. “Can I trust you enough to remove your bindings?”

    Agatha didn’t answer right away. Her throat ached, lips cracked. Instead, she rasped out, “More water.”

    After a split-second pause, she heard the slosh of the canteen returning. The lip pressed to her mouth again, steadier this time. She tilted her head slightly, and drank.

    Then Agatha spat.

    She poured every ounce of bitterness into it, hoping her aim was true. The canteen clattered as it was pulled back.

    There was a quiet curse. A splash of movement. She could feel it, someone flinching away from her.

    “Damn,” the voice said, low and stunned. Not angry. More like someone trying not to laugh at the wrong time. “So that’s how it’s going to be.”

    Agatha licked the last of the water from her teeth and sat taller, despite the bindings.

    “Come near me again and I’ll bite you,” Agatha rasped. “I won’t let go.”

    A quiet laugh broke the air. “I don’t doubt it.”

    Agatha bared her teeth and hissed, “Try me.”

    “I believe you,” the voice said, still laughing gently. “But I’d rather not bleed before supper, if it’s all the same to you, princess.”

    Agatha growled low in her throat, and the bindings bit into her wrists when she strained against them. Whoever had tied these knots knew what they were doing.

    The cart bumped over a rut. She rocked with it, lips pressed in a hard, bloodless line.

    “You’re awfully prickly for someone dragged out of a void pool,” the voice said after a moment, still maddeningly calm. “Are you always this hard to help?”

    “I don’t need your help,” Agatha snapped.

    A rustle, maybe a shrug. “You were drowning.”

    “Better than this ,” she spat.

    That earned a longer silence.

    “I am only trying to help.”

    “Unbind me then.”

    The stranger sucked their teeth then sighed softly. “I am afraid that is not something I can do.”

    “Then maybe you should’ve left me where I was.”

    “I almost did.”

    That surprised her. Not the threat—people always said things like that—but the evenness of it. No venom. Just fact.

    Agatha shifted against the ropes, trying to keep her voice steady. “So why didn’t you?”

    A beat passed.

    Then, quieter: “Because you said a name. Right before you went under.”

    That stopped her cold.

    Agatha’s breath caught in her throat. “What name?”

    “You know.”

    The cart rattled on, she heard the soft trill of birdsong, the crunch of hoofsteps through mud. Agatha's heart thundered. Her voice, when it came, was soft and scathing. “If you hurt her—” “I would do no such thing,” the voice cut in, surprisingly sharp.

    That pulled something tighter in Agatha’s chest.

    The voice continued more gently, “She is the reason I fought to pull you out. The pool didn’t want to give you back.”

    Agatha pressed her tongue to the back of her teeth, swallowing whatever emotion threatened to rise. She wouldn’t show that here. Not bound. Not blind. Not yet.

    “Where are we going?”

    There was no answer.

    “Don’t play riddles with me,” she snapped. “I don’t have time for this. I need to get back to the Empire. It is imperative.”

    The cart hit a bump. She braced against it, shoulders tight.

    The voice was quiet for a long moment.

    “Word from the Empire is grim.”

    “I don’t care about the Empire.”

    “You should.”

    Something in the woman’s tone shifted—still calm, but touched with weariness. “There’s a hunt on. Witches. Wildbloods. Dissidents. Anyone touched by anything the court doesn’t understand or thinks it can’t control. Sound familiar?”

    Agatha’s mouth went dry.

    The voice continued, softer now, but no less pointed.

    “They say heads have already rolled, and if I let you go now, yours will be next. Right beside all the others on spikes lining the front gate.”

    “My people wouldn’t allow that.”

    “I would not be so sure. People love martyrs more than their royals.”

    Silence settled again around that truth.

    Agatha leaned her head back against the cart wall and closed her eyes.

    Evanora had moved quickly. She always did.

    “I am not afraid of her.”

    “Perhaps not,” the voice agreed. “But if I may be so bold, perhaps, you should be afraid of what she has done. A person can be dealt with. Their actions however, those become ripples and then waves. You cannot easily dam a river when it already runs high”

    Agatha scoffed. The phrasing was too poetic. It ached in a familiar way.

    “You always talk like that?” she muttered. “Is it just a dialect spoken exclusively in the Wilds? Cryptic with a note of smug?”

    The woman laughed. “And here I thought your people prized diplomacy.”

    “Despite my mother’s gaudy taste, my people like things plain,” Agatha snapped.

    “I noticed,” the voice said. “But no. There is no one tongue spoken in the Wilds. That’s an Empire assumption, if I have ever heard one.”

    Agatha blinked behind the blindfold and tilted her head in question.

    “The Wilds are not a singular people or nation,” the woman went on. “We are everything and everyone else. What’s left when your Empire redraws the map and calls everyone outside it ‘lost.’ My father was a poet from the river cities. He spoke like a bell ringing. My mother came from the far north. She spoke from the back of her throat like the crunch of snow under your boots.”

    The image caught Agatha off guard. She didn’t know why. Maybe because it sounded too much like Rio, who sometimes spoke in the same lilting way.

    Her mouth twisted into a grimace. “What happened to them?”

    “Gone,” the voice said simply. There was no grief or elaboration. Just a statement of fact that had been said many times before.

    “Of course.”

    Gently, the woman added, “Out here, we inherit our language and patterns of speech the way mercenaries inherit armor. Mismatched. Mended. Made to fit as best it can. I speak how I was raised. Sing how I remember.”

    Agatha tilted her face toward the sound. “And you think that makes you better than us?”

    “No,” said the woman. “Just more appreciative of what I have. None of it was mine to begin with. One day, if I’m lucky, I’ll pass it on to someone else.”

    Agatha clenched her jaw, grinding her molars until the pressure steadied her. It gave her something to press back against.

    But when she spoke again, her voice was lower. Quieter. No less wary, but no longer on the verge of drawing blood.

    “I may have been too harsh before, I won’t threaten someone who has not threatened me,”

    she said.

    The woman gave a small, satisfied hum. “I’ve been waiting for that.”

    Agatha felt quick fingers work at the knots at her ankles first, and then her wrists.

    Then the blindfold was pulled away.

    Agatha blinked, squinting against the sudden onslaught of sun. It bled through the leaves above in wide shafts, painting everything in gold and shadow. The cart rolled forward at a steady pace along a dirt path, framed by ancient, leaning trees.

    The woman sat across from her, knees drawn up loosely, arms resting atop them with relaxed precision. She looked to be in her late thirties or so, her face angular and strong with high cheekbones and a dusting of freckles that disappeared into a dark braid streaked with early silver.

    Her eyes were a sharp, unreadable kind—warm brown, but constantly moving, cataloging. A faint scar split her left brow.

    Agatha studied her for a moment, but what struck her most wasn’t the woman; it was the horses.

    There were two of them. No reins. No driver. Just trotting along the path like they knew it by heart.

    Agatha’s brow furrowed. “How are they doing that?”

    The woman followed her gaze, then glanced back with a brow lifted. “I asked them to.”

    Agatha stared at her.

    The woman smiled slightly. “Not everything needs a bridle to move forward. Sometimes, you just need to ask the right way.”

    “That’s not how horses work.”

    “Maybe not in your Empire.”

    There was no smugness to the words.

    Agatha leaned back, feeling the ache in her spine, the tension in her ribs, the weight of everything she didn’t understand pressing in. The sun was too bright. The path too quiet. “Do they have names?” she asked suddenly.

    The woman blinked, then gave a small nod. “Of course.”

    Agatha hesitated. “What are they?”

    The woman’s smile returned, a little softer this time. “The front one is called Promise.” Agatha swallowed. “And the other?”

    “Regret.”

    She couldn’t help herself, she laughed, brittle and breathless.

    The woman didn’t press her. Just leaned back on one hand and said, “I’m called Alice.”

    Agatha looked away. “Well, you already know who I am.”

    “I know what I have been told,” Alice said. “But I am hoping you will become someone new.”

    Chapter End Notes We're at the "it gets worse before it gets better" part of the tale. But I assure you it gets better. Feel free to yell at me on Twitter in the meantime.

    Scars Chapter Summary A night in a tavern. Secrets shared. A flower blooms.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes Rio woke choking.

    Water surged up her throat in a cruel rush as her body convulsed. Base instinct overrode any thoughts, dragging her upright with a wet gasp. She doubled over, coughing hard enough to see spots as the cot beneath her creaked.

    She tried to breathe. But the air tasted wrong.

    Her hand flailed but found only empty blankets.

    “You do not need to fear the water, it knows your name. You were named for the river, were you not? That means you carry it in you. It won’t take you before your time.”

    She had been young then. Shivering on the bank of a flooded ravine, refusing to cross. Her boots had been soaked, and so had her eyes and cheeks.

    Her mentor had crouched beside her, one arm slung casually across their knee, the other hand trailing through the current like it was a friend rather than a threat.

    “There’s a story,” they had said.

    “A legend that when we die, we each must cross the River That Remembers. It holds every name, every grief, every joy you ever touched. The current doesn’t drag you down—it invites you to become weightless. And if you’ve loved someone truly, the water sings as you pass.” “I bet it sang for my parents.”

    “I’m sure it did, little one.”

    “Do you think it will sing for you?” she’d asked.

    Their eyes crinkled, distant. “A long time ago, I loved someone. And I remember how they felt, even now. But I did something I did not want to do, even though it was my duty, and I hurt them.”

    “You wounded your love?”

    “We all wound the ones we love. It is inevitable. Then we carry them as scars.”

    “She is yours?”

    “Yes. She is my scar.”

    “Where is she now?”

    Their smile faded then, quiet and solemn. The sort of expression Rio would come to recognize later, on battlefields and in last goodbyes.

    “She’s gone. Long gone.”

    Rio’s small brow furrowed. “Did the river take her?”

    “No,” they said, after a beat. “The world did. The river only received her.”

    They sat down beside Rio fully, drawing one knee up and rolling the sleeve of their coat past the elbow. Their skin was dark and sun-marked, but the rune along their forearm stood out: twisting, branching lines that reminded Rio of roots.

    “There,” her mentor said, touching the center with two fingers. “Used to be a joining mark.” Rio blinked. “Used to?”

    They nodded. “She bore the other half. A rook.”

    Rio leaned closer, eyes wide. In the middle of the rune, just where the lines curved inward like a nest, was a patch that had dulled and faded almost to nothing.

    “Is that where she was?” Rio asked, her voice hushed.

    “Yes,” they said. “And this,” they brushed the edge of the faded piece, barely touching it, “is how I know she’s truly gone. That part of a soul mark only fades when the one it belongs to crosses the river.”

    “But it’s still there.”

    Their hand stilled. “A trace remains. Not all things die cleanly.”

    Rio had reached out then, small fingers brushing their wrist. “Will mine ever look like that?” “If it does, it means you were very lucky,” they said. “To have found something that the loss of leaves a mark.”

    The ache in her chest sharpened. She pressed her hand against her own collarbone, where her rune pulsed faintly beneath her shirt. And for just a moment, she imagined it dimming.

    Splitting. Losing shape.

    “Agatha,” she whispered.

    But no reply came.

    And with Agatha gone, there was no reason for her to linger.

    Agatha kept her hood low, fingers curled loosely around the chipped edge of the table she sat at.

    The tavern was crowded for such a remote outpost. Packed with bodies and the smell of sweat and spilled drink, and old wood. Heat rose from the fire pit in the center of the room, but Agatha’s skin felt cold still.

    Her back stayed pressed to the wall as her eyes scanned the room.

    Alice stood at the bar, leaning one elbow casually against the counter as she exchanged coin for drinks and laughed at something the barkeep said. She had an ease to her, a looseness that hadn’t been there on the road.

    The weight of watchfulness had settled, if only for a moment, and Agatha was counting on that.

    The tavern itself was rough-hewn and dimly lit, its rafters thick with old cobwebs. But the actual web was spun in the hush beneath the music, in the murmurs passed from mouth to ear to mouth again.

    “Three witches burned just last week…”

    “…a whole family, they say. Cursed bloodline. Gone overnight…”

    “…trial didn’t last an hour. Just a sentence and then the swing.”

    “Awful way to go.”

    “Eh, better them than me.”

    “Idiot, what do you think happens when they run out of ‘them’”

    Agatha’s stomach turned. Alice hadn’t been exaggerating.

    The Empire had always ruled through fear, but now it ruled through fire.

    She knew this pattern, how cruelty could become policy overnight. How fear could be weaponized in the span of a single morning if the people peddling it were persistent.

    But something about it seemed different now.

    Her mother had never been kind, but she had always been calculated. Evanora was the serpent coiled among the roses, always preferring poison to spectacle. Her brutality wore the face of civility.

    This was rash. Reckless. Surely, she couldn’t fully control the narrative when there was blood spilled in the streets. When there weren’t enough hours of night to handle the dead, so pyres were burning in daylight.

    Agatha thought of Nicky, his tiny hands clutching petals in the garden, his careful words, the questions he always asked when they were alone. What did her mother say to him now?

    What stories had she spun in her honeyed voice, what lies dressed sweetly had she fed him? She could imagine Evanora standing before a crowded court, voice steady as ever, as she spoke of duty, of legacy, of the heavy burden of guiding a broken nation back to glory.

    All while resting a possessive hand on the shoulder of a boy too young to understand the weight of a crown or the shape of manipulation.

    Too young to know that sometimes monsters weren’t under the bed but the ones singing bedtime lullabies. That sometimes the people who tucked you in were also the ones who lit the fire under you.

    Because this woman wasn’t a mother.

    And this certainly wasn’t love.

    It was the Empire’s perfected brand of strategy wrapped in theater.

    And if Agatha didn’t stop it, Nicky would never even know what had been stolen from him.

    The sharp creak of the bar stool opposite her made her look up. Alice slid into the seat, two mugs in hand. She pushed one across the table.

    “You look like you could use it,” she said.

    Agatha offered a smile she didn’t feel. “You first.”

    Alice laughed. “Wary of poison?”

    “Wary of being beholden,” Agatha countered, lifting her drink after Alice took a generous swig.

    She barely sipped it, holding the drink in her mouth for a long moment.

    Alice didn’t seem to notice—or if she did, she didn’t care. She drummed her fingers lightly on the tabletop.

    “Word travels fast out here. People don’t have much else. News comes twisted, though. You have to pull at the threads to find the truth yourself.”

    Agatha tilted her head, watching Alice's posture slacken just slightly. Her shoulders rolled back. The edge in her tone had softened, and she reached for her mug with the slow comfort of someone forgetting to be guarded.

    “I heard something,” Agatha said lightly, as if in passing. “Someone said they saw the Queen’s own hand desert the court. Said she fled.”

    Alice blinked, her face unreadable. “A rumor I’m sure.”

    “Is it?”

    “You’d know better than most what your people are capable of.”

    “I might,” Agatha said, swirling her drink. “But the Empire’s been known to lie.”

    “That it has.”

    She let her gaze flick toward the hearth where the tavern crowd had begun to quiet. An old man was recounting something grisly now. Agatha caught the words ‘child heir’ and ‘marked by death’ .

    She looked back at Alice.

    “How do you know her?” she asked, keeping her voice steady.

    Alice didn’t blink. “Who?”

    “Don’t play dumb. Rio.”

    “She was the ward of someone I knew,” Alice said simply. “A relative.”

    Agatha studied her, but Alice didn’t offer more.

    “Did you know her well?”

    A pause. Then a slight shake of the head. “Not really. I was young. Not old enough to understand who she was.”

    Agatha sipped at the honeyed mead she still didn’t want. “But your relative must’ve spoken of her. After.”

    Alice’s brow lifted at that and she shook her head. “They didn’t get the chance. There wasn’t an after.”

    Agatha went still.

    “There was an ambush,” Alice said, softly now, the words careful. “During Rio’s training. I don’t know the full story. Only that Rio was the the last one to speak after the burial rites. I remember standing at the edge of the grove, watching her.”

    There was a reverence in her voice, not for Rio, but for the moment. A wound she hadn’t expected to name tonight.

    “That’s all I know,” Alice finished. “But I’ve wondered what she remembered. About her time with my kin. About the things I never got to ask.”

    Agatha’s grip on her mug tightened. She thought of Rio’s steady hands. Her quiet courage. Her terrible, beautiful grief.

    The way she held Agatha like she was both breakable and sacred. The way she had looked at Agatha, and wouldn’t even say anything. Just look at her in the specific way that Agatha had spent a lifetime wishing someone would.

    And yet, Agatha didn’t even know where she’d learned it.

    Was that gentleness her mother’s? Her father’s? Or had Rio carved it out of hardship, chiseled it through discipline until it became something tender and deliberate?

    She had always seemed so sure of herself. But now, in the lull of this dim little tavern, surrounded by the echos of other people’s fear, Agatha realized how little she knew.

    What songs had Rio been sung as a child? What food had she craved when she was sick?

    Who buried her parents, were there graves at all?

    She didn’t even know their names.

    Not her mother’s. Not her father’s. Not even the name of the mentor who’d shaped her hands and steadied her path.

    And now here she was—hood drawn and heart fraying—all because of a woman whose past she could not name.

    How foolish she must seem. How utterly undone.

    And for a breath, she didn’t know who the question was for—Alice or herself—when she asked, “Do you think she would tell you?”

    Her voice was quieter than she intended. Raw at the edges. Not a challenge, but a longing. And across the table, Alice didn’t answer right away.

    “Well, we’ll need to find her first,” she said finally.

    Agatha blinked. “You’re looking for her?”

    Alice tilted her head. “Of course I am. She’s missed four reports. No message. No runner. No sign of her from the last province or the roads beyond. The Wilds don’t operate on Empire time, but we keep count all the same. And Rio Vidal has never once gone silent.”

    Agatha sat back, her pulse thrumming louder than the tavern noise around them. “You volunteered?”

    “I insisted,” Alice corrected. “When she missed the second report, I left the next morning.” Agatha frowned. “So when you found me—?”

    “A stroke of luck,” Alice said simply. “I didn’t expect to find the woman that the First Knight of the Wilds became an oathbreaker for.”

    The words knocked the air from Agatha’s lungs.

    She opened her mouth to respond—denial, maybe, or protest—but nothing came.

    Alice leaned forward slightly, voice low, like she didn’t want to be overheard.

    “I thought she’d gone to ground. Maybe she was injured or covering a trail. But you…” Her gaze narrowed, not unkind, but deeply curious. “ You mean she was compromised.”

    Agatha swallowed. “How can you be so sure she’ll come?”

    “Because lovers,” Alice said, picking up her drink again, “make the worst soldiers.”

    She took a long sip, then added, “They break protocol. Disobey orders. Miss reports.

    Disappear into the dark with half a plan and no backup.”

    She set the mug down with a quiet thud.

    “And Rio? She’s many things. But she’s never once, never , neglected her post.” Alice’s mouth twisted, not quite into a smile. “Until you.”

    Agatha looked down at her own hands, suddenly too aware of how empty they were.

    Alice sat back in her chair, eyes scanning the crowd.

    “So I don’t need to find her anymore,” she said simply. “She’ll come to find you.”

    Agatha straightened, spine sharp with sudden hauteur. “I don’t have time to wait around for your little reunion,” she snapped, the weight of too many sleepless nights catching fire behind her eyes. “I need to get back to the Empire.”

    Alice didn’t flinch. Just raised a brow and took another sip of her drink. “Then you’d better hope Rio’s efficient.”

    Agatha’s jaw clenched. Her mind began turning, fast and vicious. Routes. Exits. Weapons she could steal. Weak points in Alice’s attention. If she could slip the room just for a moment, she could be halfway to a road before…

    “Don’t,” Alice said, voice flat and clear.

    Agatha’s eyes narrowed. “And why not?”

    Alice leaned in, just slightly, enough to cut through the tavern’s din with the chill of her words. “Because you don’t realize what kind of bounty has been placed on a missing princess.”

    Agatha smirked, too sharp to be amused. “Then maybe it’ll get me back faster.”

    Alice didn’t blink. “They’re not looking for a living princess.”

    Agatha’s smirk faltered as Alice’s gaze held hers.

    “Dead’s easier. Neater. Costs the Empire less to bury a traitor than to try her.”

    For once, Agatha found herself at a loss for words.

    Alice leaned back again, as if the moment had passed. But her words lingered, weighty as iron.

    “Run if you want. But if you do not care for your own precious head, I am willing to wager you care for hers.”

    Agatha didn’t respond right away.

    She sat with the words, chewing them like bitter lemon rind. Her mind ran through the scenario: through every corner and corridor of court politics, every whisper that might’ve curled beneath the marble floors of the palace.

    Evanora wouldn’t risk a public trial unless she already controlled the outcome. But executing Agatha in absentia would risk questions. Dissent. Sympathy.

    Killing her en route? That would be cleaner. More deniable. A tragic accident, a rebellious escape, a “necessary intervention.”

    The Empire could spin any story if the body was delivered cold on its doorstep.

    Alice was right. A live princess was a liability. A dead one? A solution.

    Agatha’s gaze dropped to her half-empty mug. She was outnumbered, outmatched, and far from anything familiar—but she still had her mind. And she wouldn’t waste it gambling on bravado.

    So she’d stick to the plan. For now.

    She looked up with a smile.

    “Let me get us another round,” she said, standing with grace that belied the knots still tightening in her gut, as she had been taught. “What are you having?”

    Alice raised her brows mildly. “Just water. I don’t drink.”

    Agatha turned away before the expression twisted on her face. “Of course you don’t,” she muttered under her breath.

    She kept her stride smooth, unhurried, as she made her way to the bar.

    In the end, Agatha didn’t stick to the original plan.

    She sat across from Alice with a second mug of mead in hand and listened to her talk about trade routes slowed by new tariffs, of cracked roads no one dared repair, of rumors that even the most loyal houses had begun to burn their family crests.

    Agatha asked idle questions and made thoughtful noises in all the right places, but her plan had already shifted.

    She would wait until nightfall.

    After their mugs were drained and the tavern had emptied to low murmurs and sputtering lanternlight, Alice led her upstairs to a plain wooden door at the end of the hall. It creaked as it opened onto a narrow room—bare walls, a straw-stuffed cot, a pitcher of cloudy water beside a chipped basin.

    Agatha raised an eyebrow. “Don’t bother tucking me in. I sleep alone.”

    Alice didn’t flinch. She just stepped back and gave a shallow nod. “Not a problem. I’ll be in the hall.”

    Agatha blinked, taken off guard by the easy agreement.

    Then, softer, Alice added, “I’m not your enemy, Agatha.”

    She closed the door without waiting for a reply.

    Agatha stood there for a moment, staring at the empty cot like it might bite her. Then she sat on its edge, slowly peeling off her gloves, her boots. She stretched out with her back to the wall, cloak still wrapped around her shoulders.

    The room was cold. Too quiet.

    But beneath the door, a sliver of lamplight glowed faint and steady. A shadow shifted once. Then stilled.

    Agatha stared at it.

    In the palace, she hadn’t needed to see Rio’s shadow beneath the door to know she was there. She could just feel it. Feel her.

    The quiet presence, the weight of someone trained to wait.

    At first, Agatha had hated it—how Rio stood out there without complaint, without comment. It felt like a performance. A punishment in reverse. A guard she didn’t want or need.

    But over time, it had unraveled her.

    She remembered the ache in her chest when the footsteps never faded, when Rio didn’t walk away even after the lights dimmed and the rest of the palace hushed into sleep. Agatha used to lie in bed with her back to the door, heart pounding, imagining what she’d do if it ever opened.

    Her first fantasies were violent with longing for splinters and heat and ruin.

    She imagined the slam of the door against the wall, the echo cracking through the stone.

    Rio’s breath rough with fury and need as she crossed the room in three strides and caught Agatha by the wrist, by the throat, by the edge of her nightdress. A woman turned to a primal thing with clawing hands and gnashing teeth.

    She’d throw Agatha down, or pull her by her ankles, or flip her to her stomach. She wouldn’t be gentle, and Agatha wouldn’t complain because she knew better than to want gentle things. Tearing fabric. Splintered wood beneath her spine. Her body folded like parchment beneath Rio’s hands, her hips popping as her legs were wrenched apart.

    There’d be no sweet words. No courtly preamble. Just teeth at her throat, a palm pressed flat against her sternum to hold her down.

    Agatha would arch like a flame, offering herself up to be consumed. Because in those nights of aching solitude, she didn’t want tenderness.

    She wanted to feel Rio’s devotion in the bruises she left behind.

    She’d bite back every sound, every whimper, until her voice broke. Until Rio dragged them out of her one by one with lips and teeth and fingers that knew exactly where to press. She wouldn’t ask for mercy.

    She didn’t believe in soft things, not then.

    Only fire. Only hunger. Only Rio.

    But as the weeks passed, those fantasies had changed.

    The door would creak gently instead. And Rio would slip in like a shadow. Say nothing. Just ease down beside her on the mattress, fully clothed, warm and solid and real.

    She’d curl her body behind Agatha’s, one arm slipping over her waist. A slow exhale against her neck. Fingers brushing her knuckles, her ribs. Not asking for anything. Not taking.

    Agatha would pretend to be asleep, holding still so she wouldn’t scare her off. So she wouldn’t ruin the moment.

    Until she couldn’t stand it anymore.

    Until the ache in her chest swelled too large, too loud to be ignored. Then she’d shift, slowly, carefully, as if Rio were made of the seeds of a dandelion that could be carelessly blown away in a child’s wasted wish.

    But even as a child, Agatha had wished for only one thing.

    So she’d roll in the circle of her arms, needing to see her face, to look into those dark eyes and be seen in return.

    Her hand would reach for Rio’s cheek. She’d whisper her name.

    And Rio would let her turn, but as Agatha leaned in, desperate for her mouth, her warmth, the taste of something true, Rio would tilt her head gently aside. Would press a kiss instead to the line at the center of her brow.

    Her voice would be so quiet. Impossible to hear if Agatha wasn’t close enough to breathe in every exhale.

    “We have time.”

    Agatha would close her eyes then, throat tight, heart shaking against her ribs like a bird in a gilded cage.

    That was the fantasy after all.

    The idea that someone could hold her like that and ask for nothing.

    That someone could wait.

    That someone might love her so much that they could stand to.

    She blinked hard now, lashes burning.

    Her hand clenched the edge of the blanket, knuckles white. She hated how much she missed her. Hated that the ache felt like a hollow carved just for this. That her body still reached for a shape that wasn’t there.

    There was a sound outside the door. Just the soft shift of weight, a sigh maybe.

    But it wasn’t Rio.

    Still, Agatha watched the sliver of light beneath the door, as if the shape of the boots outside might change.

    Outside, the night pressed on. Branches creaked, and the wind teased the eaves, but inside, nothing stirred except Agatha, caught in a familiar restless night.

    Sleep did not come.

    Minutes passed like hours, each one coiling tighter in her chest.

    Then a sound.

    Soft. Barely there.

    Agatha sat upright in the narrow tavern bed, pulse prickling beneath her skin. Shadows pooled thick in the corners.

    Her eyes snapped to the window.

    Another tap. Like the knock of a fingertip against glass.

    Agatha stood slowly, barefoot on the warped wooden floor, reaching for the knife she’d swiped from the bar and tucked beneath her pillow. Her heart kicked hard in her chest.

    Alice was supposed to be on watch. Had she left? Had someone gotten past her?

    She stepped closer.

    The curtain twitched as the window eased open just the tiniest crack.

    Something thin slipped through. A curling green tendril, no thicker than a reed of grass, nosing forward like it was testing the air.

    It moved deliberately, brushing the sill. Unthreatening. Curious.

    She didn’t dare breathe.

    The vine grew slow and sinewy, threading forward until it reached her nightstand. And there, with an almost reverent stillness, it began to bloom.

    A single, open-throated orchid. Purple. Velvet-soft and trembling as if it had been coaxed into existence by her longing alone.

    Agatha’s hand dropped from the knife.

    She moved to the window, drawn like the tide.

    Fingers trembling, she drew back the curtain and pushed the glass out.

    The night air spilled in cool and damp. Rain had begun to fall, a soft hush of it brushing the leaves.

    And there, barely lit by the golden halo of the tavern’s lanternlight, stood Rio.

    Dripping. Breathless. Alive.

    Agatha pressed one hand to the frame. The other to her chest, as if to hold something inside her from breaking.

    She couldn’t survive another dream lost to waking.

    Rio looked up, lashes dark with rain. Her jaw clenched. One hand still pressed to her side, tunic still stained dark.

    “You called,” Rio whispered.

    It was not a question. It was not a plea. It was the most devastating kind of truth: a simple one.

    Then Rio shifted her weight and reached for the ivy running up the side of the tavern wall. “No…” Agatha gasped, stumbling back from the window. “Don’t—don’t do that.”

    She turned away sharply, pressing her eyes shut with both hands. Her nails bit into her scalp as she tugged at her hair.

    Please let this end, she thought. Let me wake up. Before it turns cruel.

    The rustle of ivy. The soft scrape of boots against stone. A sharp breath, caught and stifled. Agatha didn’t turn.

    She stood frozen in the middle of the room, every muscle drawn taut like a bowstring. Her eyes were still shut, clenched hard enough to sting. If she didn’t look, maybe it wouldn’t shatter. Maybe it would fade away before it could ruin her.

    “Agatha.”

    A voice, ragged and tired, buried in her chest like a knife.

    Agatha flinched and shook her head.

    There was whisper behind her, soft steps across the creaking floorboards.

    And then fingers, slow and tentative, brushed lightly through her hair.

    Agatha gasped at the contact, undone in an instant.

    Her breath shuddered out of her in a sound more like a whimper. She didn’t mean to lean into the touch, but her body moved before her mind could protest.

    And then she turned.

    Rio stood only a half step away. Rain-slicked and pale. Her tunic clung to her skin.

    She did not speak, just drew her dark eyes as she looked at Agatha.

    Rio sighed then too, as if seeing her quieted something in her chest.

    Then she moved.

    Slowly, deliberately, she sank to one knee.

    Then both.

    Her head bowed slightly, not like a soldier offering fealty, but like a woman unguarding herself. Like someone weary and raw laying something sacred at another’s feet.

    Agatha trembled. Her hands hung uselessly at her sides.

    Rio reached forward.

    Her fingers, still calloused, still strong, still real, closed gently around Agatha’s right hand. She lifted it with reverence and pressed the gentlest kiss Agatha had ever felt to her knuckles. Just a whisper of skin against hers.

    Agatha's knees nearly gave out.

    This was real.

    It had to be.

    “Say something,” she whispered, voice cracking. “Please say something before I am ruined.” Rio didn’t let go.

    She held Agatha’s hand like it was sacred, grounding them both in that fragile moment between belief and breaking. Her gaze stayed locked, looking up at Agatha’s face, steady and soft and endless.

    “I heard you,” she murmured. “I came.”

    Agatha swallowed hard. Her vision blurred.

    “You ran from me,” Rio said as she traced her thumb over Agatha’s knuckles. “You didn’t trust me to follow. Or maybe you did—and that’s why you ran.”

    The words weren’t angry. They weren’t even sad. They were simply true.

    “You thought you had to face it alone. That leaving would keep me safe. Did you truly believe that I would not follow?”

    She turned Agatha’s hand over and pressed her lips to her palm.

    “There is no place for me that isn’t beside you,” she whispered against Agatha’s life line. Agatha’s chest clenched.

    Rio drew in another shaky breath. “Do you remember what I told you in the garden? When you asked for my name? That I would never stray so far from you that you would have need to use it.”

    Agatha nodded, her throat tight.

    “That was never a promise you had to keep to me,” she said. “It was one I made to you. You will never be lost to me. I will always find you.”

    Her hand tightened around Agatha’s.

    “I do not serve the Empire. I do not serve the Wilds. I serve you. ”

    Her voice wavered just once.

    “My duty is to protect. But my heart… belongs to you alone.”

    Agatha let out a sound that cracked open her ribs. Like something inside her had finally caved.

    “I’m not strong enough,” she whispered. “Not to lose you. Not to risk it.”

    “You won’t,” Rio said, immediately. “I will burn every bridge behind you before I let you walk alone.”

    Then she leaned in, pressing another kiss to Agatha’s hand, softer this time, like the closing of a prayer.

    Agatha dropped to her knees. Not gracefully. Not like royalty bending the knee in deference. Just as a woman who had been standing on the edge of a cliff and finally stepped forward. She crashed into Rio’s arms, fingers curling into the back of her soaked shirt, and held on like the only way to survive was to be held in return.

    Rio didn’t flinch. She folded around her like their shapes had always been meant to fit.

    Without hesitation, one hand came up to cradle the back of her head, the other still wrapped around her waist.

    For a long moment, neither of them spoke. There was only the sound of the rain and the wind, the creak of the tavern walls.

    Then Agatha whispered, barely audible against Rio’s shoulder.

    “I saw you die.”

    Rio froze.

    “I didn’t know if it was a warning, or a curse…” Agatha said, the words cracking open as they came. “But I saw it. You, on your knees in a golden field. Blood in your mouth.

    Reaching for me. And I couldn’t reach back.”

    Rio pulled her in tighter and pressed her lips to Agatha’s temple.

    “It wasn’t real,” she said softly.

    “It felt real.”

    Rio shifted so their foreheads touched, breath mingling.

    “I’m here, Agatha,” she murmured. “I bled to reach you. I chose to bleed.”

    Agatha’s hands fisted tighter in Rio’s shirt.

    “I’m so tired,” she admitted.

    “I know.”

    “I keep trying to...”

    Rio shook her head, voice low and steady.

    “You don’t have to,” she said. “You just have to stay.”

    Agatha broke then, not in fury, not in fire, but in the quiet, shattering relief of someone finally being allowed to rest.

    And Rio held her through it all.

    They stayed like that, pressed together on the wooden floor, the storm rumbling outside.

    But after a while, Agatha noticed it.

    Rio was shaking.

    It wasn’t violent, but it was constant. A subtle tremor beneath her soaked clothes. Agatha pulled back just enough to look at her, brow furrowing.

    “You’re freezing,” she said, brushing damp hair from Rio’s face. “You’re soaked through.” She started to rise, reaching for the blanket folded at the edge of the narrow bed.

    Rio caught her wrist, not roughly, just enough to stop her.

    “No.”

    Agatha looked back, startled.

    Rio’s eyes searched hers for a long moment, quiet and unreadable.

    She reached out and cupped Agatha’s jaw in her hand, her thumb brushing just beneath her cheekbone.

    Her touch was still trembling, and her voice, when it came, was a rasp.

    “May I kiss you?” she asked.

    Agatha blinked. A soft, surprised laugh broke from her, watery and raw around the edges.

    “We have already,” she said, her lips twitching into a smile. “If I recall correctly.”

    Rio shook her head slowly. Her eyes never left Agatha’s.

    “Not like this.”

    Agatha’s smile faltered.

    “I don’t want to kiss you in a rush,” Rio whispered. “Not because we’re running or hurting or afraid. I don’t want it to be stolen. I want it to be given. ”

    She leaned in just slightly.

    “I will never regret kissing you before,” Rio said. “Only that I didn’t do it right.”

    “And what makes you think you didn’t?”

    Rio’s smile bloomed faint and sure.

    “I will show you,” she said. “If you will allow me to.”

    There was a long, trembling pause.

    “Please,” Agatha whispered. “Kiss me.”

    Rio did.

    Not in a rush. Not because the world was ending. Not with desperation or teeth or fire.

    Slow.

    Certain.

    And Agatha burned.
