    Promise Til Dawn Chapter Summary One night shared. Time enough to whisper every vow they never thought they'd live to make.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes Agatha remained molded into the cradle of Rio’s arms for longer than she meant to. She found herself caught up in the moment of listening.

    To the wind. Rio’s breathing, still uneven. A thousand questions and declarations spun through her head, each seeming less suited for this moment.

    Rio pressed her palm to her cheek, but Agatha couldn’t bear to look at her right now. So instead, she looked down.

    At the place beneath the soaked hem of Rio’s shirt, dark with blood and rain, where she’d once pressed her magic into flesh.

    The wound was still healing, angry and raw.

    But the flowers were gone.

    No blooming thorns. No curled violet petals pushing through skin. Just a smear of dried blood and the faint purple-green bruise.

    “They’re gone,” she whispered.

    Agatha reached out, fingertips ghosting above the wound but not touching.

    “I didn’t know if they would stay,” Rio said finally, voice low. “I think… maybe they were only meant to protect me until I found you again.”

    A silence fell between them, quiet and heavy.

    “Perhaps I took too long,” Rio added, softer.

    Agatha’s eyes snapped to her. “No. Don’t you dare.”

    She pressed her palm gently to Rio’s ribs, just beside the wound.

    “You bled for me,” Agatha said. “You crossed gods only know what to reach me.”

    Rio’s throat bobbed. “That was my oath.”

    Her voice was steady but her body was not. Too far past the point of breaking to be controlled. Agatha could feel the tremble beneath her palm, faint but insistent, a chord straining to hold.

    Her oath. Of course.

    As if devotion could only be justified if it was carved into duty. As if what they shared wasn’t already bigger than any vows.

    Agatha’s hand lingered just above the wound, close enough to feel heat. Not the heat of fever. The heat of her. Of blood and breath and barely restrained want.

    Her fingers moved, slow, deliberate, sliding just to the edge of torn fabric.

    “Let me see,” she said, voice quieter now. “Let me take care of you.”

    Rio looked at her again, and in that moment, she seemed younger than Agatha remembered.

    Uncertain. Ashamed, even.

    “You know that’s not how this works,” she said.

    Agatha didn’t flinch. “No? Why can it not be that way?”

    Rio exhaled, surrender and frustration tangled together in that single breath.

    “Knights serve,” she said, shaking her head. “Princesses are served. That is the way of things.”

    Agatha’s lips twisted, not in mockery, but in grief for the shape Rio had been forced to bend herself to be.

    “Maybe so. But there are no titles here,” she said. “Just us.”

    She let her hand slide higher, fingertips grazing Rio’s shoulder then her collarbone, slow and careful. She wanted to map it. To know every pockmark and scar, each dip and dimple.

    “Rio, have you ever been… cared for?” Agatha asked.

    She didn’t need to ask. The answer was obvious.

    Agatha could see it in the way Rio sat—rigid, braced, like someone waiting for orders. Even here. Even now. After everything.

    Still clinging to the scaffolding of service because it was safer than wanting something for herself.

    “I don’t know how,” Rio admitted, after a beat. Her voice wasn’t small. It was raw, and aching. “When I’m wanted, it’s for what I can offer. What I can protect. My ability. My body was built to be a weapon. To be a shield. Not… this.”

    “Then let this be the start,” Agatha said.

    She lifted her eyes, met Rio’s gaze without flinching.

    “Let me want you. Just as you are . ”

    When Rio finally broke, it was not through violence, but through grace.

    Rio bowed her head, not in deference, but in relief, and let Agatha reach for her again. She sat still for a long moment, as if her body had forgotten how to move.

    Then, slowly, Rio’s hands moved to the ties of her tunic.

    “It can wait,” she said. “You matter more.”

    I’m sorry.

    You shouldn’t have had to come after me.

    I wanted to keep you safe, but I left you alone.

    I’m angry.

    I’m ashamed.

    Now everything was cracked open.

    “I’m not.”

    And that was the difference between them, wasn’t it?

    Agatha had spent her life holding back pieces of herself to survive.

    But Rio?

    Rio only ever knew how to give.

    She hadn’t once moved from her post outside the door.

    Alice tightened her grip on her cloak.

    And prepared to face the dawn.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes Before Alice knew definitively that her bloodline was cursed, she’d had some suspicions.

    For a time, she just thought she was unlucky. That was what the elders had called it with a shake of their heads. Misfortune, misalignment, old spirits not yet appeased. Some whispered about atonement. Others warned of restless dead, reborn into new flesh to toil again and again until their debts were paid.

    Maybe she had been born wicked in a past life. Maybe her mother had too.

    But even the old ones stopped calling it poor luck after a while.

    When the fire took her cousins. When the fever took her brother. When her father died after the storm without so much as a wound on his body. Just gone, like the Wilds swallowed him whole.

    And when her mother’s body followed, piece by piece, until it gave out entirely.

    Then they didn’t have the decency to call it what it was. They told her, perhaps because she was still a child, that her mother had died of a broken heart.

    Her mother hadn’t been fragile. And she hadn’t gone without love.

    She’d sung into Alice’s hair that she was her “heart outside the body.” She’d drawn runes in charcoal and clover ash even when her fingers ached. She bore a soulmark of stag antlers across her shoulders, velveted with moss—an emblem of guardianship, of strength, of wild devotion .

    That kind of love didn’t break.

    It burned out, maybe.

    Her mother didn’t die of a broken heart. She died carrying too much of it alone.

    Alice knew it was the curse. And as surely as she carried her mother’s blood in her veins, she knew she was marked too.

    Her mother never believed that.

    Not really.

    She believed in the old ways; in the gods that might still sleep beneath roots or behind rivers. She taught Alice the runes. How to draw them with ash and spit. How to mark her skin at night and wash it clean by sunrise.

    But the protective runes never glowed under Alice’s thumb. Not once.

    Alice learned them anyway. Every line, every shape. She drew them on her wrists, her ribs, her spine.

    Her mother had insisted it didn’t matter. “Some things protect you whether or not you see them shine,” she said, always with that soft, aching smile.

    Alice believed her. Or she tried to.

    She hadn’t always been alone. There had been a time before the curse claimed her mother, too. A time when her mother smiled more easily. When stories weren’t warnings. When every day didn’t feel like a borrowed gift.

    Before the silence and the long nights and the too-quiet house… There was laughter.

    There was a man who made toys from whittled sticks and painted each one with care. A tinkerer who carried a cart full of brass scraps and clockwork odds and ends from the Empire’s outer ring. He sang while he worked and kissed Alice’s cheeks until she squealed from the tickle of his mustache.

    Her father.

    He wasn’t magic. He didn’t have the spark. But he loved them both.

    Alice once asked her mother why he didn’t have a soulmark.

    Lorna had tucked the hair behind Alice’s ear and answered, “Because he wasn’t born to be mine, birdie. He chose it. Love doesn’t always need a mark to matter.”

    “They went to chase it out of the Wilds,” her mother said once, staring too long at the window. “Said if it was their doing, they’d undo it. But you cannot chase a curse out of you like a beast from your paddock.”

    Her voice rose, anger breaking through the fear that kept her spine straight.

    “Our borders burn, Vidal. The fires from the capital carry sparks all the way to the Wilds. War is coming for us, and the one meant to stop it, meant to stand guard over us all, has been distracted.”

    A slow grin cut across Agatha’s face. “Eliminating the Harkness line? Oh, I’ve no problem with that.” She gestured lazily to herself, her robe slipping at one shoulder, her smirk deliberate. “With a few exceptions, of course.”

    Alice’s eyes narrowed. “You think this is a jest?”

    “I think,” Agatha drawled, “we all want the same thing. You and your Wilds kin want the Queen dead. Conveniently, I’d like to see my mother burn. And Rio—” She glanced at the knight with a flash of something almost tender. “Rio will follow through on her vows one way or another.”

    Alice shook her head. “And what is it you want, First Knight? Truly. You vow to protect the broken and shield the lost, but which oath binds you now?”

    “The same oath that has always bound me.”

    Alice scoffed, bitter heat in her throat. “Not to one woman, then. Not to one place. Not even to the Wilds that raised you. Just to words.”

    “Not just words,” Rio said, voice low. “Ideals. And ideals do not burn when palaces fall.” “I swore to carry light where there was none,” Rio said, her voice quiet. “To shelter, to protect, to keep beauty from being ground to dust. That oath has not changed.”

    For a heartbeat, silence pressed against the room.

    Alice’s anger slipped into the shadow of an old wound. “And you think bedding her fulfills that oath?” she demanded, voice raw.

    Rio’s jaw flexed, but she didn’t answer. It was Agatha who laughed, soft and cutting.

    “Careful, Alice. I’ve been called far worse than a light in the dark.”

    The corner of Rio’s mouth twitched but her gaze stayed locked on Alice, steel-hard and steady.

    Alice almost spat back, but Agatha cut between them with a flick of her hand, her tone light and merciless at once.

    “Enough posturing. The two of you can measure whose oath is longer later. For now, we have common cause. Either we walk toward the fire together, or we all burn separately.”

    Her gaze flicked between them, cool and commanding. “So choose.”

    The road narrowed as the land tilted downward, stones jutting like teeth through the soil. Agatha walked ahead, robe replaced now with her dark riding coat, her hair loose. She moved as if each twist and rut had been waiting for her return.

    Rio kept a half step behind her, shoulders tight beneath her armor. She didn’t like walking with her back unguarded, especially not with Alice’s gaze burning holes between her shoulder blades.

    “The Three of Swords,” Agatha said, breaking the silence, her voice cool and assured. “We’ll go there first. It’s a smithy. A refuge. The proprietor, Lilia, she’s not only a blacksmith. She’s a seer. She sees through the smoke, through the sparks. She’s helped me before.”

    Rio glanced at Agatha, catching the faint smirk playing at her lips. Always three steps ahead, always keeping her secrets tucked up her sleeve.

    She adjusted the strap of her pauldron, eyes sweeping the horizon where the smoke of the capital bled into the sky.

    They didn’t take the main road. Too many guards, too many watchtowers with their mirrored lenses catching every glint of movement.

    Instead, Agatha veered them toward a narrow cleft in the hillside, half-choked with ivy and stone. She pressed a hand against the rock as though coaxing it open, and a passage yawned, cool air whispering out.

    Alice hesitated, but Agatha didn’t. She ducked inside without a backward glance, her voice echoing lightly off the stone.

    “Come along, darlings. Unless you’d like to be pikes before supper.”

    Alice drew a torch from her bag and stepped inside.

    Rio followed, blade-hand brushing the tunnel wall as her eyes adjusted to this place long forgotten by sunlight. The air smelled of stagnant water and damp clay.

    When the narrow throat of the passage opened, Rio’s steps slowed. The tunnels stretched vast around them, thirty feet wide and twenty tall, carved impossibly smooth. Pillars flared like ribs, holding up a stone spine.

    Rio tilted her head back, unsettled by how small she felt inside it. “What is this place?” Agatha’s grin flickered in the torchlight. “Oh, this? It’s where we keep the dragons.”

    For a long moment, Rio couldn’t tell if she was jesting. The scale of the place, vast enough for wings, for coiled bodies, made it plausible.

    She narrowed her eyes. “You’re joking.”

    Agatha’s smirk widened, wicked and unreadable. “Am I?”

    The echo carried her voice down the endless cavern.

    Agatha strode several paces forward, her torch throwing long, careless shadows across the ribs of stone.

    That left Alice and Rio side by side.

    Neither spoke at first. Alice’s jaw was set, her hands steady on the bow slung across her shoulder. Rio kept her gaze forward, though she could feel Alice watching her.

    Finally, Rio cleared her throat, the sound rough in the hush.

    “I don’t know who they were to your family,” she said, voice low, deliberate. “But I am sorry for the wound they left.”

    “They were everything to her,” Alice said. “And when they left, my mother carried it like a second curse. You ever hear them speak of that? Curses?”

    Rio’s hand brushed the wall, fingertips tracing an old groove in the stone. She thought of long nights on watch, her mentor’s voice weaving between lesson and story.

    “They never spoke of curses,” Rio admitted, quietly. “Only burdens. Some are born to us, some are chosen. They said the difference was how we carried them.”

    Alice scoffed. “Easy words for someone who could walk away.”

    Rio didn’t answer. Her hand stayed on the stone, steady, though her chest felt as if it were caving in.

    Ahead, Agatha’s laugh rang back through the tunnels, sharp and echoing.

    “Keep up, you two. Or you’ll find out what else these tunnels keep besides dragons.”

    The torchlight flickered ahead, distant enough to cast Agatha in silhouette, her coat flaring as she walked the path.

    Alice’s voice broke the silence again, low enough only for Rio to hear.

    “What is it about her?”

    Rio turned her head. Alice’s expression was hard, but there was no mistaking the earnestness under the barb.

    “What is it about Princess Agatha Harkness that’s got the First Knight in such a hold?” Alice pressed.

    For once, Rio didn’t bite back. Her gaze shifted to Agatha’s silhouette in the dark. She exhaled, slowly.

    “I was raised to be a shield,” she said. “Unyielding. Interchangeable. The body between the blade and what mattered more.”

    Her eyes narrowed faintly, following the tilt of Agatha’s head as the woman laughed at something unseen.

    “She was raised to be a symbol. A torch held aloft even when it burned her alive.”

    Rio looked sideways then, meeting Alice’s sharp gaze.

    “And perhaps you…” Her voice softened. “Perhaps you were raised to be something else entirely. Not shield. Not symbol. Something cursed into service you never asked for.”

    Alice’s jaw tightened, but she nodded slowly.

    Rio’s voice lowered, almost a confession meant for the stones more than for Alice.

    “I’ve learned the world and its rules, even magic itself… they’re all accessories. Chains dressed as guides. We’re told to serve this oath, that mark, that destiny. But it’s not truth. It’s just the shape of the cage we’re handed.”

    Her hand brushed the hilt at her belt, fingers flexing.

    “I wish…” The word caught in her throat. “I wish the three of us had grown to just be ourselves. Not burdens. Not tools. Not what someone else demanded we become.”

    “I’m sorry this wasn’t the case,” Alice said.

    “Me too.”

    They walked awhile longer, the tunnel splitting into smaller arteries. The grade grew steeper, walls slick with old moss. Rio stayed close behind Agatha, one hand braced at the small of her back as she climbed a twisted vine to pull herself higher.

    At the crest, Agatha swung over with the ease of someone who’d done this before. She didn’t look back.

    Rio paused at the lip of the rise. She could’ve followed without thinking, without looking. But something in her chest tugged, and she turned.

    Alice was below, boot slipping against the damp wall, jaw set tight as if she’d rather fall than admit the climb wasn’t made for her.

    Rio reached her hand down. “Take it,” she said simply.

    Alice glared at her, suspicion sharp. “You think a hand makes up for what’s been done?”

    Rio didn’t flinch. Her arm stayed steady, offered without flinching.

    “No,” she said. “But it’s what I have.”

    For a moment, Alice’s breath hitched, anger and grief clearly warring behind her eyes. Then, with a hiss of frustration more at herself than at Rio, she caught the knight’s wrist.

    Rio hauled her up with ease, not letting go even once Alice was standing. Their forearms pressed together, bone against bone, until Alice finally jerked her hand back as if burned. “Why follow at all, Alice? You could turn back. Leave me to my vows and her to her feud." “Because I didn’t come only for myself,” Alice said. “I came to see the will of the Wilds carried through.”

    “Then we walk the same path.”

    Her gaze flicked downward, catching the faint glimmer at Alice’s throat. A charm, some bird bone strung on worn leather cord, lines of charcoal pressed runes carved into the side.

    “You made that? It looks powerful.” Rio asked.

    Alice’s hand went to it instinctively, curling the talisman in her palm. “I did. But it doesn’t work.”

    Rio shrugged, almost gently. “Perhaps it just hasn’t worked yet.”

    “Well,” Agatha’s voice rang out ahead, “if you two are quite finished kissing and making up, we do have business at hand.”

    She strode to a heavy wooden door banded with iron and rapped her knuckles three times.

    There was silence for a moment. Then the door creaked, a sliver of lamplight spilling into the tunnel.

    From above, a voice called out warm, amused, and edged with surprise.

    “Agatha Harkness, as I live and breathe.”

    “Lilia, it’s been a while.”

    Chapter End Notes Follow me, my friend. To glory at the end.

    Til Death Do Us Part Chapter Summary The journey together ends. At dawn, a battle begins.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes Prince Nicholas was born during a rainstorm unlike the empire had seen in a century.

    Lightning blooming white across the horizon, thunder splitting the dark in sharp cracks. The palace whispered it was the work of a coastal coven drawing omens in the sky, as angered gods hammered at the veil between worlds.

    Agatha didn’t care if she believed that. She only cared that in the hours since his birth her son and deteriorated rapidly while her own mother had tutted, “Sometimes boys die, Agatha.

    There nothing to be done. Bad blood I suppose.”

    She arrived at the door to the Three of Swords, soaked through, one hand clutching her cloak to her breast, the other curved protectively around the small bundle that whimpered against her chest.

    “Something’s wrong.” She gasped the moment Lilia opened it. Her lips were blue, her hair plastered flat, her voice raw from the tearing of birth and storm both.

    Lilia stood for just a moment, soot still smeared across her cheek from her forge. She looked at Agatha once, at the way her arms shook beneath the weight of the child, and pulled her inside without a word.

    The forge was still hot, sparks breathing in the low light. Nicholas let out a thin, wheezing cry, and Agatha thought she might splinter apart from the sound alone.

    “I can only give you time,” a voice whispered in the roar of her blood, words pressed into the air like a rune carved unseen. For a heartbeat, she thought it had been the storm itself. But when her gaze lifted, it was Lilia bending over the child, her hands steady, her breath hissing between her teeth as if she carried some private grief with her work.

    Lilia’s fingers were thick with calluses, blackened from years of hammer and flame, but they cradled Nicholas with impossible gentleness. She pressed her broad palm to his tiny chest, over the frantic flutter of his heart. Her eyes closed. Something in the air thrummed, faint as a plucked string.

    The boy’s cry steadied. His lungs opened. His face softened from blue toward pink.

    Agatha, trembling, tried to reach for him, but Lilia shook her head. “Not yet. Let the heat settle.”

    When she finally returned Nicholas to her, he was breathing more evenly. Still fragile, and impossibly small. But he was alive.

    Agatha stared at Lilia, desperate for an explanation. “What did you do?”

    Lilia wiped her hand on her apron roughly. “Stole from the fire. And from myself.”

    Later, when Nicholas slept at her breast, she whispered, “Why?”

    Lilia leaned in the doorway, eyes on the storm that still raged over the city. “Because I saw you would not ask.”

    She gathered a yellow shawl and pulled it close around Agatha and the child.

    “You should sit. We have… some time before the guards come for you.”

    Far in the distance, a bell tolled three times.

    “But not much,” Lilia said with a grim half-smile.

    “Before this place, I lived in a hut that walked on two legs,” she said.

    “It carried me through the forest wherever I asked. I thought myself happy enough there, eating what I caught, foraging what I needed. The Wilds were my company, and that was plenty.”

    Agatha turned, startled by the odd confession. “Why leave it?”

    “Because one day, a stranger found me. Scarred, travel-worn, carrying grief as a cloak.”

    Agatha’s throat went dry. She didn’t speak, didn’t dare.

    “They asked for help,” Lilia went on. “Said there was a curse on a bloodline, a sickness that gnawed through kin like wolves at a bone. They thought they’d brought it on. That it was their doing, their burden to undo.”

    “I tried,” Lilia admitted. “Poured every ounce of my magic into the working. Drew sigils in flame and bone. Cast prayers to gods I wasn’t sure still breathed. But I didn’t lift a curse.” Her hands stilled, resting on her knees. Her eyes, one clouded with an old scar, the other sharp as a hawk’s, fixed on Agatha.

    “I received a prophecy instead.”

    The forge’s low glow seemed to pulse with her words as Nicholas whimpered against Agatha’s breast.

    “When the child is born,” she recited, voice deepening into rhythm, “the Queen’s shadow will begin to end. The Wilds will awaken. A knight will bleed, and rise again. A mother will burn, and through her burning, so too will the crown. And what is spared will not be spared for free.”

    Agatha’s chest constricted. She clutched Nicholas tighter, though he slept, unaware. “What sacrifice?” she asked, voice cracking.

    Lilia shook her head slowly. “Prophecies don’t name the price. They only promise it will come due.”

    Her gaze softened, almost kind. “But it will not all be ash. That much I saw. If the child lives, if the knight stands, if the mother endures—there will be something left worth keeping.”

    Agatha’s eyes burned. She looked at Nicholas, at the small rise and fall of his chest. If the child lives.

    The words etched themselves into her bones, into her marrow. She never forgot them.

    It was why, years later, when she whispered, “Lilia will help us,” she did not hesitate.

    She knew the woman had already seen it.

    The three of them spilled out through the cellar door and into Lilia’s living area.

    The seer looked from Agatha to Rio, her eyes finally resting on the talisman around Alice’s neck.

    “You’re late.”

    “Took a few wrong turns,” Agatha said breathlessly, hurrying towards the window. But before she could take more than a few steps, Rio’s arm on her shoulder stopped her.

    “Your highness. Allow me to search the area first.”

    Her voice was steady, but Agatha could feel the tension in the hand braced against her.

    Agatha exhaled, impatient. “Fine. But quickly.”

    While Rio swept the perimeter, Lilia busied herself at the hearth, stirring the coals back to life with a poker that looked suspiciously like it could double as a weapon. She didn’t look at them as she spoke.

    “The city hasn’t softened in your absence. If anything, it’s grown meaner. They say the pyres don’t cool before the next ones are lit.”

    Alice’s mouth tightened, but she said nothing.

    “And the Queen?” Agatha pressed.

    Lilia’s scarred eye caught the firelight, pale and unblinking. “She gave a speech three nights ago. Claimed your death as fact. Said it was a pity—such promise, cut short.”

    Agatha’s nails dug into her palm. “She dares—”

    “Listen.” Lilia’s voice cut sharp, the same way a hammer corrected steel. “She did not gloat. That’s not her style. She framed it as a sacrifice. Said she was ready to cede the crown to the boy, but…”

    Her gaze flicked briefly to Agatha, gauging the tremor in her jaw.

    “…with such a young heir, there would be no choice but for her to serve as regent until he comes of age.”

    The words hung heavy, settling like ash over the room.

    Agatha’s throat worked as she swallowed. “And Nicholas?”

    Lilia’s silence stretched long enough for Rio to return and stand at Agatha’s side, her hand brushing once against hers in quiet warning.

    “He’s safe,” Lilia said finally. “For now. He’s kept close. Watched. Paraded when needed. They call him the future. But a future in the Queen’s hands is no future at all.”

    “And Billy?” Rio asked, her voice taut as she turned, satisfied with her search, “My squire, who retrieved the helm you worked on.”

    Lilia turned to her then, her scarred eye still clouded, her seeing eye sharp as flint. “That, I cannot say. His fate is muddled. Some threads I follow end in fire. Others in chains. He’s not lost—but he is not free either.”

    Agatha closed her eyes, chest rising and falling with deliberate control. She could almost hear her mother’s voice curling through the city squares, soft as honey and sharp as glass.

    A pity. Such promise. Cut short.

    She opened her eyes again. “Then we don’t waste time. We go to the palace.”

    Alice frowned. “Not without a plan.”

    Agatha’s lips curved into something closer to a snarl than a smile. “Oh, I have one.”

    They all objected to the plan, but none louder than Rio.

    Agatha realized that of all the shades of the knight she had seen, not once had she seen her spiraling out of control in a rage.

    “No,” Rio snapped, her voice like flint striking steel. “You will not walk into her hands alone.”

    Agatha opened her mouth to counter, but the storm came faster than she could keep pace with.

    “You think I bled just to watch you deliver yourself ?” Rio’s voice broke and climbed in the same breath, fury twisting sharp and then soft.

    “You think I crossed half the Wilds, half-dead and half-heartbroken, only to—”

    She stopped, choking on the words. Her hands flexed uselessly at her sides, clenching and unclenching like they couldn’t decide if they wanted to grasp Agatha by the shoulders or tear down the walls around them.

    Agatha had expected the argument, but not this. Not the tremor in her voice, the wet edge in her eyes.

    “Rio—”

    “You don’t understand,” Rio cut her off, raw now, her composure falling apart in ragged pieces. “I can fight armies. I can stand against steel and spells alike. But if you go—if you leave me again—I have nothing to fight. Nothing to stop it. I will just… wait for it to end.” Her breath came unsteady, as though each word took something from her. “I can’t do that. I can’t.”

    The silence that followed was unbearable.

    Lilia’s gaze cut between them once, and then—mercifully—she turned to Alice. “Come. I have things you’ll need.”

    Alice hesitated, but followed. The door to the spare room shut behind them, leaving Agatha and Rio alone.

    For a moment, Agatha thought the quiet would ease her. That, with no audience, Rio would let herself be soothed. But when she stepped forward, hand lifting to touch her arm, Rio flinched back.

    Her eyes were dark and wild. “Do you think I’m a fool?” she demanded, her voice low and shaking.

    “Do you think I don’t know what you’re trying to do? You’ll smile, you’ll whisper, and then you’ll walk out the door with your clever plans while I’m left standing guard again.”

    “Rio, listen—”

    “No!” The word cracked like a whip, Rio’s hand striking the table hard enough to rattle the cups on it. “I will not lose you to her. Not to fire, not to shadow, not to your own damn stubborn pride.”

    Her chest heaved. Her jaw clenched as if she were biting down on words that wanted to tear free. For a moment she looked every inch the knight Agatha had first met—unyielding, terrifying, forged into steel.

    But then something in her faltered.

    Her fists unclenched. Her shoulders caved in. And the fury, as sudden as it had come, guttered into something far worse.

    Heartbreak.

    “You don’t understand,” Rio said again, but this time her voice was barely more than a whisper. She stared at the floor, at the grooves worn into the wood by boots and years, unable to look at Agatha.

    “All my life, I’ve been told I was a shield. That my worth was measured in what I could endure for others. Bite your tongue, brace your body, bleed if you must—but never want.

    Never keep anything for yourself.”

    Her throat bobbed, and still she wouldn’t look up. “And then I met you. And gods help me, I wanted. I let myself want. And now it’s going to kill us both.”

    Agatha felt her pulse stumble, the sharp ache in her chest unbearable.

    Rio’s voice broke. “Because now you tell me you’ll walk into the fire alone. That after I’ve given every piece of myself, after I’ve sworn my breath to you… you will leave me standing here with nothing.”

    The silence swelled until it was almost cruel.

    “If you do this, Agatha… My heart is sworn to you and if you command it, I will step aside. But if you do this, I will hate myself forever.” Rio choked out.

    Agatha’s lips parted, but no words came. She’d told herself this was strategy, sacrifice, survival. But seeing Rio now splintered open, trembling from the weight of her own heart, it stripped every excuse bare.

    Agatha stepped forward, slow and careful. She lifted her hand, hovering near Rio’s cheek but not touching. Not yet.

    Her voice came hoarse. “Do you think I don’t fear it too? That every step I’ve considered taking hasn’t been weighed against the thought of losing you?”

    Rio finally looked at her, and Agatha wished she hadn’t. The anguish there was more terrible than the blade of any enemy.

    Agatha’s hand finally cupped Rio’s cheek, thumb brushing the damp edge of her lashes.

    “You think this is nothing?” she asked softly, almost bitterly. “That leaving you is emptiness? It is not. It is everything. It is the only way I know to shield you.”

    Rio shook her head, but Agatha pressed on, voice growing rougher, fiercer, though her touch stayed unbearably gentle.

    “You say you will hate yourself forever if I walk into the fire alone. I will not allow that. Hate me instead. Hate me with every breath you have left. If that is the price, I will pay it. Gladly.” Her forehead rested against Rio’s, and her voice dropped to a whisper.

    “Because even if your hatred burns me hollow, I will still love you longer. Longer than any crown. Longer than any curse. Longer still than the flames that surely wait for me.”

    Her thumb trembled against Rio’s skin, but her eyes held steady, aching and sure.

    “Hate you?” Rio’s voice cracked, raw with disbelief. She shook her head sharply, as if Agatha had struck her.

    “Do you think me capable of that? Of hating the only thing in this cursed world that ever made me feel whole?”

    Her breath stuttered, chest rising and falling against Agatha’s. “No. Never. I could not.” And before Agatha could argue, before the heat in her eyes could turn into one more cruel word meant to keep Rio safe, Rio closed the distance.

    The kiss wasn’t desperate. It wasn’t a plea. It was the weight of inevitability, the surrender of someone who knew she would go to her knees a thousand times if it meant being allowed this once.

    Her mouth trembled against Agatha’s, reverent and unyielding, as though she were already carving the memory into stone. She kissed her like it was both vow and elegy—like she believed this was the last one she’d ever be allowed.

    And Agatha, for all her schemes and deflections, broke under it. Because this wasn’t a knight obeying. This wasn’t a shield cast to the ground. This was Rio, wholly herself, offering love as her final oath.

    Rio’s kiss lingered against her mouth, unmoored, until Agatha pulled back only far enough to breathe. Her cheeks were wet, though she hadn’t realized she’d begun to cry.

    “I have a dream,” she whispered, her voice ragged.

    Rio stilled, her thumb brushing at the damp trails on Agatha’s skin.

    Agatha’s lips curved into something fragile, half-broken humor. “Not of thrones. Not of crowns. A cottage, small and crooked, with smoke curling from the chimney. Briars cut back at last. No guards, no courts. Just me, you, and my boy.” Her breath shivered. “I dream of us there like it’s already been carved into the world.”

    Her shoulders trembled, but she tried for a smirk even as her tears betrayed her. “So you’ll have to make it so, won’t you? That’s an order, knight.”

    The jest frayed on her tongue, half a plea. Before Rio could answer, Agatha kissed her again, fierce and aching, like she might graft the dream to her lips and force it to stay.

    The door creaked then. Lilia’s boots pressed against the worn planks of the floor. She carried a folded bundle of dark leather in her arms, its surface etched with runes that shimmered faintly in the lamplight.

    “Best I could fashion in short notice,” she said, her tone brisk but not unkind. “It’ll turn the edge of steel. Buy you seconds where otherwise there would be none.”

    She held it out, and Agatha accepted with steady hands. But when she turned, it was Rio who stepped forward silently, taking the armor from her and guiding it around her waist.

    Agatha stood still, her robe slipping from one shoulder, as Rio’s fingers worked the laces. She didn’t look down. Didn’t need to. She could feel the tremor in Rio’s hands against her back with each pull of the cord.

    Agatha’s throat tightened, but she said nothing. She only stood there, allowing herself to be bound into protection.

    Rio’s fingers were still trembling at Agatha’s back when something else caught Lilia’s eye. She turned her head toward the corner of the room, where Rio’s satchel lay slouched against the leg of a chair.

    Something inside it pulsed once, faint as a coal hidden under ash.

    Lilia set down the poker she’d been using to stir the fire and crossed the floor without a word. She knelt, pulled open the flap, and drew out the stone cupped in both hands.

    It was smaller than her palm but gave off a muted glow, a color that didn’t belong to firelight or torch. It was the same green-gold as moss catching the first glimpse of dawn.

    Behind her, Agatha felt Rio’s whole body stiffen. “That’s mine.”

    “Is it?” Lilia murmured, turning it carefully, her scarred eye clouded, but her good one bright with intent. “Stones like this don’t belong to anyone.”

    Agatha shifted, her gaze darting between them. “What is it?”

    “Not stone,” Lilia said. She held it closer to the fire, and the glow deepened, swallowing flame like it fed on it. “Scale.”

    Rio blinked, her jaw tightening. “Scale?”

    Lilia nodded. “Long hardened. Returned to the land and weathered into the shape of rock.

    But I’d know it anywhere.”

    She finally looked up at Rio, weighing her like a piece of steel. “It is a gift to carry one.. To be chosen.”

    “I wasn’t chosen - it’s just a token. In the attic where I grew up there-”

    “You think you found it, but stones like this only ever find their way into the hands they want. And when they wake…”

    Lilia pressed it back into Rio’s palm, curling her fingers firmly around it.

    “…they demand blood before they give back what was thought lost.”

    The glow dimmed to nothing, as though soothed by Rio’s touch.

    “I got a few extra arrows.” Alice mumbled under her breath, “In case anyone is interested.” Alice’s mutter cut through the heavy silence like a hammer nicking metal. Agatha let out a short breath that was not quite a laugh. It bought them all a moment to breathe again.

    Alice slung her bow higher on her shoulder and stepped toward the door. She paused only long enough to extend her forearm to Rio.

    The knight looked at it for a heartbeat, then clasped it. Their grips locked, hard enough to whiten both of their knuckles.

    Rio murmured something in an old tongue, so soft even Agatha barely caught the cadence. It sounded more like a benediction than a farewell.

    Alice held her gaze, something sharp in her eyes, and answered simply: “And also yours.”

    She left without looking back.

    The door shut, and the room seemed smaller without her.

    Rio stood still, her hand flexing once at her side. Then, as though she’d wrestled every frayed edge of herself back into armor, she crossed the space to Agatha.

    Her composure was steady now, but her eyes betrayed her.

    She dropped to one knee, the old reflex. But instead of bowing her head, she caught Agatha’s hand. She turned it palm-up, pressed her lips to it once, soft as a vow.

    Her breath lingered there, warm against Agatha’s skin, before she finally let go.

    “My lady,” Rio whispered. It sounded nothing like duty.

    Then she stood, squared her shoulders, and walked out.

    Agatha stayed very still, her fingers tingling where Rio had kissed them.

    At last, she turned to Lilia. “Tell me,” she said, her voice cutting through the hush.

    “You’ve seen it. You know. Do we succeed?”

    The seer did not answer at once. She stood unmoving by the fire, the stone’s afterglow fading from her scarred palm.

    Finally, her voice came low.

    “Yes. The Queen will fall.”

    Relief surged through her, but the warmth it carried curdled as Lilia’s one clear eye fixed on her.

    “But you should know,” she added softly, “she will not fall alone.”
