    Just Chapter Summary A queen of shadows, a knight of steel, a witch of flame. A ward is found, a sacrifice is made, a boy who lives, a dragon who breathes.

    To battle.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes A few blocks away, a bell tolled the quarter hour.

    Alice slid a hand into her satchel and drew out the ring Lilia had pressed into her palm just minutes ago. Black iron, set with a seam of dull glass like a trapped tear.

    “This will help you. Borrow the world’s inattention,” Lilia had said. “Keep your eyes down, your hands busy. Don’t be spoken to by name. Don’t speak your own. Walk like you belong, and their gaze will slide off you.”

    Alice turned the ring between finger and thumb. The glass seam caught a shy ember of lamplight. She wondered if being unseen could unmake a curse. Lilia hadn’t promised her that.

    Only time.

    “Alice.”

    She stopped her fidgeting and turned back towards the smithy.

    Rio stood a few paces down the alley, half in torchlight, half in shadow. Her eyes were rimmed red, her face carved hollow.

    Alice had imagined this moment before: facing the First Knight, with the old sharp burn of being overlooked stinging in her gut. But envy withered against the ache in Rio’s eyes.

    It was the gaze of someone whittled down to an oath, a promise worn so long it had scoured away the self beneath.

    Perhaps not being chosen had spared Alice after all. Not from solitude. Not from the curse that haunted her blood. But from this, the hollowing that came when a person was carved into nothing but what others demanded.

    Agatha had seemed to carry it too.

    The Empire gilded its chains; the Wilds whittled theirs from bone. Different masters, but the same ultimate cost. Neither had left room for what they might have been, had they simply been allowed to live.

    Wind slid down the alley, lifting the loose ends of Rio’s hair. For a heartbeat, she looked young. Like the girl who might’ve run barefoot through ferns, who might’ve laughed with a mouth full of river water. Then the armor closed back over her.

    “I meant what I said in the tunnels,” Rio murmured. “I’m glad we walk the same path. I only wish they had crossed differently.” She shook her head. “I do not know where this one ends.” Alice nodded, turning the ring over between her thumb and forefinger.

    “If you reach him first,” Rio added, voice scraped thin, “take the prince to ground. Cellars. Servants’ stairs. Get him out. I’ll draw the heat.”

    “What about Billy?”

    “If you see him, tell him to keep breathing.” The start of a smile flickered but died just as quickly. “He forgets when he’s afraid.”

    “I can do that.”

    “Thank you.”

    Alice hesitated, then said the only thing she trusted to hold. “Try not to die.”

    The smallest half-laugh cracked Rio’s mouth. “You too.”

    Alice lifted her hand, and Rio met it. There was no warrior’s clasp this time, just the brief press of forearms.

    She slipped the ring on.

    The glass seam cooled against her skin. The alley tilted. The city’s gaze skimmed over her like rain over oiled leather. The world did not forget her, but it looked past her. For a time at least.

    And a few precious moments were all they needed.

    “Go,” Rio said.

    Alice drew her hood and stepped into the current of the city. Head down, gait unremarkable, she let the street’s attention slide from her to louder things: a shout at a corner, the clatter of a cart, the hiss of a guard’s torch.

    She was no one. And no one would notice her.

    The streets narrowed as Rio closed on the palace, smoke rising from braziers and torches, guards shouting somewhere beyond the walls. She did not skulk, did not hide. Every step was measured, loud enough to echo off the stones, deliberate enough to draw notice.

    Let them see her. Let them follow.

    Let them try.

    By the time she reached the outer gate, shadows peeled off the walls behind her, steel flashing, boots pounding. She did not break her stride.

    Her hand brushed the hilt at her side, the scale thrumming faintly in her satchel.

    She thought of Agatha’s dream, the smoke curling from the chimney of a cottage that did not yet exist. She let the image burn in her chest and propel her faster.

    The guards fell on her before she reached the palace steps, but Rio was already in motion. Steel hissed as she parried, cut low, and drove one to the ground. Another guard lunged from the side. She caught his wrist and broke it clean, the sound cracking through the courtyard. When the last one faltered, Rio slammed him into the wall so hard his helm rang like a struck bell. She pinned him there with the flat of her forearm across his chest, blade angled beneath his jaw. His pulse thrashed against the steel, frantic as a trapped bird.

    “Speak,” she said, voice low, precise. “How many in the hall? Where does she wait?”

    The guard’s mouth opened and closed, soundless. Sweat slicked the rim of his brow beneath the helmet.

    Rio pressed the blade just enough to let him feel the bite, not yet the cut. “If you lie, I’ll know. And I’ll carve the truth out of you.”

    His eyes rolled, white and panicked. “The throne room,” he gasped. “She—she hasn’t left it in days.”

    Rio’s jaw ticked. “How many guard her?”

    “None,” he wheezed. “She said she needed no shield. Only her voice. Only her… shadow.”

    Rio studied him, weighing his words against the tremor in his throat. Then, slowly, she eased the blade back. Relief shuddered out of him—too soon.

    With a swift motion, she knocked him unconscious with the pommel of her sword. He sagged sideways, crumpling onto the stones, alive but irrelevant.

    She stepped back, chest heaving once, steadying again. The courtyard smelled of blood and torch oil, heavy and acrid.

    The palace loomed ahead.

    She rolled her shoulders, resettled her grip. She could walk in now, her fury burning hot enough to take the whole place apart brick by brick.

    But instead, her hand brushed the satchel at her side, the faint thrum of the scale pulsing against her hip.

    And with it, a memory.

    Thirteen, back in the Wilds, blade unsteady in her hand as her mentor circled her in the clearing. Their staff tapped once against the dirt.

    “Too tight,” they said. “You hold the sword like it owes you something.”

    Rio gritted her teeth. “It does. I hold it, it holds my life.”

    They smiled, tired and knowing. “Then you will die with cramped hands. A blade isn’t a debt, Rio. It’s a breath. You take it in, you let it out. Too much grasping, and you’ll cut yourself.”

    She hadn’t understood then.

    Not until just now, with fresh blood drying on her armor.

    She exhaled, steadying, loosening her fingers on the hilt until it once again moved like part of her arm.

    Only then did she once more put one boot in front of the other.

    The steps to the palace were slick with ash, and she left bloody prints as she climbed.

    The great doors stood ajar. No guards at the threshold. No heralds, no resistance.

    Rio stepped across the threshold and finally caught a sound. It was so out of place, it took her a moment to identify the long weep of strings.

    A violin, bow dragging teeteringly, notes curling through the cavernous hall.

    The throne room yawned before her, its gilded pillars casting warped reflections in the torchlight. The air smelled of incense, roses left too long to rot in their vases, and something acrid beneath.

    Her boots struck the marble with each step, echoing against the vaulted ceiling. No one moved to stop her. She realized then that Evanora wanted this walk. The queen wanted her to feel each yard, each heartbeat, as the music wound around her like a noose.

    The violinist was a boy, no older than twelve, standing in the far corner. His bow trembled slightly as he played, eyes fixed not on Rio but on the figure reclined at the dais. He was gangly and thin. Rio wondered how long he had been made to stand there.

    Evanora sat waiting, draped in silks dark as spilled ink. She didn’t rise, didn’t so much as tilt her head. Her crown caught the light, a thin line of fire above the shadow of her face.

    “You’ve made quite the entrance, good Ser,” she said at last, voice carrying easily over the strings. “The city sleeps. My guards bleed. And still you come to kneel.”

    The violin struck a harsher note then, bow scraping just off true, but the Queen didn’t flinch. Rio didn’t answer. She simply kept walking, blade loose in her hand, each step louder than the music, but not enough to drown out her own heartbeat.

    “You’ve emptied your halls,” Rio said. “Not out of fear. Out of arrogance.”

    Evanora smiled. “I see both in the way you carry yourself. In your certainty that steel alone makes you righteous.”

    Her gaze slid over Rio. “Tell me, Ser, how many did you cut down on your way here? Do you think it makes your oath brighter, all that blood in the gutters? Or do you see it for what it is, shadow feeding shadow?”

    Rio didn’t rise to the bait. She let the blade tilt in her hand, a line of reflected torchlight running its length.

    “I swore to stand between the light of this world and the shadows cast upon it.”

    At that, Evanora finally let her smile fall. She leaned forward, one long finger tapping the arm of her throne in idle rhythm with the violin’s bow. “And what happens, knight, when those you swore to shield carry my mark, wear my name, breathe at my mercy?”

    The violin swelled, an ugly sweetness, filling the pause.

    Rio’s grip tightened, but she forced her breath to steady. She would not let the Queen see her falter.

    “I’ll carve the shadow from the body,” she said. “And leave them free to stand on their own feet again.”

    For the first time, Evanora laughed, low and lilting, rich with cruelty. “Ah. And there it is. That beautiful self-deception. No wonder my daughter clings to you. You make her believe in fairy tales again.”

    The queen rose, each movement languid, her gown whispering across the marble as she stepped off the dais.

    “You tell yourself stories, knight.” Her voice was velvet over steel. “Perhaps because you had no parents to tell them to you. Shall I guess the shape?”

    She lifted her hand, tracing images in the air as though she were painting them.

    “A cottage. Quaint. Smoke curling from a hearth where no blade hangs. Inside… a princess untangled from her crown. A boy whose muddy feet are the gravest trial of your days. A world so small you might keep it safe.”

    Her smile sharpened. “Fantasy tales.”

    Rio did not answer.

    Evanora stepped closer. “Do you think you are the first to dream this? The first to stand here, heart burning, blade raised, imagining you might unmake me?”

    Her lip curved. “No. But you may be the one my daughter mourns most.”

    She let the name roll out like poison. “Agatha.”

    The violin snagged a string, shrill, as Evanora’s eyes glittered.

    “I smelled the Wilds on you from the first, the dirt in your blood. Did you think me blind? I let it be. I thought it charming, once, that my daughter’s shadow should drag strays into her orbit. And when you both vanished, I rejoiced. It spared me the trouble of killing her myself. It gave me time.”

    She turned slowly, the silks of her gown dragging across the marble. “Time to reign as regent until the boy was of age. Time to shape him into an heir this empire deserves.”

    The music bent into an ugly sweetness.

    “And yet you return.” The queen's tone cooled.

    “That does not trouble me either. I prefer it this way. It is better to tie up my loose ends with my own hands. My only mistake was mercy. Twenty years ago, I might have scoured every ember of your kind from the Wilds. A single stroke, and gone.”

    Her hand cut the air like a knife. “Instead, I stayed it. I thought sparing some of the forgotten might prove wisdom. Prudence. Strength.”

    She leaned close, her breath cold against Rio’s cheek. “I have since learned. Mercy is the coward’s refuge. Every ember must be crushed. Every shadow scattered. Lest the flame rise again.”

    “There was a world where you got to live out your fantasy. If you could have only stayed away. So tell me, while you still draw breath, why have you come?”

    Rio’s hand clenched on her hilt, the old training whispering: strike now, end it, drive clean between the ribs. Her body ached to obey, every nerve begging for the release of action.

    But beneath the roar of her pulse, another voice coiled, the one she had carried since her mentor first set a blade in her palm.

    You are not just the weapon. You choose the cut. You decide what the steel becomes.

    Rio’s chest heaved once, shallow, then steadied. She let the breath out slowly, like drawing a bowstring. The image of Agatha flickered behind her eyes: the smirk she used to hide fear, the toss of her hair, a flash of blue eyes that shamed sapphires.

    That was why she was here. Not for vengeance. Not for duty alone.

    For them.

    “Why have I come?” The blade lifted, an extension of her will, gleaming between them. “To end the shadow that smothers everything it touches.”

    She drew her arm back, every muscle taut with purpose. For the first time since stepping into the throne room, Rio believed she could win.

    This was the moment.

    This was the strike.

    “Do it then.” Evanora sneered, taking a step forward and lifting her jaw.

    “Rio. Wait.”

    The voice cut across the hall. Footsteps followed... slow, dragging, uneven.

    Rio’s arm stilled mid-strike.

    From the shadows, a boy emerged. A blade dangled in his grip, as if it belonged to someone else.

    Her heart lurched, breath tearing short. “No.”

    Billy.

    He moved with stuttering jerks, his limbs obeying not will but strings, like a puppet pulled too hard by an unseen hand. Every falter in his step screamed wrong, yet his eyes—blank, unblinking—were the worst of all.

    Evanora’s voice softened, mocking. “A squire should follow where his knight leads. You left him behind, and so I gave him purpose.”

    Rio’s blade wavered, still pointed at her throat. “Release him.”

    Evanora leaned into the steel until it drew the faintest bead of blood. “Strike me down, and you strike him too. My blood is his blood now. Do you dare?”

    Billy’s arm lifted, blade trembling but aimed at Rio’s heart. His face flickered—pain, fear, then blankness again. She could see a mirrored bead of crimson trickling down the hollow of his throat.

    Rio’s chest seized. She wanted to drop her sword. She wanted to throw it away, catch him, shake him free. But Evanora’s magic coiled tight, and Billy’s body lurched forward another step.

    Evanora’s smile widened. “So tell me, knight. Will you cut down a child to save your princess? Or will you stay your hand and watch her burn?”

    The weight of the sword dragged heavily in Rio’s grip. For the first time since she’d lifted it, her hands trembled.

    Elsewhere, Agatha pushed open a door that should have been barred.

    The back stairs spat her into a narrow passage, air stale with dust and old stone. No guards. No locks. Only the quiet creak of a hinge as she eased into the room beyond.

    Nicholas sat cross-legged on his bed, a book propped in his lap, lips moving faintly as he read to himself. The lantern on his nightstand threw a soft glow over his curls, over the small frown of concentration that vanished the moment he looked up.

    “You came back!” The book tumbled from his hands as he scrambled across the mattress.

    Agatha caught him before his feet hit the floor. His weight was solid, his laugh warm in her ear, and for a heartbeat, she let the world shrink to just this.

    The smell of parchment and beeswax candles wafting through the air. The brush of his hair against her cheek. The feel of his little arms locked around her neck as though he could anchor her here forever.

    She pressed her lips to his temple, eyes shut tight.

    “I promised I would.”

    Her boy snuggled closer against her chest.

    “I was very good while you were away. Like you said.”

    Her throat tightened, but she managed a low hum. “Were you now?”

    He nodded fiercely, curls brushing her chin. She almost believed in the safety of this room, the hush of lanternlight, the spell of his small voice anchoring her here.

    “Are we going somewhere?” Nicholas asked, wide-eyed, unaware of the chaos clawing through the city outside. “Do I need my boots?”

    Agatha drew back just enough to look at him. Her hands framed his face, memorizing every line, every shade of his eyes. She smiled, though her chest ached.

    “Soon,” she said. “But for now, we stay. Just for a little while. Just us.”

    She sat on the edge of his bed, pulling him close again, letting him chatter for a moment about the book, about the story he hadn’t finished. She listened, every word carving itself into her like scripture.

    The moment they left this room, nothing would ever be the same again.

    So she took a breath and allowed her child to be a child.

    Agatha smiled and nodded, storing away every syllable, every curl of his hair against her shoulder, as if she could keep them somewhere the world could never touch.

    A knock broke through the quiet, and Agatha’s spine went rigid. Her hand stilled on Nicky’s back.

    “Under the bed,” she whispered, urgent but even. His eyes widened, but he obeyed without protest, crawling beneath the frame just as her hand flicked the quilt down to hide him.

    The knock came again, followed by the slow turn of the handle.

    Agatha’s breath narrowed. She moved back, palms tingling, every nerve braced.

    The door crept open, shadows bending as the lantern light spilled across the floorboards. A face appeared in the crack, unfamiliar and half-hidden.

    She didn’t think. Couldn’t. Instinct swallowed any reason whole.

    All that mattered was keeping her son safe.

    Her magic lashed out, fast and merciless, snapping like a scythe across the room.

    The intruder dropped before the door had even finished opening, a body crumpling against the threshold with a sound that seemed to hollow the air.

    The ring hit the floor first, skittering across the planks. The spell unraveled.

    Alice’s face was the one left staring up at her.

    Agatha’s breath fractured. For a moment, she couldn’t move, couldn’t think. Her hands, still crackling faintly with the aftershock of power, hovered uselessly in the air.

    Alice’s body lay slack, a dark bloom spreading beneath her head. The sight clawed at Agatha’s chest, panic and disbelief colliding so violently that it nearly drove her to her knees. Her heart lurched against her ribs, bile burning the back of her throat. She wanted to reach for her, to undo it somehow, but her body betrayed her—frozen, trembling.

    “What was that?”

    Agatha’s head snapped toward the bed. Nicky’s wide eyes blinked out at her from beneath the coverlet, his face haloed by dust motes. His voice was calm, curious, untouched by the blood in the air.

    “Is it safe now?”

    Her throat closed. She forced her hands to lower, fingers curling into her palms to hide their shaking.

    “Yes,” she whispered, though the word nearly tore her in two. “Yes, my love. It’s safe.”

    And she turned her body, just enough to block his view of the woman she had killed.

    Agatha’s hands shook as she picked up the ring from the floor. The glass seam caught the light.

    She slipped it onto Nicholas’s small hand. The band looked too large for him, but as it settled, the shimmer of it folded around his body, softening his edges, dimming him into the background.

    “We’re going to play a game,” she whispered, pressing her forehead to his as she lifted him over the threshold, past Alice, and into the hall.

    “What kind of game?”

    “Hide and seek. You’re going to find the best hiding place you can. You won’t come out, no matter what you hear. Not until I come for you. Do you understand?”

    His eyes, wide and solemn, searched hers. “Okay.”

    She kissed his temple once more, heart breaking against his small, steady warmth. “What are you waiting for?”

    He blinked at her, a flicker of mischief lighting his face. “For you to start counting.”

    It cut her so deeply she almost laughed. Almost. In that flash, she swore she saw Rio in him —the tilt of the mouth, the smirk that carried so much love.

    She swallowed hard. “One,” she said hoarsely.

    That was all he needed. Nicholas slipped away, ring shimmering once as he darted into shadow, already gone.

    Agatha stayed only long enough to breathe, to fix the memory of him in her chest. Then she rose, turned toward the throne room, and walked toward the fire.

    The great doors groaned at her touch.

    Agatha stepped through, the reek of iron still clinging to her hands, leather runes biting at her ribs with every breath.

    The hall opened wide and terrible before her: Rio, with her blade raised, steel poised at Evanora’s throat.

    The queen smiling with a feral glee.

    Some boy in the far corner, bow trembling as the violin wheezed out a dying note.

    Billy, dragged from the shadows, glass-eyed, a blade clutched awkwardly in his hand.

    Bile rose in her throat as Evanora leaned forward to whisper something to Rio.

    “Stop!”

    The violin shrieked and cut off.

    Evanora’s smile turned toward her. “Daughter. You do pick your moments.” She didn’t so much as flinch at the sword kissing her skin.

    “Leather charms stitched by a blacksmith? Borrowed courage.” Her gaze dropped to Agatha’s bare hands. “No rings. No crown. How small you look.”

    Agatha’s voice sharpened to a single edge. “Let them go.”

    “They breathe because I allow it.” Evanora tilted her head, “As do you. As does your son.” Rio shifted back to guard her flank. Agatha stepped wide, forcing the queen’s gaze to flick between them.

    Billy’s arm jerked higher at Evanora’s gesture, blade quivering toward Rio’s chest. “You left your stray in my halls,” she said. “I gave him purpose. You should be thankful.”

    “Billy,” Rio whispered, steady as she could manage. “Breathe.”

    His chest hitched once.

    Evanora’s lips curled. “Kneel, and perhaps I will show mercy.”

    This time Agatha didn’t blink. “You have mistaken me for someone else. Someone afraid.”

    As her voice rose, the satchel at Rio’s side hummed. The scale’s glow seeped through the leather, moss-gold against the marble floor.

    Evanora’s head snapped toward it.

    Her hand slashed down. “Strike.”

    Billy lunged.

    Rio twisted, taking the blow to her side rather than cutting the boy down. Steel tore clean through leather and skin. Her blood spattered the steps, bright against the white marble—and splashed across the satchel.

    Agatha’s eyes snapped to it, to the inlaid dragon coils etched around the dais. Her runes burned against her ribs. Understanding struck like lightning.

    She held out her hand. “Rio.”

    Rio tore the satchel free and hurled it. The scale arced, molten-bright and bloodstained, into Agatha’s palm. It seared her skin, drank in the blood, and the corset’s runes flared in reply. Agatha dragged her thumb hard, smearing blood over the scale. Then she raised her hands and screamed.

    The marble split. Torchflames bent inward as if the hall itself drew breath. The violin string snapped with a whip-crack, the boy collapsing in a whimpering sob.

    From beneath the throne, a sound was not heard but felt. The slow, resonant thrum of a heart the size of the city.

    Evanora lashed out, shadow spilling in a torrent of black veins, thick and fast. It wasn’t merely darkness, it carried a shriek inside it, a wail like glass tearing through bone.

    The force clawed for the nearest bodies, tendrils latching at Billy first, dragging at the whites of his eyes, his mouth falling open as if something else meant to breathe through him. Then it swept for the guards slumped at the doors, their limbs jerking like marionettes.

    This wasn't a summoning but a scattering. Splinters of her own life-force racing for vessels to wear.

    Agatha braced for the lash, heat gathering at her ribs. For an instant, the shadow brushed her cheek, and she felt it. Evanora’s voice not as words but hunger, whispering against her blood, searching for a crack it could slip through.

    The hall filled with a banshee’s chorus, every tendril keening with its own distorted pitch, as though the queen’s spirit had been splintered into a hundred shrieking mouths.

    Agatha’s runes flared white-hot. She thrust her hands outward, a wall of power bursting from her chest. The tendrils recoiled with a hiss, writhing back like smoke torn by wind. For a moment the shrieking chorus faltered.

    Evanora only laughed. “You would deny me a vessel? Then I’ll claim the one you’ve already bound your soul to.”

    Her hand snapped, and the shadows whipped like lashes straight for Rio.

    Agatha screamed, but the tendrils struck before her voice could reach. They coiled tight around Rio’s throat and chest, burrowing in, sinking. Her blade slipped in her grip, her eyes flooding black as ink.

    “Please,” Agatha cried, stumbling toward her. “Please, my love. Stay with me.”

    For an instant, something flickered—the faintest tremor at the corner of Rio’s mouth, like she’d heard. But the shadow closed around her again.

    Billy, freed in the chaos, lunged forward. “Rio! Wake up! Fight it!”

    Rio’s head snapped toward him, blank and merciless. Her fist struck hard across his temple. The boy crumpled, body tumbling across the marble, stunned but alive.

    Agatha caught the shallow rise of his chest—proof of breath, nothing more.

    Evanora’s voice split the air. “Fall on your sword, knight.”

    Rio’s body obeyed. Shaking, unsteady, but obeying. She turned the blade toward herself, the point kissing the fabric over her stomach.

    “No!” Agatha screamed, and the runes across her blazed wild, spreading and curling down her arms. She flung her hands skyward, magic ripping from her like a storm unbound. Sparks erupted, scorching the air, splitting the marble.

    Then the roof shook.

    A roar like mountains breaking open split the hall.

    The wall above the dais fractured, stone tumbling in a hail of dust and fire.

    A head broke the surface, vast and terrible—scale-veined marble, ember eyes burning awake after decades. A dragon’s first exhale rolled through the hall, scattering incense smoke and shadows alike.

    Then came the wings—vast, scaled, alight with a gold-green shimmer that turned the torches pale. The shadows screamed, keening like a thousand voices at once.

    The dragon roared back.

    Evanora staggered once, shadow coiling back into her skin. For the first time, the mask of amusement slipped. “Fools,” she hissed. “You think this thing answers to you?”

    The dragon lifted higher, heat warping the gold from the walls. Its gaze fixed first on Agatha, runes searing; then on Rio, bloodied and unbowed.

    Then it turned its head toward Evanora.

    And roared again.

    The sound shattered windows. The gilt peeled from the columns. Every shadow Evanora had clung to tore loose and fled.

    The dragon’s fire licked the dais, burning away half the throne, cutting off her retreat. She shrieked, shadows tearing as fast as she could knit them.

    Rio’s blade cleaved through the dark, each strike ripping tendrils away before they could latch. Her movements were vicious and precise. She drove Evanora back step by step, every cut a refusal, every parry a vow.

    “Kneel,” Rio snarled, her voice low and feral. She swept her blade across Evanora’s knees with such force that the queen collapsed forward, catching herself on clawed fingers.

    “Kneel before your monarch, witch.”

    Evanora lifted her head, and for the first time, fear cracked her face.

    Agatha stepped forward. The runes blazed along her arms, across her throat, alive with a light that made the dragon bow its massive head in recognition. She reached out, fingers trembling.

    “You wanted to feed on others,” Agatha whispered. “Now you’ll feel what it is to be devoured.”

    She pressed her hand flat against Evanora’s chest.

    The siphon began.

    Evanora screamed. Her body arched, tendrils shriveling into smoke. Her skin withered in seconds, beauty collapsing into brittle ruin. Hair blanched white, flesh sagged, bones strained against parchment skin. She clawed at Agatha, but her fingers broke like twigs.

    Her crown slid from her brow and clattered to the floor.

    “Daughter,” she croaked, lips shriveled, “please—”

    Agatha leaned closer, voice like ice. “I am not your daughter.”

    The last of the queen’s breath rattled out of her, her body collapsing into dust and ash that scattered across the marble.

    The hall went still but for the dragon’s low growl, reverberating deep in the stone.

    Agatha straightened, chest heaving, the glow of her runes dimming slowly.

    Behind her, Rio planted her blade tip-down into the stone floor and bowed her head.

    “Your Majesty.”

    The title hung in the wreckage, heavier than ash.

    Agatha’s throat tightened. She stepped toward her, bent, and cupped Rio’s jaw. She urged her chin up until their eyes met.

    “No,” she whispered. Her thumb brushed across blood and grit. “Just Agatha. Just yours.”

    Rio’s breath stuttered. Then she rose until they were standing face-to-face.

    Agatha leaned into her, their foreheads touching, holding each other upright as the throne behind them smoldered to ruin.

    For the first time, she was not queen, not witch, not heir.

    Just Agatha.

    And she was Rio’s to hold.

    Chapter End Notes Only the epilogue remains.

    Ever After Chapter Summary An epilogue.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes A stone arced through the air; there was too much force behind the toss, and it promptly sank beneath the surface.

    “Again, buinneán.”

    A second stone quickly returned to the river it had just been plucked from, and Agatha laughed.

    Her toes sank into cool moss from her perch higher up on the river bank. She watched as her son flung stone after stone, each one awkward arcs and large splashes no matter how many times Rio patiently adjusted his grip.

    “Like this, keep your arm straight, like you’re trying to carve a blade over the water.”

    As the stone left Rio’s hand, it skipped several times. Agatha lost count as she got distracted by her son’s laughter, high and bright, chasing the sound of the river itself.

    Rio bent to grab another one, but Nicky squealed in protest.

    “You can’t throw that one!” he cried, snatching the stone from Rio’s palm. “It’s too pretty.” “Too pretty to skip?”

    “Yes! Look, it shines. I think it’s magic.”

    “Maybe so.”

    He tugged insistently at her elbow until she bent down to meet him. He cupped his hands around her ear, whispering in a rush.

    “I see,” she said, nodding. “A grave responsibility.”

    Nicky grinned and darted along the bank, scanning the shallows for more treasures, his pockets already sagging with “gems” that were smeared with mud.

    Rio dusted her palms on her trousers and climbed the short slope back to Agatha. Sinking onto the moss beside her with a sigh, she braced her forearms across her knees.

    For a while, they both watched the boy’s small figure weaving among the rocks, his voice carrying in excited bursts whenever he spotted another glimmer.

    There was time for quiet now. And neither of them were in a rush to break it.

    “He’s searching for pretty stones,” Rio said at last, the corner of her mouth tilting upward. She angled her head toward Agatha. “To give to his mama.”

    Agatha’s chest tightened in that peculiar, tender way it always did when Rio was near. She was getting used to it. It didn’t frighten her as it once did.

    She slid her hand across the moss until her fingers brushed Rio’s.

    “He’s a sweet boy.”

    “Like his mother,” Rio confirmed.

    It had been startlingly simple, the way he accepted the truth that Agatha was his mother. When she first told him, Nicky had tilted his head, considered it, and then declared she was too old to be his sister anyway.

    He’d added, with childlike certainty, that she always did things mamas did, like reading to him, telling stories until he fell asleep, helping him with his boots when he pretended not to need help at all.

    The next day, he found Rio alone, cleaning a knife by the fire.

    “Are you my mama too?” he asked, voice low and conspiratorial.

    Rio looked up, caught off guard. “Why would you ask me that?”

    “Because,” Nicky said seriously, “you’re teaching me things. Like how to fish. And you don’t get mad when I climb the tallest trees. You climb with me. And you help me when I get stuck.”

    His little brow furrowed, as though working through something complicated. “That’s what mamas do too, I think.”

    Rio’s mouth parted, but couldn’t manage what to say. Something in her chest loosened, unsteady and frightening in its depth. She reached out, ruffling his curls until he swatted at her hand with a grin.

    “I don’t know what title I fit. I’ve had too many,” she admitted softly. “But I love you. And I love your mama. And together…” She pressed her palm against his back, steadying him as though the swell of affection itself might carry him away. “Together we are a family. Just the three of us.”

    Nicky beamed, wide and certain. “Okay,” he said, and as though sealing the promise, he leaned against her side.

    Rio bent to press her lips to his hair. Before she could overthink it she whispered against the crown of his head, “Love you, little fox.”

    She didn’t tell Agatha about their conversation. Weeks later, when Nicky called “Mama, look!” and they both turned, Agatha didn’t seem surprised in the slightest.

    “What are the odds of him catching that frog he’s chasing?” Agatha asked now, watching Nicky dart after a splash of movement between the reeds.

    Rio leaned back on her palms, squinting toward the commotion. “About the same as you kissing one, I imagine.”

    Agatha laughed, bumping her shoulder against hers. “Bet you would kiss one though, wouldn’t you?”

    “Of course. They’re majestic creatures.”

    “If you do that,” Agatha warned with mock severity, “you’ll never kiss me again.”

    Rio shifted, the humor sparking into something softer as she caught Agatha’s eyes. “Don’t make promises you can’t keep, my love.”

    For a moment, all she could hear was Nicky’s delighted shriek as the frog eluded him again. Rio leaned in slightly, her voice quieter now. “Lilia sent word.”

    “About?”

    “The Festival of Renewal,” Rio said, brushing her thumb along the moss at her side. “She offered to keep an eye on Nicky so we could go. Just us.”

    Agatha made a face, sharp and certain. “No.”

    Though she wasn’t surprised, Rio huffed a laugh. “You didn’t even think about it.”

    “I’ve thought about it enough for a lifetime.” Agatha shook her head. “The empire has had its fill of my life. Let them build their own pageantry. Something new should rise. Not me. And there’s not a world where I’d pass that burden to him.”

    For a moment, Rio only studied her profile, the way her gaze followed their son, weaving between reeds. At last she said, quietly, “Maybe. That may be a little naïve.”

    Agatha’s lips pressed together, but she didn’t argue. She just slid her hand over Rio’s where it rested in the moss, twining their fingers together.

    Rio squeezed once, letting the subject drop, and murmured, “Peace looks good on you.”

    “Honey, everything looks good on me.”

    “True. But some things look better without half the kingdom watching.”

    Agatha turned her head, smirking now. “Careful, knight. That almost sounded like an invitation.”

    “We could send Nicky to Lilia anyway,” Rio said, leaning in, her grin slow and sly. “A day. A night. Just us.”

    “And what exactly would we do with all that time?”

    Rio’s gaze dipped to Agatha’s mouth, still kiss swollen from a few stolen moments this morning. “I have a few ideas.”

    The space between them shrank until a triumphant squeal broke it.

    Nicky bounded up the bank, curls wild, hands cupped around something wriggling. “Mama!

    Look what I found!”

    Before Agatha could protest, a fat green frog was plopped directly into her lap.

    She shrieked, flinging her hands in the air, while Rio doubled over in laughter.

    “I think he likes you,” she managed between gasps.

    Agatha shot her a murderous look, the frog still clinging to her skirts. “You taught him to do this,” she hissed, “and you are sleeping outside tonight.”

    “Worth it.”

    But it was an empty threat.

    That night Rio took her place in bed next to Agatha, Nicky snuggled up between them, hair still damp from the river, curls sticking out every which way, cheeks pink with sun.

    Agatha smoothed a hand over his forehead, thumb brushing stray wisps aside. “Shall I tell you a story?” she asked softly. “About the fox who fell in love with—”

    Nicky scrunched his nose, already tucking his chin against Rio’s shoulder. “Heard that one.” Agatha feigned indignation. “Impossible. I only just thought of it.”

    “You told it before,” he said matter-of-factly. Then he jabbed a small elbow into Rio’s side. “You tell one.”

    Rio blinked, caught off guard. “Me?”

    “Yes,” Nicky said, utterly certain. His little hand patted her chest. “Your turn.”

    “Go on, knight. Let’s hear what kind of fairy tales you’ve got hidden away.” Agatha smirked, settling back against the pillows, her eyes glittering with amusement.

    Rio sighed dramatically, earning a giggle from Nicky. “Alright, buinneán. One story.” She paused, as though rummaging through her memories, then began in a low voice.

    “Once, long ago, there was a river that thought she was only good for carrying stones.”

    “Like we saw today!”

    “Mhm. She rushed and rushed, tumbling them smooth, until her whole body ached from the weight. And she thought, ‘This is all I am. A path for other things.’”

    Nicky’s eyes were wide, his small hand clutching the blanket beneath his chin. “What happened then?”

    Rio’s lips quirked. “One night, the river looked up. And she saw a star caught in her reflection. She thought it was only a trick of light.”

    “Rivers don’t have eyes!”

    Agatha shushed him and kissed his cheek, “Shh, this one does. Let her tell the story.”

    “Sorry.”

    “It’s okay. This river is magic, like the ones where I grew up. So it looked up and the star whispered down. ‘I see you. Not just the stones you carry, but the way you shine when you’re still.’”

    “Did the river believe it?”

    “Not at first. Rivers don’t think too much of themselves, you see. They just keep rushing on. But the star kept watching. Night after night, no matter how the current twisted, the star was there.”

    Nicky’s lashes fluttered, his voice drowsy. “What about the star? Didn’t she have her own sky?”

    Rio’s gaze slid briefly to Agatha, the corner of her mouth softening.

    “She did. But the skies can be lonely, too. The star thought it was worth the risk to fall down to earth. To see what it was like to rest in the river bed instead of shining all alone.” Beneath the blanket, Agatha laced their fingers together without a word.

    Nicky yawned. “So the star fell? Like a wishing star?”

    “She did,” Rio murmured, her voice as steady as the current she described. “And the river carried her as gently as she could.”

    His head lolled against her arm, his voice a whisper now. “And the star?”

    Rio glanced at Agatha, her thumb brushing lightly across the back of her hand. “Against the surface, the star shone twice as bright.”

    Nicky’s breathing deepened, drifting into the soft rhythm of sleep. The frog he’d insisted on keeping croaked faintly from the basket at the foot of the bed.

    For a moment, neither woman moved. They only watched their son, the curve of his cheek pressed to the pillow, the safety of him here in this small, quiet cottage.

    At last, Agatha turned, her voice low against Rio’s shoulder. “That was no fairy tale,” she whispered.

    Rio leaned her head back, closing her eyes as though she might rest there forever. “No,” she said softly. “It wasn’t.”

    Agatha let out a long, quiet breath, her forehead resting against Rio’s. And in the hush, with their son safe between them and the night holding them close, the world was still.

    Rio brushed her lips over Agatha’s temple in a kiss. “Sleep, sweetheart,” she whispered. “I’ll carry him to his bed.”

    Eyes half-lidded, Agatha tugged Rio closer until their mouths met, slow and unhurried. “Stay a little longer.”

    “As long as you’d like.”

    Some time later, Rio shifted carefully, slipping her arms beneath Nicky. He stirred, but only nestled closer, still dreaming.

    In his room, she drew the blanket up to his chin and bent to smooth a hand over his brow. That was when she saw, just for an instant, a shimmer beneath the skin at his temple, faint as fireflies, vanishing as quickly as it had come.

    The beginnings of a rune. Not yet whole, but unmistakable.

    Her breath caught and she pressed a kiss to his forehead as if to seal it away.

    When she left the room, she didn’t return to bed straight away. She stepped outside instead, letting the night air cool her flushed face.

    She tipped her head back, drawing in a long breath as the night pressed against her skin. In the distance, a river whispered over stones, steady and indifferent, and above, the stars burned sharp against the velvet dark.

    Her thoughts lingered on the shimmer she’d seen at Nicky’s temple. It hadn’t been the crude, jagged flare of cursed blood like Alice had carried, nor the searing blaze that once marked Agatha. This was gentler, curious, as though it had only just begun to recognize itself.

    A rune not yet written, waiting for a hand to guide it.

    Rio’s fingers brushed her own collarbone, where vines curled deep, scars of magic she had never been taught to wield, only to endure.

    She thought of Agatha’s runes, power bound tight to leather and blood, a gift and burden her mother had tried to twist into chains.

    Nicky’s path could be different.

    They could make it so.

    If they were careful, if they were kind, he could learn what they hadn’t been given. Not fear, not punishment. Patience. Curiosity.

    The way a rune might hum in the quiet before sleep, or flare bright in joy instead of rage. She smiled faintly, imagining Agatha bent over a page of ink and parchment, teaching Nicky to trace the shapes with stubborn precision while Rio showed him how to feel them in his breath, in his body, like the rhythm of the current or the flex of muscle before a strike. He would be stronger than both of them for it. He would be free.

    But for now, they would let him be a child. That was most important of all.

    The sound of soft steps behind her tugged her from the thought.

    Agatha slipped her arms around Rio’s waist, drawing her back against her. Her cheek pressed into the crook of Rio’s shoulder, her breath warm through the night chill.

    “You’re brooding,” Agatha murmured, half fond, half exasperated.

    “Only thinking,” Rio said quietly, her hand finding Agatha’s and covering it.

    “About him?”

    Rio nodded once, leaning back against her. “I don’t want him to grow up too fast.”

    Agatha squeezed her hand, her voice steady. “He won’t. Not while we’re around.”

    For a moment they stood like that, the hush of the night wrapping them close. Then, on the horizon, a sudden burst of light cracked the dark. Fireworks bloomed beyond the hills, their glow painting the underbellies of the clouds in shifting reds and golds.

    “The festival,” Agatha said, her voice unreadable.

    Rio hummed. “So it seems.”

    They watched in silence as another flare split the night sky. To Rio, the thunder of it felt far away, like something from another life. Here, in this quiet hollow, the world belonged only to them.

    “You don’t miss it at all?”

    Agatha tightened her hold, pressing closer against her back. “Let them have their revelry,” she whispered. “This is enough.”

    Rio turned her head, brushing her lips over Agatha’s temple, her gaze still on the horizon where the fireworks faded one by one. “Hm,” she murmured. “More than enough.”

    Above them, for the briefest moment, a vast shadow wheeled across the moon a sweep of wings, coils like smoke against silver.

    Agatha’s breath caught, but she didn’t flinch. She only followed Rio’s gaze upward, her hand tightening where it rested against Rio’s chest.

    “Do you think it watches us still?” she asked softly.

    Rio’s mouth curved, not quite a smile, not quite solemn. “I think it remembers us. As the ones who set it free.”

    The shadow passed, leaving only the moon, bright and unbroken. In the quiet that followed, the last of the fireworks guttered away, their light swallowed by the night.

    Agatha turned in Rio’s arms until they faced one another. The hush pressed close, broken only by the murmur of the river and the distant crackle of fireworks fading against the sky. “Then let it,” Agatha whispered, her thumb tracing the line of Rio’s jaw. “We have grander adventures ahead than witches and dragons.”

    Rio bent, pressing their foreheads together. And for the first time in a long time, she let herself believe.

    The dragon might still circle the moon. The empire might raise its throne again. But here, with Agatha’s heartbeat steady against her own and their son sleeping safe inside, there was no need for any other kingdom.

    Not when she already could hold the world in her arms.
